# Act 1 · Why buy

## EP 01 · Every way to buy a business, from 100% down to 0% down (the 3C map)

There are two types of people who want to own a business. The ones who save until they can afford one. And the ones who work out the business can pay for itself.

I'm Josh Li. At 25 I was unemployed, with no savings and no experience. My first business was a physio clinic. I put in no deposit. The seller was paid over time, out of the clinic's own profit.

Here's every way to buy a business, from 100 percent down to zero, and the five-stage map that gets you to the bottom of that ladder.

Take a business making 300 thousand a year in profit. At four times profit, it's priced at 1.2 million. How you fund that 1.2 million decides how much of the 300 thousand you keep, how much of the company you own, and who gets the exit. Same business. Very different outcomes.

Before I owned anything I worked in banking. More than 30 million dollars of deals by 21, and I've been involved in over 100 million of transactions. Now I run a healthcare group full time.

This series is for owners, executives and people with capital who want to own cash flow. If you want a four-hour week, it isn't for you.

You were told you need a deposit. You were told the bank decides. You were told that if you don't have the money, you raise it.

None of that is the whole picture. The cheapest money in most deals is the seller's. The scarcest thing is credibility.

There are four levels of funding a business. Most people are stuck on Level 2. And if you've been stuck there, it has nothing to do with your salary or your discipline. It has everything to do with the fact that nobody showed you Levels 3 and 4.

I help owners and operators do exactly this, through the Owners Academy or one-on-one. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1: Pay cash.**

You write a cheque for 1.2 million. You own 100 percent and you keep all 300 thousand.

That's a 25 percent return on your cash before tax. Not bad. The problem is you can do it once. Your capital is now locked in one business, and the next one needs another 1.2 million.

The rule: your own cash is the most expensive money in a deal, because it's finite.

**Level 2: Deposit plus bank.**

Say the bank wants 30 percent down. That's 360 thousand of your money, and the bank lends the rest.

Better return on your cash. But you spend years saving the deposit. Then the bank takes months to decide, and you're bidding against buyers with more money than you.

This is where most people sit. They're not short of ability. They're short of a structure.

**Level 3: Investors.**

You put in nothing. Investors put in the lot.

It sounds like the answer. It isn't. Every cheque is months of meetings. Then you've given away a large slice of the company, a say in how it's run and usually a say in when it's sold. If investors end up with half, you get half the exit on a business you run every day.

The rule I use: debt over dilution. Borrow inside safe limits rather than sell the company.

**Level 4: The structure.**

Here's the third way. The seller gets paid over time. Sometimes the seller keeps a slice, which we call a rollover. The bank fills the gap. Little to none of it is your cash.

The limit is one number. Free cash flow has to cover the debt payments at least 1.5 times. On 300 thousand of profit, that's no more than 200 thousand a year going to payments, and 100 thousand of buffer.

What you give instead of cash is equity to a board. Experienced operators, two to ten percent each, who make banks and sellers take you seriously. I built mine through LinkedIn and cold outreach with no network. The founder of the Australian Institute of Fitness is a shareholder. I still own 73 percent of my company.

My physio clinic was this level. 100 percent vendor finance. Seven years at four percent, principal and interest. A nine-month payment holiday. Rent in arrears. No deposit. I pulled 150 thousand out in year one and built mid six figures in equity. **[JOSH: one line on the clinic's price and profit at purchase.]**

Before it closed, four deals fell over and I'd made about a thousand cold calls. It's a process. Most of it is volume.

**The map.**

Level 4 runs in five stages, in order.

Credibility. No track record, so you borrow one. A board, good lawyers, good accountants.

Capital. Seller, rollover, bank. The 1.5 rule and debt over dilution sit over all of it.

Closing. You call owners directly. Before any offer you get three years of financials, matching tax returns and revenue by year. You offer three to five times profit in a letter of intent. Then due diligence on whether the earnings are real, then legals.

Grow. Every extra dollar of profit is worth several dollars when you sell.

Exit. Small businesses sell at small multiples. Groups sell at bigger ones. Buy ten at around four times, combine them, and the group can sell at eight, ten, sometimes fifteen.

The first three Cs get you the business. Grow and Exit are where the money is made.

Level 4 caps payments at $200K a year and leaves $100K of buffer. You keep the cash flow and the exit, and your own money stays free for the next one.

Where people lose money.

- Saving for years at Level 2 while retiring owners sell to someone else.
- Giving away half the company at Level 3 to fund a business that could have funded itself.
- Agreeing to payments the profit can't carry. Break the 1.5 rule and one bad year takes the business.
- Trying to master all five stages before starting the first one.

From here there are two paths. You work through it alone, lose deals learning each stage and take years. Or you work with someone who's already made those mistakes.

On the call we work out your strategy, your first structure and whether the Owners Academy or one-on-one advisory fits. The Academy is the full course, every template and script, and daily deal discussion. One-on-one, I work your structures and lender presentations with you.

If you're someone who's done waiting for the number, someone who can give this 40 hours a week from Monday, and someone who'd rather own the business than work for one, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either still saving, or with an industry picked and a board in progress.

## EP 02 · $800K cash vs $1.3M over time: the offer a retiring owner takes

The person most likely to fund your next business isn't a bank. It's the owner selling it.

Paid over time, a seller can often get six to seven times profit instead of the usual three to five, and close in two to four weeks instead of six to sixteen.

I'll put two offers on the same business side by side and walk them in the order the seller reads them.

If you already own one business, this is how the second one gets funded without another deposit. The difference between the two offers here is half a million dollars to the seller and a deposit you never have to find.

This is for operators who'll put serious hours in. If you want something passive, this isn't it.

You were told the seller wants all their money on day one. You were told the bank decides. That's why so many good operators run one business for years and never buy a second.

The deal is decided in the seller's chair. Sit in it, and the offer writes itself.

There are four lines on the offer that matter: what the seller wants, the price, the timeline, and the terms. Then the words you use to say it.

I structure these with owners every week, through the Owners Academy or one-on-one. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The seller's chair.**

Picture someone who's run a business for decades. It pays well. It also owns them. They get the call when a staff member doesn't show. They haven't had a real holiday in years.

That's the burden of ownership. A whole generation of owners in traditional industries is carrying it and ready to retire. They want out, and they want the business, the staff and the customers looked after.

Their options are a cash buyer who needs a bank, private equity that only wants bigger businesses and beats the price down, or a broker listing that brings bargain hunters.

**Line 1: the price.**

Round numbers. The business makes 200 thousand a year in profit.

Offer A is cash through a bank at four times profit. 800 thousand.

Offer B is paid over time at six and a half times. 1.3 million over ten years. About 130 thousand a year, plus interest.

The seller reads the headline first. Offer B is half a million bigger.

**Line 2: the timeline.**

Offer A waits on a bank. Six to sixteen weeks, a thousand questions, and it can still fall over.

Offer B closes in two to four weeks, because the seller is the lender. For an owner who's done, speed is worth money. Every extra month is another month of staff calls, another month of the burden. And there's interest on top while they wait. Most owners have never had a buyer explain that to them.

**Line 3: the terms, and your test.**

Now your side. Profit of 200 thousand. Payments of 130 thousand. 200 divided by 130 is about 1.54. That passes the 1.5 rule. The business pays for itself with a buffer.

If it doesn't pass, you don't do the deal. That's the line between a good structure and a trap.

Then the order. Agree the headline price first. Once that's agreed, ask for a payment holiday of nine to twelve months, framed honestly: it gives you time to make sure the business keeps performing, which protects their payments. If you need to, offer a slightly higher rate in return.

My first deal looked like this. Physio clinic, 100 percent vendor finance, seven years at four percent, principal and interest, a nine-month holiday, rent in arrears, no deposit. **[JOSH: one line on how the physio seller reacted to being paid over time.]**

A friend of mine in the US, Elliot, made around 40 offers and got three letters of intent signed at 100 percent seller finance. Some on 99-year "pension plan" terms. One example: 350 thousand a year for life, on 500 thousand of net operating income. For some owners, a steady yearly payment is exactly what they want.

**The words.**

The seller is taking a risk on you. Three things make it small. A credible board behind you. The seller staying on for a handover, usually six months to three years. And a structure the business can carry.

Then the language. Open with one question: "Have you ever thought about selling?" Then listen.

When you get to terms, never say "vendor finance". Say "getting paid over time". Don't say "I can't afford it upfront". Say "I can pay you more in total if it's paid over time". Don't say "I need a payment holiday". Say "I'd like time to make sure the business keeps performing for you".

Same structure. One sounds like a favour. The other sounds like a plan.

Read the signals too. Financials within a day or two of an NDA, questions about your timeline, talk about life afterwards: motivated. Weeks to send basics and only talk about price: not ready. Check back in six to twelve months.

The seller gets more. You get the business without a deposit. The business funds both of you, as long as the 1.5 rule holds.

Where people lose money.

- Saying "vendor finance" on the first call. The seller hears "can't pay" and the deal is gone.
- Asking for the payment holiday before the price is agreed. It reads as weakness and the price goes up.
- Agreeing to a price the profit can't cover at 1.5 times. You've bought a business that eats you.
- Chasing sellers who aren't ready instead of calling more owners.

You can keep doing what you're doing. Keep running one business at the ceiling. Keep saving for the next deposit. Keep taking structures to banks that were never going to fund them. Or you can learn the structure a retiring owner says yes to.

On the call we build your first "paid over time" offer and work out whether the Owners Academy, with every seller script, or one-on-one advisory fits you.

If you're someone who's built one business and knows you could run more, and someone who'd rather pay a seller more than ask a bank for permission, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now, you're either at the same ceiling or you've run the 1.5 rule on a real target and started the seller conversations.

## EP 03 · $1M in a rental vs $1M in a business (the math)

James did everything he was told. He saved a deposit, bought a rental, then another.

Sam didn't. Sam bought a boring business from a retiring owner and paid for it over time out of its profit.

Ten houses are worth ten houses. Ten businesses, put together, can be worth a lot more than ten businesses. Here's the math.

If your capital sits in property or the market earning a thin return, this is the comparison nobody ran for you. Same million dollars. One option pays a yield. The other pays cash flow now and a bigger multiple later.

If you want something you can buy and forget, property may suit you better. I'll say so honestly.

Most people think property is the safe road and a business is a job with extra risk. That's not how it works.

A business is priced on what it earns. A property is priced on what people think it's worth. Once you see that, the comparison changes.

Three differences: cash flow, time and the multiple. Then a self-test and a verdict.

And if most of your money is in bricks, that's not bad judgement. It's what everyone around you did, and nobody showed you the other column.

I help people buy their first business and plan the group from day one, through the Owners Academy or one-on-one. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A: the rental.**

A million-dollar rental. The bank wants a deposit, say 200 thousand, saved or tied up first. Rent after costs, say 40 thousand a year. Then interest on 800 thousand of loan. Depending on rates, you keep a little, or you top it up from your wages.

**Option B: the business.**

A business priced at a million, at four times profit. That's 250 thousand a year in profit.

Pay the seller over time. Under the 1.5 rule, no more than about 166 thousand a year goes to payments. That leaves around 84 thousand of buffer every year, and you didn't need a deposit.

Dollar for dollar, the business throws off far more cash. The reason it's priced cheaper is that it needs work, and buyers discount anything that needs work. That discount is your margin.

And the property you already own isn't wasted. It's an asset a lender can see, which can make the business deal easier and faster.

**Time, both sides.**

Be honest about this. Property is mostly passive. A business isn't, at first. Staff, customers, suppliers. That's the work you're paid for.

But the time that matters more is how long it takes to start. A deposit takes years. A retiring owner paid over time can get you in the door in weeks.

And growth compounds differently. More property usually means another deposit, or borrowing against the last one. With businesses, the first one's cash flow and track record make the second easier, because lenders and sellers have seen you do it.

You also have levers. You can't double the rent because you ran the house better. You can lift a business's profit by fixing price, cost and sales, and every dollar you add is worth several at the exit.

**The multiple.**

This is the one property can't do.

A small business with 250 thousand of profit sells for three to five times. It's small, it leans on the owner, buyers price in the risk.

Put ten of them under one management team, with proper reporting and no single owner holding it together. That's a group. Groups sell to private equity and bigger buyers at eight, ten, sometimes fifteen times.

Ten businesses at 250 thousand profit, bought at four times: ten million in purchase prices, mostly paid over time. Combined profit, two and a half million. Sold at eight times: twenty million. Before debt, costs and everything that can go wrong. Bought at four. Sold at eight. Same profit, bigger multiple.

A friend of mine, Jason Nagy, did a real version. His first deal was a five and a half million dollar radiology business with zero down, on a 60/40 structure. Seven years later he exited to private equity for 200 million.

**Self-test and verdict.**

Three questions.

Can you give this 40 hours a week or more for the first stretch? If not, stay in property for now.

Do you want to improve an asset, or wait on one? If you'd rather wait, property is fine.

Do you have equity in property already? Then you're not choosing. That equity can make your first business deal faster and easier.

The verdict: property for passive. Businesses for cash flow and the multiple. For most people with capital, both, in that order: property as the base, businesses as the engine.

The rental's return depends on the market. The business's return depends on you, which is the risk and the point.

Where people lose money.

- Keeping all the capital in property because it feels safe, while the yield barely covers the interest.
- Buying the first business with no idea what the group looks like. Pick an industry nobody buys at scale and the multiple never shows up.
- Treating a business as passive. Without the hours in year one, the buffer disappears.
- Paying a property-style price for a business. Pay ten times profit for a small business and you've bought a yield, not a multiple.

Two paths. Alone, people buy one business, never plan the group, and sell it at the small multiple. Or you plan the exit before the first offer, with someone who's seen how groups get built.

On the call we map your first business and the group behind it, and decide whether the Owners Academy or one-on-one advisory fits.

If you're someone with capital earning less than it should, and someone who can see the gap between buying at four and selling at eight, you already know which side of James and Sam you're on.

Thirty days from now your money will still be somewhere. Either earning the same thin return, or thirty days into a plan with an industry picked and the first numbers run.

## EP 04 · 5 mistakes that make a business impossible to fund (buy boring)

Every day, someone online is selling you the exciting business. The app. The brand. The café with the queue out the door.

The exciting ones are the hardest to fund, the easiest to copy, and they often walk out with the owner.

Here are the five mistakes that make a business impossible to fund, ranked, with the costliest last.

If you already run one business and you're stuck in it, the next one has to be different. The wrong target costs you three months of diligence, legal fees and a deal the bank won't touch. The right one funds itself.

If you want trendy, this is the wrong video.

Buy boring. Businesses that have done the same useful thing for twenty years and will for twenty more. Healthcare, trades, commercial and professional services. Fragmented markets full of owners ready to retire.

If you've looked at businesses before and nothing fit, it has nothing to do with your eye for a deal or your budget. It has everything to do with where you were looking. The exciting stuff and the listings are where everyone looks.

Five mistakes. Each one costs you the deal.

I help people pick the right industry and the right business, through the Owners Academy or one-on-one. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Mistake 5: Buying exciting.**

Fads fade and anything trendy is easy to copy. Commodity businesses where the cheapest wins leave no margin to pay a seller.

Run the money. A café doing 1.2 million at under eight percent margin makes about 90 thousand. Under the 1.5 rule, that's 60 thousand a year to pay a seller. On a business priced at four times profit, 360 thousand, that's six years of payments with nothing left to pay you or a manager. One bad winter and you're funding it yourself.

The rule: a problem people will still have in ten years, at net margins of fifteen percent or more. Pipes leak. Backs get sore. Offices need cleaning.

**Mistake 4: Buying what a bank can't lend against.**

Asset-light businesses with messy books don't get funded. Equipment, vehicles and receivables give a lender security. On bigger deals, typically over about five million, that can mean no personal guarantee. Every lender and deal is different.

Clean numbers matter as much as assets. Before any offer, ask for three years of financials, the matching tax returns and revenue by year. If they don't match, walk. A lender won't fund profit it can't see, and neither should you. If a lender can't understand the business in five minutes, you'll struggle to fund it.

**Mistake 3: Buying the owner.**

If the owner is the business, you're buying a job, and a risky one. Check who the customers actually call.

Buyers price that in, and so do banks. A business that depends on its owner is worth less the day the owner leaves, and you're the one holding the payments when that happens.

You want an owner ready to go within a couple of years, no one in the family taking over, and willing to stay for a handover. That's also who says yes to being paid over time. That was episode 2. An owner who wants to walk out the day you settle is a red flag. If you already run a business you can't step out of, you know exactly what that costs.

**Mistake 2: Buying a ceiling.**

Two ceilings to check. Location: a single café is capped by its street, a trades business covering a whole city isn't. Concentration: if one or two customers are more than about thirty percent of revenue, one lost contract breaks your payments.

And look for the upside. Most boring businesses run on referrals and do no marketing. That isn't a dealbreaker. It's what you fix in the Grow stage.

**Mistake 1: Buying too small.**

The costliest, because it looks like the safe option.

A business priced at 250 thousand, making 60 thousand. Under the 1.5 rule, no more than 40 thousand a year goes to the seller. That leaves 20 thousand. That doesn't pay you, doesn't cover a manager and doesn't survive a bad year.

Under about a million dollars, there's rarely enough cash flow to pay the seller, pay you and keep a buffer. Same work, more risk. A bigger business also usually comes with a team, so you're buying a business and not a job.

My own first business was a physio clinic, healthcare, traditional, with an owner ready to be paid over time. **[JOSH: one line on why the physio clinic passed these filters.]**

Nobody posts about Business B. That's exactly why you can buy it well. At 450 thousand profit, the 1.5 rule allows about 300 thousand a year in payments, with 150 thousand of buffer.

Here's a real one. Saul, a member of the Academy, didn't wait for a listing. He cold called the owner of an electrical and aircon business. Twenty-five staff, around 3.8 million in revenue, owner retiring in two years. NDA signed, early due diligence. Not a secret listing. A phone call.

Where people lose money.

- Falling for the business before running the filters, then finding the owner is the business three months in.
- Waiting on broker listings with every other buyer, so you pay full price in an auction.
- Skipping the three documents and paying lawyers to find what the tax returns would have shown in a day.
- Buying under a million because it feels safer, then working for free to service the debt.

You can keep doing what you're doing. Keep working in the one business you've got. Keep scrolling listings. Keep waiting for the right one to find you. Or you can test your target before you lose three months to it.

On the call we look at your industry and your first target, and whether the Owners Academy, where members put targets up for others to pull apart, or one-on-one advisory fits.

If you're someone who's built one business and is tired of holding it up, and someone who'd rather own a reliable plumber than a trendy brand, you've already decided. And wanting a portfolio isn't greed. It's the next step.

Thirty days from now, you're either still scrolling listings, or you've got twenty businesses scored and the first calls made.

## EP 05 · 4 failed deals and 1,000 cold calls before my first no-money-down business

How come capable people with good careers find it so hard to make a first offer on a business?

They've got experience and discipline. Most have watched hours of content on how to do it. The offer still never goes out.

Before my first deal closed, four fell over and I'd made about a thousand cold calls. Here's what that year taught me about when to move.

Waiting feels free. It isn't. On a business making 200 thousand, paying the seller inside the 1.5 rule, that's around 67 thousand a year you don't have, and a year of equity and track record you don't build.

This is for people who'll act before they feel ready. If you're planning to watch for another year first, be honest with yourself about that.

Decide at 60 percent. You'll never have all the information, because most of it only shows up once you start.

If you've been stuck at "nearly ready", it has nothing to do with your intelligence or your courage. It has everything to do with waiting for a feeling that only arrives after you start. Confidence comes last.

Four parts: the year, the diagnosis, the 60 percent, and the rule.

Most people I work with don't need more information. They need someone to make the next move with. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The year.**

About a thousand cold calls. Most said no, hung up or didn't pick up. Four deals got far enough to matter and then fell over. **[JOSH: one line on why one of the four fell over.]**

Before I understood what those four were, every no felt like a verdict. After, they were information. Each one told me something about sellers, banks or structure that no book had.

The fifth was the physio clinic. 100 percent vendor finance, no deposit. 150 thousand out in year one, mid six figures in equity.

Not because I got lucky, and not because I knew everything. If I'd waited to know everything, I'd never have made call one. **[JOSH: one line on what kept you calling after deal four.]**

**The diagnosis: two kinds of risk.**

Active risk is the risk of acting. A deal falls over, a seller says no, you sound green on a call. It's visible, so it feels big.

Passive risk is the risk of doing nothing. Another year in the same job, owning nothing. It feels safe because nothing happens. That's the problem. Nothing happens.

Most people weigh the first and ignore the second. That's backwards. The active risk costs you a few bad calls and some legal fees. The passive risk costs you the 67 thousand a year, compounding, for as long as you wait.

And in this model the active risk is managed. No deposit. Payments the business carries at 1.5 times. Due diligence before you sign. An offer isn't a marriage. If something turns up, you walk.

**The 60 percent.**

The 60 percent you need before acting: you understand the map, Credibility through Exit. You know the 1.5 rule. You've picked an industry with the filters from episode 4. You know the three documents to ask a seller for. And you've cleared the hours. That's a couple of weeks.

The 40 percent you only learn by doing: how sellers in your industry talk, what your local banks will lend against, how your first offer lands, which board members say yes. You can't read your way to any of it. Reading feels like progress. It isn't. A tenth book teaches you less than one real seller call.

You don't carry the 40 percent alone. That's the Credibility stage: a board of people who've run businesses and closed deals, and advisers who've done a hundred of these. You act at 60 because the team covers the other 40.

**The rule.**

In order. The business passes the filters. The three documents line up. The numbers pass the 1.5 rule. Once all three are true, you make the offer. You don't wait for a better one and you don't wait to feel confident.

Then the part nobody likes. Time, money and energy. You can make up for less money with structure. You can make up for less experience with a board. You can't make up for energy.

I've had people pay 20 to 30 thousand for advisory and still not take action. The money wasn't the problem. Their energy went everywhere except the phone. Energy is the eleventh call after ten people said no.

And background isn't the excuse people think. Christian Dawes was unemployed, with no higher education and a criminal record. His first deal was 2.4 million dollars, funded 100 percent by the bank.

Waiting doesn't send you an invoice. It just costs this, every year, before anything you'd improve.

Where people lose money.

- Researching for another year. Another course, another podcast, no offers sent.
- Treating the first no as a verdict and stopping before deal five.
- Paying for help and then not making the calls.
- Waiting for the market. Retiring owners don't wait for it.

Two paths. Alone, it goes wrong quietly. Nobody fails. They just keep researching, and a year goes by without an offer. Or you work beside someone who's already lost four deals and expects to see your next call made.

Some moments work like thresholds. Before, you're someone thinking about buying a business. After, you're someone doing it. It doesn't take certainty. It takes a decision.

On the call we get you to 60 percent, set your first structure, and work out whether the Owners Academy, where it's hard to stay at 40 percent when everyone around you is making calls, or one-on-one advisory fits.

If you're someone who's been nearly ready for too long, and someone who'd rather lose four deals than never try for one, you're at 60 percent already.

Thirty days from now, either you've watched another month of content, or your hours are blocked, your industry is picked and your first board conversations have started.

# Act 2 · Credibility

## EP 06 · Unemployed at 25: how I borrowed a track record and kept 73%

The first-time buyers who get funded have one thing in common, and it isn't money.

At 25 I was unemployed. No savings, no experience. My first deal was a physio clinic, 100% vendor finance, no deposit. I pulled $150,000 out in year one.

The thing that got me taken seriously cost me no cash at all. It was a board. Here's how I built it and what it costs you.

Every deal has two gatekeepers. The seller decides whether to take your call. The bank decides whether to lend. Both are asking one question: is this person going to run it and pay the money back?

If you can't answer that, you pay for it in cash. A bigger deposit, a worse price, or a seller who sells to someone else. This is for people putting 40-plus hours a week into buying a company and aiming for a group or an exit. If you want a four-hour work week, this isn't it.

You don't need a track record. You borrow one. Three parts: the seats, the price, and the outreach.

If you've been knocked back, or never tried, it has nothing to do with your age, your bank balance or the title on your LinkedIn. It has everything to do with who's standing next to you when you ask.

You were told you need a track record first. Your own money in the deal. The right network. None of that is how this stage works. The buyers who get through didn't wait to earn a track record. They borrowed one.

I help people build this. Which seat first, what to send, how to set the equity. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The low point.**

At 25 a bank had no reason to lend me a dollar. A retiring owner had no reason to take my call. I'd done more than $30 million in deals in banking by 21, but on the buy side I was nobody.

So I stopped selling them me. I sold them my board. Every person on it came from LinkedIn and cold messages. No network. The founder of the Australian Institute of Fitness is a shareholder today, and I still keep 73% of the company.

[JOSH: one line on who said yes first and what that first message said.]

**The diagnosis: four seats.**

A credit team doesn't lend to enthusiasm. It lends to people it thinks will run the business. A seller who spent 30 years building something won't hand it to a 25-year-old. But they'll talk to a company whose board looks like it knows what it's doing. Your board is the evidence.

You want four seats.

A chair with weight: a former executive or seasoned director. A finance lead who speaks the bank's language, a former CFO or credit person. A legal lead, an M&A lawyer or former general counsel. And a sector operator who's run businesses in the industry you're buying.

Friends and family don't go on it. A weak board is worse than none. It tells the bank you don't know what good looks like.

**The price: equity, not cash.**

You pay in founders' equity. Usually 2 to 10% each, scaled to the seat. They get a ground-floor stake in a group. You get their name, their judgement and their network.

The people who say yes are mostly recently retired. Experience, time, and they miss being involved. You're not asking a favour. You're offering a seat.

The first call is 30 minutes. The industry, why owners are selling, what you're building. Then what you need: a few hours a month, their name on the executive summary, and help on lender and seller calls. Equity that vests on the first deal. Don't haggle percentages on the first call. That's next episode.

**The outreach.**

Pick the seat you need most. Build a list. Look for three things: a title a bank manager would recognise, real time in the role, and signs they're winding down: "former", "non-executive", "adviser".

That's the whole message. Then follow up twice, a week apart. A lot of yeses come on the third message.

It moves fast. Jy Kingston is one of my mentees. IT background, no finance experience. Board in under 30 days through LinkedIn. Accountants and lawyers within about two weeks. Twelve deals active, closing his first.

Another of my students got the former CEO of IBM to join their company. Same method. They asked.

Where this doesn't apply: if you're buying one small business with your own cash, you may not need a board at all. The board earns its equity when someone else's money is in the deal, a bank's or a seller's.

A few hundred messages is a few weeks of work. The board costs you no cash, and it's what lets the business and the seller fund the deal instead of your savings.

Where people lose money.

- They fill seats with mates. The bank reads it in one glance and the deal gets a worse structure, or no structure.
- They send 20 messages, hear nothing and quit. The yes was in message 60.
- They hand over equity on a call with nothing written. That's the slice that costs them control later.

The rule: board first, then deals. Deal first works too, but you'll be recruiting under a seller's deadline. A one-page executive summary with your board on it and a handshake on equity. You walk into a seller's office with a team, not a story.

Two paths from here. Do it alone: pick friends because it feels safer, promise equity with no vesting, send three messages and decide it doesn't work. Or do it with someone who's already made those mistakes.

## EP 07 · 5 board equity mistakes that give away 40% before your first deal

Four board seats at 10% each is 40% of your company gone before you've bought a single business.

There are two types of owners building a group. The ones who spend equity like currency, and the ones who sit on 100% of one business for twenty years.

I pay my board in equity and keep 73%. Here are the five mistakes that stop people doing the same.

Equity is the one thing you can't easily take back. Give too much and there's nothing left for you, the next hire or the exit. Give too little and nobody good says yes.

This is for owners building something to scale or sell. If you want one business you run on your own, you don't need a board, and this isn't for you.

Small slices, earned on a deal, with the job written down.

If you're stuck at one business, it has nothing to do with your work ethic or your industry. It has everything to do with never being shown how to pay people before you have cash.

Most owners think a bigger percentage is a safer percentage. That's not how it works. Equity is the currency you spend before you have cash. Five ways people spend it badly, ranked from cheapest to most expensive.

If you want me to look at your split before you offer it to anyone, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Mistake 1. Too few shares.**

People set up the holding company with 100 shares. Then someone needs 7.5% and it's a restructure.

Issue a million at the start. Handing someone 70,000 shares out of a million is easy. The holding company owns the group. Each business you buy sits in its own company underneath, so each deal's debt stays in its own box, and the board owns a slice of the whole group, not one clinic.

It also protects your number later. When a seller keeps a stake in the business they sell you, that stake usually sits in the company that owns that one business. So a seller's rollover doesn't dilute you or the board at the top.

**Mistake 2. Same slice for every seat.**

Founders' equity runs 2 to 10% per person. Where each seat lands depends on three things: how much their name moves a bank or a seller, how many hours they'll give you, and how hard they'd be to replace. The chair usually wins on the first, so the chair goes higher.

Weigh the work, not the title. A sector operator on seller calls every week can earn more than a lawyer who reviews two documents a quarter. And leave headroom. Keep yourself at 70% or more after the board, so there's room for a group CEO later, or another seat when you move into a second industry. Four seats at 10% leaves you 60% and nowhere to go. Four seats between 6 and 8% leaves you 73% and room to grow.

**Mistake 3. Vesting at the handshake.**

Nothing vests on a handshake. A portion vests when you sign the sale agreement on your first deal, the SPA. The rest vests over the 12 months after closing. Leave early, and the unvested shares come back. Your lawyer calls these leaver terms.

If they don't help you get a deal done, they don't own anything. That one rule filters out people who only wanted a title. It's also fair to the good ones: they know exactly what earns their shares, and when.

**Mistake 4. No written job.**

A board seat is a senior hire paid in equity. Set the job before they say yes, not after.

Put it in a one or two page letter per person. Role, percentage, vesting, monthly commitments, leaver terms, confidentiality. Signed before their name goes on your executive summary. If someone won't sign a page that fair, you've learned something cheaply.

**Mistake 5. Giving away the exit.**

This is the expensive one. Minority holders get no veto and no voting control. Add a drag-along, so if you sell the group they sell with you on the same terms. Add transfer restrictions, so nobody sells to a stranger. Set it up from day one, not when there's a problem. A minority holder with a veto can block a sale years later.

Good board members will ask about one more thing: directors' and officers' insurance. Have it. It protects them, and it tells them you're serious.

[JOSH: one line on how you set control terms with your own board.]

That's your pitch to a former CFO. Not "help me out". A ground-floor stake in something worth a lot if you build it. And you still hold 73%.

If giving away 27% feels like losing, run it the other way. 27% of a $20 million group is real money for them. 73% of it is real money for you. 100% of one business capped at your own hours is a ceiling. And none of their 27% exists unless a deal gets done.

Where people lose money.

- Promising "a piece" on a handshake that two people remember differently. That ends in a lawyer's letter, not a deal.
- Starting at 60% after the board. There's no room left for the people who'll actually grow the group.
- Haggling percentages on the first call. The best people walk, and you're left with the ones who only wanted a title.

Or you can keep doing what you're doing. Keep 100% of a business that only grows as fast as you work. Keep waiting for cash before you buy the second one.

Or get the split right before you've offered it to anyone. On the call we go through your goal, the seats you need, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy has the board equity terms and recruitment scripts. In 1:1 I work your structures with you.

If you're someone who'd rather own 73% of a group than 100% of a ceiling, this call is for you.

Thirty days from now you're either 30 days older with the same ceiling, or 30 days older with a split on paper and your first seat offered. Book a call with me below. Not a setter. Me.

## EP 08 · $70K of deal fees: pay upfront vs pay at settlement

Owners who can read a P&L better than most buyers still don't buy a second business, because of one question: who pays the lawyers?

On a $3 million deal that's about $70,000 of legal and accounting fees. Most people pull it out of the business they already own.

You don't have to. Here's the version where the deal pays its own costs.

$70,000 out of your current business is a buffer gone, a hire you don't make, or a slow month you can't survive. Do it on a deal that dies and it's gone.

This is for people buying real businesses, usually a million dollars or more in value. If you're buying a small online store off a marketplace, skip this one.

Two ways to pay your deal team. Option A: pay as you go, from your own cash. Option B: pay at settlement, from money the bank lends the business on the day.

If you've stalled here, it has nothing to do with being cheap or scared. It has everything to do with nobody telling you fees can sit at settlement. I'll cost both, give you a test, and a verdict.

Getting good firms to wait for their money comes down to how you pitch it. I help people with that. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A. Pay as you go.**

You engage a firm on hourly terms. They bill monthly. You pay from your current business.

What it costs: the full bill, upfront, whether the deal closes or not. And open hourly terms invite fee-jacking. Every small point becomes a memo, and the bill doubles.

What it gets you: control over the firm, and nothing owed if you walk. That's it.

**Option B. Pay at settlement.**

Three moves.

Build the bench first. An M&A lawyer. An accountant who can do a quality of earnings review, which is a check that the profit is real. Big Four or a strong regional firm. And an industry adviser if the sector has rules you don't know.

Ask for deferred or rolled fees. Some or all of the bill is paid at settlement instead of as they go.

Then at settlement, the bank funds a working capital line into the new company. As a rough guide it's often around 10% of annual revenue. It pays the firms that afternoon, and the rest is runway.

**Why a good firm says yes.**

You're not one deal. You're a group buying business after business. To a law firm that's a repeat client. To an accountant it's years of work: diligence, then the books, tax and audits for every business you add. You pitch the pipeline, not the job.

And top-tier matters for the same reason the board does. A bank trusts a quality of earnings report from a firm it knows. A cheap firm nobody's heard of costs you more in delay than it saves in fees.

A proper quality of earnings review checks five things. Whether revenue will keep coming. Whether one or two customers carry too much of it. Whether the seller's adjustments to profit are fair. How much working capital the business really needs. And whether the cash flow holds once you own it. That's the report your bank leans on to say yes.

Where to find them: ask your board's legal and finance leads who they'd use. Then LinkedIn, partners who list acquisition work. Then ask your lenders which firms' reports they trust.

Some firms will say no. Some defer half. Some do the early work at a fixed fee and the heavy work at settlement. Ask five.

[JOSH: one line on how your own first deal's fees were paid.]

**The proof.**

"A bank won't do that for me." My good friend Cam did two deals in 23 days with 100% vendor finance, and $500,000 of working capital from the bank. Not from his pocket. From the deal.

And Jy Kingston, one of my mentees, had accountants and lawyers on board within about two weeks of starting.

Option A: $70,000 out of your pocket before you know the deal closes. Option B: $0 out of your pocket, fees paid on the day, $230,000 left to run the business. The bank sets the size of the line, not you. That's Act 3.

**THE TEST AND THE VERDICT.**

The self-test. Before you spend a dollar on lawyers, has the seller sent three years of financials, matching tax returns and revenue by financial year? If not, they're not serious, and neither option is worth paying for yet.

The verdict: Option B, in this order. Board, then bench, then deals. Fixed fees per stage, deferred to settlement, with a capped amount if it dies. Aim for about four weeks to paper the contracts.

Where people lose money.

- Hiring a cheap firm the bank doesn't trust. The saving comes back as weeks of delay and a tougher credit team.
- Signing open hourly terms. No scope, no cap, and the bill doubles. The fix is leadership: a tight scope, a timeline, and a ranked issues list so the big points get solved first.
- Spending the working capital line on yourself. It's runway for the business. Spend it and you're back at the bank in six months with a worse story.

Two paths. Alone, this goes wrong in cash: a cheap firm, an open meter, or diligence on a seller who was never selling, paid from the business you've already built. Or set up the bench and the fee terms with someone who's done it.

## EP 09 · The $7,000 offer letter and 3 other documents sellers read first (copy them)

When you buy what everyone else is offered, you pay what everyone else pays.

Another property. Another fund. Another listed business with a crowd already bidding. The alternative is going direct to owners ready to retire, at three to five times profit, with four documents that make you the most serious buyer they'll speak to.

A client was quoted about $7,000 for one of them. Here are all four, in the order a seller reads them.

Listed deals get auctioned. Off-market deals get negotiated, and the buyer who looks most serious wins on terms, not just price. A packet is the cheapest way to look serious before you've closed anything. It costs a weekend and some templates. Being one more offer in the pile costs you the price and the terms.

This is for people who want to be taken seriously before their first deal. If you're happy to buy one small business with cash and a handshake, you don't need it.

One packet, three readers: sellers, lenders, board recruits. Four documents, one job each.

The market has moved. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. They don't want to wait 16 weeks for a bank. They want out, and to be looked after. Being paid over time can get them more than most buyers offer, and a close in two to four weeks instead of six to sixteen. The buyers who understand that go direct. Everyone else fights over what's listed.

And if you've looked at businesses and couldn't tell a good one from a dressed-up one, it has nothing to do with your experience as an investor. It has everything to do with never having a thesis and a checklist.

Academy members get every one of these as a template, and I'll go through yours on a call. Book below. Now the teardown.

**The executive summary.**

The first thing a seller, banker or board recruit sees. One or two pages.

The board section does the heavy lifting. One line each, the strongest true fact: former CFO of this, thirty years running that. "How we work with owners" is where you win sellers: fast close, staff kept on, a handover that suits them.

One rule. Never write "vendor finance" to a seller. Write "getting paid over time". Same thing. It reads like you're looking after them.

Then a two-line cover email. For a seller: who you are, and have you ever thought about selling? For a banker: you're building lender relationships before you bring deals. Same attachment every time.

[JOSH: one line on what the board section of your own summary said.]

**The thesis memo.**

Two or three pages. Why this industry, what exactly you buy, what you won't, and how it ends.

What you won't buy matters more. One or two customers carrying the revenue. Industries being eaten by technology. Owners who want out tomorrow with no handover. And very small deals. Under about a million in value, there usually isn't enough cash flow to pay the debt and pay you.

The end game goes in too. Buy at small-business multiples, grow, combine, sell the group for more. That's Act 7.

**The NBIO.**

The non-binding indicative offer. A short offer letter. This is the one a client was quoted about $7,000 for.

Five parts. A headline price, around three to five times profit, and how you got there. The structure: how much is paid over time, the term, any payment holiday. Conditions: diligence, finance, lease, licences. Exclusivity, usually 60 to 90 days. And a timeline to close. Economics up front, fine print later. Episode 22 goes line by line.

The point of having it as a template: when a seller says "what would you pay?", you answer in days, not weeks. Speed reads as seriousness, and a retiring owner is choosing who to trust as much as what to accept.

**The diligence checklist.**

It runs in order. Before any offer: three years of financials, matching tax returns, revenue by financial year. If a seller won't send those under an NDA, they're not serious. After the offer's accepted: quality of earnings, customer concentration, adjustments to profit, staff interviews, leases, licences.

Keep one issues log, and use it when you talk price. And put your finance lead's name next to the sign-off. That's a different document from one you ticked yourself.

Behind the four, keep two more in a folder: a board roster with full bios, and each board member's signed terms. A lender may ask how your board is engaged. "Here are the signed terms" beats "we've got a handshake".

One packet serves all three readers. A recruit reads the thesis and the upside. A seller reads the summary and how you treat owners. A lender reads the board, the criteria and the checklist. You change the cover email, not the packet.

The packet doesn't change what a business earns. It changes who you're competing with, and that changes the price and the terms.

Where people lose money.

- Writing "vendor finance" on page one and losing the owner before the first meeting.
- Sending a packet that promises a team you don't have. A seller can tell in one phone call whether there's a real board behind the PDF.
- Polishing documents for months and never sending one. Draft in a weekend, let the board pull it apart, fix what they find.

Or you can keep doing what you're doing. Keep the money where it earns little. Keep bidding on listed businesses against everyone else. Keep turning up to owners with a story instead of a packet.

Or build it properly. On the call we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy has every template here. Join and the NBIO template is yours, plus a 1:1 with Jy Kingston, who built his board in under 30 days. In 1:1 I build the lender packet with you.

## EP 10 · Every partner setup from 50/50 handshake to bankable, explained

Buying a business with a partner doubles your hours. Done on a handshake, it can also halve your equity.

It goes from sharing the work to arguing about the split. From one plan to two. From a mate to a lawyer's letter. And it usually happens when the money shows up, not before.

Here's every level from a 50/50 handshake to a setup a bank and a seller will back.

In years one to three you're paying the seller and the bank, settling staff in, working long hours. Later the debt's paid down and the business throws off cash. That's when an unwritten split starts to cost real money, and you can't renegotiate it calmly once there's money on the table.

If you're buying alone, stay anyway. This applies to anyone who'll ever own a piece of what you build.

A partner should bring something you can't borrow. Four levels. Most pairs are stuck on level one.

When partnerships blow up, it usually has nothing to do with one of you being greedy or lazy. It has everything to do with terms written for year one still being used in year five.

Before you know that, a partnership feels like a question of trust. After you know it, you see it's a question of conditions. Conditions change. The paper has to change with them.

I help co-buyers set this up before a seller and a bank are asking who's in charge. If you're buying with someone, book a call below and bring them. Now the detail.

**Level 1. The handshake.**

Two people, 50/50, nothing written, often trading as a partnership.

The trap: a partnership gives you no protection between the partners and the business's liabilities. A 50/50 split with no tie-break means every disagreement is a deadlock. And the bank will ask who owns it and who signs, and you won't have one answer.

The one move to level two: decide whether you need a partner at all. There are three good reasons. Hours, because this takes 40 to 100 a week. Assets, because cash, an existing business or property makes it easier and faster. And skills you don't have. No to all three? Buy alone. You can borrow credibility from a board without giving anyone a co-owner's share.

**Level 2. A company and separate jobs.**

Use a company, not a partnership. Then split the jobs so they don't overlap.

Two of you can split three jobs. Two of you can't both own "deals". Each person has final say in their own lane: the operator on staff, the deal-finder on which seller to chase. Nobody relitigates the other's area. For anything that crosses lanes, write a deadlock rule now: who breaks the tie, or who you bring in to break it. A 50/50 with no tie-break isn't equal. It's stuck.

**Level 3. Shares that match the work.**

Each person's shares vest on their role. The deal-finder's on signing the first SPA. The operator's over the first 12 months of running it. Write in a date each year to review the terms, because conditions will change. One of you may drop to part-time. One may put in more cash. The review date is when you adjust, calmly, instead of in the middle of a fight.

And a partner doesn't replace the board. A partner is a co-owner with a full-time job in the business. A board member is a few hours a month, a name a bank trusts, and a small slice that vests on a deal. Two co-buyers with no board are still two people with no track record.

**Level 4. Bankable: signed before the LOI.**

A shareholders' agreement with five things.

Inside it, the buy-sell clause: if one of you leaves, gets sick or dies, who buys the shares, at what price, worked out how, paid over how long. Agree the formula, not the number.

Then personal guarantees. If a lender wants one, someone signs. Larger deals, typically over about $5 million, often don't need one, because the business's assets give the bank its security. Every lender and deal is different. Agree now who signs and how you share that risk.

And it all goes before the LOI. Once a seller signs, you're in 60 to 90 days of exclusivity. The seller wants to know who'll run it. The bank wants to know who owns it and who signs. You don't want to negotiate with your partner and the seller in the same week.

[JOSH: one line on a co-buyer setup you've seen go well or badly.]

The split is identical. The difference is what happens when one of them stops working in year four. Level one can cost you the business. Level four costs a few hours.

Where people lose money.

- Signing the offer letter first and arguing about shares during due diligence while the seller watches.
- Both wanting the same job. One of you is paid for work the other is doing.
- Leaving personal guarantees unspoken until the bank asks. That's the worst week to find out you disagree.

Each of you writes one page, separately: role, hours, what you bring, what you expect to own, what happens if you leave. Then swap. Where the pages match, you're done. Where they don't, that's the conversation.

"We're mates, we don't need paperwork." That's exactly why you do it now, while it's easy. "We can't afford a lawyer." You can't afford the fight, or a deal that stalls because the bank doesn't know who's in charge. "We're a couple." Same rule. Different jobs, written down.

Two paths. Work it out between you, alone, and it goes wrong between people who trust each other. Or sit down with someone who's seen how this breaks and set it up before it can.

On the call we go through your goal, who does what, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. Co-buyers can work through the Academy together. In 1:1 I work your structures with both of you.

If you're someone who'd rather have the awkward conversation now than the expensive one later, this call is for you.

# Act 3 · Capital

## EP 11 · A $1.66M Offer on $250K Profit (Cash Buyer vs Paid Over Time)

How come the buyer with less money can pay the seller more?

A business making $250,000 a year in profit sells to a cash buyer for about a million. The same business can carry an offer of about $1.66 million, paid over time, out of its own profit.

Not your money. The business's. Here's the two-minute maths, and the three places it lies to you.

That gap is the whole of Capital. The cash route needs a deposit, a bank's yes and months of waiting. The paid-over-time route has the seller fund the deal, you bring nothing, and you still put the biggest number on the table.

This is for owners, executives and people with capital who want a cash-flowing business without tying up their own money. If you want a four-hour week, this isn't your channel.

One line: don't ask what a business is worth. Ask what it can pay.

Two offers for the same business. Option A, the cash buyer. Option B, paid over time. Then a test that tells you which one you can actually make.

And if you've spent years thinking you need a deposit first, that has nothing to do with your salary or your savings. It has everything to do with a question nobody taught you to ask.

I help owners build these structures and put them in front of sellers. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A. The cash buyer.**

A cash buyer pays three to five times profit. On $250,000, that's $750,000 to $1.25 million. Call it a million.

To pay it, they need a deposit and a bank. The bank takes six to sixteen weeks. The seller waits, and the deal can still die in credit.

For a retiring owner that's the worst of both. A lower price, and a slow close with no certainty.

**Option B. Paid over time.**

Three steps. I call it the speed run.

One. Real profit. Say $250,000.

Two. Divide by 1.5. About $166,000 a year. That's the most this business should pay in debt each year. The other $84,000 stays in the business as a buffer. That rule is next episode.

Three. Multiply by the term. Ten years at $166,000 is $1.66 million. About 6.6 times profit.

You've just outbid the cash buyer by about $660,000. And you can close in two to four weeks, not six to sixteen.

Now, you were told you need a deposit, a track record and a bank's permission. That's not how retiring owners sell. My first deal was a physio clinic. I was 25, unemployed, no savings. 100% vendor finance, seven years at 4%, a nine-month payment holiday, rent in arrears, no deposit. I pulled $150,000 out in year one.

The seller got paid by the business. Not by me.

**The test. Three checks before you say a number.**

The speed run is a ceiling, not a price. Three checks turn it into an offer.

Check one. The profit is real. Every seller's profit number is a story. Some tell it high, some tell it low, most don't know it. Get three years of financials, the matching tax returns, and revenue by financial year. If the financials and the tax returns don't match, that's not a negotiation. It's a walk.

Then normalise it. Add back what the owner runs through the business that you won't. Take out a proper wage for whoever runs it after you. Say the books show $280,000. Add $20,000 of the owner's personal costs. Take out a $50,000 manager. Real profit, $250,000. Skip the manager and you'd use $300,000, and offer $2 million for a business that can only carry $1.66 million. That's how people buy a job with a debt attached.

Check two. Interest. The $166,000 is principal and interest together. At 4% over ten years, it repays about $1.35 million of price. That's your real number. Still above any cash offer.

Check three. The levers. Term, rate, a payment holiday before the first instalment, and sometimes a balloon. My good friend Cam did two deals in 23 days, both 100% vendor finance, interest-only for ten years with balloons in years three and ten. Interest-only keeps the payments small. The balloon is the principal in one lump. Never sign one you haven't planned to pay, from cash you've built up or a bank refinance once you have a track record.

Option B wins when three things are true. The profit survives normalising. The value is big enough to be worth the work, roughly a million and up. And the seller can wait to be paid. If they need every dollar on day one, you blend with a bank. That's episode 14.

Self-test. Can you name this business's real profit, why the seller is selling, and how long they can wait for their money? If not, you're not ready to make either offer.

One more rule. Never say "vendor finance" to a seller. Say "getting paid over time". Something like: "I can get you more than a cash buyer would, paid over time out of the business you built, and we can close in weeks, not months."

Even after interest you're about $100,000 above the top of the cash range. And the seller's money is doing the funding.

Where people lose money.

- Using the seller's profit instead of the normalised one. On these numbers that's about $330,000 overpaid.
- Opening with the ceiling. You've got nowhere to move, and the seller anchors on your top number.
- Signing a balloon with no refinance plan. The deal you won becomes the debt you can't pay.

So there are two paths. You can do this alone. My version of that was four failed deals and about a thousand cold calls before the first one closed. Or you can do it with someone who's already made those mistakes.

On the call, it's a short application, then me. We run the speed run on a real business you've found, sketch your first structure, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather run the numbers than read about them, and you'll put real hours in from Monday, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either you'll still be asking what businesses are worth, or you'll know what three of them can pay and which seller to ring.

## EP 12 · Every DSCR From 0.96× to 2× (and Why I Sign at 1.5)

Two buyers look at the same business making $300,000 a year. James wants it, so he stretches the debt until the seller's price fits. Chad won't sign below 1.5.

A year later, one big customer leaves. James is topping the business up from his own pocket. Chad's capital is still his.

Same business. One number. Here's every level of it, from 0.96 to 2.

If you've got capital, this number decides whether a business pays you or you pay it. Get it wrong and the downside isn't a lower return. It's writing cheques into a business you bought to receive them.

This is for people who want to own businesses for the long run. If you're trying to squeeze every last dollar of debt out of a deal, this will annoy you. Good.

DSCR, debt service coverage ratio: the cash the business makes before debt, divided by every debt payment you owe in a year. Bank, seller, equipment finance, all of it.

There are four levels. 0.96, 1.2, 1.5 and 2. Most stretched deals sit on level two and look fine until the first bad year.

And if you've struggled to tell a safe deal from a dangerous one, it has nothing to do with your intelligence or how long you've invested. It has everything to do with what you're shown. Sellers and brokers show you the good year. Nobody hands you the bad one.

I help owners and investors structure deals that survive their worst year. Book a call below. Now the levels.

**One term first. The covenant.**

When a bank lends, it writes a covenant into the loan: a minimum DSCR, tested regularly. Every lender sets its own, so ask. For this video, say 1.25. Drop below it and you're in breach, and a breach hands the lender rights over your business you don't want them to have.

So here's how most people think about a deal. They think it's safe if it pays in an average year. That's not how it works. A deal is safe if it still pays in its worst year.

All the levels below use the same business. $300,000 profit in the base case. A bad year is profit down 20%, to $240,000. One customer leaves, or one key person walks.

**Level 1. 0.96×. The business can't pay its own debt.**

James's deal. He stretches to $250,000 a year of payments on $300,000 of profit. In the bad year, $240,000 comes in and $250,000 goes out.

The business can't cover its own debt. He's $10,000 short, in breach, and explaining himself to a bank. That's the bottom of the ladder, and you get there from level two with one bad customer.

**Level 2. 1.2×. Fine on a good year.**

Same deal, base case. $300,000 over $250,000 is 1.2. On a good year it pays, just. It's already under a 1.25 covenant, so a bank won't touch it, and it only gets done because the seller carries the debt without stress-testing it. Any dip and it's level one.

This is where most stretched deals sit. They start with the seller's price and work backwards until the debt fits. Then the add-backs grow and the bad year gets gentler until a 1.2 looks like a 1.5.

**Level 3. 1.5×. The floor.**

$200,000 of payments on $300,000. For every dollar you owe, a dollar fifty comes in.

The real number is headroom: how far profit can fall before you breach. At 1.5 with a 1.25 covenant, profit can fall to $250,000. About 17%. A 20% bad year takes you to 1.2. Still a breach.

So 1.5 is the floor in the base case, not the target. It's the least that gives you a chance.

[JOSH: one line on a deal you walked from because it failed 1.5.]

**Level 4. 2×. Change the structure, not the spreadsheet.**

Say the debt is a $1.2 million seller note at 4%. Over seven years, about $200,000 a year. That's level three.

Ask for ten years instead. About $148,000 a year. Same price. Same seller. Base DSCR about 2. Bad year about 1.6. You've bought a bad year just by asking for a longer term.

Other levers do the same job. A payment holiday at the start. An interest-only period, though watch interest cover, profit divided by interest alone, and the year the principal comes due. Part of the price as an earn-out. Or the seller rolling part of their value into shares. That's episode 14.

**The rule.**

1.5 in the base case. Above the covenant in the bad year. If the bad year fails, change the structure. Never the numbers.

Build the bad year from the actual business. Take out the biggest customer. Price in replacing the one person everyone relies on. Look at the worst of the last three years. Then add year-one takeover costs: systems, lawyers, a manager overlapping with the old owner.

Self-test. If you can't name the biggest customer and the DSCR without them, you're not ready to sign.

And once you own it, check one thing every month. Cash collected, minus costs, minus debt payments. Track DSCR on the last twelve months, so if it slides from 1.6 to 1.4, you see it months before the bank does.

Same business, same price. The term alone moves the bad year from minus $10,000 to plus $92,000.

Where people lose money.

- Starting from the seller's price and stretching the debt to fit. You win the deal and fund its first bad year.
- Leaving small debts off the bottom of the fraction. Equipment finance counts.
- Signing interest-only without testing the year the principal lands. The DSCR looks great until it doesn't.

Or you can keep doing what James did. Keep starting with the seller's price. Keep letting the bad year get gentler. And keep finding out in the first bad quarter. Alone, you fall in love with a business and the spreadsheet starts lying for you. You need someone in the room who isn't in love with the deal.

On the call, short application, then me. We pressure-test your deal or target against its worst year, sketch the structure that passes, and see whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather walk from a deal than fund it from your own pocket, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either you'll still be looking at deals you can't quite judge, or you'll have a base case, a bad year and a floor you trust.

## EP 13 · The Lender Matrix That Finds $300K More Borrowing (Copy It)

Most business owners have one bank, and they ring it the week they need money. They've got a deadline, the bank can smell it, and they get one answer.

Same business, same rule: one lender will lend you about $1.04 million, another about $1.34 million. You only know which if you asked before the deal.

Here's the system, in the order the bank sees it.

$300,000 of borrowing is $300,000 more you can pay a seller without bringing a cent. Or it's the difference between a deal that settles and one that dies in week ten.

This is for owners who want a second and third business, not just one. If picking up the phone fifty times is a deal-breaker, it's not for you.

One line: interview fifty lenders before you need one, and go in as the interviewer.

Four parts, in the order the bank meets you. The email. The signature and the one-pager. The call. Then the hypothetical, which feeds the lender matrix.

And if a bank has said no to you before, it has nothing to do with your business or your credit. It has everything to do with asking one bank, at the worst time, for a favour.

I build lender lists and lender presentations with owners, so the money's lined up before the deal is. Book a call below. Now the teardown.

**The email.**

A bank is set up to lend. That's the whole business. But every bank lends differently: sectors, ticket sizes, terms. One will say no to a deal another would happily fund.

So you write to the business banker, not the front counter. Big banks, regionals and specialist lenders. On LinkedIn, look for business banking manager or commercial lending.

Notice the word "interviewing". No begging, no life story. You're someone with options, because you're about to have them.

**The signature and the one-pager.**

The banker's second look is who's behind you. Put your board in your signature. "Board includes a former CFO and a sector operator" reads very differently from a name and a phone number. Send the one-page executive summary ahead: board, sector, size of business, roughly how you'll structure it. That's episodes 6 and 9 paying off.

Now, I know what you're thinking. "Banks don't lend to people like me." Christian Dawes was unemployed, with no higher education and a criminal record. His first deal was $2.4 million, and the bank funded it 100%. Over-financed, in fact. So the question was never whether banks lend to people like you. It's which bank, and on what file.

**The call.**

Five groups of questions. They're the columns of your matrix.

Sector. How many deals have you done in this industry, at what size, and what worries you about it?

Credit. What DSCR and interest cover do you need? How do you calculate profit here, and what do you add back?

Structure. How much of the price will you lend, over how many years? Balloons? Standard covenants?

Security and guarantees. What do you take as security? What's your personal guarantee policy, under $5 million and over?

Process. Who makes the credit decision, you or a committee? How long from application to money? What do you need to see?

That last one matters more than people think. A banker who decides is worth ten who pass your file to someone you'll never meet.

Run your first five calls with lenders you care about least. Put the one you most want at number fifteen, when you sound like you've done this before. No reply? A short email a week later, then a call. Bankers are busy, not rude.

[JOSH: one line on how many lenders you spoke to before JC Health, and what one told you.]

**The hypothetical and the matrix.**

Then ask: "Say I brought you a healthcare business, profit around a million, buying at four times. How would you look at it?" Some give you a term sheet. Some give you a conversation. Both go in the matrix.

Here's what one column is worth. A business makes $300,000. At 1.5 you can carry $200,000 a year in payments. At 8%, Lender A amortises over seven years. That $200,000 borrows about $1.04 million. Lender B amortises over ten. The same $200,000 borrows about $1.34 million.

A no is a row, not a failure. "We don't do that sector" is data. By lender thirty you'll know the market better than most people who've bought businesses for years. Sort the rows: who likes your sector, who decides locally, who's flexible on guarantees. That's your top five. Self-test: if you can't name five lenders who like your sector and who decides at each, you're not ready to make an offer. When a deal lands, they get a call the same day with a file that answers the questions they told you they'd ask. And if you're in the US, the same approach works. I have a lender network there too.

Same business, same rule, same rate. The only variable is which lender you knew to call.

Where people lose money.

- Waiting for a deal before meeting a bank. The seller won't wait while you start from scratch, and the deal goes to whoever had a banker on speed dial.
- Turning the interview into a pitch. Ask for money on the first call and you burn the relationship.
- Going quiet after the call. A short email each quarter on your pipeline, and tell them when you close, even if they didn't fund it. Bankers like lending to people who close.

Two paths. Alone, most people ring their own bank, get one no and decide banks won't lend to them. Or they build the bench with someone who's already built one, and walk into every call knowing what to ask.

On the call, short application, then me. We map your strategy, sketch your first structure, and work out whether the Owners Academy, with the lender email and question list, or 1:1 advisory, where I build your lender presentation with you, fits.

If you're someone who's tired of one bank deciding what you can buy, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either you'll still have one bank and one answer, or a matrix with thirty rows and five lenders who know your name.
