## EP 14 · $5M Deal, Nothing Down: 3 Ways to Fund the Seller's 40% (Ranked)

Getting paid over time is a phenomenal way to buy a business. Until the seller wants cash. Then it goes from an easy yes to "I'm not waiting ten years", and from one business to no deal.

My friend Jason Nagy's first deal was a $5.5 million radiology business. Zero down. A bank funded part of it. The seller funded the rest by staying in.

Here are the three ways to fund the seller's share, ranked. One fails.

[JOSH: confirm Jason is happy to be named, and which of his terms, if any, can go on screen.]

This is the structure that gets you past one business. Seller finance alone caps you at sellers who'll wait. A blend lets you buy bigger, asset-rich businesses where the profit is measured in millions, with nothing of yours in.

It's for owners who want bigger deals and will bring in a bank. If you only want small, quick flips, this one's more than you need.

One line: the bank funds about 60%, the seller funds about 40% by not taking it as cash.

The 60 is the easy part. Commercial debt at market rates, and it's what puts cash in the seller's hand at settlement. The question is the 40. There are three ways to handle it, and I'll rank them from worst to best.

If you've lost a deal to a seller who wanted cash, it has nothing to do with your offer or your bank balance. It has everything to do with having only one structure to offer.

I work through blends like this with owners every week: which mix, which lender, how to say it to the seller. Book a call below. Now the ranking.

**The deal.**

Round numbers, illustrative. A business makes $1.25 million profit. You buy at four times: $5 million. The bank lends 60%, $3 million, over seven years at 8%. About $575,000 a year. The seller's 40% is $2 million.

Blends work best on businesses like radiology. Expensive equipment, referrals that keep coming. Asset-rich, steady, boring. That's what a bank lends against.

**#3. Seller note, fully amortising. Fails.**

The seller lends you the $2 million. Seven years at 4%. About $333,000 a year.

Add the bank and you're paying about $910,000 a year on $1.25 million of profit. DSCR about 1.37. Under the 1.5 floor before anything goes wrong. It fails. Walk away from this version, not from the deal.

**#2. Seller note, interest-only. Passes, with a catch.**

Same note, interest-only. About $80,000 a year. Total payments about $655,000. DSCR around 1.9. Passes.

The catch: the $2 million is still owed one day. That's a balloon. It works if you've planned how you'll pay it: cash built up, or a refinance once the business has a track record with you. Never sign one you haven't planned for.

**#1. Rollover equity.**

The seller rolls their 40% into shares in the new company. No repayments to the seller at all. Your only debt is the bank's $575,000. DSCR about 2.2. Plenty of room for a bad year.

And the seller now wins when you win. They get most of their money at settlement, and a stake, so when you grow the business and sell it as part of a group, they get paid a second time. The bank likes it too: it's lending only part of the value, with the seller's money sitting behind its own. How big a stake they end up with is negotiation. Your accountant and lawyer build the share structure.

Here's why sellers say yes. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. Some will take paid over time. Some want cash now. A buyer with one structure loses every seller who wants cash. A buyer who can blend talks to both.

**Making #1 work.**

Three things people skip.

Minority rules. A seller with a stake is a minority shareholder. Drag-along, so if you sell the whole thing they come with you. No veto and no voting control for minorities. Set it from day one. It's very hard to add later.

The bank, early. The bank will want the seller's money behind theirs. Tell the seller up front, so it's not a surprise in legals. And if you can't agree on value, part of the price can be an earn-out, paid only if the business hits a target after you buy. One measure, ideally revenue, because it's harder to argue about.

The order. Agree the headline price first, because that's what sellers focus on. Then how it's paid. Then bring in the bank. And how you say it: not "vendor finance", not "rollover equity". "You get most of your money at settlement, and you keep a share, so when we grow it and sell the group, you get paid again."

Where it breaks. The seller wants 100% cash and won't hear anything else. The bank doesn't like the sector, which you find out in your lender matrix, not the week before settlement. Or the seller wants to stay in charge. Then they're not selling. Walk.

Jason's first deal ended in a $200 million exit to private equity, seven years later. That's episode 34.

Same price, same seller, same bank. How you treat the 40% decides whether the deal fails the floor or clears it with room to spare.

Where people lose money.

- Walking from every seller who wants cash, and staying stuck at businesses small enough for one structure.
- Giving the seller a stake with no drag-along. Two years later they have a say in your exit.
- Picking interest-only for the DSCR and forgetting the $2 million that comes due.

Or you can keep doing what you're doing. Keep walking from sellers who want cash. Keep running one business while the bigger ones go to whoever knew how to blend. And alone, a blend gets complicated fast: two parties to keep happy, a bank that wants things the seller doesn't.

On the call, short application, then me. We look at your seller, run all three options on the real numbers, and see whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. On bigger blended deals, equity or advisory roles are considered case by case.

If you're someone who's hit the ceiling of one business and would rather blend than walk, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either you'll still be walking from sellers who want cash, or you'll have run all three options on a real business and know which one fits.

## EP 15 · Investor Money Is the Most Expensive Money in a $2M Deal

There are two types of first-time business buyers. The ones who borrow the money and keep the company. And the ones who sell half of it to get started, and spend years working for their investors.

On an $8 million exit, that choice is worth about $4 million.

Debt ends. Equity you give away never comes back.

Your first deal sets the cap table for everything after it. If you're building toward a group and an exit, the share you keep is the most valuable thing you own. Give it away to avoid a few bank meetings and you've sold the exit before you've started.

This is for people who want to own the company, not a slice of it. If you want a lifestyle business, this series isn't for you.

One line: borrow the money, keep the company.

Four parts. The claim, in numbers. Why everyone raises anyway. The test for when equity is actually right. And the three protections that make debt safe.

And if your first instinct was to find a partner with money, it has nothing to do with being naive or scared. It has everything to do with how buying a business gets explained. Everyone talks about raising money. Nobody shows you the bill at the exit.

I help owners build first structures they keep control of. Book a call below. Now the numbers.

**The claim, in numbers.**

Round numbers, illustrative. A business makes $500,000 profit. You buy it at four times, $2 million.

Route one, investors. They put up the money and take half the company.

Route two, debt. Seller note or bank, seven years at 4%. About $333,000 a year. DSCR 1.5, right on the floor. Over seven years you repay about $2.33 million. The interest is about $330,000, and the business pays all of it.

Now you grow it. Profit gets to $1 million. You bolt it into a group and sell at eight times. $8 million.

Investor route: you get half. $4 million. The investors get the other $4 million, and they had a vote on when and how you sold.

Debt route: the debt's long gone. You keep the lot, less your board's equity.

Think of it like a house. A loan is renting money for a few years. Equity is selling a room in your house, forever, to someone who also gets a say in when you sell.

**Why everyone raises anyway.**

Because raising feels safer. No repayments, someone else's risk. But it's slow. Months per cheque. And you give away equity, and with it control and the exit. A bank is set up to lend. Debt is easier to find than investors, and when it's paid, it's gone.

The other reason: people think you need to give away a big chunk to get serious people around you. Here's mine. My board was found entirely through LinkedIn and cold outreach. The founder of the Australian Institute of Fitness is a shareholder. I still keep 73% of my company.

My board does get equity. Founders' equity, earned by helping build the company. That's paying for credibility, not cash. You can bring in serious people and keep control at the same time.

[JOSH: one line on whether you were offered investor money early and why you said no.]

**The test. When equity is right.**

Debt is only safe inside the 1.5 rule. Debt the business can't comfortably cover isn't leverage. It's a trap.

So here's the test. If the debt won't fit inside 1.5 however you structure it, and the deal is good enough, a small amount of equity can close the gap. But try the longer term, the payment holiday, the blend and the earn-out first. Equity is the last tool, not the first.

And if you do bring investors in, give them economics, not control. Drag-along, no veto, set up from the start. A lender wants its money back with interest. An investor wants a share of everything, forever.

**Three protections that make debt safe.**

One. Personal guarantees. A guarantee lets the lender come after you personally if the business can't pay. On larger deals, over about $5 million, you often don't need one. An asset-rich business gives the bank its security. That's typical, not universal. On smaller deals, expect to be asked. Don't just sign. Ask what else would satisfy them: tighter reporting, a cash reserve requirement, security over specific assets. Buy through a company set up for that deal, and have your lawyer read every line of what you're guaranteeing.

Two. Working capital. Buying the business is one loan. Running it is another. Get a working capital line approved before settlement and drawn on the day. It pays fees at close, integration and runway. As a rough guide, often around 10% of revenue: about $300,000 on a $3 million business. My good friend Cam got $500,000 of working capital from the bank on his two deals. The word that matters is approved. Not "should be fine".

Three. A reserve floor. A number the business's cash never drops below. A few months of payroll and debt payments. No distributions under it. And never stack credit cards on top of a seller note to get through a bad month.

The lender's money cost about $330,000, paid by the business. The investor's money cost $4 million, paid by you.

Where people lose money.

- Bringing in a mate's money "just for the deposit" and giving away a third of the company for it.
- Signing a guarantee without asking what else the lender would take.
- Settling without approved working capital and paying the lawyers from your own pocket in week one.

That's all of Capital. A business can pay for itself. The 1.5 rule keeps it safe. A lender bench funds it, and a blend keeps a cash seller in. Before this act, you'd have gone looking for investors.

Two paths from here. Alone, this is where people panic: they sign the guarantee unread, or sell a third for a deposit. Or you build the structure with someone who's done it, so control stays with you from day one.

On the call, short application, then me. We work out your first structure, debt the business can carry, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who wants to own the company, not a slice of it, and you'll read every line before you sign, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either still wondering who'll fund you, or with a structure on paper and your equity still yours.

Borrow the money. Keep the company. Book a call with me below. Not a setter. Me.

# Act 4 · Finding deals

## EP 16 · I made 1,000 cold calls before my first 100% vendor-financed deal

There's a well-kept secret in buying businesses. The one you should buy isn't for sale.

It's not on a listing site. No broker has it. It belongs to an owner nobody has rung yet.

Before my first deal I made about a thousand of those calls and watched four deals fall over. Then one closed, with no deposit.

Where you find a business decides what you pay for it. A listed business is an auction: cash buyers, sometimes private equity, a broker paid to push the price up. An owner you call direct has one buyer on the line. You. That's where terms come from, and terms are where the money is. This is for people who'll pick up the phone forty hours a week. If you want a listing to land in your inbox, this isn't your episode.

The rule in one line: go direct to owners, and treat a thousand calls as the unit, not fifty.

Three parts. What happened on my thousand. Why off-market pays. And the system that turns calls into deals.

If you've looked for a business before and found nothing worth buying, it has nothing to do with your salary, your age or your contacts. It has everything to do with where you were looking. Most good businesses never go in the "for sale" window.

I help people run this, from the list to the first offer, in the Owners Academy or one-on-one. Book a call below if you want help with yours. Now the detail.

**The confession.**

At 25 I was unemployed. No savings, zero experience, no network.

You were told you need a network to find a deal. You were told good businesses come through brokers. You were told no owner sells to someone with no track record. I had none of that. So I called.

About a thousand calls. Four deals that got close and died. **[JOSH: one line on what killed one of the four failed deals.]**

The one that closed was a physio clinic. 100% vendor finance. Seven years at 4% principal and interest. A nine-month payment holiday. Rent in arrears. No deposit. I pulled about $150K out in year one.

The thousand calls and the four failures were the training. Nobody puts that part in the thumbnail.

Here's the diagnosis. The calls didn't fail because owners won't sell. Most of them said "not now," not "never." What I didn't have early on was a list I trusted, a fixed time to call, and a date to ring each owner back. Once I had those three, the calls stopped being a mood and became a pipeline.

**Why off-market pays.**

A generation of owners in traditional industries wants to retire. Clinics, electricians, cleaners, plumbers. Twenty or thirty years carrying the business. They want out, and they want their staff looked after.

Most have never listed. Listing means a broker, months of tyre-kickers and a buyer's bank process that can run sixteen weeks.

Call direct and three things change. There's no auction. You can talk about the seller getting paid over time. And you set the pace. Paid over time, a seller can get above market, six to seven times profit instead of three to five, and close in two to four weeks instead of six to sixteen.

Do the maths on that from the seller's side. A higher price, paid from a business they already know makes money, with no auction and no sixteen-week wait. For a lot of retiring owners that beats a lower cash cheque from a stranger.

One rule. Never say "vendor finance" to a seller. Say "getting paid over time." One sounds like a loan. The other sounds like a pension.

**The system.**

Volume before judgement. Fifty calls tells you nothing. At call one hundred you're reading off the page. By three hundred you sound like a person. By six hundred you hear an owner's mood in the first sentence. You can't skip to six hundred.

Pick one industry and one city. You want to sound like you know the space by week two.

Build a list of one to two thousand businesses from directories, associations, map searches and company registers. Owner's name, phone, rough revenue. Aim for roughly half a million to ten million in revenue. Under about a million in value, a deal rarely throws off enough cash to pay the debt and pay you.

Block the same three hours every morning. Forty to fifty calls. Afternoons are follow-up.

Run a tracker. Status goes Cold, Warm, Hot, Financials, Offer, Due diligence, Close. The column that makes money is "next action date." Every "not now" gets a date. Health changes. A partner wants out. The kids say no. Whoever calls back in the right month gets the deal.

It works without a track record. Saul, an Owners Academy member, found an electrical and air-conditioning business by cold call. Twenty-five staff, about $3.8 million revenue, owner retiring in two years. NDA signed, early due diligence. No broker, no auction.

Sixty hours to put yourself in front of most of the owners in one industry and one city. People spend longer picking a company name.

Where people lose money.

- Stopping at call sixty and deciding the industry's wrong. The first thousand was the cost of entry, and you've thrown it away.
- No follow-up. Every "not yet" goes cold, and the owner sells to whoever rang back.
- Talking price on the first call. You've set the anchor before you've seen a tax return.
- Calling five industries at once. You sound like a tourist in all of them.

Two ways to do your first thousand calls. Alone, which usually means years, deals lost to silly mistakes, and quitting around call sixty. Or with someone who's made the thousand and lived through the four dead deals, looking at your list and your calls every week.

On a call with me we work out your industry, your list and your first structure, and whether the Academy or one-on-one advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather make the calls than wait for a listing, and you can hear no forty times and dial the forty-first, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with most of your first thousand calls made and a tracker full of next-action dates.

## EP 17 · The 6-word question that finds off-market deals (copy it)

There are two types of people who ring a business owner. The ones who get a conversation, and the ones who get a click.

The difference is six words, asked inside twenty seconds. Most people bury them under two minutes of small talk, then quote a price to sound serious.

Here's the whole call, line by line, in the order the owner hears it.

The first sixty seconds decide two things: whether you get a second call, and what number you'll be negotiating from later. Get it wrong and you either get hung up on or you anchor a price you can't walk back. If you run a business, you've been on the other end of this call. You know how fast you decide.

The rule: earn ten seconds, ask the question, then ask, don't tell.

Five parts, in order. The opener. The question. The three answers. Discovery. The exit.

If your calls have been dying early, it has nothing to do with your voice, your age or your accent. It has everything to do with your first sentence.

I help people with this, from the first script to the signed offer, in the Academy or one-on-one. Book a call below. Now the call.

**The opener.**

Word for word.

Four parts. Name and company, so you sound like a business. "Building a company in your industry," so you sound like a buyer with a plan. That line only works if it's true, which is why Credibility comes first. "Wanted to introduce myself," which says relationship, not one-off. Then the question.

No "how are you going today." Every owner knows that line means a sales call.

[JOSH: one line on the opener you actually used on the physio clinic call, if it differed.]

**The question.**

"Have you ever thought about selling" is soft on purpose. It doesn't ask them to commit. It's easy for an owner to say, "Yeah, actually, I have." And a fair few do.

Compare "Are you looking to sell?" That's a yes-or-no about today, and today the answer is nearly always no. "Ever thought about it" lets them tell you about the bad year, the sore back, the kids who don't want it.

**The three answers.**

"Yes, actually." Don't pitch. Ask, "What's got you thinking about it?" Then shut up.

"Not right now." Say, "No problem. I'm building relationships in the industry. Mind if I check back in six to twelve months?" Date in the tracker. A "not yet" owner is still an owner.

"No," or the click. "No worries, thanks for your time." Next call.

**Discovery.**

Most people think a good first call is a good pitch. That the owner needs to hear about you, your plan, your money. That's not how it works.

You have the most leverage before you've said a number. The moment you say one, that's the number. So on call one you ask.

That last one is the one people skip. A spouse, a partner, the accountant. If someone else decides, you want them on the next call, not hearing about it over dinner.

Learn the questions, then have a conversation. It should sound like two business owners talking.

Here's why discovery is worth money. The answers become your structure. An owner who cares about the staff wants a handover period, and that's time for you to learn the business on their watch. An owner who wants steady income is the one who'll take getting paid over time. You can't design that offer if you spent the call talking.

Don't explain the 3C model. Don't say "vendor finance." Don't mention a multiple. And never say you've got no money.

If they ask how you'd pay: "We'd look at options once I've seen the numbers. Some owners like getting paid over time, some want more up front."

Here's an illustrative call.

"Hi Gary, this is Josh. I'm building a company in commercial cleaning here in Melbourne and wanted to introduce myself. Have you ever thought about selling?"

"Funny you ask. My wife's been on at me about it."

"What's got you both thinking about it?"

"I'm sixty-three. Back's had it. Kids aren't interested."

"If you did step back, what would matter most?"

"The staff. Some have been with me fifteen years."

"Would your wife want to be part of the conversation?"

"Probably, yeah."

No price, no structure, nothing about me. Gary did the talking. I know why he'd sell, what he cares about, and who decides.

**The exit.**

Leave every live call with an NDA going out or a date to call back. Send the NDA within the hour, not tomorrow. How fast it comes back is your first read on how serious they are. Once it's signed, you ask for three years of numbers, and that's where pricing actually starts.

On call one you have no tax returns, no revenue breakdown, no idea what the add-backs are hiding. Any number you say is a guess with your name on it. Saying a number early doesn't make you look serious. It hands the seller $280K of expectation that you created and now have to take back.

Where people lose money.

- Quoting a multiple to sound smart. The owner anchors on it for the rest of the deal.
- Saying "vendor finance." The owner hears "this buyer has no money." Say "getting paid over time."
- Finding out about the spouse or partner three months in, when they quietly kill the deal.
- Talking about yourself. Calls that die around two minutes usually died there.

You can keep pitching on call one, quoting a multiple, and finding out who really decides after you've spent three months on the deal. Or you can have someone listen to your calls and tell you what to fix.

On a call with me we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Academy or one-on-one advisory fits.

If you're someone who already knows what it's like to carry a business alone, and you'd rather ask the question than give the speech, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with an opener that sounds like you and a few NDAs out.

## EP 18 · Broker vs off-market: same $250K-profit business, $1M vs $1.66M

Every day, almost everyone looking for a business does the same thing. Same listing sites. Same brokers. Same auction.

A broker's job is to get the seller the highest price, in cash, fast. Fair job. It's the opposite of yours.

Same business, two ways to buy it. One costs about a million in cash. The other pays the seller more and costs you nothing up front.

If you've got capital, you can buy a listed business outright and plenty do. The question is whether you should pay auction prices with your own cash when the seller would fund it for you. Every dollar you don't put into deal one goes into deal two. This is for buyers who want terms, not just a purchase.

The rule: at least 80% of your deal-finding goes direct to owners. Brokers get 20%, at most.

Option A, buy listed. Option B, buy direct. A self-test. Then the verdict, and when a broker is still worth a call.

If you've lost listed deals before, it has nothing to do with your offer, your capital or your judgement. It has everything to do with the room you were in. That process is built for one outcome: the highest cash bid at settlement.

I help people buy with little to no money down, which mostly means getting them off listing sites and onto the phone. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A: buy listed.**

Three costs.

Competition. A listing is an auction. Cash buyers, sometimes private equity. With little down you lose most of them. With money, you pay for the privilege of winning.

Price. The broker is usually paid on the sale. Their incentive is a high headline, cash at settlement, done quickly. An offer paid over time is what that process is built to filter out.

Leverage. The broker sets the timeline. You're one name on a spreadsheet. "Offers by Friday."

Brokers do real work. For a seller who wants the most cash on the day, a good one earns the fee. Just remember who they work for.

And the listing has been dressed for sale. Read it the way a lender would: the profit on the tax return, not the profit in the brochure.

**Option B: buy direct.**

Here's what a lot of buyers haven't caught up with. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. The listing sites still look the same, but many of the owners who'll sell in the next few years haven't rung a broker yet.

For that owner, getting paid over time can be the better deal. Six to seven times profit instead of three to five. Two to four weeks to close instead of six to sixteen. You set the pace, you shape the structure, and you can walk away.

That conversation is hard to start once a broker has told the seller the goal is maximum cash.

And there's no bidding war, so the price is set by what the business can carry, not by the most excited buyer in the room. That's the number you want to pay.

**The self-test.**

Three questions.

Do you need the seller to carry part of the price? Do you want your capital free for the next deal? Would you rather not wait sixteen weeks on a buyer's bank?

Two yeses and you go direct. Three noes and you've got cash, patience and one deal in mind. Buy listed and pay the market. Just know that's what you're doing.

**The verdict.**

Direct first. Brokers for three things.

Stale listings. Six months or more on the market. The cash buyers have looked and passed, and the seller is more open than on day one.

Open brokers. Some will put terms in front of their client once they see a credible team behind you. Ask straight:

Market intel. What's selling, at what multiples, which owners asked about value and never listed. Take them for a coffee. Never tell a broker your walk-away number.

Same rules on a broker deal as any other. Three years of financials and matching tax returns before an offer. The 1.5 cover rule. And a willingness to walk.

In hours, that's four mornings a week on owner calls and one block on brokers and stale listings. Do that for a month and you'll know your market's prices better than most buyers, so the stale listing that's actually a bargain jumps out.

[JOSH: one listed deal you looked at and passed on, and why.]

You pay more in total. The seller gets more in total. The business pays the seller from its own cash flow with 1.5 times cover, and your capital stays free for the next one. For someone deploying money, the return on cash you didn't put in is the number that matters.

Where people lose money.

- Trusting add-backs. If "adjusted" profit is miles above the tax return, the price is built on air.
- Financials that don't match the tax returns. Walk away, listed or not.
- Letting "other buyers are interested" or a Friday deadline set your pace. You don't do your best work on someone else's clock.
- One or two customers making up a big slice of revenue. One contract leaves and so does your debt cover.

Two paths. Six months on listing sites, a handful of offers beaten by cash or won at the top of the range, then deciding little-to-no-money-down doesn't work. Or the same six months spent talking to owners, with someone who's read the numbers on the deals you're looking at.

On a call with me we look at your capital, your strategy and your first structure, and whether the Academy or one-on-one advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather set the terms than bid at an auction, and you want your capital in real businesses bought properly, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with owners on the phone, two brokers who know what you buy, and a stale listing worth a proper look.

## EP 19 · 1,000 calls to 1 business: every stage of the deal-flow maths

Two owners both want a second business. One reads listings on Sunday nights and calls owners when the shop's quiet. The other calls in the same block four mornings a week and writes down five numbers every Monday.

A year later the first one has a folder. The second has offers out.

A friend of mine in the US, Elliot, sent around forty offers. Three came back signed, all at 100% seller finance. Forty to get three. That's what deal flow looks like.

If you own one business and want a second, the constraint usually isn't capital. The deal gets funded by the structure, the seller and the lender. The constraint is a pipeline you can't measure. This is for owners who like knowing where they stand. If you're counting on one lucky call, it might happen. Don't plan on it.

The rule: work backwards from one close to a weekly number, then track five stages.

Calls. Conversations. NDAs. Offers. Closes. Five levels. Each one leaks for a different reason.

If you've been at this for months with nothing to show, it has nothing to do with your work ethic or your market. It has everything to do with what you were counting. Most people count deals, and deals lag by months.

I help people build this pipeline and read it every week, in the Academy or one-on-one. Book a call below if you want someone to find your leak. Now the levels.

**Level 1: Calls.**

A call is a dial, answered or not. Say a thousand.

This is the one stage you fully control. If few calls turn into conversations, your list is weak or you're ringing at the wrong time of day. Mornings suit most owners.

Set a weekly call target you can hit four weeks running. Not a heroic one. Two hundred a week is four mornings of fifty. If you've only got evenings, the target is smaller and the timeline is longer. Plan with your real hours.

[JOSH: rough weekly call count in the run-up to your first deal.]

**Level 2: Conversations.**

A conversation is the owner, on the phone, hearing the question. Not the receptionist. Not voicemail. Say two hundred.

If you've got conversations but no NDAs, it's your opener and your questions. That was episode 17.

Track one more thing here: who asked you to call back, and when. Those "not yet" owners are Level 2 conversations you've already paid for. Ring them on the date and they cost you nothing to restart.

**Level 3: NDAs.**

An NDA counts when it's signed and back, not when you sent it. Say twenty.

A lot of these come from owners you first rang six or twelve months ago. That's why weeks one to five feel like nothing's happening. You're planting.

If NDAs pile up with no offers, you're over-analysing. Get the three documents, then send an offer.

Most of the twenty won't be right. The numbers won't stack up, or the owner isn't really ready. That's the stage doing its job. An NDA is cheap. It buys you a look at the books before you spend real time on the deal.

**Level 4: Offers.**

An offer counts when it's in the seller's hands with a price and a structure. Say four.

This is the number I care about most early on. So, honest question. You've been watching for months. How many offers have you sent?

Sending one feels huge. It isn't. A non-binding indicative offer is exactly that: non-binding. A price range and the shape of the deal, subject to diligence and finance. If diligence turns up problems, you adjust or you walk.

I know what you're thinking. "I've already got a business eating my week." Look at Jy Kingston, who I mentor. IT background, no finance experience. Twelve deals active while he closes his first. Not because he had advantages you don't. Because he had volume.

When you've got ten deals moving, you can walk from a bad one. When you've got one, you'll talk yourself into it.

**Level 5: Close.**

A close is settlement. Keys, not a handshake. Say one.

Offer to settlement can take three to six months. Diligence, finance, contracts. That's the lag, and it's why you never stop calling while a deal is in diligence. The next deal starts at Level 1.

You don't control closes. Owners and lenders do. You control calls, follow-ups and offers sent. Set targets on those three and treat the rest as the scoreboard.

If you've got offers but no closes, either your structures don't work for sellers or you're picking the wrong businesses. More calls won't fix that.

About sixty hours on the phone for one business, on these made-up ratios. Once you've made two hundred calls of your own, bin my numbers and forecast from yours: calls times your conversation rate, times your NDA rate. Now you can predict next month instead of hoping.

Fifteen minutes every Monday. Fill in last week, set this week. "Follow-ups due" is the owners whose next-action date lands this week. Often your cheapest deal flow.

Where people lose money.

- Fudging the definitions on a bad week. A voicemail isn't a conversation, and a sent NDA isn't a signed one.
- Sitting on twenty NDAs with no offers out. The deal goes to the buyer who sent one.
- Fixing the wrong stage. A month rewriting your script when the leak was your list.
- Working one deal at a time. You overpay for the only thing in your pipeline.

You can keep doing what the first owner does. Not knowing whether a ten percent conversation rate is good or terrible. Spending a month fixing the wrong stage. Twenty NDAs and nobody asking how many offers you've sent. Or you can have someone ask you every week.

On a call with me we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Academy or one-on-one advisory fits.

If you're someone who's hit the ceiling of one business and knows the next step is a second, not more hours in the first, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with four rows on the Monday sheet and your first offer out or close to it.

## EP 20 · Same $300K profit: why the bank funds the cleaner, not the agency

How come smart people with good jobs so often pick the one business a bank won't touch?

The café. The agency. The startup. Then the lender says no.

The best business to buy is usually the dullest one on the street. Here's how a bank picks, and a seven-question test to run before you make a single call.

With little to no money down, the lender and the seller are funding your deal. If they won't back the business, there's no deal, however good it looks. Five weeks of calls into an industry no bank likes is sixty hours gone. This is for people who want something bankable and build from there. If you want to buy the café you love, this will be a buzzkill.

The rule: the lender is your first customer. Pick the business they'd lend against.

Four parts. Why smart buyers pick wrong. What a lender pays for. Where to look. The test.

If you've been drawn to the interesting business, it has nothing to do with your judgement. It has everything to do with nobody telling you how a lender thinks.

I help people pick the industry and get the first deal funded, in the Academy or one-on-one. If you want a second opinion before you spend five weeks calling, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Why smart buyers pick wrong.**

Before you know how a lender thinks, you pick like a customer. Is it exciting? Would I use it?

After you know, you pick like a bank. Will this cash flow still be here in five years? What's there to fall back on if it isn't?

The customer lens costs you twice. The bank says no, and the seller, who needs the bank or the business to pay them, won't carry an offer either. You've done the work on a deal nobody will fund.

My first business was a physio clinic. Nobody's making a film about it. But the seller, the bank and I could all see exactly how it made money. **[JOSH: one line on why you chose a physio clinic for your first deal.]**

**What a lender pays for.**

Two things. Cash flow that lasts and assets to fall back on.

A business that's traded twenty years with repeat customers and vans, equipment and receivables gives them both. A two-year-old business with one big client and some laptops gives them neither.

Assets also change your personal risk. On larger deals, over about five million, an asset-rich business often gives the bank its security without a personal guarantee. Typically. Every lender and every deal is different.

You go into the bank as the interviewer. Part of that is bringing them a business they already like lending to.

One more filter: what caps this business? A location, one platform, one big customer, the owner? Some caps you fix after you buy. The one to fear is the owner. If the owner is the product, the customers might leave with them.

**Where to look.**

Healthcare. Allied health, GP, dental, imaging. Steady demand, regulated, clinicians tired of the admin. My friend Jason Nagy's first deal was a $5.5 million radiology business with no money down. He sold the group to private equity seven years later for $200 million.

Trades. Electrical, air-con, plumbing. Saul, an Owners Academy member, is in early diligence on an electrical and air-con business he found by cold call. About $3.8 million revenue, twenty-five staff.

Commercial services. Cleaning, logistics, waste. Contracts, vehicles, repeat work.

Professional services. Accounting practices and the like. Recurring fees.

What they share: traditional, fragmented, lots of owners near retirement. Fragmented is what makes a roll-up possible later. Buy several at around four times profit, combine them, and a group can sell at eight, ten, fifteen times.

**The test.**

Seven yes-or-no questions. Score the industry first, then each business.

Most of these you can answer from the outside. Years trading. Vans in the photos. Reviews naming the same staff for years. A website that talks about contracts, not one-off jobs.

Then a size check. Under about a million in value, a deal rarely has the cash flow to pay the debt and pay you. Five or more yeses and the right size, keep going. Fewer, move on.

Then the order. Score three industries and pick one. Score five businesses in it. Call two lenders and ask their appetite for that sector. Once a lender tells you what they like, build the list and start the calling block from episode 16.

Illustrative, not real businesses. Same profit. A gets funded, because the bank and the seller both believe the cash flow outlives the sale. B might be a fine business. It's just not a first acquisition with little to no money down.

Where people lose money.

- Falling for the interesting business, then losing five weeks and a keen seller when no lender will touch it.
- Buying a business where the owner is the product. The customers leave with them, and so does your debt cover.
- Concentration. One client at 40% is one phone call from breaking your 1.5 cover.
- Industries being eaten by technology, or commodity work on thin margins. Cheap today, cheaper next year.
- Financials that don't match the tax returns. Walk, however much you like it.

That's finding deals done. You know the deal is usually off-market, the six words, why brokers get 20%, the five numbers, and which businesses a bank will follow you into. The person who knows that doesn't go back to scrolling listings.

Two paths from here. Pick the industry you find interesting, and find out after five weeks of calls that no lender wants it. Or pressure-test the industry with me before the first call, so the five weeks go somewhere.

On a call with me we look at your industry, your strategy and your first structure, and whether the Academy or one-on-one advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather own the dull business than admire the shiny one, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with an industry scored, two lenders' appetite in your notes and your first few hundred calls made.

# Act 5 · Closing the deal

## EP 21 · Never name a price before these 3 documents (the $600K gap)

There are two types of business buyers. The ones who read the tax returns before they name a price. And the ones who pay four times profit for profit that was never there.

Illustrative numbers. The P&L says $500,000. The tax return says $350,000. At four times, that gap is $600,000 of price.

So here's my rule. No documents, no number. And the way a seller sends them tells you more than anything they say on the phone.

The first number you say becomes the anchor for the whole deal. Say it too early and every later conversation is you climbing down from it, or the seller holding you to it. This is for people who'll put serious hours into buying a business and want to pay for profit that exists. It isn't for anyone who wants to fall in love with a business on the first call.

Price is the last thing you discuss, not the first. Three documents, one test, then a number.

If you've talked price too early before, it has nothing to do with being naive or bad with numbers. It has everything to do with the order you were shown. Listings put the asking price at the top and the documents after you're attached.

Most buyers think naming a number early shows you're serious. That's not how it works. A number without documents shows the seller you'll pay for a story. The serious move is the request.

I help owners and operators do this, from the first request to a funded offer. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Three years of financials.**

Profit and loss plus balance sheet, for three years. One year can be a fluke: a big contract, a bad winter, an insurance payout. Three shows the pattern.

The P&L tells you what the business earns. The balance sheet tells you what it owes: tax, staff entitlements, equipment loans. Those become your problem on settlement day. Illustrative: a $200,000 tax debt is $200,000 off your price, or the seller's to clear before settlement. Either is fine. Finding it after you've signed isn't.

Ask for accountant-prepared statements, not management accounts. Management accounts are where the optimistic numbers live.

**Matching tax returns.**

This is the one that sets the price. The financials are what the owner tells you. The tax return is what the owner told the government. Banks lend against what can be proven.

If the two don't match, one of two things is true. The owner under-reported to the tax office, or over-reported to you. Neither is a business you pay the higher number for. And "we run a few things through cash" is income no bank will lend against. The business is worth what it can prove.

When the owner asks why you need returns, tell them straight. "The bank lends against the returns. If I want to pay you properly, I need them."

**Revenue by financial year.**

Split the way the business earns it: by service line, location or customer group. In healthcare, billings by practitioner.

Same three-year total. One is growing, one is shrinking, and the owner of the shrinking one will quote you last year's profit. The split also shows where the risk sits. If the retiring owner bills half the revenue, half the revenue retires with them.

And read gross margin across the same three years. Revenue up with margin slipping means the owner has been buying growth with price. You'd be paying a multiple on a trend that's about to turn.

**The NDA test.**

Send a confidentiality agreement within a day of the "maybe". Once it's signed, send one paragraph: three years of financials, matching tax returns, revenue by year, straight from the accountant.

Then count the days. Serious sellers send in a day or two and start asking about your timeline. Curious ones take weeks and only want to talk price. They don't get deleted. They go back on the follow-up list, and your week goes to the ones who sent.

Saul, an Owners Academy member, found an electrical and air-conditioning business by cold call, direct to the owner. About 25 staff, around $3.8 million in revenue, owner retiring in two years. NDA signed, now in early due diligence. No broker, no bidding war. Just the sequence.

[JOSH: one line on a seller whose returns didn't match the P&L, and what you did]

The range comes from the returns. The 1.5 rule tells you how much of that range the business can actually carry. Paid over seven years at 4%, $266,000 a year carries about $1.6 million. So on these numbers your real offer sits near four times, and you know that before the seller has heard a number.

Where people lose money.

- Naming a price on the first call. You anchor to the seller's story and spend the deal climbing down.
- Accepting management accounts. A $150,000 gap at 4× is $600,000 you pay for nothing.
- Over-asking. Three documents, not a 200-line checklist. The long list comes in due diligence, after the letter.
- Reading them alone. Send them to your accountant and your board the day they land.
- Treating a mismatch as a negotiating point. Statements that don't match the returns end the deal. You walk, politely.

You can do this alone. You skip the documents because you like the owner, offer on a number no bank will lend against, and lose three months finding out. Or you sit next to someone who's done it and read the same documents together before you name a number.

On a call with me we work out your strategy and your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy, with every template and daily deal discussion, or 1:1 advisory fits you. It's for people who'll give this 40-plus hours a week and want a big outcome: a roll-up exit, a legacy business or flips. It's not for lifestyle-business hunters.

If you're someone who'd rather read the numbers than be sold the story, and who'll send an NDA this week, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either your "maybe" sellers are still sitting in your inbox, or you know which are real and what their profit actually is.

## EP 22 · The 2-page offer behind a 100% seller-financed deal (copy it)

There's a well-kept secret in buying businesses: the deal is won in a two-page letter, not the contract.

Illustrative. A business making $400,000. Offer $1.6 million. All of it paid over time, out of the business's own profit. No deposit.

Get 80% of the deal agreed in that letter and the lawyers just write it down. Here it is, line by line, in the order the seller reads it.

Everything you leave out of the letter gets settled later by two law firms on the clock. Everything you put in gets settled now, in a five-minute conversation with the owner. If you already own one business and can't see how you'd fund the second, this letter is usually how.

An offer letter is an NBIO or LOI: a non-binding indicative offer, or letter of intent. Same job. What you'll pay, how you'll pay it, and what has to be true first. Four sections.

If you've hit a ceiling at one business, it has nothing to do with your ambition or your bank balance. It has everything to do with a structure nobody showed you. Banks and brokers talk as if you pay cash on the day. Many retiring owners don't need that. They need to see how they get paid.

I help owners write these and get them signed. Book a call below. Now the letter.

**Price and method.**

Write one number, not a range. Then show the working: "Based on FY24 financials and tax returns, adjusted profit of $400,000, at four times." Small businesses usually trade at three to five times profit.

When the owner sees your method, the conversation is about the inputs, not whether you're being cheeky. If they argue, they argue the profit or the multiple, and you've got the tax returns for one and the market for the other. And you agree the headline first, because that's where sellers fight hardest.

**How you get paid.**

This is where no-money-down lives. Cash at settlement, amount paid over time, term, rate, payment holiday, any rollover where the seller keeps a slice, any bank debt alongside.

Two words never appear: vendor finance. Not in the letter, not on the phone. Say "paid over time". Same thing, and it sounds like what it is: the owner getting looked after.

You were told you need a deposit. You were told sellers want the biggest cash cheque. You were told nobody takes a letter seriously without a track record.

My first deal was a physio clinic. 100% vendor finance, seven years at 4%, principal and interest. Nine-month payment holiday. Rent in arrears. No deposit. I was 25, unemployed, no savings. The structure did the work.

[JOSH: one line on how you asked for the nine-month holiday, and the seller's reaction]

Ask for the holiday after the headline is agreed. Once they have their price, time to make sure the business keeps performing reads as sensible.

Why would an owner take this? Because paid over time, owners will often accept more than they'd get in cash today, and close in two to four weeks instead of the six to sixteen a bank takes. They want out, and they want to be looked after. The letter shows them both.

**Conditions.**

What must be true before you're bound. Satisfactory due diligence. Finance approval if a bank is in. Lease assigned or renewed. Licences confirmed. Key staff on agreements. The seller's transition in writing.

These are your exits and your renegotiation levers. If diligence finds a problem, the conditions are how you change the price or walk. A letter without them is a promise to pay for whatever you find.

**Exclusivity and timeline.**

Exclusivity, usually 60 to 90 days, so the seller doesn't talk to other buyers while you pay accountants to check the business. Then target dates for diligence, contracts and settlement. A tired owner wants to know when it ends. Give them a date.

Then put the small money items in as one line each: stock on hand, staff leave entitlements, how long the owner stays, whether the vehicles are included. Five minutes now. Three invoiced emails later.

Call the owner, walk them through it in plain English, and send it the same day. Their accountant and their kids will read it, so it has to explain itself without you. One line on why the structure is good for them, in their words: paid in full, over time, out of the business they built. Some clauses, usually confidentiality and exclusivity, are often binding. Your lawyer reads it once, on a fixed fee.

At four times the business carries the letter with room for a bad year. At five times on the same terms it doesn't. When the owner wants more, change the shape, a longer term or some rollover, never the 1.5.

Where people lose money.

- Writing a range. The seller hears the top of it.
- Saying "vendor finance". It sounds like a loan the owner is giving a stranger, and the conversation goes cold.
- Leaving the economics for the contract. Every open item becomes billable hours.
- Waiting for the perfect letter.

A friend of mine in the US, Elliot, sent about 40 offers. Three came back signed at 100% seller finance, some on "pension plan" terms, like $350,000 a year for life on $500,000 of net operating income. Forty letters. Three signed. People who get deals send letters.

Or you keep doing what you're doing. One business, waiting for enough cash to buy the next, sending vague letters and leaving half the deal for the lawyers.

Or you book a call with me. We work out your strategy, sketch your first structure, and decide whether the Academy or 1:1 fits. Joining comes with the NBIO template I use. A client was quoted about $7,000 for the same document. It's for people who'll put in 40-plus hours a week and send an offer before they feel ready. Not for anyone after a four-hour work week.

If you're someone who's built one business and knows it isn't the last, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you're either thirty days older with a blank page, or thirty days older with a signed letter the business can pay for.

## EP 23 · 5 negotiation mistakes that add $400K to a business price

Two buyers get the same message: another buyer has offered $2 million.

James has one deal. He goes up $400,000. Chad has eleven other conversations. He replies, "No worries, good luck." Often the seller comes back to Chad at $1.6 million.

The best negotiation tactic in buying a business is being genuinely able to walk away. Here are the five mistakes that make it impossible.

A business is paid for over seven years or more. One phone call where you cave sets the payments for all of them. If you already run a business you know this from the other side. The customer who needed you more than you needed them always paid more. This is for people who'd rather lose a deal than overpay for one.

Walking away is a position, not a mood. You can't fake it. The second-best tactic is a time limit.

If you've caved in a negotiation before, it has nothing to do with being soft or bad at deals. It has everything to do with having one option. Anyone with one option negotiates badly, and the seller hears it in your voice.

I help owners build the pipeline and hold the line. Book a call below. Now the five, worst first.

**Mistake 1. Negotiating with one deal.**

If this is the only seller you're talking to, you've lost before you start. Run ten or more conversations at once. Most die at the documents stage, which is the point.

Jy Kingston, one of my students, came from IT with no finance background. Twelve deals active, closing his first. Twelve is what lets you say no to one. My own first deal came after about a thousand cold calls and four deals that fell over. With one deal at a time I'd have quit after the first.

Rule of thumb: if you can't name three other sellers you'd happily buy instead, you're not ready to negotiate this one. Go make calls.

**Mistake 2. No written walk-away line.**

Write it before the call. Mine: debt payments covered at least 1.5 times. Statements match tax returns. Seller commits to a transition. No single customer big enough to sink the business. None of those is a feeling. Decide at your desk, not on the phone with your heart rate up. Keep it on a card next to the phone. If a deal breaks one line, you're out, and you can be polite about it.

**Mistake 3. Matching the "other buyer".**

Most of the time it isn't true. And the market has moved. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. They don't want to wait sixteen weeks for a bank. They want out and looked after. The highest number on paper often loses to the offer that feels certain.

Even if the other buyer is real, your maths didn't change. So you say: "No worries, good luck with them. My offer stands until Friday."

To test the claim without calling it a lie, ask a normal buyer's question. "Are they paying cash at settlement or over time? Is their finance approved?" A real buyer has answers. A made-up one goes quiet.

Often they come back. Sometimes in a few days. Sometimes in six months, after the other buyer's bank said no. Either way you didn't pay $400,000 to find out.

[JOSH: a time you walked away and the seller came back, if you have one]

**Mistake 4. Moving price instead of terms.**

When a seller says the price is too low, that's rarely the whole story. Ask: "Price aside, is selling to us the right move for you? What would need to be true?"

Usually it's the staff, whether they'll actually get paid, or what they'll do on Monday. You solve those with terms: a transition role, staff commitments, monthly reporting so they can see the business is healthy. None of it costs you cover. And let them keep the work ute. Spend leverage where the money is.

**Mistake 5. Deadlines you don't keep.**

"This offer is open for 24 hours." It's respect for both people's time. A seller who's sat on your letter for three weeks either wants it or doesn't. But a deadline you extend twice teaches the seller your words are soft. If they let it lapse, let it lapse. You can always send a fresh letter in three months, on the same numbers.

Watch for the reverse too. "I need an answer today" from a seller is artificial urgency. Your answer is the same: your offer, your timeline, your line.

*Illustrative.* At 1.2 one bad year and you can't pay. Matching would cost $400,000 of price to buy a business you can't safely carry. And the extra $66,000 a year comes out of the cash you'd use to grow it, every year for seven years.

Where people lose money.

- Chasing after a no. Check in every six to eight weeks, then quarterly. Health scares, bad years and kids who don't want the business change sellers' minds. The polite buyer who kept in touch gets the call.
- Confusing liked with respected. You can be warm and still say no.
- Spending your week negotiating when you have fewer than ten conversations. That week is for calls.
- Negotiating against yourself. Dropping your own terms before the seller has asked for anything. State the offer, then wait for the counter.

You can do this the hard way: one deal, no written line, and a seller who senses it. You overpay, or you freeze and lose it, and start again from zero. Or you take the path where the pipeline is built properly, the line is written before the call, and someone in your corner tells you to walk when you don't want to hear it.

On a call with me we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. In the Academy, when a seller bluffs you post it and get a straight answer that afternoon. It's for people who'll put in 40-plus hours a week and want a roll-up exit, a legacy business or flips. Not for the four-hour-work-week crowd.

If you're someone who wants more than the one business you're stuck inside, and who'll make the calls to have somewhere to walk to, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you're either still holding your breath over one seller's reply, or you've got a pipeline and two sentences you can say without flinching.

## EP 24 · Every layer of due diligence, from $500K "adjusted" to $400K real

How come investors who'd never buy a share without reading the annual report will buy a whole business on the seller's adjusted number?

Illustrative. "Adjusted" profit, $500,000. Real profit, $400,000. At four times, that's $400,000 paid for nothing.

Due diligence is the last point where you can still change the price. Here are the five layers between the seller's number and the real one.

You priced the business at a multiple of profit. Every dollar you prove or disprove now moves the price by three to five. This is for people with capital who want it in a business whose profit is proven. It isn't for anyone who'd rather skip the boring work and trust the seller.

Due diligence is about the quality of earnings: is the profit real, will it stay after the owner leaves, and how much cash does it take to run.

If you've been burnt before, it has nothing to do with being careless. It has everything to do with who wrote the number. The adjusted figure is written by the one person who benefits from it being high.

Most buyers think diligence is about finding reasons to say yes. That's not how it works. It's where you find out what you're buying, and reprice it.

I help investors and owners run this and take the findings back to the seller. Book a call below. Now the layers.

**Level 1. Reported profit.**

The tax returns from episode 21. This is the floor: the profit the business has proven to the government. Everything above it has to earn its place. If the seller's pitch starts above this line, your diligence starts here.

**Level 2. Adjusted profit.**

Add-backs are the owner's adjustments to show what a new owner would earn. Some are fair: a personal car through the books, a genuine one-off legal bill. Test every one.

The "one-off" legal bill that shows up every year isn't one-off. The owner adding back his own salary forgets someone has to do his job, and that person costs money. Rule: an add-back survives only if a stranger could justify it with paperwork. Ask for the invoice, the payslip, the contract. No paper, no add-back. Each $10,000 that fails is $40,000 off a 4× price.

**Level 3. Durable profit.**

Profit that stays after you settle. Two checks.

Concentration. Revenue by customer. One or two customers over about 30% is a red flag. One over half, you walk or price it heavily. Check their contracts can be assigned to you. And look at the share across all three years. A customer that's gone from 15% to 35% is a concentration problem in the making.

Source. Where does each dollar come from: repeat customers, referrals, a contract, or the owner's golf mates? If the honest answer is "the owner's relationships", that revenue might leave with him.

[JOSH: one diligence finding from your own deals that changed the price or structure]

**Level 4. Cash profit.**

Working capital: the cash tied up in stock and unpaid invoices that pays wages before customers pay. If the seller strips the cash before settlement and you put it back, that's a deposit by another name. Agree the level in the contract and line up the working capital facility before close. Watch debtors too. If customers have been taking longer to pay each year, profit can look fine while cash runs dry.

**Level 5. People.**

The financials tell you what happened. The staff tell you what's about to. Interview key people before you settle, with the seller's agreement. Ask what they'd change, who customers actually call, who's thinking of leaving.

If the business runs on two or three people, put retention bonuses in the deal, say at 90 days and one year. Often it isn't the owner the business runs on. It's the office manager of fifteen years. The worst finding is the one you get on day three, when your best technician resigns.

**Who does what.** Your accountant runs quality of earnings. Your lawyer covers contracts, lease, licences, employment and disputes. The operator on your board walks the site. You run the timeline, 90 days at the outside.

Same multiple you agreed in the letter, new input. That's why diligence was a condition. You're not haggling when you go back. You're applying the formula the seller already accepted to a corrected number, and showing the paperwork for every line.

Where people lose money.

- Accepting the adjusted number. The table above.
- Ambushing the seller. Sit down with the log: here's what we found, here's what it does to profit, here's what that does to price. Most owners respect working, not haggling.
- Only using price. A concentration risk can be fixed by paying part of the price only if that customer is still there in a year. A staff risk, by the seller staying longer.
- Killing a good deal at the first finding. Issues under about 10% of profit, accept or adjust. Statements that don't match returns, or a seller who won't transition, you walk.

Or you keep doing what you're doing. Capital sitting in low-yield assets. Deals you look at without knowing which add-backs to believe. Either accepting them all, or panicking and killing a deal that only needed a price change.

Or you run diligence like it's your money, because it is. On a call with me we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy, with the diligence checklist and daily deal discussion, or 1:1 advisory fits. You don't need to be a finance person. Jy came from IT. You ask the right questions and make your team answer them. It's for people who want their capital in real businesses and will put the hours in. Not for passive hope.

If you're someone who reads the fine print before you commit capital, you've already decided. Thirty days from now your capital is either sitting where it is, or you have a log and a way to prove profit before you pay a multiple on it.

## EP 25 · Share sale vs asset sale: from signed LOI to keys in 4 weeks

This one isn't for anyone buying a business one day. It's for the four weeks between a signed letter and the keys.

Run them right and the business pays its own legal bill. Illustrative: a $300,000 working capital line, $60,000 of fees paid from it at settlement, $240,000 of runway on your first morning.

Run them wrong and the deal dies in a lawyer's inbox.

Good deals rarely die at contracts over a big problem. They die because nobody's driving. The bill doubles, the seller gets nervous at month three, and you're back at the start of the pipeline. This is for people who'll run the last stage like a project, not wait on it.

Two decisions and one plan. Which contract. How the paid-over-time paperwork protects you. Then four weeks to settlement.

If a deal has stalled at contracts before, it has nothing to do with not being a legal person. It has everything to do with nobody owning the timeline. Lawyers are trained to find risk, not to finish. You finish.

I help owners get contracts done on time and on budget, on the terms they agreed in the letter. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A. Share sale (SPA).**

A share purchase agreement means you buy the company itself. Staff, contracts, lease and supplier accounts carry on automatically. Simpler on day one.

But you inherit its history, known and unknown. That's why share deals need more diligence and stronger promises from the seller in the contract: warranties and indemnities.

**Option B. Asset sale (APA).**

An asset purchase agreement means you buy the parts you want, equipment, customer list, name, and leave the old liabilities with the seller's company. The cost is admin: contracts, leases and licences re-done or assigned to your company, and some customers told.

**Self-test and verdict.**

Three questions. Did diligence come back clean? Are there contracts or licences that can't be easily assigned? Does the seller have a strong tax reason to sell shares?

If diligence found gaps, or the history is messy, lean asset. If everything is clean and the key contracts can't move, a share sale can be the simpler path.

For no-money-down deals, an asset sale often gives you more control and less risk. A share sale is fine with clean diligence and proper warranties. On bigger deals, ask about warranty and indemnity insurance. Tax changes by country and deal, so your lawyer and accountant make the call. And raise it at the letter stage, not week three of contracts.

**Four weeks, run like a project.**

Before most buyers know how this stage works, they hand over the contract and wait. They approve another round of drafting, watch the bill climb and the seller go quiet. That's fee-jacking.

Once you know a contract without a deadline expands to fill the time, you stop waiting. Fixed scope before they start. A list of the six or seven issues that matter, in order of money, with an instruction not to reopen what the letter settled. A weekly call. A deadline in writing. Good firms will defer fees to closing, so they want to get there too. That's the point of putting 80% of the deal in the letter. Less to argue about means fewer hours.

[JOSH: how long your first deal took from signed LOI to settlement]

**The paperwork that protects you.**

For the part paid over time, five checks. The seller's note sits behind any bank debt. You can repay early without penalty. Liability sits in the buying company, not your house. A default on one agreement doesn't automatically trigger the others. The seller gets regular reporting.

Personal guarantees: larger deals, typically over about $5 million, often don't need one, because the business's assets are the bank's security. Typically. Every lender and deal is different.

Then minority rules, set from day one. Your board holds founders' equity, two to ten percent each. No veto or voting control for minorities, and a drag-along, so when the majority sells, the minority comes on the same terms. Without it, one person with 3% can hold up an exit years from now.

**Settlement day.**

In order. Sign the purchase and paid-over-time documents. Bank money moves. Keys, passwords, system access. Insurance live the same day the seller's stops. Working capital line drawn, fees paid from it.

Before you sign, tick off every condition in your letter: finance, lease, licences, key staff agreements, and the seller's transition in writing, anywhere from six months to three years.

And plan the first week before you settle. Who tells the staff, and when. Which customers get a call from the seller introducing you. Which accounts move to your company. Settlement day is your team's first impression of you as the owner.

A good friend of mine, Cam, closed two deals in 23 days. 100% vendor finance, interest-only for ten years, balloons in years three and ten, and $500,000 of working capital from the bank. Fast settlements need a tight letter and someone driving the lawyers.

Working capital lines are often around 10% of revenue. Drawn on settlement day, the business funds its own close.

Where people lose money.

- No deadline. Hours grow to fill the time, and the seller's nerve doesn't.
- Switching share to asset sale in week three. It reopens the deal.
- No drag-along. The cheapest clause now, the most expensive to fix at exit.
- A seller who wants to hand over the keys and vanish next morning. That's a finding you should have had weeks ago.

There are two ways through this stage. Alone, the lawyers set the pace, the bill doubles and the seller backs out at month three. Or with someone who's done it holding the timeline with you, so four weeks means four weeks.

On a call with me we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. In the Academy you ask how others handled the issues list before your lawyer bills you for the answer. It's for people who'll put in 40-plus hours a week and want a big outcome. Not for lifestyle-business hunters.

If you're someone who's tired of "one day" and wants to hold the keys, not just the letter, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you're either waiting on a draft, or four weeks into a plan with a settlement date on the calendar.

# Act 6 · Grow what you bought

## EP 26 · 5 mistakes that sink a $1M vendor-finance deal in the first 100 days

Two new owners buy the same $2 million business on the same seller note.

James restructures in week two. By month three his best supervisor has gone, a big customer has followed, and the first seller payment is due on profit that no longer exists.

Chad changes nothing big for a month. He makes every payment.

Same business. Same debt. The difference is five mistakes. Here they are, ranked, with what each one costs.

In a 3C deal the seller and the bank are paid from the business. Your whole structure rests on one ratio: free cash flow covering debt payments at least 1.5 times. That's DSCR, debt service cover. Break the cash flow in the first 100 days and you break the structure, before you've pulled a single growth lever.

Keep one number in your head. A business doing $250,000 profit can carry about $166,000 a year to the seller. That's the speed run from episode 11, and it leaves less room than it sounds.

This is for people who've bought, or are about to, and plan to own for years. If you want to buy a job and coast, skip this act.

The first 100 days have one job: keep the machine you paid for running long enough to understand it.

Most new owners think day one is where you make your mark. They think the new boss has to look decisive. They think change is what they bought. That's not how it works. You bought cash flow that already exists. Your first job is to not interrupt it.

And if you came out of a corporate job, the urge to change everything isn't your fault. It has nothing to do with your judgement or your ambition. It has everything to do with years of being rewarded for visible change. In a business paying off its own purchase price, visible change is the risk.

I help owners plan this before settlement, so the structure and the first 100 days fit together. Book a call below. Now the five.

**Restructuring before you measure.**

Week two, new org chart. Week three, new software. Every change you make before you have a baseline is a guess with borrowed money.

The rule: thirty days of real numbers before your first big change. Revenue by source. Gross margin, meaning what's left after the direct cost of the work. How full the team's week is. Cash in the bank every Friday.

Here's why it costs so much. Most changes are cheap to make and expensive to undo. New scheduling software takes a week to install and three months to bed in, and while it beds in the crew bills fewer hours. On a $2 million business at a 40% gross margin, a 5% dip in billable work is $40,000 of profit you didn't plan for.

**Going silent with the staff.**

You paid for the people. They walk faster than customers do. In silence, staff fill the gap with the worst story.

Day one, stand in front of them and say what isn't changing. Pay, roles, how the work gets done. Be specific. Your key people should already be on employment agreements as a condition of closing, not an afterthought. Lose one supervisor and you can lose the customers who trust them.

**Not visiting the top customers with the seller.**

Pull your top 20 customers and the share of revenue each one is. Then visit or call them with the seller beside you, introducing you as the person who looks after them now.

If one or two customers are over 30% of revenue, that's a red flag in diligence. If you bought one anyway, those customers get the first visits and a call from you every month.

**Letting the seller disappear.**

Negotiate a written transition, six months to three years. A seller being paid over time wants you to succeed, because their money depends on it. Use that.

Book a weekly handover. Ask for what isn't written anywhere. Who to call when the van breaks down. Which customer pays late but always pays. Then hand each relationship to a named person in the business, not just to you. Put the handover in the sale agreement, with hours and a schedule, so it's a term and not a favour.

**Buying growth before you find the ceiling.**

This is the expensive one, because it feels like progress.

Ask one question. If sales doubled next month, what breaks first? The one supervisor? Scheduling? Quoting? That answer is your first hire or your first system. It is never your first marketing campaign. Buying customers you can't serve costs you the customers, the staff and the reviews at once. A capacity fix pays twice. It protects the revenue you bought, and it's what lets every other lever in this act work.

The order: days 1 to 30, listen. Days 31 to 60, measure and find the ceiling. Days 61 to 100, make one fix.

My first deal, a physio clinic, came with a nine-month payment holiday. That wasn't luck. It was a term in the deal, there to buy time to learn the business, not rebuild it. **[JOSH: one line on what you changed, and didn't, in your first 100 days at the clinic]** I pulled $150,000 out in year one. That's not typical. Look at the shape: no deposit, a holiday before the first payment, and a business that kept running while the new owner learnt it.

One supervisor and one customer. You've gone from comfortable to underwater without changing a single price.

Where people lose money.

- Spending the working capital line on a rebrand instead of runway. That line is drawn at settlement for fees, integration and time.
- Not matching the payment holiday to the integration plan. Negotiate the holiday you need for the plan you've written.
- Raising prices on every customer in month one, before they know you. Price is next episode, and timing is half of it.

You can work this out alone, with a restructure in week two and the seller payments landing before the business has settled. Or you write the 100-day plan before settlement with someone who's already made the mistakes.

That's what the call is for. We work out your strategy and your structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits you.

If you're someone who'd rather listen for a month than guess with borrowed money, and you're buying to own for years, you've already made the decision.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with a baseline sheet, a seller handover in the calendar and a business still running the way you bought it.
