## EP 27 · A 10% price rise vs 10% more customers ($450K vs $330K profit)

Most professional buyers look at the price list before they look at marketing.

Here's why. On the same $2 million business, 10% more customers adds about $80,000 of profit. A 10% price rise adds about $200,000.

Same revenue. More than double the profit. I'll show you the maths, the test for whether you're cheap, and the order to do it in.

Profit sets the price of a business, not revenue. Every extra dollar of profit is worth three to five dollars when you sell, and it makes every seller payment safer from the first month. At four times, $200,000 of extra profit is $800,000 of extra value.

This is for owners and buyers who want more out of capital already in the business. It isn't for anyone who wants to be the cheapest in town. That's a race you win by going broke slowly.

Price is the biggest profit lever in a boring business, because a price rise carries no extra delivery cost.

Everything you were told points the other way. You were told the cheapest quote wins. You were told a business grows by adding customers. You were told a price rise loses the customers you've got. And that's exactly why so many boring businesses sit on a price list from years ago while costs rise every year.

If you own one of these businesses, being cheap isn't your fault. It has nothing to do with your skill or your work ethic. It has everything to do with nobody putting the margin maths in front of you.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down and then do exactly this. If you want the numbers run on your deal, book a call below. Now the two options.

**Option A. Win 10% more customers.**

Revenue goes from $2 million to $2.2 million. But every new job carries its delivery cost. At a 40% gross margin, $200,000 of new revenue leaves $80,000.

And it isn't free. You need the capacity to deliver it, and you pay to win those customers. Profit lands around $330,000 before acquisition costs.

Now add them. Say the average job is $5,000, so you need forty new customers, and each one costs $2,000 to win. That's another $80,000. In year one, the volume play nets close to nothing.

**Option B. Raise prices 10%.**

Same $2.2 million of revenue. Same jobs, same crews, same materials. So the extra $200,000 lands almost entirely as profit. You go from $250,000 to about $450,000.

And the part that matters for a sceptic. At a 40% gross margin, a 10% price rise still breaks even if one in five jobs walks. Most businesses lose far fewer than that.

**The self-test.**

Three checks for whether you're cheap.

One. When was the last price rise? If it's more than a year ago and your costs have moved, you're almost certainly undercharging. Line the price history up against wages, materials and insurance. If costs are up 20% since the last rise, a 20% rise only gets you back to where you were.

Two. What's your quote win rate? Count last quarter's quotes and how many you won. If you win almost every quote, you're not great at sales. You're cheap. **[JOSH: confirm]**

Three. Is there a budget option that eats your crew's time? Kill it or price it properly. One offer, one price.

Price first, then volume.

Start with new customers. New quotes, new contracts. Nobody has a reference point. Then move existing customers with at least a month's notice, a short letter, the new rate and no apology. Put the seller beside you on the calls to your biggest accounts, because those customers trust the seller more than you yet.

Move in jumps big enough to read. A tiny change disappears in the noise. Pick a step that shows up in the numbers, run it on new quotes for a few weeks, and watch your win rate. You don't double prices overnight in a trades business. **[JOSH: confirm]**

If you have contracts, read them for a review clause. Plenty of service contracts allow an annual increase that nobody has used in years. That's a price rise the customer has already signed.

And never discount to win. If a customer pushes back, change the terms, not the price. Deposit up front, staged payments, a longer contract.

The ceiling is your team and your customers' satisfaction. Push past it and you'll know, because good customers start leaving for the right reasons.

For the capital deployer, here's the opportunity. A business that hasn't moved its prices in years is the cheapest value you'll ever buy. You pay a multiple of today's profit. The price rise is profit the seller left on the table.

[JOSH: one line on whether you repriced at the physio clinic after settlement, and what happened]

Same top line, and the price rise is worth almost half a million dollars more at exit than the volume play, before you've paid to win a single customer.

Where people lose money.

- One letter to every customer in month two, before they know you. The biggest account walks, and so does a fifth of your cover.
- Tweaking prices by 2% a year. Too small to read, too small to cover rising costs.
- Discounting to close. Every dollar off comes straight out of profit, and out of the multiple.

Or you can keep doing what most owners do. Keep the price list from five years ago. Keep paying the seller out of thin margins. Keep calling rising costs a tough market.

On the call, we put the price move into your deal model, so the seller payment and the price rise are designed together. Then we work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather fix one number than hire five people, and you can read a margin and act on it, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with a new price on every new quote and a win rate you can read.

## EP 28 · Every level from referrals to buying customers at $4,000 each

Every owner of a boring business gets the same growth advice: post more, hire an agency, wait for referrals.

When it doesn't work, you're out the money and the months. And referrals arrive when they feel like it, not when the seller payment is due.

How about something different. You buy customers, on purpose, at a price you worked out before you spent a cent. Here's every level from word of mouth to that.

A business that grows on referrals is valued on what it did. A business that can show cost per customer, lifetime value and revenue by source is valued on what it will do. That's a better story in front of a bank and a better multiple in front of a buyer.

This isn't for anyone hoping a logo refresh will do the job, or anyone who wants to spend on ads before the business can deliver.

A real business can turn money into customers at a known return. Five levels get you there. Each one adds one constraint.

If your business has stalled on referrals, that's not your fault. It has nothing to do with your service or your reputation. It has everything to do with how most boring businesses were built: one owner, one phone, and word of mouth.

I help owners buy boring businesses with little to no money down and build this on top. Book a call below. Now the levels.

**Level 1. Referrals only.**

Good customers send friends. It's a gift, not a system. You can't choose to grow in a given month, and you can't plan seller payments around a gift.

When I look at a deal, I ask one question: can revenue be bought here, with outbound or ads? If yes, there's upside the seller isn't pricing in. And it cuts both ways at the exit. A referral-only business sells for less, because the buyer can't see where next year's customers will come from.

**Level 2. The owner sells.**

Growth now equals the owner's hours. Quotes, follow-ups, the big accounts. It works, and it's a ceiling. If the owner can quote fifteen jobs a week, the business tops out at fifteen quotes a week, whatever the market wants. It's also key-person risk, and a buyer will discount it. That's episode 29.

**Level 3. Know what a customer is worth.**

Lifetime value is the gross profit a typical customer brings over the whole time they stay. Gross profit, not revenue, because revenue doesn't pay for marketing. If you can't calculate it yet, take your last twenty customers and what they've paid you so far. A rough number beats none.

A common rule: lifetime value should be at least three times what it costs to win the customer. Below that, you're buying revenue at a loss once overheads land.

Then work backwards to a budget per quote. In the example on screen, that's up to $1,000 to get each quote in front of a decision-maker. Now you can judge a channel before you spend.

And from today, tag every new customer with its source. Referral, outbound, ads, website. Blended numbers hide which channel actually pays.

**Level 4. One channel with an input you can count.**

I know what you're thinking. My industry runs on relationships. Outbound doesn't work here.

I made about 1,000 cold calls before my first deal. Saul, a member of the Academy, found an electrical and air-con business, 25 staff, about $3.8 million in revenue, with a cold call straight to the owner. The question that finds a seller is the same muscle as "who looks after your cleaning at the moment?"

For a business-to-business service, outbound is often the first machine. A list, a script, a number of dials a day. For a consumer business, it's usually paid ads. Either way, the input is counted.

Here's how that reads, illustratively. One caller on $70,000 makes 40 dials a day. Two hundred dials a week gets twenty conversations, four quotes and one new contract. Fifty-two contracts a year at about $1,350 each, against a $4,000 ceiling. That's a channel worth feeding.

Before you scale it, check the offer sells to strangers, not just to friends of existing customers. If cold leads don't buy, fix the offer first.

**Level 5. The volume knob.**

Once a channel pays back, you feed it. Turn it up when you want growth. Turn it down to build systems. Growth stops being something that happens to you.

Two limits. Delivery first: run the "double the work" test from episode 26 before every push. And keep the 1.5× cover in front of you every month, because your marketing is paid from the same cash flow that pays the seller.

The first marketing money often comes from the deal itself. The working capital line is drawn at settlement. Cam, a good friend of mine, did two deals in 23 days with 100% vendor finance and got $500,000 of working capital from the bank.

It's borrowed money. Test small, set the test size before you start, and never fund marketing with unsecured debt.

A channel that gets you in front of a decision-maker for under $1,000 a quote is worth running. Above that, it's not, however good the agency's report looks.

Where people lose money.

- Hiring an agency in month two and spending the working capital on clicks the crew can't deliver.
- Calculating lifetime value on revenue, not gross profit, and overpaying for every customer.
- Judging a channel on lead count. A cheap channel full of tyre-kickers can lose money while it looks busiest.

You can build this alone, by trial and error with the money that's meant to pay the seller. Or you build the acquisition plan into the deal model before you close.

On the call we work out your strategy and structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone whose business has hit a ceiling it shouldn't have, and you'd rather know your cost per customer than guess it, you already know what to do.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either still waiting on referrals, or thirty days older with your lifetime value worked out, every customer tagged and one channel running every week.

## EP 29 · Why $380K profit sells for more than $500K

There are two types of business owners: the ones who own a business, and the ones who own a job with a business around it.

They can make the same profit. They sell for very different prices.

Here's the contrarian part. Cutting your profit by $120,000 can add $400,000 to what the business is worth.

Small businesses trade at roughly three to five times profit. Where you land in that range has a lot to do with one question: does the business need its owner?

If the owner wins the customers and keeps them happy, a buyer sees key-person risk. If the owner leaves, so do the customers, and the buyer prices that in.

This is for anyone who wants to own more than one business, or sell one for a proper price. It's not for people who want to be the hero who does everything.

Owner-independence is valuation. Three boxes, three named people, and proof that it runs without you.

If you're the owner who's still the business, that's not a character flaw. It has nothing to do with being a control freak or not trusting your people. It has everything to do with how small businesses grow. The founder does every job first, and nobody ever hands them on.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down and set them up to run without the owner. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Why people believe the opposite.**

Owners are paid on profit, so every salary feels like money out of their pocket. "Why pay a manager $120,000 to do what I do for free?"

Because you're not free. Your time is the cost the P&L doesn't show, and the buyer sees it straight away.

Think of two rental properties with the same rent. One has a tenant on a five-year lease. The other only earns if you live in it and run it as a guesthouse. Same income. Nobody pays the same for both.

**The market has moved.**

A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. They want out, and they want to be looked after. And most of their businesses run through them. Twenty years of being the first call for every customer, every quote, every problem.

That's often why they're selling. Saul, an Academy member, is in early due diligence on an electrical and air-con business: 25 staff, about $3.8 million in revenue, and an owner retiring in two years. That's the pattern. So the buyers who know how to take the owner out of these businesses are the ones who capture the gap between three times and five times. Once you've bought, the owner in the middle is you.

**Three boxes, three names.**

Split the business into three boxes.

Win the work: everything that brings customers in. Quoting, sales, the big accounts.

Do the work: everything that delivers and keeps customers. Crews, clinicians, account managers.

Protect the business: everything that keeps the other two out of trouble. Finance, payroll, compliance, HR.

Write a name under each. Every box with the owner's name, or the seller's, is key-person risk. Fix one box at a time. Low revenue is a Win-the-work problem. Churn is a Do-the-work problem. A missed invoice or a licence that nearly lapsed is a Protect-the-business problem.

In a 3C deal, your board and external team already cover a lot of Protect the business. Your job is a named person in the business for each box, not just on a board.

**Build it, then prove it.**

Set the scorecard before you hire for a box. A manager who writes their own scorecard writes one they can hit.

Pay by box. Win the work and Do the work earn money, so tie part of their pay to results. Protect the business is a cost centre, so it's salaried. Buyers like a pay structure they can see tied to results.

Write the SOPs. Every repeatable job written down or screen-recorded, so a new hire does it well in week one. Record one job a week and in a year you have fifty jobs that don't need you.

Watch labour cost per revenue dollar. As systems make jobs easier, it should fall as you grow. If it's rising, you're adding people, not systems. Work it out for the last twelve months now, so you have a starting point a buyer can compare against.

Use the seller transition. The seller's job isn't just to answer questions. It's to hand their relationships to named people, so the next buyer doesn't depend on you either.

Then prove it. The strongest evidence you can hand a buyer is a stretch where the owner was away and the numbers held. Two weeks is a start. A month is better. Write down what broke and fix that next.

I run a healthcare group full time. I built my board from LinkedIn and cold outreach, and I keep 73% of my company. **[JOSH: one line on who runs the clinics day to day, and the longest you've been away with the numbers holding]**

Less profit, more value. The $120,000 salary bought $400,000 of exit value, and the owner's week back.

Where people lose money.

- Hiring a general manager with no scorecard, then paying them for a year to define their own job.
- Keeping every big customer relationship personal. It feels like loyalty. To a buyer it's a discount.
- Never taking the test month. Claiming the business runs without you is worth nothing next to the numbers that prove it.

Or you keep doing what you're doing. Keep being the first call. Keep holding every relationship yourself. Keep telling yourself you'll step back next year, while you can't buy business number two because you can't leave business number one.

On the call we plan the handover and the three boxes into the deal before you close, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who's tired of being the first call, and you'd rather build something a buyer wants than a job nobody can take off you, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either still the name in every box, or thirty days older with a name in each one, a scorecard written and four jobs recorded.

## EP 30 · The 9-number Monday sheet buyers read before your P&L (copy it)

How come people who've made real money elsewhere find it so hard to tell a good small business from a bad one?

They can read a statement. They have a good accountant. And they still get handed a box of paperwork and a "trust me".

It has nothing to do with intelligence. Nobody tracks the right few numbers. Here's the one-page sheet that fixes it, line by line, in the order a buyer reads it.

If you own a business, these nine tell you every Monday whether it's safe, growing and sellable. If you're buying, they're the questions to ask a seller, and what they can't answer tells you something too.

And at the exit, a buyer's diligence starts here. Clean, consistent numbers mean shorter diligence and a multiple that holds.

This isn't for people who'd rather check the bank balance and hope.

Few numbers, every week, same order. Three for survival, four for growth, two for the sale.

Before an owner tracks these, they run on the bank balance and a feeling. A bad month shows up three months late, in the accountant's report. After, the bad month shows up on a Monday, while there's still time to fix it. Same business. Different owner. **[JOSH: one line on what you tracked weekly before and after your first deal]**

If you've been running on feel, that isn't your fault. It has nothing to do with being bad at maths. It has everything to do with nobody handing you a short list and a habit.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down and run them on numbers like these. Book a call below. Now the sheet.

**Survive.**

Line one. **Cash against your floor.** A simple floor is two months of fixed costs plus the next seller payment. **[JOSH: confirm]** Below it, nothing else on the sheet matters until you're back above it.

Line two. **Debt service cover.** Free cash flow over debt payments. The 3C rule is 1.5× minimum. Track it monthly, look at it weekly, and know your headroom before the bank asks.

Line three. **Customer concentration.** Share of revenue from your biggest customer and your top five. Over 30% in one or two customers is a red flag in diligence. Don't own a business you'd walk away from buying.

**Grow.**

Line four. **Revenue by source.** Referral, outbound, ads, repeat. Blended numbers hide which channel pays.

Line five. **Cost to win a customer.** Total sales and marketing spend divided by new customers. Include everything: ads, the salesperson's wage, the free first visit. Leave costs out and the number lies to you.

Line six. **Lifetime value to that cost.** From episode 28: at least three to one.

Line seven. **Gross margin.** What's left of each dollar after the direct cost of the work. From episode 27: it's where price shows up.

Read four and five together. A channel can bring in a small share of your leads and a large share of your revenue. Look at lead counts alone and you'll cut the one that pays the bills.

**Sell.**

Line eight. **Labour cost per revenue dollar.** From episode 29. It should fall as systems make jobs easier.

Line nine. **Churn, or repeat rate.** How many customers you lost, or how many came back. Recurring, sticky revenue is what lenders and buyers pay for.

**The Monday habit.**

At the bottom of the sheet, one line: what you expect next week, in numbers. Next Monday, compare. Predicting and checking is how you learn the business faster than anyone who just reads reports. When the guess is wrong, the gap tells you which line to look at first.

Twenty minutes, same time every week. Update the nine. Anything red gets one question: why, and who owns the fix? Then write next week's guess. If you have a general manager, they run it and you listen. The business reports to you, not through you. **[JOSH: confirm]**

**What the buyer sees.**

In Act 5 we asked sellers for three years of financials, matching tax returns and revenue by year. These nine are the same lens, pointed at a target. Ask the owner for as many as they have.

Now flip it. Two owners each say their business makes $500,000. One hands over a box of statements. The other hands over two years of these nine, every week, with tax returns that match. The second gets the cleaner offer and the faster diligence. And speed matters in money. Every month diligence drags is another month for a buyer to find something to renegotiate.

On my first deal I pulled $150,000 out in year one while the seller was paid over time from the same business. When the seller is paid from the business, lines one and two are the ones that let you sleep.

One sheet, nine lines, two to watch. That's a business you can run in twenty minutes, and a story a buyer can check.

Where people lose money.

- Calculating cover only when the bank asks. By then the headroom's gone.
- Starting the sheet in January and stopping in March. Two years of weekly numbers is the asset. Ten weeks isn't.
- Buying on a box of statements and a handshake, when the nine questions would have shown the gap in an afternoon.

That's the end of Grow. You've kept the business, raised the price, built a customer machine, taken your name out of the three boxes, and put it on nine numbers.

You can do this alone: a spreadsheet you abandon, a cover ratio you never check, and a buyer who marks your price down because the numbers don't line up. Or you set up the numbers before you close, and they follow you all the way to the exit.

That's the line. Before this act, a business you bought was something you hoped would work. Now you know how to keep it, grow it and prove it. Crossing that line takes a decision, not certainty.

On the call we work out your strategy, your structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone with capital who wants to see it work every Monday, and you'd rather vet a deal on nine numbers than a handshake, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with four Mondays of numbers on one sheet and your first forecast checked.

# Act 7 · Scale and exit

## EP 31 · Buy at 4×, sell at 8×: every multiple from 3× to 10× explained

There are only two types of people who put money into businesses. The ones who get paid the day they buy. And the ones who get paid the day they sell.

The second group buys small businesses at around four times profit and sells them together at eight, ten, sometimes fifteen. Same profit. Double the price.

Here's every rung on that ladder, what moves you up one, and why I never model the top.

A multiple is the price divided by one year of profit. On two million of profit, the gap between four times and eight times is eight million dollars. That's bigger than all the cash flow you'll take out along the way.

This is for owners and investors who want a big outcome. If you want one business to run quietly until you retire, that's a fine life. This episode isn't for you.

You were told the money is in buying cheap. Or in working harder inside the business. Or in having a big pile of capital. None of that is where the big number comes from.

Here's the whole idea in one line. A buyer pays less for risk and more for certainty. A roll-up buys risk cheap and sells certainty dear.

There are four levels. Most owners sit on level one their whole lives and think it's the market price.

And if nobody showed you the higher rungs, that's not your fault. It has nothing to do with how smart you are or how much you've saved. It has everything to do with only ever seeing one business at a time.

I help owners and investors buy the first business with little to no money down, then build the group with the exit written down from day one. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1: the owner's business, ~3×.**

A business making four hundred thousand a year. The owner does the quoting, holds the relationships, and keeps the books for the tax return.

A buyer asks one question: what happens to this profit when the owner walks out? Nobody knows. So they pay three times, maybe four, and carry the risk on the rest.

This is where you buy. You don't fight this multiple. You harvest it. My first deal was a physio clinic at this level, bought at 25 with 100% vendor finance.

**Level 2: runs without you, 4–5×.**

Same four hundred thousand. Now a manager runs it, the numbers tie to the bank every month, and no customer is more than a tenth of revenue.

The risk dropped, so the multiple rose. That's the work from Act 6: price, a customer machine, owner-independence. One rung up on four hundred thousand of profit adds four to eight hundred thousand of value. Without a dollar more profit.

**Level 3: the integrated group, 6–8×.**

Five of those, one industry, one region. One back office. One set of numbers. Two million of profit.

Now a different class of buyer turns up: private equity and larger companies who need size before they'll look. They pay more because the profit is bigger, steadier and spread across sites.

The key word is integrated. Five businesses with five sets of books is a collection. A collection sells at level-one prices. Episode 33 is how you build the group.

**Level 4: the platform, 8×+.**

A group with a proven way to buy and bolt on the next business. The buyer isn't paying for what you own. They're paying for what they can add to it.

This is where ten and fifteen times live. It happens. I never put it in a model. If your deal only works at ten times, it doesn't work.

Here's the rule behind the whole ladder. Every rung is one less risk the buyer has to price. Owner risk goes at level two. Size and concentration risk go at level three. Growth risk goes at level four. You don't climb by working harder. You climb by removing a reason to pay less.

Most owners watching this are on level one with one business. The next move isn't a second business. It's getting the first one to level two, because that's what makes it the anchor of a group.

Four million of value came from nothing but putting them together properly. The rest came from two engines running at once: profit going up, and debt going down, paid by the businesses themselves.

And every deal in it still passes the rule from episode 12 on its own: profit covers the repayments at least one and a half times.

Where people lose money.

- **Paying seven times for business three** "because we'll sell at twelve." You just handed the seller your arbitrage. Buy at three to five. Every deal stands on its own cash flow.
- **Building a collection, not a group.** Five books, five habits. The buyer pays level-one prices. On two million of profit, four times instead of seven is six million gone.
- **The wrong vertical.** If three big players own the market, there's nothing to roll up. You want fragmented and boring, with owners near retirement: healthcare, trades, commercial services. Test: can you list a hundred businesses in your industry and region?
- **Modelling the exit at ten.** The exit is the bonus on a deal that already works.

From here there are two paths. You can do it alone: buy business one, get swallowed running it, and never build the group. Or buy five, never integrate, and sell five small businesses at small multiples.

Or you can do it with someone who's built these structures and already paid for the lessons.

On a call with me we write your vertical and your exit thesis, structure your first deal inside the one-and-a-half-times rule, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. If neither does, I'll tell you.

If you're someone who wants the group, not just the job. Someone who'd rather own cash flow than wait on a yield. Then you've already made the decision.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older either way. Or you're thirty days older with your vertical in one sentence, a hundred businesses on a list, and the gap between four and eight times worked out on a real target.

## EP 32 · Business #1 vs business #2: the $250K business that pays for itself

Owning one good business is a phenomenal thing. Until it stops growing past you.

Then you go from building to maintaining. One income. One valuation, at a small-business multiple.

The second business is cheaper to buy, faster to close, and pays for itself. My good friend Cam did two deals in 23 days. Here's business one against business two, the test to run, and the verdict.

Business two is where a group starts. Last episode showed why a group sells for more than its parts. You can't build one without a second deal.

This is for owners with one business that's running and profitable. If you haven't bought your first yet, keep this one for later. If one business is plenty, skip it.

One line: on deal one you borrow everything. On deal two you spend what deal one built.

Before deal one, you called owners who'd never heard of you. You walked into lenders as a stranger. After deal one, you know what a seller needs to hear, what a lender needs to see, and which lawyer to call.

And if number one felt like a grind, that's not your fault. It has nothing to do with your talent or your stamina. It has everything to do with it being the first time.

Four things change between deal one and deal two. Then a three-gate test, then the verdict.

I help owners buy the first business with little to no money down, then the next one faster. If you want number two planned around number one, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Credibility: borrowed vs owned.**

On deal one you borrowed credibility from a board you paid in founders' equity. On deal two you have your own.

One sentence does it: "We own a clinic in this region, and here's how it's performed since we bought it." That does more on a seller call than any board bio.

The board doesn't go away. It still matters to a lender. But you're no longer asking a retiring owner to trust people they've never met. They can ring the seller you bought from last year and ask how it went. That reference is worth more than anything you can put in a deck.

**The lender: stranger vs customer.**

A lender who funded deal one, or just watched it, now has twelve months of your reporting. They've seen the cover ratio hold. You're not a hypothetical anymore. You're a customer, and a bank is set up to lend to customers.

Cam's second deal came with five hundred thousand of working capital from the bank. Both his deals were 100% vendor finance, interest-only for ten years, with balloon payments in years three and ten.

**The cash: nothing vs a surplus.**

Business one makes three hundred thousand. Repayments are two hundred. That's one and a half times cover. A year of Act 6 work takes profit to three hundred and sixty. Repayments don't move. Now there's a hundred and sixty thousand left after repayments.

Keep sixty as a reserve, in its own account, never touched. Point the other hundred at deal two: fees, a buffer the lender can see, or a small upfront amount if a seller wants one.

Business two pays for itself. It's the speed run: two hundred and fifty thousand of profit, divided by one and a half, is about a hundred and sixty-six thousand a year to the seller. Ten years, one point six six million. A strong offer, funded by the business you're buying.

**Your job: operator vs owner of operators.**

On business one you were the operator. On business two you own two operators. You stop doing the work and start checking it.

That's why number two goes in the same industry and region. Then it isn't a distraction. It's the same thing done twice, sharing a back office later.

[JOSH: one line on how your second acquisition differed from your first.]

**Process and pipeline: new vs run once.**

Deal one, every document was new. Deal two, you have the NBIO template, the diligence checklist, and lawyers and accountants who know how you work. Diligence goes faster when everyone's done it with you before.

And your pipeline is warm. Remember the owners who said "not yet" during your first round of calls? Some of them are ready now. Call them back. That's how Cam moved fast. The machine was ready before the deals showed up.

Two businesses, still above the line. "I don't have the capital to buy more" stops being true, because number two pays its own way.

**THE SELF-TEST.**

Three gates. Business one runs without you: a real stretch away and nothing breaks. Its cover has held at one and a half or better, every month, for six months. Number two is in the same vertical.

Verdict: pass all three, buy number two. Fail one, fix it first. It's cheaper than watching two businesses slide.

Where people lose money.

- **Buying #2 before #1 runs without you.** Your attention splits and both slide. The cover on business one is usually the first thing to go.
- **Signing a cross-guarantee nobody read.** Each business sits in its own company under your holding company, so a bad number two can't take number one with it. Ask your lawyer before you sign.
- **Draining #1's reserve.** One bad quarter and you're missing repayments on the business that was paying for everything.
- **A cheap deal in a new industry.** You're learning from zero again, with nothing to share.

Or you can keep doing what you're doing. Keep running one business at its ceiling. Keep watching owners in your industry retire and sell to someone else.

On a call with me we check business one against the three gates, then structure number two so it pays for itself and sits in its own company. We work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone whose business has stopped growing past you, and you'd rather build a group than polish one shop, this is the next move.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older either way. Or you're holding six months of cover ratios, a reserve in its own account, a coffee booked with your lender, and twenty "not yet" owners from your first round of calls rung back.

## EP 33 · The structure behind 10 businesses and 1 back office (copy it)

Take two owners. One owns one business and does every job in it. The other owns ten and does none of them.

The one with ten works less. And his back office alone can be worth close to a million at exit.

That comes down to one thing: structure. Here it is, layer by layer, in the order a bank and a buyer read it.

In a group, structure is money three ways. Ring-fencing protects what you already own. A shared back office adds profit. Clean, separate numbers lift the exit multiple.

Getting it right before deal one costs a few legal bills. Fixing it at deal five costs months, and sometimes a lender's consent you can't get. Every deal you add either slots into the structure or becomes another job for you. This is for people building a group. If you'll only ever own one business, take layer one and skip the rest.

Most owners think more businesses means more work. They think the owner has to be the best operator in the building. That's not how it works.

Here's how it works, in one line. The job in a group isn't running businesses. It's building the structure that runs them, then choosing the people who sit in it.

And if you're stuck inside one business right now, that's not your fault. It has nothing to do with your work ethic. It has everything to do with one business, set up the usual way, putting every job on the owner.

Four layers.

I help owners set this up before deal one, so they're not unpicking it at deal five. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The top: you and the holding company.**

At the top is you, often through a family trust or your own company. Under that, the holding company: the permanent vehicle. Your board holds its founders' equity here.

Two things to set on day one. Issue a lot of shares, a million not a hundred, so handing a director ten thousand is easy later. And give minority holders drag-along, with no veto and no voting control. If a director with three per cent can block a sale, a buyer will price that in.

I keep 73% of my company. **[JOSH: one line on how your healthcare group's holding structure is set up.]**

**One company per deal.**

Every business you buy goes into its own new company under the holding company. One deal, one company. Its own bank account. Its own facilities.

That's the ring fence. If business seven has a bad year or a lawsuit, it stays in business seven. Lose one arm, the octopus lives. And each company's numbers are clean on their own, which is how a buyer wants to see them.

Once you're across several industries or regions, add one more level: separate arms under the holding company, each with its own businesses, its own manager and its own lenders.

Read any cross-guarantee with your lawyer. On larger deals, often over about five million, an asset-rich business can give the bank its security without a personal guarantee. Typically. Every lender and deal is different.

**The shared back office.**

Split every business in two. The front wins and serves customers: sales, service, the team on the tools. That stays local.

The back is the same everywhere: bookkeeping, payroll, HR, insurance, legal, compliance. Move it up to the holding company and run it once for all of them. That's where a group gets cheaper to run than its parts. One set of systems also means your Monday numbers arrive in the same format from every business.

Build it in order. First one strong finance person who can run the books for three or four businesses. Then, as the group grows, split that job into specialists: a controller, a head of people, eventually your own legal and compliance. Hire slowly, and hire people who've done it in bigger companies.

**A manager in every business.**

Each business gets a general manager who owns its results. Not you. Not the seller, long term. Often the best GM is already inside the business: the senior person staff go to when the owner isn't there.

Set the numbers before they start. Never let a senior hire write their own targets. Pay a base, plus a bonus tied to that business's profit and a few of its Monday numbers.

Then your job fits on one line. Read the numbers every Monday. Meet each GM once a month. Run a quarterly board. Keep lenders and the sellers you're still paying informed. Find the next deal. You allocate capital and choose people. The group gets easier once every GM is better at running their business than you would be.

That's nearly a million of exit value from a back office. Before buying power with suppliers.

Where people lose money.

- **Everything in one company.** One lawsuit or one bad site puts every business at risk, and the bank prices it that way.
- **A back office never centralised.** The group costs more to run than its parts, and a buyer sees a collection and pays small-business multiples.
- **The owner as GM of everything.** Ten jobs, no business, and a buyer who knows the profit walks out with you.
- **Handshake equity without a drag-along.** A small holder can stall your exit.

Two paths from here. You can build it alone, one fix at a time, and find out at deal five that it all sits in one company. Or you can set it up with someone who's already built these structures.

On a call with me we draw your structure, plan the back office you'll share, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who's tired of being the bottleneck, and you want to own several businesses without working inside any of them, this is how.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older either way. Either still doing payroll on a Sunday, or with your structure on one page, your back-office costs totalled, and a GM job description written before you need one.

## EP 34 · $5.5M zero-down to a $200M exit: 5 things private equity pays for

There's a well-kept secret in selling a business. A buyer only pays for what they can check.

At seven times profit, every hundred thousand they can't verify is seven hundred thousand off your price.

My friend Jason Nagy bought a five and a half million dollar radiology business with zero down. Seven years later, private equity bought the group for two hundred million. Here are the five things a buyer like that checks, ranked by how much they cut your price.

The exit is usually the biggest cheque of your life. Most owners find out what it's worth three weeks before settlement, when the buyer's accountants tell them.

This is for owners who want to sell one day for a lot more than they paid. If you plan to hold forever and hand it to your kids, some of this helps. The rest isn't for you.

I know what you're thinking. Radiology. Two hundred million. My situation is different.

Look at the shape of it. One deal, a sixty-forty structure, nothing down. Then years of building what a buyer pays for. Jason's result isn't typical, but the shape is the same at five million.

[JOSH: one line on what Jason built in those seven years, if he's happy to share.]

The whole idea in one line: a buyer pays a multiple of the profit they can prove will stay after you leave. Five checks, smallest cut first.

I help owners buy the first business with little to no money down and build it for the exit from deal one. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Check 1: a clean structure.**

Each business in its own company, under one holding company. Minority shareholders with drag-along and no veto, set up from the start.

If a director with three per cent can block the sale, the buyer prices in the delay. Cheap to fix on day one. Expensive the week before settlement.

The same goes for every business sitting in its own company. A buyer wants to see each piece on its own, with its own accounts, leases and contracts, so they can value it and finance it.

**Check 2: room to grow.**

A buyer at this level wants a platform: a fragmented industry, a process for buying the next business, a team that's done it before. That's what episodes 31 to 33 built. No room to grow, lower multiple.

Know who your buyer is. Private equity, a bigger company in your industry, or selling part now and part later. Each one pays for different things, so ask your advisers early.

**Check 3: price and margin.**

Private equity looks for one number to change, usually price, then multiplies the new profit. If you haven't fixed your pricing before you sell, they will. They'll pay you for today's profit and keep the uplift. Do episode 27 before you sell, not after.

**Check 4: it runs without you.**

If the buyer thinks the profit walks out with you, they cut the price or tie you in for years with an earn-out. An earn-out is part of your price paid later, only if the business hits targets after you've handed over control.

A GM in every business and a back office at the holding company is what makes this check pass. So does size and stability: no single customer carrying the place, and profit that doesn't swing year to year.

**Check 5: numbers they can verify.**

This is the big one, and it isn't growth. It's proof.

The buyer's accountants run a quality-of-earnings review. Every add-back gets tested: owner expenses, one-offs, things you say won't happen again. Anything they can't verify comes out before they multiply.

And if your books aren't ready for that, it's not your fault. It has nothing to do with being careless. It has everything to do with books kept for the tax return, not for a buyer. Nobody tells owners the difference until a buyer does.

They'll also ask where every dollar of revenue came from. Which channel, which referrer, which contract. If you can't show it, they can't see where growth comes from, so they won't pay for it.

You checked the seller's quality of earnings on the way in. One day someone checks yours. I spent my early years in banking and I've been involved in more than a hundred million dollars of transactions. The buyer's side tests every number, and the ones that can't be proven don't get paid for.

Three and a half million gone, because the books couldn't prove what you knew was true.

Exit-ready, in order. Two to three years out: monthly accounts that reconcile to the bank, Monday numbers on every business. One to two years out: fix price, fix key-person risk, put the GMs in. About a year out: your own quality-of-earnings review, so nothing in theirs surprises you. Then a data room: every contract, lease, licence and staff agreement in one place.

Where people lose money.

- **Add-backs with no document.** No document, no add-back. At 7×, each $100K cut costs $700K.
- **No drag-along.** A small holder holds up the sale, and the buyer discounts for it.
- **Selling before fixing price.** The buyer keeps the uplift you could have banked.
- **Staying the key person.** The price drops, or you sign a multi-year earn-out.

Or you can keep doing what most owners do. Keep the books for the tax man. Keep the add-backs in your head. Keep a shareholders' agreement nobody's read. And find out what that costs on the day a buyer tells you.

On a call with me we set up your structure and books so a buyer can check them, and plan every deal you add to be bought the same clean way. We work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather a buyer find nothing than find out, and who builds for the exit from the first deal, you already know what to do.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older either way. Or you're thirty days older with every add-back matched to a document, your drag-along checked, and a data room started.

## EP 35 · 4 failed deals, 1,000 cold calls: the 3 mistakes that cost the most time

How come smart, capable people find it so hard to buy their first business? They have the salary, the skills, and can read a set of accounts better than the owner they'd buy from.

The expensive mistakes aren't the deals that fall over. I had four of those. The expensive ones are quiet: waiting, thinking, and the people you let near your decisions.

Here's what they cost, and the rule that fixes them.

Nobody sends you a bill for the deal you didn't make. But it's real. Take a business making two hundred and fifty thousand a year. After paying the seller, about eighty-four thousand a year stays with you. Wait three years and that's a quarter of a million you never kept, plus three years of debt you didn't pay down.

This is for people who are serious about buying. If you're here for entertainment, fine, but this one gets personal.

The idea in one line: in this game, time costs more than mistakes. Most people only count active risk, the chance a deal goes wrong. They never count passive risk: doing nothing while the years go by anyway.

First, my story. Then the three mistakes. Then the rule.

I help people stop waiting and buy a business with little to no money down. If you're stuck on one of these three, book a call below. Now the detail.

**The confession.**

At 25 I was unemployed, no savings, zero experience. If I'd waited until I felt ready, I'd still be waiting.

What I had was volume. Around a thousand cold calls. Four deals that fell over. Then a physio clinic: 100% vendor finance, seven years at four per cent, a nine-month payment holiday, rent in arrears, no deposit. I pulled a hundred and fifty thousand out in year one.

[JOSH: one line on what the four failed deals had in common.]

**Mistake 1: waiting for perfect.**

There is never a perfect moment. Decide at sixty per cent. When you have sixty per cent of the information and the deal passes the rules, move. The other forty is what due diligence is for.

And the market has moved. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. They don't want to wait sixteen weeks for a bank. They want out, and to be looked after. Paid over time, a deal can close in two to four weeks. Every month you wait, those owners sell to someone else.

Count both risks. Active risk is real: you might buy a deal that struggles. The rules exist to manage it. Passive risk is real too: another year in the same job, no ownership, and a business's profit paying down someone else's debt instead of yours.

**Mistake 2: analysis instead of action.**

This one gets engineers, accountants and consultants. A beautiful model of a deal they haven't found. Six months reading about vendor finance. No cold calls.

And if that's you, it's not your fault. It has nothing to do with your intelligence or your discipline. It has everything to do with a habit your career trained into you: learn first, act second. It gets you promoted. In deals, it keeps you at zero.

So here's the test. You've been watching for months. How many offers have you sent?

If it's zero, you don't have a knowledge problem. I've had people pay twenty to thirty thousand for advisory and still not take action. Money wasn't the issue. Where their energy went was.

**Mistake 3: the wrong people.**

On your board, friends and family who've never done a deal make you feel supported. They don't make a bank say yes. No relevant experience, no seat.

I had no network at 25. I found my whole board through LinkedIn and cold outreach, and the founder of the Australian Institute of Fitness is now a shareholder. The right people are reachable. You just have to ask them, with equity instead of cash.

In your advice, check who's actually done the thing before you learn from them. That includes me. Ask what I've done.

In your evenings, if everyone around you thinks buying with no money down is a scam, you'll start to believe it. Change the room. It's easier than changing yourself.

**The rule.**

Set a quota before you allow yourself more research. Fifty calls this week. One real NBIO to a real seller this month. Hit the number, then read whatever you like.

Careful is having rules: one and a half times cover, the three documents, walk away when it's wrong. Waiting is having no deadline.

Where this doesn't apply: if a deal fails the rules, walking away isn't waiting. That's the best negotiating tactic you have. The quota is for offers, not for closing bad deals.

That's before the equity you'd have built paying the debt down. The cost of waiting is the one number nobody puts in a model.

Where people lose money.

Once people own something, the time-wasters change shape.

- **Buying #2 before #1 runs without you.** Both slide, and you spend a year fixing what should have compounded.
- **Closing under 1.5× cover** because the price looked good. One bad quarter and you're feeding the business instead of it feeding you.
- **Letting the seller leave too fast.** No handover means relationships and know-how walk out the door.
- **Buying with no exit in mind.** You build a collection a buyer pays small-business multiples for.

Two paths. Alone, every mistake in this episode is easier to fall into. When nobody's asking how many offers you sent, the answer stays at zero. Or you can have someone in your corner who's already made the time-wasting mistakes and asks you every week what you sent.

On a call with me we set your quota, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. There's so much value in just being able to ask someone.

If you're someone who's been watching for months and has done enough research, this is where you swap waiting for a quota.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older either way. Either with another month of videos watched, or with two hundred calls made and one real NBIO in a seller's inbox.

# Act 8 · Objections and the close

## EP 36 · I bought my first business with $0 down. Here's where every dollar came from

One profitable business is a great position. Until you want the second one.

Then the question goes from "what should I buy?" to "where's the deposit coming from?"

On my first deal the answer was nowhere. Zero down. Seven years at 4%, and no repayments for the first nine months.

Here's where every dollar came from instead, in the order the seller and the bank see it.

If you own one business, your cash is the obvious way to buy the second. It's also the most expensive. Every dollar of deposit is a dollar pulled out of the business that's already paying you. And the bank still might say no, or private equity outbids you. This is for owners and operators who'll put 40-plus hours a week into building a group. It's not for anyone after a lifestyle business.

Zero deposit is a structure, not a sacrifice. Four parties fund the deal: the seller, the business's own profit, the bank, and your board.

You were told you need a deposit. You were told the bank decides. You were told that if you don't have the cash, you raise it and hand over equity, control and the exit. None of that is how most of these deals get done.

And if you're still on one business, it has nothing to do with your savings rate. It has everything to do with the fact that nobody showed you how a seller, a lender and the profit fund the deal between them.

Take money off the table for a second. Would you want a second business that pays you, with a manager running it? If yes, money is now a design question.

I help owners build exactly this structure. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The seller.**

The seller is your first bank. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. Their problem isn't price. It's the burden of owning the thing.

Sold the normal way, they wait 6 to 16 weeks for a bank and get offered 3 to 5 times profit. Paid over time, the price can go to 6 or 7 times, and it can close in 2 to 4 weeks. For someone who wants out, speed and certainty beat a cheque on day one.

One rule. Never say "vendor finance" to a seller. Say "getting paid over time."

My first deal was exactly this. A physio clinic. 100% vendor finance. Seven years at 4%, principal and interest. A nine-month payment holiday. Rent in arrears. No deposit. I took $150,000 out in year one and built mid six figures of equity. About a thousand cold calls and four dead deals came first.

**The profit.**

The business pays for itself, or you don't buy it. Free cash flow has to cover the repayments at least 1.5 times. That's the debt service cover ratio, DSCR.

Profit here means profit after paying someone to run it day to day. Otherwise you've bought a job, not a business.

So the seller's price is set by what the profit can carry. Divide profit by 1.5. That's the most you pay the seller each year. Multiply by the years. That's your price. That's the speed run: five minutes on your phone for any business you know.

**Settlement day.**

The deposit is zero because the seller carries the price. That leaves the costs at the closing table.

Lawyers and accountants go on deferred fees, paid at closing. A working capital line from the bank, drawn at settlement, covers them and gives you runway. The deal pays its own closing costs, not your savings. My friend Cam did two deals in 23 days, both 100% vendor finance, interest-only for 10 years with balloons in years 3 and 10, and got $500,000 of working capital from the bank.

Your board gets equity, not cash. That's next episode.

**The bank blend.**

A hundred percent seller money isn't the only shape. A common blend is 60% from a commercial lender and 40% from the seller. My friend Jason Nagy did his first deal that way: a $5.5 million radiology business, nothing down.

Debt over dilution. A bank is set up to lend, investors aren't, and debt lets you keep control and the exit. But only inside the 1.5 rule.

Personal guarantees: on deals over roughly $5 million, lenders often don't need one, because an asset-rich business is their security. Typically. Every lender and every deal is different.

Your offer, paid over time, beats most cash offers the seller will see. And the $84,000 of headroom is what keeps you alive in a bad year.

Where people lose money.

- Forgetting the 1.5 rule. "No money down" with no headroom is how a slow quarter becomes a default.
- Buying a job. If the profit only works with you behind the counter, the price is wrong.
- Saying "vendor finance" to a retiring owner. It sounds like a trick. "Paid over time" sounds like a plan.
- Paying lawyers and accountants in cash up front, with money the deal should be paying.

You can work this out alone. Most people who do it alone take years and lose deals. A seller says "I need half up front" and they fold. Or they sign something that clears 1.5 on paper and breaks in the first slow quarter, because nobody stress-tested it.

Or you have someone who's made those mistakes check your structure before a seller ever sees it. That's the call. Short application, then you and me. We work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who already owns one business and wants the next, someone who'll give this 40-plus hours a week, someone who'd rather design the deal than save for it, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older either way. Either you're still saving for the deposit, or you've run the numbers on real businesses and you know the structure your next deal needs.

## EP 37 · Every level of credibility, from no track record to bankable (for ~20% equity)

There's a well-kept secret in buying businesses. The buyers who get the meeting never ask to be taken seriously.

They give away a slice of equity to people who already are.

I keep 73% of my company. The founder of the Australian Institute of Fitness is a shareholder. I found my board on LinkedIn, at 25, with no money and no network.

A seller is handing over thirty years of work and staff they care about. A bank is lending against a plan. Neither will do it for a stranger with a CV. Get this wrong and every offer you send is noise. Get it right and you're the only serious buyer in the room, for the price of some equity that only pays out if you win. This is for people who'll send a lot of messages and hear a lot of no.

Credibility gets borrowed before it gets earned. There are five levels, from you alone to a lender asking what you need.

If you've been ignored, it has nothing to do with your age, your CV or your bank balance. It has everything to do with who's standing next to you when you ask.

Most people sit at level one for years, waiting to feel senior enough.

I help people build the board, the bench and the packet. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1: Just you.**

A seller asks two things. Will this person look after what I built? Will I get paid? A bank asks one. Who's behind this, and have they done it before?

At level one the answer to both is "me", and that isn't enough. The trap here is spending another decade building credibility inside someone else's company. None of it transfers to a seller.

**Level 2: The packet.**

Put it on paper. A one-page executive summary. A short thesis: the industry, why now, what you'll buy and how you'll fund it. An offer template. A diligence checklist.

The packet costs nothing and makes you look like a team before you are one. It's also what you send to level three. Once the board is built, add one line: our board brings this many years of combined experience and this much in transactions. Real numbers only. Never round up.

**Level 3: The board.**

Three or four seats. A chair with a big career behind them. A finance person who understands deal structures. A legal person. An operator from your target industry.

You pay them in founders' equity, usually 2 to 10% each, not cash. Tie vesting to a signed purchase agreement, so nobody gets paid for turning up to one meeting.

The sweet spot is recently retired executives. They're not short of money. They're short of being useful. Pitch the vision and the ground floor in three or four lines. Something like: "I'm building a group that buys businesses in this industry. I want someone with your experience at the table from day one. There's founders' equity in it. Twenty minutes this week?" No forty-page deck. No apologising for your age. Silence isn't a no. Follow up twice.

This isn't faking it. Faking it is claiming experience you don't have. This is real people, with real equity, doing real work.

Before I knew this, I was a 25-year-old asking people to believe in me. **[JOSH: one line on what those early conversations with owners or lenders were like.]** After I knew it, I stopped asking for belief and started offering seats. Not everyone said yes. Enough did.

My student Jy Kingston came from IT with no finance experience. He built his board in under 30 days on LinkedIn. Another student got the former CEO of IBM to join their company.

**Level 4: The bench.**

A proper M&A lawyer. An accounting firm that can do quality-of-earnings work, checking the seller's profit is real and will still be there after you buy.

You don't pay them up front. You negotiate fees deferred to closing, covered by the working capital line at settlement. Not every firm will. Plenty will. Jy had accountants and lawyers on side within about two weeks.

**Level 5: The lenders.**

Now you go to banks as an interviewer, not a beggar. "Here's my board. Here's what we're buying. What are your parameters?" Ask their minimum debt service cover, the deal sizes they like, and whether they'll do a working capital line at settlement. Build three or four of these relationships before you need one.

Same person as level one. Different conversation. Board first, then the deal, because the first time a seller asks "who's with you?", you want an answer.

Volume in, a small board out. Twenty percent of something real beats a hundred percent of a deal no seller will take.

Where people lose money.

- Equity with no vesting. You pay 20% for a board that never does anything.
- Recruiting mates instead of credibility. The bank reads the bios.
- Overstating who's on the board. Never round up a track record. Their real one is enough.
- Ten messages, eight no's, done. The numbers above need two hundred.

You can keep doing what you're doing. Keep building a track record for someone else's company. Keep waiting to feel senior enough. Keep hoping a seller judges you on potential.

Or you build the board now, with someone who's done it. That's the call. Short application, then you and me. We work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. Join and you also get a 1:1 session with Jy, the same Jy who built his board in under 30 days.

If you're someone who's tired of building credibility for someone else, someone who'll send the messages and hear the no's, someone who'd rather be judged by your board than your job title, you already know the next step.

Thirty days from now, either you're still waiting to feel ready, or you've got a one-page summary, a list of names, and two or three people saying yes to a seat.

## EP 38 · "My situation is different" vs the $2.4M deal a bank funded 100%

There are two types of "my situation is different." One is a fact. The other costs you a year.

Christian Dawes was unemployed, with no higher education and a criminal record. His first deal was $2.4 million, and the bank funded all of it. Over-funded it, in fact.

Today: how to tell which of your differences is real.

Business owners say it more than anyone. "I'm too busy running my own place." "My industry's different." "I couldn't run two." Some of that is true and changes the plan. Most of it is a story, and a story costs the most expensive thing you have: a year in which someone else buys the business you should have. This is for people who'll be honest about which is which.

A fact changes the plan. A story stops you starting. "I'm not a finance person" is a story. "I can only give this ten hours a week" is a fact.

If you've told yourself your situation is different, it has nothing to do with your background or your postcode. It has everything to do with never being shown what a lender and a seller actually check. When you don't know the checklist, every difference feels like it might be on it.

Two options, a test, and a verdict.

Sorting this out is half of what my first call does. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**What they actually check.**

A lender asks four things. Does the profit cover repayments with room to spare? Is there something to secure the loan against? Who runs it after settlement? Who's on the board?

A seller asks two. Will you look after what I built? Will I get paid?

Your CV, your degree and your past sit a long way down both lists. That's why Christian's bank said yes. They lent against a business, not a biography.

**Option A: The story.**

I know the thought. "That sounds good, but my situation is different." So here are three people.

"I'm not from finance." Jy Kingston, my student, came from IT. Board in under 30 days. Accountants and lawyers within two weeks. Twelve deals active, closing his first. He didn't become a finance person. He put finance people around him.

"I've got nothing going for me." Christian. Less than most people watching this, and a $2.4 million deal the bank funded in full.

"My market's different." Saul, an Owners Academy member, made one cold call direct to an owner. It turned into a signed NDA and early due diligence on an electrical and aircon business. 25 staff, around $3.8 million in revenue, owner retiring in two years. Trades, clinics, services. Boring markets aren't different. They're ideal.

None of them had an advantage you can't get. They had the mechanism.

**Option B: The fact.**

Some differences are real. They change the plan, not the possibility.

You have cash, property or a business. Real, and good news. It's faster, and lenders read you differently.

You already own a business and you're stretched. Real, and it's the reason to buy properly. You pay a manager out of profit before you work out what the seller gets. You're adding a business with a manager, not a second job.

You're outside Australia. Real, and it changes the team, not the method. There's a lender network in the US. I can only be a director in Australia. Elsewhere I come in as an advisor or equity holder.

Your industry isn't asset-rich enough to lend against. Real, and it changes which businesses you target, not whether you can buy one.

"I couldn't run two." You're not meant to run them. You're meant to own them, with someone running each one, paid before the seller is paid.

You can give this ten hours a week. Real. That's a no for now. Not forever.

**The self-test and the verdict.**

Finish the sentence: "My situation is different because…" Then ask: does a lender's list or a seller's list care? If neither does, it's a story. If one does, it's a fact, and the plan bends around it.

Then answer what I ask on every call. Your goal: big exit, legacy, lifestyle or flips. Your hours from Monday. Your assets. What you've done so far. Your industry. And what would stop you starting this week. Those six answers are your facts. Everything else is story.

Verdict: facts change the speed and the shape. Only one fact changes the answer, and that's hours. If you can give this 40-plus a week, your situation isn't the thing stopping you.

Two of the three thought they were the exception. Only one was, and it wasn't about money.

Where people lose money.

- Treating a story as a fact. You never start, and the owner sells to someone who did.
- Treating a fact as a story. You start without the hours and burn a seller's trust.
- Buying a second job. No manager in the numbers means the owner pays for it in time.
- Being too smart. Three weeks on a spreadsheet of why your city is the exception is two hundred calls you didn't make.

So two paths. Sort this out alone, and most people who do take years and lose deals, because nobody tells them which difference is real. Or have someone who does this every day tell you in one conversation.

That's the call. Short application, then you and me. We sort your facts from your stories, then work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. Outside Australia, that's where the advisor or equity role comes in, case by case.

If you're someone who's built one business and knows it could be more, someone who'll be honest about your facts, someone with the hours from Monday, then your situation isn't the thing stopping you.

Thirty days from now you'll have the same background and the same business. Either you're still calling it different, or you've got a plan built around your real facts and your first calls made.

## EP 39 · Free content is enough to buy a business (the $125K catch)

Most people watching this series shouldn't book a call with me.

Everything in the 3C model is learnable for free. This series is enough to act on.

The catch is the clock. A business making $250,000 profit hands someone $125,000 every six months. While you're still researching, that someone isn't you.

If you've got capital sitting in assets that barely move, you're paying for every month you spend learning. This isn't for you if you already send offers every week. It isn't for you if you've got someone who has closed deals to ring when it gets strange. It's for people who've watched for months and still couldn't say how they'd find and vet a real deal.

Information is free. What you pay for is speed and someone to ask. And one number tells you which you need: offers sent in the last 90 days.

If that number is zero, it has nothing to do with your intelligence or your discipline. It has everything to do with the fact that the hard moments in a deal are live, and the videos are recorded.

If you're in the second group, that's what I do. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The claim: you don't need me.**

Here's the do-it-alone path, honestly. Recruit a board through cold messages. Find a lawyer and an accountant who'll defer fees. Interview lenders before you need one. Make something like a thousand cold calls. Get three years of financials out of owners who don't trust you yet. Write the offer. Run diligence. Get through legals.

Every step is in this series, and it's enough to act on. I mean that. I did it that way at 25, and four deals fell over before the first one closed. It works. It's slow.

**Why people think they need more.**

Most people think one more video will make them ready. They think the next book will answer the question they're stuck on. They think action comes after enough information.

That's not how it works. People don't stall on information. They stall on the first live moment.

A seller says, "I've got another buyer offering cash." Usually a bluff. The right answer is "no worries, good luck." If you've never heard it, you panic and raise your price. The video can't read your LOI before you send it. It can't tell you the add-back in their numbers is nonsense. The accountant's numbers come back lower than the seller said. Walk away, adjust, or normal? The lawyer's bill is double, three weeks into a four-week job.

None of that is hard the fiftieth time. All of it is hard the first time. **[JOSH: one line on a live moment where having someone to call saved a deal.]**

**The analogy.**

You can read the road rules for a year. You learn to drive in traffic, with someone in the passenger seat the first few times. The rules were never the hard part. Traffic was. A seller's counter-offer at nine at night is traffic.

That's why there's so much value in just being able to ask someone.

**The test.**

So, one question. How many offers have you sent?

A friend of mine in the US, Elliot, sent about 40. Three came back as signed LOIs at 100% seller finance, on 99-year "pension plan" terms. For example, $350,000 a year for life to the owner, on a business with $500,000 of net operating income.

Forty. Not four. That's what Closing is. Not knowing how to write an offer. Sending enough of them.

And notice the terms. The owner is paid out of the business's income over decades instead of a lump sum. You don't find structures like that by reading about them. You find them by asking enough owners until a few say yes.

**Where paying doesn't help.**

The flip side, because paying fixes nothing on its own. I've had people pay for advisory and still not take action. Joining doesn't send offers. You do. Where your energy goes decides whether this works, not where your money goes.

So if you go alone, do three things. Find one person who has closed a deal like yours and ask if you can ring them. Set a date for your first offer and tell someone. Measure offers, not hours of learning.

And do one thing today. Write down your offer count for the last 90 days. Then write the five questions you've actually been stuck on. Not "how do I start". The real ones. Those five are what a call is for.

That's the real comparison. Not free versus paid. How long versus how long. And the owner you didn't call this year retires anyway, to someone who did call.

Where people lose money.

- Counting learning as progress. Every video feels productive while the offer count stays at zero.
- Folding to the cash-buyer bluff and raising the price on the first counter.
- Making an offer before getting the three documents: three years of financials, matching tax returns, revenue by financial year.
- Paying for help and still not sending anything.

You can keep doing what you're doing. Keep watching. Keep your capital where it is. Keep telling yourself you'll start once you've seen enough.

Or put a date on it, with someone in your corner who's seen the live moments. That's the call. Short application, then you and me. We work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy, with deal discussion every day, or 1:1 advisory on your actual deals fits. If you're already sending offers and doing fine, I'll tell you to keep going alone.

If you're someone who's watched enough, someone who'd rather send forty offers than read forty more books, someone who wants capital working in a real business, the next step isn't another video.

Thirty days from now, either your offer count is still zero, or you've asked for the three documents, run the numbers on real businesses, and your first offer is out.
