## EP 40 · Every stage from zero deposit to a 10× exit (the whole 3C model)

Everyone tells you to buy a business one of three ways. Save, raise or bid. All three are expensive.

Save, and it takes years, and the bank can still say no. Raise, and you give away equity, control and the exit. Bid on what's listed, and you're one offer in a pile.

How about something different. Buy at four times profit with the seller's money. Sell a group at eight or ten. That spread is the whole game.

This is the Season 1 finale. Forty episodes on one line: Credibility, Capital, Closing, Grow, Exit. If you own one business, it's the route from one income to a group worth a multiple. If you're employed, it's the route out without a deposit. It's for people who'll give this 40-plus hours a week, want a big outcome, and will act before they feel ready. Not for anyone after a four-hour work week.

The first three Cs get you the business. Grow makes it worth more. Exit is how it pays off. Five stages, and one way to lose money at each.

The save-a-deposit advice was written for a market that's moved. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. They don't want 16 weeks with a bank. They want out, and to be looked after. The buyer who offers that wins, often without the most cash.

And if none of this was obvious before, it has nothing to do with you. It has everything to do with the fact that nobody teaches the third way.

I help people run this whole line. Book a call below. Now the five stages.

**Credibility.**

You don't have a track record, so you borrow one. A board of three or four: a chair, a finance lead, a legal lead and an operator from your industry, paid in founders' equity, 2 to 10% each. Lawyers and accountants paid at closing. A one-page packet.

The mistake here is waiting to feel qualified. I found my board through LinkedIn and cold outreach at 25 with no network, and I still keep 73% of my company. My student Jy Kingston came from IT and built his board in under 30 days.

**Capital.**

The seller is your first bank. Paid over time out of profit, a retiring owner can get 6 to 7 times instead of 3 to 5, closed in 2 to 4 weeks instead of 6 to 16. Add a commercial lender where it helps, like a 60/40 split.

Two rules. Free cash flow covers repayments at least 1.5 times. And debt over dilution, so you keep control and the exit.

The mistake here is getting excited about "no money down" and forgetting the business has to carry the debt. $250,000 profit divided by 1.5 is about $166,000 a year to the seller. Over ten years, about $1.66 million. The $84,000 left over keeps you alive in a bad year.

My first deal ran on that logic. A physio clinic, 100% vendor finance, seven years at 4%, no deposit. I took $150,000 out in year one.

**Closing.**

Find the business before it's listed, with cold calls and one question: "Have you ever thought about selling?" Before any offer, get three years of financials, matching tax returns and revenue by financial year. Offer 3 to 5 times profit in an LOI, with the structure in it. Diligence on the quality of the earnings. Top-tier lawyers.

The mistake here is too few attempts. About a thousand calls before my first deal, and four that fell over. My friend Elliot sent about 40 offers to get three signed LOIs at 100% seller finance.

And when it gets tight, the best negotiation tactic is to walk away. The second best is a time limit: "You've got 24 hours to accept."

**Grow.**

The mistake here is thinking the work ends at settlement. It starts there.

First hundred days: keep the staff, keep the customers, keep the seller involved through the handover. Change slowly. Then pull three levers. Price, which in a business you've just bought is often the lever nobody has touched in years. A way to win customers on purpose instead of waiting for referrals. And a business that runs without its owner, because that's what the next buyer pays for.

**Exit.**

The mistake here is thinking one business is the goal. It's the start.

Buy several at small-business multiples, combine them, run them properly, and sell the group at the multiple a bigger buyer pays. That's multiple arbitrage. Business two is easier than business one, because now you have a track record, a lender who knows you and cash flow from the first.

My friend Jason Nagy did the real version. A $5.5 million radiology business, nothing down. Seven years later, a $200 million exit to private equity.

Illustrative, before debt, not a promise. It takes years, not months. But that's the gap between owning a job and owning a group.

Where people lose money.

- Waiting for a perfect moment. There isn't one. I decide at 60%.
- Doing nothing because it feels safe. That's passive risk: a salary that never builds anything you own. A structure that has to clear 1.5 before you sign is active risk, and it's the one you control.
- Stopping at one. The money in this model is in the second, the fifth and the tenth.

So, the most direct version of the turn I've made in every episode.

You can do this alone. Every stage is in this series, and it's free. Most people who do it alone take years and lose deals. They watch for a year. They sign a structure that breaks in a slow quarter. They give equity away with no vesting. Not because they're not smart. Because nobody was there at the moment it mattered.

Or you do it with me. Two ways in. The Owners Academy: the private community, the full course, every template and script, and deal discussion every day. Or 1:1 advisory through G&L M&A Advisory, where I work your strategy, your structures and your lender presentations with you. Equity or advisory roles are considered case by case.

The call works like this. Short application, then you talk to me. I'll ask your goal, your hours from Monday, what you have to leverage, your industry, and what would stop you starting this week. If it's nothing, we get to work: board first, then a structure run through the 1.5 rule before any seller sees it, then calls, documents and your first LOI, checked before it goes out. If neither option fits, I'll tell you.

Join, and you get the NBIO template, the offer document we use. A client of mine was quoted about $7,000 for the same document. And a 1:1 session with Jy Kingston.

Not for: four-hour-work-week seekers, lifestyle-business hunters, or people who'll watch for another year without sending an offer.

You now know a business can be bought without a deposit from you. You know credibility can be borrowed. You know the only thing between you and the first offer is a decision.

If you're someone who's done being the most capable person in a company you don't own, someone who can give this 40 hours a week from Monday, someone who'll act at 60%, you made this decision somewhere around episode one.

Thirty days from now, two things can be true. You're forty episodes in and nothing's changed. Or you've got a board forming, a structure that clears 1.5, and your first offers out to real owners.

# Act 9 · Deals that died

## EP 41 · Inside a $3M no-money-down deal that died

There's a well-kept secret in buying businesses. Almost everyone who owns a few has a drawer full of deals that died. You see the closings. You never see the drawer.

This was a $3 million business, structured with no money down. It died **[JOSH: how far in, e.g. after the LOI / six weeks into diligence / days before settlement]**.

Today I open the drawer. And I'll show you the one gap that tells you a deal is dying months before it's dead.

A $3 million business that clears its debt properly can leave you around $300,000 a year after repayments. Illustrative. Chase the wrong deal for six months and the fees are the small part. The real cost is half a year of that cash from a deal you could have closed instead.

This one is for owners who already run a business and want the second. Your hours are the scarcest capital you have. If you're after a lifestyle business, this isn't for you.

Welcome to Season 2. The map in two lines. Credibility, Capital and Closing get you the business with little or none of your own money. Grow lifts the profit, and Exit is where you get paid for it.

This act is deals that died, and they die in Closing.

The whole idea in one line: deals rarely die at the end. They die at the start, and you find out at the end. What it costs you is the gap between the first warning sign and the day you walk.

Five parts to this post-mortem. The business. The structure. The timeline. The break. The rule.

And if a deal of yours has died, it has nothing to do with how smart you are or how much money you had. It has everything to do with nobody showing you where deals crack.

I help owners buy businesses this way, and spot the dead one before it eats six months. Book a call below. Now the post-mortem.

**The business.**

[JOSH: the business in one breath: industry (general if the seller needs privacy), city or region, years trading, staff, rough revenue and profit, why the owner was selling, and how you found it.]

On paper it ticked the boxes I teach. Boring. Asset-rich enough for a bank to lend against. An owner who wanted out.

[JOSH: which boxes it really ticked, and the honest reason you wanted it, e.g. "it would have been the platform for a group".]

Hold on to that last part. Wanting a deal badly is what makes you look past the warning signs. The more a deal means to you, the worse you read it.

**The structure.**

First, how a $3 million deal like this is usually stacked. Illustrative, not this deal.

The bank lends 60%, $1.8 million, secured on the business. The seller carries 40%, $1.2 million, paid over time as a note or rolled into equity. A working capital line at settlement covers the advisers. Your deposit is zero.

One rule sits over all of it. Free cash flow covers every repayment at least one and a half times. That's DSCR, debt service coverage. Below 1.5, one bad quarter puts you in trouble.

Here's what we actually agreed.

[JOSH: price and multiple; the real stack (% seller, % bank, any rollover, term, rate, payment holiday, working capital line); DSCR if you remember it; anything unusual, e.g. earn-out, property, rent deferral.]

**The timeline.**

Now the dates. The gaps tell you more than the documents do.

[JOSH: first conversation, financials in hand, LOI signed, diligence started, the day it died; total months; which stretch ran longest, and why.]

A stage running twice as long as it should is rarely a scheduling problem. It's the deal telling you something. A seller who takes a month to send the tax returns is answering a question you haven't asked yet.

**The break.**

Here's the moment a $3 million deal died.

[JOSH: what killed it (bank said no / diligence found lower profit / seller walked / lease / key practitioner), the scene and the words used, and what you tried in the next few days to save it.]

And looking back, the first sign was there early.

[JOSH: when the first sign showed, what it was, and what you did with it, e.g. "decided it would sort itself out" / "didn't push because I wanted the deal".]

That's the bit to remember. Not the cause. The gap between the first sign and the day it died.

One more thing about the table. Everyone at it is paid by a different outcome. The seller's accountant, a broker, the bank, a lawyer billing by the hour. None of them carries the deal the way you do.

[JOSH: if it applied here, one line on who didn't carry your interest and how. Otherwise cut.]

**The rule.**

Here's what most people get wrong about a dead deal. They think it means the model doesn't work. They think it means they weren't ready. They think the answer is another year of study.

That's not how it works. A dead deal is the checks doing their job. The expensive deal is the bad one that closes.

[JOSH: the rule this deal left you in one sentence (e.g. "If X isn't true by week Y, I stop"), and the two or three things you now do on every deal because of it.]

And here's the order I'd give anyone, whatever killed their deal. First, three years of financials, the matching tax returns and revenue by year, before any offer. Then write your walk-away lines, before you start wanting it. And once you're in diligence, keep two or three other deals moving, so no single seller owns your year.

The fees on a dead deal are usually small. The cost is the cash flow from the deal you didn't close while you were chasing this one. About $25,000 a month, on these numbers.

Where people lose money.

- **One live deal.** You overpay and explain away the warning signs. It costs you this deal and every one you didn't chase.
- **An offer before the documents.** No three years of financials and matching returns, no offer. Otherwise you pay for diligence on a business that was never what it said.
- **No walk-away date.** Without one written down at the start, six weeks becomes six months. On the numbers above, that's about $25,000 a month.
- **No deal log.** A dead deal with no written rule is a lesson you paid for and didn't collect.

You can do this alone. Path one: you run your business all week, chase a deal at night, it dies, and you work out why months later, if ever. Path two: someone who has watched deals die looks at yours in week one, and the crack shows while it's still cheap to walk.

That second path is the call. A short application, then you talk to me. We look at your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. If neither does, I'll tell you.

If you're someone who already runs one business and wants the next, who'd rather find the crack in week two than month six, and who'll act before it feels perfect, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either with the same single deal and the same hope, or with a deal log, a live pipeline and someone checking your deal before it costs you. The difference is the decision you make today.

## EP 42 · The $15M deal that died (who said no, and why)

Buying a $3 million business is a manageable thing. One seller, one lender, a deal you can hold in your head.

Until it gets to eight figures. Then it goes from a deal to a table. From one lender to a credit committee. From one adviser to six.

This was a $15 million business, no money down. The person who killed it was **[JOSH: who, in two or three words, e.g. "the bank" / "our own accountants" / "the seller's son"]**.

At this size the upside is real. An illustrative $15 million business might clear $1.3 million a year after repayments, and above about $5 million banks often don't need your personal guarantee. But every month the deal drifts is about $110,000 you're not collecting. And there are more people who can stop it.

If you've got capital to put to work and want to know how big deals get vetted, this is the episode. If you want this to be easy, it isn't.

The idea in one line: a big deal dies at the chair you didn't map.

Three things change as deals get bigger. More people can say no. The checks get harder. And time becomes a risk on its own.

Four parts. The deal. The table. The break. The rule.

And if a big deal of yours has died, it has nothing to do with whether you were big enough for that size, or had enough grey hair. It has everything to do with how many doors a big deal has, and whether anyone showed you how to walk the table before they started closing.

I help people structure deals, big and small, and map who can kill them first. Book a call below. Now the post-mortem.

**The deal.**

[JOSH: the business: industry (general if needed), region or country, years trading, staff, rough revenue and profit, why the owner or owners wanted out, how it came to you, and what was different from the $3M deal at the start, e.g. "this time I had a board".]

Here's the general shape at this size first. Illustrative, not this deal.

A bank, or a group of lenders, takes most of the price against the business's assets and cash flow. The seller carries the rest as a note, or keeps real equity and gets a second payday when the business sells again. The 1.5 rule doesn't move with size. Free cash flow covers all repayments one and a half times.

Above about $5 million, the bank's security is often the business's assets, so a personal guarantee often isn't needed. Typically. Every lender and deal is different. The price of that is scrutiny. Every number gets tested.

[JOSH: what you actually put on the table: price and multiple; % bank, % seller note or rollover, any other layer, term, rate, payment holiday; the lender in general terms and how you found them.]

**The table.**

Let me walk the table, because this is where it got complicated.

[JOSH: who sat on your side, who sat on the seller's side, who else had a say (landlord, regulator, major customer, key staff), and who you marked as the biggest risk at the start, and why.]

At $3 million, it's mostly you, the seller and one lender. At $15 million there's often a credit committee, a bigger accounting firm doing quality-of-earnings work, lawyers on both sides, sometimes a landlord, sometimes the seller's family. Every one of them is a door that can close.

And time works against you. The longer the process, the more room for a bad month, a nervous partner or a seller who changes their mind.

**The break.**

Here's where it broke.

[JOSH: the timeline in one line (first contact, LOI, diligence, any milestone, the day it died, total months); what killed it (credit committee / earnings lower than the accounts / seller walked late / lawyers ran out the clock); how you heard and what was said; what you tried to save it, e.g. re-pricing, another lender, a longer seller note.]

[JOSH: was it the chair you'd marked as riskiest, or someone else, and what that taught you about reading a table.]

[JOSH: what it cost: rough out-of-pocket (or "mostly deferred adviser fees"), months, any relationship it touched, and what it did to your pipeline the month after.]

**The rule.**

Plenty of things in a dead deal sit outside your control. A credit committee. A seller's family. That doesn't make them outside your responsibility. The useful question is what you'd do differently, even when it wasn't your fault.

[JOSH: the rule this deal left you, in one sentence, and two or three things you now do on bigger deals, e.g. meet the credit team earlier, test a term sheet before the LOI, meet the seller's family or advisers in week one, a hard diligence date.]

And the order I'd give anyone moving up in size. First, talk to lenders before you need them. Then, in the first seller meeting, ask who else has a say. And once the LOI is signed, keep diligence tight. Ninety days is a ceiling, not a target.

Now, I know what a story like this does. It makes big deals sound like something for your tenth deal. So look at my friend Jason Nagy. His first deal was a $5.5 million radiology business. Nothing down, a 60/40 structure. Seven years later he sold to private equity for $200 million.

Not because big is easy. Because big isn't reserved for people with a long track record. Size isn't the risk. An unmapped table is.

The bigger the deal, the more each month costs and the more chairs can end it. Mapping the table is how you protect both.

Where people lose money.

- **Running six parties from your inbox.** Nobody owns the timeline, diligence drifts past 90 days, and each extra month costs about $110,000 here.
- **Assuming the bank's fine because nobody's said no.** Silence from a credit committee isn't approval. Ask what would make them decline, before the LOI.
- **Never meeting the person the seller listens to.** A spouse, a son, an accountant. You meet them at the end, when they can only say no.
- **Not knowing your guarantee position before the term sheet.** On a big deal, that's your personal balance sheet on the line.

Or you can keep doing it the way most people do. Keep managing six parties from your inbox. Keep assuming the bank's fine. Keep finding out who mattered after the deal is dead.

Or you can do it with me. A short application, then you talk to me. We find where your deal is weakest, work out your first structure, and decide whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits, where I sit on your side of the table.

If you're someone with capital to put to work, who'd rather walk the table before diligence than after, and who'll give this real hours, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. The question is whether you're thirty days older with your deal table on one page and someone on your side of it.

## EP 43 · 1,000 cold calls and 4 dead deals before my first business

How come smart, capable people find it so hard to close their first business? Good job, good brain, years of running other people's numbers. And the first deal doesn't close. Then the second doesn't either. Most people stop right there.

I had none of those advantages. About a thousand cold calls. Four deals that died. Then a physio clinic. Today is deals one to four.

Here's why it matters in money. Deal five was 100% vendor finance, no deposit, and I took $150,000 out of it in year one, with mid six figures of equity on top.

The four dead deals cost me time, not capital. A bad deal that closes costs you years. So the early failures are the cheapest lessons you'll ever pay for. This is for people with a good salary and no ownership, who can take a lot of no. If you need the first deal to work, this isn't for you.

The idea in one line: the first deal is a volume game, and each dead deal makes the next one more likely to close. Not because you get lucky. Because you stop making the same mistake.

Three parts. The calls. The four. What changed before the fifth.

When I started I was 25, unemployed, with no savings and no experience. And if your first deals have died, it has nothing to do with your CV, your savings or your age. It has everything to do with volume and pattern. Nobody tells you the first few are supposed to die.

I help people get through deals one to four faster, so they don't learn every lesson the hard way. Book a call below. Now the four.

**The calls.**

The four deals came out of the calls, so start there.

[JOSH: what kind of businesses you called and where the list came from; over how many months; roughly how many calls a day or week; one reaction you still remember (a hang-up, a laugh, a yes you didn't expect).]

The question was simple. "Have you ever thought about selling?" Calls still beat email for getting an owner talking. The best deals are off-market, and off-market deals come from conversations, not listings.

Here's how a funnel like that can look. Illustrative, not my numbers. A thousand calls. Maybe a hundred real conversations. Maybe twenty owners who'll talk numbers. A handful who send financials. One or two offers.

[JOSH: your rough real split if you remember it, e.g. conversations, financials received, offers. Otherwise cut.]

The exact ratios don't matter. The top has to be big for anything to come out the bottom. If you want one offer a month, work back from that number to your calls a day. That's the job, not a feeling.

**The four.**

Deal one.

[JOSH: deal one in one line each: business and rough size, how far it got, what killed it (owner not ready / numbers didn't hold / didn't know what to offer), the lesson.]

Deal two went further.

[JOSH: deal two: business and size, stage reached, what killed it (seller wanted all cash / no bank would look at me / financials didn't match the returns), the lesson.]

Deal three.

[JOSH: deal three: business and size, stage reached, what killed it (family member said no / landlord / key staff / ran out of credibility), the lesson.]

Deal four hurt the most.

[JOSH: deal four: why it hurt most (closest / you'd told people), business and size, stage, what killed it, the lesson, and what you nearly did next, e.g. go back to job-hunting, and what kept you going.]

**What changed.**

Put the four next to each other and a pattern shows. You can't see a pattern in one dead deal. You can in four. Most people never get to four, because they stop at one and decide the model doesn't work for them.

[JOSH: the before and after: before you knew X, you used to Y and got a dead deal every time; once you understood it, you stopped Y and started Z. Name the common thread, or say there wasn't one.]

Not because I got lucky. Not because I worked harder. Because I could finally see the pattern. That's why I keep saying volume. The calls aren't the cost of the deal. They're how you buy the pattern.

And here's what didn't change between deal four and deal five. My bank balance. Still zero.

Deal five was a physio clinic. 100% vendor finance. Seven years at 4%, principal and interest. A nine-month payment holiday. Rent in arrears. No deposit.

That payment holiday matters. For the first nine months, the profit isn't going out to the seller.

[JOSH: which lessons from deals one to four showed up in the physio deal, e.g. what you asked for earlier, how you made the offer, how you talked about getting paid over time.]

Four dead deals cost me time. The fifth paid me $150,000 in year one. The funnel is why the fifth existed at all.

Where people lose money.

- **Stopping at deal two.** The four dead deals are the cost of the fifth. Stop early and you've paid for the lessons without collecting.
- **Calling five industries at once.** Pick one boring, asset-rich industry with owners near retirement. One list, not five, so the pattern shows.
- **Offering before the documents.** Three years of financials, matching tax returns, revenue by year. Then an offer.
- **No tally.** Calls, conversations, financials, offers, every Friday. Without it, a slow month feels like a dead model.

You can do this alone. Path one: you keep the job, make calls when you can, and stop at deal two because nobody told you that's normal. Path two: you learn from dead deals that aren't yours, mine and the ones members bring to the community, and you get to the one that closes with fewer scars.

That second path is the call. A short application, then you talk to me. We work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy, with the calling scripts and daily deal discussion, or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone with a good job and no ownership, who can take a no and make the next call, and who'd rather learn from four dead deals than live through them, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either with the same job and the same plan for "one day", or with a call tally, a target industry and someone in your corner for deal one.

## EP 44 · 4 reasons sellers walk before settlement (ranked)

There are only two types of buyers. The ones who get the seller to settlement day, and the ones who get a phone call the week before.

The difference has nothing to do with price. A seller rarely pulls out on the day they pull out. They pull out weeks before. They just tell you on the day.

Here's why price is rarely the reason. Take a business making $250,000 profit. A cash buyer at four times offers about $1 million. Paid over time inside the 1.5 rule, the seller gets about $166,000 a year. Over ten years that's $1.66 million. Illustrative.

When a seller walks from that, something other than money got louder. If you've taken one business as far as it goes and the next step is buying a second, this is the episode that keeps that deal alive.

The idea in one line: the objection at the end is a question you didn't ask at the start.

Sellers walk for four reasons. I'll rank them from least to most dangerous, by how late they show up and how hard they are to fix. Staff. Advisers. Remorse. Family.

You were told sellers sell to the highest bidder. You were told the price is the deal. You were told a seller who signs an LOI has decided. None of that is the full story. A lot of sellers sell to whoever makes them feel safe about their name, their family and their people. Every one of those is something you can deal with.

So if a seller has pulled out on you, it has nothing to do with your offer being too low or you being too young. It has everything to do with a conversation nobody taught you to have in the first meeting.

I help people run their deals so the seller's still at the table on settlement day. Book a call below. Now the four.

**Staff.**

Owners often care about their people more than they let on. A deal can die because the owner pictures telling the team.

The fix: show them the plan for the staff before they ask. You keep the team. You meet key people before close, with the seller's permission, at the right time. Retention bonuses for key staff can go into the structure.

It's last on the list because it's the easiest to fix. Once the seller knows their people are looked after, it usually goes away.

**Advisers.**

The seller's accountant, lawyer, or a mate at the golf club says, "You could get more than that."

Sometimes they're right. More often they're comparing your offer to an all-cash price that doesn't exist for this business.

The fix: get the adviser in the room early and walk them through the maths. A higher multiple paid over time out of profit looks very different once someone explains it properly. And never say "vendor finance" to a seller. Say "getting paid over time."

**Remorse.**

Thirty years. Their name on the van. Their number on every invoice. Then someone tells them, nicely, that in six weeks it won't be theirs.

That isn't about money. It's about who they are on Monday morning.

The fix: ask about life after the sale in the first meeting. "What does your week look like a year after this?" If they can't answer, you've found your biggest risk. Then build them a role. A transition, typically six months to three years, with a title and dates. A seller with somewhere to go is far less likely to walk.

**Family.**

The most dangerous one is the person you never met. A spouse who wants the cash now. A son or daughter who assumed they'd inherit it. A sibling who owns 20% that nobody mentioned.

It's first because it surfaces last, when they can only say no.

The fix is one question, early. "Who else has a say in this? Who would you talk to before you signed?" Then you meet them. Over coffee in week two, not at the signing.

[JOSH: optional: one of your own deals where a seller pulled out late, and which of these would have caught it. Cut if none died this way.]

**The five questions.**

Every reason shows up in the first or second meeting, if you ask.

"What would you do with your week after this?" That's remorse.

"Who else has a say?" That's family.

"What worries you about your team?" That's staff.

"Who do you trust for advice on this?" That's advisers.

"When do you want this done, and why then?" That's timing.

You're not there to overcome the objection. You're there to understand it, before it's an objection.

**Keep the clock tight.**

Time feeds all four. The longer from yes to settlement, the louder they get.

First, an exclusivity period in the LOI, often 60 to 90 days. Then diligence, 90 days at most. And once diligence is done, legals, about four weeks. Push the lawyers on scope and dates, so fees don't creep and the deal doesn't drift.

And run more than one deal. A seller can feel the difference between a buyer who needs this deal and one who doesn't. If a seller says "there are other buyers," most of the time there aren't. Stay calm. The buyer who can walk away has the most leverage.

The seller is better off on paper. So when they walk, look for what got louder, not for a higher number.

Where people lose money.

- **Raising the price to keep them.** You pay more and the real reason stays. It comes back at settlement.
- **Meeting the family at the signing.** By then they can only say no.
- **Arguing with the adviser.** Explain the structure instead. An adviser who understands it often becomes your best seller.
- **Letting diligence drift.** Every week past the date is another week for remorse, family, staff or advisers to get louder.

Or you can keep doing what most buyers do. Keep focusing on the bank and the numbers and never meet the seller's spouse. Keep arguing with the adviser. Keep running one deal, so the seller can tell, and the price starts moving.

Or you can do it with me. A short application, then you talk to me. We work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy, with the seller conversation scripts, or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who's taken one business as far as it goes, who'd rather meet the seller's family in week two than hear from them in week twelve, and who's ready to buy the next one, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you could have a seller still at the table, the spouse met, the adviser on side and a date on every stage. Or the same hope you've got today. The difference is the decision you make now.

## EP 45 · Every red flag from first call to settlement, in 5 gates

Everyone buying a business gets the same advice. Hire a good accountant, do your due diligence, and you'll be fine.

The only problem is, by the time diligence starts, most dead deals are already dead. Nobody's told you yet.

So instead of one big check at the end, here's one page you run at every stage. Five gates. If a line goes red, you fix it, price it in, or walk.

The money here is what you don't overpay for. Miss one line and you can own a business whose cash flow covers its repayments 1.33 times instead of 1.5. That's one slow quarter from trouble, with your capital in it.

This is for people with capital who want a process they can run every time. If you'd rather go on gut, this won't suit you.

The idea in one line: a checklist read once at the end is a post-mortem. Run at every stage, it's a filter.

Five gates, in the order a deal moves. Credibility. Capital. Before the offer. The offer and diligence. Legals and settlement.

Every line gets one of three actions. Fix it. Price it in. Or walk. Decide which before you're emotionally in the deal.

And if you've ever missed a red flag, it has nothing to do with intelligence or how carefully you read. It has everything to do with when you looked.

I help people run this list on real deals, so the red line shows up in week one, not month four. Book a call below. Here's the list.

**Gate 1. Credibility.**

First you check your own side.

Red flag: your board is friends and family. If they've no relevant deals or exits, a bank and a seller can tell. Fix it before you make offers.

Red flag: no engagement letters. Advisers who said "happy to help" aren't a team. Get deferred or rolled fees in writing.

Red flag: you've never spoken to a lender. If the first time a bank hears of you is with a live deal, you're asking, not choosing.

[JOSH: one Credibility gap that helped kill one of your early deals, in one line. Otherwise cut.]

**Gate 2. Capital.**

Next, the money.

Red flag: the deal doesn't clear 1.5. Free cash flow covers repayments less than one and a half times. That's not a deal. That's a slow month away from trouble.

Red flag: no working capital line approved before settlement. You can close and run out of cash in the same week.

Red flag: too small to carry itself. Under about a million in value, there often isn't enough cash flow to pay the seller and pay you.

Red flag: a personal guarantee you didn't plan for. Above about $5 million, banks often don't need one. Typically. Every lender and deal is different. Know where you stand before the term sheet.

**Gate 3. Before the offer.**

Here's why this gate matters more every year. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. Clinics, trades, services. When that many owners want out, the scarce skill isn't finding a deal. It's knowing which ones to drop, early.

Red flag: one deal in your pipeline. You'll overpay and ignore the signs. Run several.

Red flag: the seller won't send three years of financials, the matching tax returns and revenue by year. That request is the motivation test. No documents, no offer.

Red flag: the financials and the returns don't match. Not a negotiation point. Walk.

Red flag: a broker process with inflated numbers and "other buyers interested." Most of the time there aren't. Stay calm.

**Gate 4. The offer and diligence.**

In the LOI, red flag: no conditions. Financing approval, quality-of-earnings sign-off, the lease, licences, key staff and the seller's transition should all be in there. No exclusivity period is another.

In diligence, the big ones. One or two customers above about 30% of revenue. Above half, walk or discount hard. Gross margin slipping behind accounting changes. Tax debt or liens. Key contracts that can't move to a new owner. Licence or compliance problems in a regulated industry. Very high staff turnover.

And a seller who wants out the day you settle, with no transition. High severity. Walk.

**Gate 5. Legals and settlement.**

Red flag: legals drifting past about four weeks. Fees creep and sellers get cold feet. Keep scope tight and dates clear.

Red flag: the lease isn't assigned or renewed.

Red flag: last episode's seller signs. Remorse with no plan for their week after. A family member you've never met. An adviser saying they could get more.

**What this act added.**

And the lines my own dead deals added to the list.

[JOSH: the three lines this act added, one each: the rule from the $3M deal, the rule from the $15M deal, and the one lesson from the first four you'd keep if you could keep only one.]

Then log every loss. Every dead deal gets one line: which gate, which red flag, which C. After five or six, your pattern shows. Fix that one first. Not all of them. The top one.

Same business, two lines. The coverage line you fix in the structure. The customer line you price in, or you find out how locked in that customer is and whether the contract moves to a new owner.

Where people lose money.

- **Running the list once, at the end.** By then you've paid for diligence on a deal that was red in week one.
- **Turning amber into green because you want it.** Concentration at 40% becomes "probably fine." That's how capital goes into the wrong business.
- **Treating every line as a walk.** Most lines are fixable or priceable. The walk lines are few. Kill too many deals and you kill your pipeline.
- **Skipping Gates 1 and 2.** A lot of deals were dead before the first call, because the board or the money wasn't there.

You can run this alone. Path one: you run the list yourself, and when you want the deal, amber turns green in your head. A seller with no plan becomes "they'll come round." Path two: someone who isn't in love with your deal runs it with you, and the red line shows while it's still cheap to walk.

That second path is the call. A short application, then you talk to me. We work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy, where members run this list on each other's deals, or 1:1 advisory, where I go through yours line by line, fits.

If you're someone with capital to put to work, who'd rather find the red line in week one than month four, and who wants a second set of eyes before you sign, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either with this list run on a real deal, or still wondering what you missed. That's decided today.

## EP 46 · The $800K customer eating your margin (P&L teardown)

There's a well-kept secret in businesses bought from retiring owners, and it isn't on the P&L.

In this one, the biggest customer brings in $800,000 and leaves the least behind. Fix that one account and the business is worth about $224,000 more.

Most new owners stare at revenue. That is… not it. Here's the teardown, in the order you should read it.

In a 3C deal you pay the seller out of profit. So every dollar of margin you find lands twice: headroom on that payment now, and value at the exit, where profit gets multiplied.

This is for someone who has just bought, or is in diligence and wants to know what they're actually buying. It isn't for anyone planning to change prices in week one.

The rule: price every job before you change anything. Revenue tells you how busy you are. Margin tells you who's paying for the business.

We read it four ways. The P&L. The service lines. The customers. The jobs. Then what one fix is worth.

And if you've owned a business for a month and can't see the leaks, it has nothing to do with how smart you are or how many years you spent reading reports. It has everything to do with how small businesses keep their books. The P&L is built for the tax return, not the owner.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down, then do exactly this in year one. If that's you, book a call below. Now the detail.

**The P&L.**

The business is plumbing. $2 million revenue, $300,000 profit after paying a manager to run it. Bought on vendor finance, paying the seller $200,000 a year. That's $300,000 divided by 1.5, the minimum cover we structure to.

One definition. Gross margin is gross profit over revenue, and gross profit is revenue less the direct cost of doing the work. Overhead is everything you can't pin on one job.

The P&L gives you one margin for the whole business. It's an average. Averages hide everything.

**Service lines.**

Split it three ways.

Take off $350,000 of overhead and there's your $300,000.

**Customers.**

Most people think the biggest customer is the safest. They think more revenue means more profit. They think the seller knew where the money was. That's not how it works.

The builder is 40% of revenue and about 22% of gross profit. That's normal, not bad luck. Big customers negotiate, and over twenty years every negotiation went their way. Small customers never asked.

In diligence, one customer over 30% of revenue is a red flag. Once you own it, it isn't a flag. It's yours to manage.

Rule: list your top ten customers by revenue, with gross margin next to each. The order usually changes.

**Jobs.**

Now go one level down. Pull twelve months of jobs. For each one: what you invoiced, hours times the real cost of an hour, materials, and any return visit.

The real cost of an hour is the wage plus super, the van, fuel, the phone and the time between jobs. Cost it at the payslip rate and every job looks better than it is.

Sort worst to best. In trades and service businesses the same leaks turn up. Variations done on the day and never invoiced. Return visits done free and never recorded. A job an hour away charged like one around the corner. An old rate the previous owner agreed as a favour. And slow payers, which is episode 50.

Then ask the techs one question: "What do we do that we never charge for?" Write down every answer.

Last cut: tag every job by where the customer came from. Referral, Google, the builder, the number on the van. Your best margin often comes from the channel you spend least on.

In a clinic the job is the appointment. You cut by practitioner and by service. Same method. **[JOSH: one margin leak you found in the physio clinic or the healthcare group, and roughly what fixing it was worth.]**

**The fix.**

Month one, you measure and change nothing visible. Month two, you find why the builder's margin is low. Say variations go unbilled and the rates haven't moved in years. At the next contract review, every variation gets billed and the rates move. Margin goes from 18% to 25%.

The seller payment doesn't move, so all of it is headroom. And at an illustrative four times, one account review is worth about $224,000 at the exit. The multiple depends on the market and the buyer.

The decision rule. Any customer running at under half your average margin gets reviewed at their next contract date. Three options: reprice, rescope, or let them go. And you only let one go when the seller payment is still covered 1.5 times without them. Here, it isn't. So this one gets repriced, not fired.

Where people lose money.

- Cutting before measuring. You lose the best tech in week two, and the emergency customers who ask for him go too.
- Ringing the builder in month one with new rates. If they walk, $144,000 of gross profit goes. Profit drops to about $156,000 against a $200,000 seller payment. Cover falls below one.
- Costing labour at the wage. Every job looks profitable and the leaks stay hidden for another year.

There are two paths here. Alone, it's your first time, so you make first-time mistakes: you cut early, ring the big customer early, or find the leak and fix it clumsily. Or you do it with someone who has already made them. Measure first, fix second, talk to customers third.

On the call we look at your deal or your target, your structure, and which way in fits. The Owners Academy: the full course, every template and script, deals discussed daily. Or 1:1, where I work your strategy, structures and lender presentations with you. If neither fits, I'll tell you.

If you're someone who'd rather know the numbers than hope, and who'll put real hours into year one, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Same P&L and the same guesses. Or every job priced and three leaks on a list.

## EP 47 · Costs customers never see vs costs they feel ($32K found in $350K of overhead)

There are two types of new owners: the ones who cut what customers never see, and the ones who cut what customers feel.

That's it.

The first group finds about 10% more profit and nobody notices. The second saves a little and loses the customer. Here's $350,000 of overhead, line by line, and which list each line belongs on.

In a vendor-finance deal, every dollar of cost you remove goes straight to cover on the seller payment. Cut the wrong dollar and you lose revenue you were counting on to make that payment.

This is for people who look at capital the way an investor does: what it costs, what it returns, what it protects. It isn't for the slash-and-burn crowd.

The idea in one line: cut once, from the list customers never see, then leave costs alone and go after income. Costs have a floor. Income doesn't.

Two options. Option A, the costs customers feel. Option B, the costs they never see. Then the test that sorts any line, and the verdict.

And if a business looks lean already, it has nothing to do with you missing something or not knowing the trade. It has everything to do with time. Twenty years of small costs pile up, and nobody running the place reads the bank statement line by line.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down, then do this work in year one. If you want me on your numbers, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A: costs customers feel.**

You were told the way to fix a business is to cut the biggest line. You were told wages are the problem. You were told a new owner proves themselves by cutting hard. None of that holds. The biggest line is usually the one customers feel. The money is in the small lines nobody reads.

List A is your best techs, the person who answers the phone, parts on the van, how fast you turn up. In a clinic it's your practitioners and your front desk. Touch those and customers notice within a week. Pay properly here. Keeping a customer is worth more than the saving.

**Option B: costs they never see.**

Same illustrative plumbing business: $2 million revenue, $300,000 profit, $200,000 a year to the seller.

Walk list B. Software: a twenty-year-old business often pays for three tools that do one job, plus seats for people who left. Say $8,000. Insurance: re-quote through a broker at renewal, same cover checked line by line. Say $5,000. Vehicles: sell the ute nobody drives and the lease, rego and insurance go with it. Say $7,000. Bank and card fees: ask. Say $2,000.

One more that sits in delivery but customers never see: materials. Put more volume through fewer suppliers and ask for better terms. A couple of points on $400,000 is about $10,000.

Leave rent alone. If the seller owns the building, the lease was part of your deal. Note it for renewal.

How you ask matters. Tell suppliers you're the new owner, you're reviewing every account and you'd like to keep them. Most would rather sharpen a price than lose the account. Do it in one sweep, over a few weeks, not one supplier at a time over a year. A new owner gets one natural moment to reopen every account. Use it once.

**The add-backs.**

In diligence you adjusted profit for the seller's personal costs: the car, the phone, sometimes a family member on payroll. That adjustment assumed those costs stop. Check that they did. If the seller's still on the fuel card six months after settlement, the profit you paid for isn't the profit you're getting.

**Labour per dollar.**

Watch labour cost per dollar of revenue, not the wage bill. Tech wages of $800,000 on $2 million is 40 cents in the dollar. Each cent you take off is worth $20,000.

You get there through hours, not pay. Driving across town between jobs. A second trip for a part. Going back to fix a job twice. Fix the scheduling, stock the vans, lift the first-time fix rate, and the same team bills more hours. Nobody takes a pay cut.

**The test and the verdict.**

The test for any line: would a customer notice within a week if this changed? Yes, it's list A. Protect it. No, it's list B. Cut it.

Verdict: list B, once, properly, in month two. Then stop. Six months squeezing the phone plan is six months not spent on income.

Where it doesn't apply. If the overhead really is lean, skip the sweep. Go straight to the add-backs and labour per dollar. That's where a lean business still hides money. And if labour per dollar is rising month on month, that's not a cost problem. It's a scheduling problem, and you fix it in the diary, not the payroll.

About 10% more profit from lines no customer will ever see. Worth having, and it has a ceiling, which is why the next three episodes are income.

Where people lose money.

- Cutting list A because it's the biggest number. The good techs leave, and the revenue goes with them.
- Adding overhead ahead of income: a GM you don't need yet, a new system, a nicer office. Every new fixed cost comes out of the cash that pays the seller.
- Leaving the add-backs running. The profit you paid for quietly leaks back to the seller.

You can keep doing what most new owners do. Cut from the wrong list. Squeeze the last $500 out of the phone plan while the builder from episode 46 keeps eating the margin. Wonder why the good techs leave. Or you can cut once, properly, with someone who knows where to look, and get back to income.

On the call we go through your target or your business, your structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy is the course, every template and script and daily deal discussion. 1:1 is me on your strategy, structures and lender presentations.

If you're someone who reads a bank statement line by line, and who'd rather protect the people customers love than the subscriptions nobody uses, you already know the next step.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older whatever you do. The only question is whether the subscriptions are cancelled and the add-backs have stopped.

## EP 48 · Every level from one-off job to contract revenue (and what each is worth at exit)

A trades business is a good business, until January, when every customer has to find you again.

You go from booked out to chasing work. From choosing jobs to taking anything.

In this illustrative business, 200 customers on an upfront plan put $72,000 in the bank before a single visit. Here's every level from one-off job to contract revenue, and what each is worth when you sell.

Revenue that repeats pays you twice. It's predictable cash for the seller payment every month. And when you sell, it's the first thing a buyer checks in diligence: how much of this comes back on its own.

This is for owners willing to build something slowly that pays for years. Not for anyone looking for a trick that doubles revenue next month.

One line: sell the next job at the moment of this one, and get paid for it upfront.

There are four levels. One-off jobs. Repeat customers. Plans paid upfront. Service agreements. Most trades businesses sit on level one or two.

And if your business has always lived job to job, it has nothing to do with disloyal customers or techs who can't sell. It has everything to do with the fact that nobody ever offered customers a reason to come back.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down, then build them up like this. If that's where you're heading, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1. One-off jobs.**

Every job is won from scratch, usually through Google or the number on the van. Revenue starts at zero each year. A buyer sees a business that has to be re-won every January, and prices it that way.

The move up: record who bought and what they'll need next.

**Level 2. Repeat customers.**

Customers come back, but only when something breaks, and only if they remember you. Better. Still not predictable.

Count it: how many customers bought once last year, and how many came back? That one number tells you which level you're on.

The move up: stop waiting for the next breakdown. Give the customer a reason to book before it happens.

**Level 3. Plans paid upfront.**

A home plan. Two visits a year, priority booking, no callout fee. $360 a year upfront, or $36 a month.

Monthly adds up to $432. That's 20% more for paying monthly, on purpose. Make upfront the obvious choice.

Sell it at the moment of the big job. The tech finishes installing a hot water system and says: "Most people with a new system get it checked twice a year so it lasts. We do that for $360 a year, and you jump the queue if anything goes wrong. Want me to put you down?" One line on why, one on what, one question. The office sends the invoice the same day.

Set the term just past how long a happy customer stays. In a trade that's usually a year. Send a reminder before it renews and make cancelling easy. A plan people regret is worse than no plan.

**Level 4. Service agreements.**

For businesses: property managers, strata, schools, shops. A fixed monthly fee for a set scope, repairs at agreed rates, signed for a year. This is the level a buyer values most, because it's contracted.

Illustrative: ten agreements at $500 a month is $60,000 a year, signed before the year starts. And every one of them brings repair work at rates you've already agreed. You sell these once a year, at renewal, to someone who already buys from you. The builder's maintenance work is the obvious first one.

In clinics, the version is packages and memberships. Check what your sector's rules allow before you design one. **[JOSH: one line on whether your healthcare group sells packages or memberships, and roughly what share of revenue repeats.]**

**What a buyer pays for.**

Here's what's changed about who buys small businesses. It isn't only the next owner-operator any more. It's groups doing roll-ups: buy businesses at around four times profit, combine them, sell the group at eight, ten, fifteen. Those buyers don't pay for a full diary. They pay for revenue they can predict. A business on level one is priced as a job. A business on level four is priced as an asset.

If you haven't bought yet, ask it in diligence. Saul, an Owners Academy member, is in early diligence on an electrical and aircon business: 25 staff, around $3.8 million revenue, owner retiring in two years. The question I'd want answered on a business like that: how much of the revenue repeats, and how much has to be won again?

That's before any repair work the plans bring. And it lands at the start of the year, not when someone's pipes happen to burst.

Where people lose money.

- Building the plan and never selling it. It sits on the website, nobody hears about it, and you decide plans don't work in your industry.
- Pushing it on every job. It feels like a hard sell, reviews drop, and the referrals that fed level two dry up.
- Locking people in past the value. Complaints and cancellations cost more than the plan earned.

Two paths. Alone, you either never sell the plan or oversell it, and you take years to find the middle. Or you start with a script, a moment and a number to hit, and someone checking the plan before a customer sees it.

If you're someone who's tired of starting every year at zero, and you'll ring ten customers this week, you've already made the decision.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either still waiting for the phone, or with your first plans sold and paid upfront.

## EP 49 · 4 guarantee mistakes that cost more than the guarantee ($12K worked example)

Every trades business tries to win quotes the same way: a sharper price, a faster reply, and "satisfaction guaranteed" on the van.

Nobody believes the van. So it wins nothing.

How about something different. A promise built from your own job data. In this illustrative business it costs about $12,000 a year, and it pays for itself if it wins $30,000 more work. Here are the four mistakes that make guarantees cost more than that.

Price is the most expensive way to win work. Every dollar you drop comes straight off margin, and margin pays the seller. A guarantee lets you win at the same price.

This is for owners who've hit the ceiling of what one good name can carry, and who'll count before they promise. Not for anyone who wants a slogan.

One line: a guarantee is only cheap when you're already good, and you only know you're good if you've counted.

Four mistakes, ranked. Mistake two is the expensive one. The last one is the one most owners never think of.

And if you've tried a guarantee before and it did nothing, it has nothing to do with your industry or your customers. It has everything to do with where the promise came from. Copied off another van, it can't mean anything.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down, then do this kind of work in the first year. If you want help with yours, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Mistake 1. A promise nobody believes.**

You were told the bigger the promise, the more it sells. You were told a guarantee has to be bold to be noticed. It works the other way. A promise that sounds too good makes people doubt the whole business.

Promise something specific and modest that you control. Pick one:

- "Fixed price before we start. No surprises on the invoice."
- "If the same fault comes back within 90 days, we come back free."
- "We text when we're 30 minutes away. Miss the window you picked and the callout fee's on us."

Why fixed price works so well: people hate a surprise on the invoice far more than a higher number on the quote. You're not promising perfection. You're removing the thing they're actually afraid of.

Put it where the decision happens: one line on the quote, near the price, and in the tech's mouth at the door. In a clinic, never guarantee a clinical outcome. Guarantee the experience: on time, a clear plan, a call back the same day.

**Mistake 2. Writing it before you've counted.**

Same illustrative plumbing business: $2 million revenue, $300,000 profit, $200,000 a year to the seller. About 4,000 jobs a year.

Say 3% need a return visit. That's 120 trips at about $150 each. $18,000 a year you're already spending, for free, with no credit. A guarantee puts in writing what you already do.

Now model the downside. Putting it in writing pushes return visits to 5%, because a few people try it on. 200 visits, $30,000. Extra cost: $12,000.

Rule: if your return-visit rate is 15%, don't write a guarantee. Fix the work first. And if you've just bought and have no data, don't launch in month one. Track return visits for a quarter. The promise you write in month four will be one you can afford.

**Mistake 3. Ten conditions.**

Conditions full of gotchas turn a guarantee into fine print, and customers read it as a trick. Worse, your own team stops believing it, and they're the ones who have to say it out loud. Keep two or three, each one something the customer wants anyway, plus one catch-all.

- Invoice paid on time. It filters out the customers hardest to look after.
- They went ahead with the fix you recommended. Choose the cheap patch and the guarantee covers the patch.
- Catch-all: nobody else has worked on it since.

One legal line. In Australia customers already have rights under consumer law, and other countries have their own. Your guarantee sits on top of those rights, never in place of them. Get the wording checked by a lawyer before it goes on a quote.

**Mistake 4. Using one tool for two problems.**

Most quotes aren't lost to a no. They're lost to hesitation. An outcome guarantee removes the fear of it going wrong. It doesn't remove the fear of starting.

So on bigger jobs, add the second tool: a small deposit to lock the booking, fully refundable until you order parts. Saying yes becomes easy. Saying no becomes a decision. And cash arrives before the work, which is next episode.

On a business quoting well over a million a year, $30,000 is a small lift in quotes won. Past that point, the guarantee is making money.

Where people lose money.

- Copying a competitor's guarantee without knowing your own return rate. It either means nothing or costs a fortune.
- Launching it in month one. Track return visits for a quarter first.
- Leaving it off the quote. A guarantee the customer never sees at the moment of decision wins nothing.

Or you can keep doing what most businesses do. Copy a promise off a van. Bury it in conditions. Keep dropping price to win the quote, and pay for it out of the margin that pays the seller. Or build it from your data, with someone checking it, and let it do its job.

On the call we look at your business or your target and which way in fits. The Owners Academy: the course, every template and script, daily deal discussion. Or 1:1, where I work your strategy, structures and lender presentations with you. If it's neither, I'll say so.

If you're someone who'd rather be trusted than cheapest, and you'll count before you promise, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Same sticker on the van. Or your return visits counted, and one sentence your customers believe.

## EP 50 · Profit doesn't pay the seller. Cash does. (60 to 30 debtor days = $165K)

How come a profitable business can run short of cash on the first of the month?

It has customers, a full diary and a P&L that says $300,000. But the bank balance never lies.

In this illustrative business, $165,000 of cash is sitting with customers. Here's how to bring it home, without finding one new customer.

In a vendor-finance deal the seller gets paid from the bank account, on a date. Every dollar stuck with a customer is a dollar that isn't there on that date. Run short twice and you're renegotiating with the person you just bought from.

This is for owners who check the balance with a knot in their stomach. Not for anyone who thinks cash flow is the accountant's job. In a 3C deal, it's yours.

One line: profit is an opinion on the P&L. Cash is what pays the seller.

Most people think profit is money. They think a good year means a full account. They think the seller gets paid from profit. That's not how it works. The bank account only knows what's been collected.

Five parts. Measure it, find it, move it forward, chase it, and use the deal's own cash tools properly.

And if you've ever been profitable and short of cash at the same time, it has nothing to do with being bad with money or soft on customers. It has everything to do with a P&L that counts money when you earn it, not when it arrives.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down, then make sure the business can carry its own debt. If you want me on your numbers, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Measure it: debtor days.**

Same illustrative plumbing business: $2 million revenue, $300,000 profit, $200,000 a year to the seller. About $16,700 a month.

Debtor days is what customers owe you, divided by revenue, times 365. At year end they owe $330,000. $330,000 over $2 million, times 365, is about 60 days. The business waits two months to get paid for work it has already done.

**Find it.**

Do the same sum customer by customer. One or two accounts usually hold most of it. Here, the builder from episode 46 owes $200,000 of the $330,000.

That's not a collections problem. That's one conversation. And you have it at the contract review, with the margin numbers from episode 46 in front of you: "We want to keep the work. To do that, we need fourteen-day terms or a rate that carries sixty." Either answer is better than what you have now. And it's the same customer with the weakest margin. Low margin and slow money together means you're funding their cash flow out of your seller payment.

**Move it forward.**

Five ways, in order of how fast they work:

- **Paid on the day** for homes. The tech takes payment on site. Card fees are real, so price for them. A couple of percent to be paid today usually beats sixty days and three phone calls.
- **Deposits** on quoted work, so materials are paid for before you order them. Last episode's refundable deposit does double duty.
- **Progress payments** on bigger jobs. Never carry a whole job on your own cash.
- **Shorter terms** for businesses. Seven or fourteen days where you can get them. At the builder's contract review, terms go on the table next to the rates.
- **Upfront plans** from episode 48. Cash before the work.

**Chase it.**

An aged-debtors list, every Monday. A reminder the day it's due. A call at seven days. A firmer call at fourteen. A written rule for when new work stops. Written down, so it isn't personal.

And take payment while you're in the room. A tech who leaves without payment has just become a debt collector. "I'll pay when I get the invoice" loses you more money than any follow-up call ever wins back. Set the techs up to take it, and make it the default, not the exception.

**Use the deal's cash tools.**

Two from the deal itself.

The working capital line. A bank will often put one in place at settlement. My good friend Cam got $500,000 of working capital from the bank across his two deals. It's runway. It isn't a permanent loan to your slowest customer. If debtors keep growing and the line keeps getting drawn, the bank will notice before you do.

The payment holiday. My first deal, the physio clinic, had a nine-month payment holiday on the vendor finance. Seven years at 4%, nothing down. A holiday like that is when you fix collections, before the first payment is due. **[JOSH: what you did with cash and collections during the nine-month holiday at the physio clinic. Cut if nothing.]**

It isn't new profit. It's cash you'd already earned, arriving sooner. At 1.5 times cover, that buffer is what gets you through a slow winter.

Where people lose money.

- Reading the P&L as the truth. Profit looks fine while the balance falls.
- Being too polite to chase because you're new and want to be liked. Respected beats liked.
- Using the working capital line to fund slow payers every month. It hides the problem until the bank finds it.

Two paths. Alone, you learn this the expensive way, on the first of a month when the money isn't there. Or you learn it from people who've already paid for the lesson, before it costs you anything.

On the call we go through your deal or your target, how the cash covers the seller, and which way in fits. The Owners Academy: the course, every template and script, daily deal discussion. Or 1:1, where I work your strategy, structures and lender presentations with you, including the working capital line.

If you're someone who'd rather be respected than liked, and you'll ring the five biggest overdue accounts this week, you already know what to do.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Same money sitting in other people's accounts. Or debtor days falling and a buffer under the seller's payment.

# Act 11 · Grow: sales and marketing

## EP 51 · 100 enquiries, 40 booked: where the other 60 go (step-by-step)

A business you buy comes with an enquiry flow nobody has measured.

A clinic or a trade is a great business. Steady work. People need you. Until the phone rings more than anyone can answer. Then it goes from booked out to busy, and somehow not growing.

In a clinic doing 100 enquiries a month, ten more bookings is about $72,000 a year. Same enquiries. No new ads. I'll walk one enquiry through the business, in the order the customer sees it, and show you where the money leaks.

When you buy at 4× profit, you pay for the bookings the business already makes. You don't pay for the ones it loses. That gap is upside the seller isn't pricing, and you capture all of it. This is for owners and buyers of service businesses: clinics, trades, local services. It isn't for a business that's already at capacity. Fix capacity first. That was episode 26.

Speed to lead means one thing: every enquiry gets a human reply in minutes, not hours, on every channel it came in on.

There are four stops on the way from first ring to booked job. The door, the first five minutes, the hours, and the chase.

If a business is slow to answer, it has nothing to do with lazy staff, or a bad receptionist, or impatient customers. It has everything to do with one thing. Nobody owns the number.

I help owners buy businesses with little to no money down, then plug exactly this kind of leak. If you want that for yours, book a call below. Now the detail.

**The door.**

Map every way a customer can reach the business. Phone, website form, email, Google messages, Facebook, Instagram, the after-hours voicemail. Next to each, write who answers it and how fast.

Most small businesses have at least one door nobody owns. Then mystery-shop it. Call at 7:30 on a Tuesday night. Fill in the form on a Sunday. Message the Google profile. Time every reply.

If you're buying, do this before you make the offer. It costs nothing and the seller never knows. Then in due diligence ask for the enquiry numbers: how many came in, from where, and how many booked. If the seller can't answer, you've found upside, and you've found a business where nobody watches the front door.

The rule: every door has a name next to it. No name, no door. Shut it, or give it to someone.

**The first five minutes.**

Three touches. An automatic text the second the form lands, so they know a human saw it. An email with what happens next. Then a real phone call.

Give it one owner. A boring business already has a receptionist, an office manager, or the owner's partner doing the books. Give one of them first response and one number: minutes to first contact. Speed you don't measure drifts back to "I'll call them after this job".

Most owners think growth means more leads. More ads, a new website, more posts. That's not how it works. If you're reaching half the people who already put their hand up, buying more just pours more water into a leaking bucket. Answer what you've got. Then buy more. Episode 54 is the buying.

**The hours.**

Enquiries arrive when customers are free. Lunch, evenings, Sunday night. Not when the office is open. In a trade it's worse. The person who should answer is on a roof.

So move the hours, not the headcount. An office person on 11 to 7 instead of 8 to 4. An after-hours answering service. In a clinic, someone clearing online booking requests in the evening, because that's when patients sit down and search.

Pull a month of enquiry times and count them by hour. The answer is usually obvious. If a third of enquiries land after 5pm and nobody answers until 9am, that third is going to whoever picks up first.

Speed doesn't mean ringing someone at 9pm. Calling-hour and privacy rules exist. Build them into the system.

**The chase.**

Some enquire and don't book. Chase on a fixed rhythm: the same day, then once a day for a few days, then a monthly check-in. Cancels and no-shows get the same. Every message carries a link to rebook, because without one people cancel and never come back.

Don't overdo it. A 70-year-old physio patient doesn't want eight emails. One text, one reminder, one call. A customer who replies "yep, see you Thursday" turns up. One who goes silent needs a call.

Then log it. Every enquiry: time in, time of first reply, source, booked or not. Four columns in a spreadsheet. After a month you know your real booking rate, and after three months you have a number the next buyer can check.

[JOSH: one line on whether you checked enquiry handling before buying the physio clinic, or what you found after settlement]

You didn't pay a dollar for that $172,800 when you bought. And a leak you plug in year one gets valued again when you sell.

Where people lose money.

- They buy software nobody opens, and the enquiries still sit in an inbox until Monday.
- They tell the receptionist to "be faster" and never give her a number, so nothing changes.
- They buy ads before fixing response, and pay for leads that ring out.
- They skip enquiry data in due diligence, so they can't see the upside or prove it to the next buyer.

You can work this out alone, on the business's money, a missed job at a time. Or you can do it with me, and look at enquiry flow during due diligence, so the fix is in the plan before you settle.

On the call we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. I made about 1,000 cold calls before my first deal. I know what speed and persistence are worth.

If you're someone who'd rather fix the leak than buy a bigger bucket, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be 30 days older either way. Still guessing how many jobs went down the road, or with an enquiry log, a five-minute rule and a number you can show a buyer.
