## EP 52 · 5 mistakes that let quotes die after you buy a business

Most quotes don't lose on price. They die in silence.

How come a business with good reviews, fair prices and a solid team still loses jobs it should win? The quote goes out, and nobody calls again. The job goes to whoever followed up.

In a trade sending 100 quotes a month, five more wins is about $40,000 a month of revenue, from quotes you've already paid to produce. Here are the five mistakes that kill them, worst last.

In a lot of small businesses the owner is the sales process. He knows who to ring back and what to say. When he retires, it walks out with him, and your profit drops in year one while you're paying him. This is for owners who've hit the ceiling of what they can sell themselves, and buyers taking over from a founder. It isn't for anyone who wants pressure tactics. They don't work on a 60-year-old getting a quote for a new roof.

A sales process is the written path from enquiry to deposit, simple enough that someone other than the owner can run it.

Five mistakes break it. If quotes are dying, it has nothing to do with your price, your workmanship or "customers these days". It has everything to do with nobody ever writing it down.

I help owners buy businesses with little to no money down, then build this so the business doesn't hang on one person. Book a call below if that's you. Now the detail.

**Mistake 1. The process lives in one head.**

You were told good work sells itself. You were told selling is pushy. You were told if they want it, they'll call. None of that's true. The businesses that win more jobs aren't better tradespeople. They ask better questions, make one clear recommendation and ring back.

So write the stages on one page: enquiry, triage, visit, quote, follow-up, deposit. But sell it yourself first. As the new owner, go on 20 quote visits or sit in on 20 consults. Write down every question, objection and reason someone said no. Then write the page.

The page is short on purpose. If it runs past one page, nobody reads it, and the team goes back to doing it their own way.

Saul, an Owners Academy member, is in early due diligence on an electrical and aircon business: 25 staff, about $3.8 million in revenue, owner retiring in two years. In a business like that, one of the first things I'd ask is who wins the work, and whether it's written down anywhere.

**Mistake 2. Pitching instead of asking.**

Five questions do most of the work. At the kitchen bench, for a ducted aircon quote:

- "What made you call us now?" Broad gets you the real reason.
- "Is it just you deciding?" Partner, landlord, body corporate. Book the visit when both partners are home and "I need to talk to my wife" goes away.
- "What's it costing you?" The power bill. The bedroom nobody sleeps in over summer.
- "Have you had anyone else out?" Either they haven't, or something didn't sit right.
- "When do you need it done?" Last. Asked early, it sounds like pressure.

The customer does most of the talking. By the time you quote, they've told you why they need it, what it's costing them and who signs off. The quote just confirms what they already said. The rule: no price until you've heard the problem in their words.

**Mistake 3. Leaving without a decision.**

You're the expert. Say what you'd do, give the price plainly, ask for the go-ahead. No fake urgency. If they say yes, take the signature and the deposit there and then, like we set up in episode 50. More jobs are lost to "I'll sign it tonight" than are won in follow-up.

In a clinic the same bones look different. A physio doesn't sell, they recommend care. But the next appointment gets booked before the patient leaves the room. A lot of clinics lose patients after visit one simply because nobody booked visit two.

[JOSH: optional, one sentence on how your clinic books the next appointment]

**Mistake 4. "Let us know" follow-up.**

Follow-up is planned, for a month, and tied to the objection. "Did you get a chance to talk to your partner?" "We've got finance options now." Any sign of interest, you ring. You don't start an email thread.

And every lost job gets one reason: budget, authority, need or timing. Once a month, fix the biggest one. Lost on budget, add a finance option. Lost on authority, book visits when both decision-makers are home. Lost on timing, put them on a three-month call list.

Start today. Pull every open quote from the last 30 days and ring each one. In most businesses I look at, that list is the fastest money in the building.

**Mistake 5. Too many options.**

This one's counterintuitive. Good, better, best feels helpful. Often it hands the customer a reason to go home and think. One recommended option gets a yes or a no.

I wouldn't rip it out on day one. Test it. One estimator quotes a single recommendation for a month. Compare win rate and average job value against the rest.

Five points of win rate. No new leads, no new staff.

Where people lose money.

- They copy a pushy script off the internet and upset customers who've been loyal for 15 years.
- They spend months moving to expensive sales software the team won't use. A spreadsheet and a one-page script beat it.
- They let the seller leave before the process is written, so the know-how retires on settlement day.

Or you can keep doing what most new owners do. Change nothing, and let quotes keep dying in silence. Or do it with me: we work out who actually wins the work during due diligence, and plan the handover around it.

On the call we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. I made about 1,000 cold calls before my first deal. Every one was a sales conversation with a stranger.

If you're someone who wants the next business to run on a process, not a personality, this is for you. Thirty days from now you can be 30 days older with the same quotes dying, or 30 days older with one page, a lost-job log and every open quote called back.

## EP 53 · Salary vs pay on cash collected: your first salesperson inside a 1.5 DSCR deal

Your first salesperson can break the deal before they sell a thing.

There are two types of owners. The ones who still quote every job at night, and the ones who hired someone and paid them for cash in the bank.

On a business making $250,000 a year and paying the seller about $166,000, there's $84,000 of room. A $90,000 salary eats all of it. Here's salary versus pay on cash collected, a test for when to hire, and my verdict.

In a vendor-financed deal, the cash flow that pays a new hire is the same cash flow paying the seller. The 1.5 DSCR rule applies every month, not just at settlement. Get this hire wrong and you're late on the seller in month six. Get it right and you stop being the business's only salesperson, which is what holds its multiple down. This is for owners whose quote calendar is full. It isn't for someone hoping a hire fixes a business that can't deliver.

Pay for cash in the bank, not for turning up.

Two options. Option A, a salary. Option B, a smaller base plus commission on cash collected, taken back if the job is refunded. First, a self-test for whether you're ready to hire at all.

If your last hire didn't work, it had nothing to do with being a bad judge of people, or "nobody wants to work". It had everything to do with the setup: no target, no trial, and pay that didn't depend on results.

I help owners buy businesses with little to no money down, then make them run without the owner doing everything, sales included. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The self-test.**

Three questions. Have you, or a manager, sold it yourselves and written it down? That was episode 52. Are there more quote requests than you can get to? And do you know what a good month looks like in dollars?

Three yeses, you're ready. Any no, you're not. Without selling it yourself, you can't set a target, you can't judge a trial, and you can't know what to pay.

And the target comes from your own month. If you sold $60,000 of work last month, that's the yardstick. Expect a new hire to do about half of you at first. Half of you, while you work on the business instead of in it, is a good trade.

**Option A: salary.**

A salary is a fixed cost. You pay it in the month they sell nothing, and month one is usually that month. On our illustrative deal, cover goes from 1.5 to below 1.0 before the first job comes in. That's a deal paying the seller out of your savings.

Salary does buy one thing: loyalty from someone who won't take a commission-heavy role. If you go this way, size the debt for it in the deal model, before you settle.

**Option B: base plus cash collected.**

A smaller base, plus commission paid when the cash lands. Part when the deposit clears, the rest when the final invoice is paid. If the job's cancelled and refunded, the commission comes back.

Cash collected, not jobs signed. A signed job that cancels has paid you nothing, so it pays them nothing. That one rule lines their month up with your DSCR.

Use tiers, so the more they sell the better the rate. A flat amount per job lets people hit a comfortable number and coast. And never change the plan mid-stream. Your best sellers leave first.

Before I knew this, I'd have assumed you need cash to attract good people. At 25 I had none. I built my board by offering founders' equity instead of fees. Good people took upside over a fixed cheque. Pay that grows with results attracts people who expect results.

**Who, and the trial.**

Hire someone who's already sold in your trade or a close one. Don't hire hopefuls you have to train. Find them like customers: ask your suppliers, your accountant, your best tradespeople. Hiring from a competitor is fine within the law. Hire the skill, never the customer list.

Everyone sounds great in an interview. So the trial is real work: the first 30 quotes, with a pass mark set from your own numbers. Judge them on revenue per enquiry, not close rate. Close rate is easy to game by only chasing the easy ones.

In a clinic, be careful. I wouldn't put a front-desk coordinator on commission for treatment sold. Measure that role on speed to lead, bookings and shows.

Option B costs you $50,000 less if they sell nothing, and pays them more if they sell a lot. That's the trade you want.

Where people lose money.

- A friend of a friend on a big salary in month one, no target, no trial, no clawback.
- Hiring before you've sold it yourself, then paying for a guess.
- Paying on jobs signed, then paying again when the job cancels.
- Putting a clinic coordinator on commission and getting a call from the regulator.

Option B, built into the deal model before you settle. Base small enough that cover stays above the line if they sell nothing for a quarter.

It depends on two things. Your buffer: if the deal has plenty of room above 1.5, a salary is survivable. And your market: if every good estimator in your trade expects a salary, you may have to pay one. Then pay less base, add a bonus on cash, and size the debt for it. Hire two if the cash flow allows, so you can compare them.

You can do this alone and learn it on the cash flow that's paying the seller. Or do it with me, and put the sales hire in the deal model before you close, so the business can carry it from day one.

On the call we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who wants a business that sells when you're not in the room, this is for you. Thirty days from now you're either still the only salesperson, or you've got a target, a pay plan built on cash, and a shortlist.

## EP 54 · Every level of ads for a boring business, from $0 to a full calendar

The cheapest lead is often the most expensive one you'll buy.

Every day someone's selling small businesses cheaper leads. "Leads for $10." "We'll fill your calendar." Then the calendar fills with people who don't show or can't pay.

In an aircon business, I'd pay up to $1,000 to win a customer worth $10,000, and not a dollar more. Here's every level of ads for a boring business, and the number that tells you when to stop.

A business that can buy customers at a known cost has upside most sellers don't price. And one that can show cost per job and revenue by channel is a better story for a bank and for the next buyer. This is for people buying their first business, or who just have, and want growth they control. It isn't for anyone who wants to boost a post and hope, or a business that can't handle more work yet.

Price your ads from what a customer is worth, then measure every dollar to cash.

Five levels. Each one has to work before you climb to the next. If ads have burned you before, it had nothing to do with your industry or your budget. It had everything to do with being sold the wrong number. Somebody measured leads. Nobody measured jobs.

I help people buy profitable, boring businesses with little to no money down, then build growth they control. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1. Strangers buy.**

Before a dollar on ads: when a stranger calls, do you win the job? If you only win work from mates of existing customers, ads will only make that louder. When ads fail, the cause is usually the offer, not the ad. Fix the offer first. And fix speed to lead from episode 51, or you'll pay for leads that ring out.

**Level 2. The ceiling.**

Work out the most you'll pay to win one customer, before you spend. I work it off gross profit, not revenue, because in a trade materials and labour eat a big chunk of every job.

Lifetime value, times gross margin, gives lifetime gross profit. I cap acquisition cost at about a quarter of that. Then work backwards through your win rate on quotes and your booking rate on enquiries. That gives you a maximum cost per enquiry, and anyone selling you leads has to beat it.

Lifetime value is install plus servicing plus the next replacement. Use what the business's records show, not what you hope. If the seller can't tell you what a customer is worth over five years, pull the customer list and work it out in due diligence.

**Level 3. Search, for urgent jobs.**

A blocked drain or a dead aircon in January, people search. They're comparing two or three firms right now. Google search ads and your Google Business profile matter here. You're paying for intent, and you're competing on speed and reviews. Episode 55. Search clicks cost more, but the person is ready to buy today. For an urgent trade, this is usually the first dollar I'd spend, in a tight radius, on the jobs with the best margin, not every job the business does.

**Level 4. Meta, for planned jobs.**

A new kitchen, solar, a ducted system. People often don't know they're in the market yet. That's where Facebook and Instagram work, in a radius around the business.

And the market has moved. On Meta now, the ad itself does most of the targeting. The platform reads what's in it to decide who sees it. Fiddling with interests matters less. So the ad should sound like your customers. Name the suburb. Show the van. Show the actual team. Say what the job is, never who the person is. No "struggling with…" call-outs. In health, advertising rules are stricter again.

**Level 5. Measured to cash.**

Every enquiry gets a source: a tracking number per channel, a source field on the form, and "how did you hear about us?" at the counter. Once a week, one table by channel: spend, enquiries, quotes booked, jobs won, cash collected.

Set the test budget and the kill rule before you start. Write them down. Give each ad enough money to produce a few enquiries at your ceiling. At $125 an enquiry, that's about $500 an ad. Nothing booked by then, turn it off. Then judge on cash collected, not leads, not clicks, not reach.

A cheap lead that never books has an infinite cost per job. A $200 lead that books half the time is cheaper. That's the whole lesson of this episode in two sentences.

The first ad money in a 3C deal often comes from a working capital facility negotiated at settlement. Cam, a good friend of mine, did two deals in 23 days with 100% vendor finance and got $500,000 of working capital from the bank.

That's borrowed money, and the same cash flow pays the seller. Test small, measure to cash, keep 1.5 DSCR in front of you.

Ten customers a month at the ceiling is $10,000 of spend for $40,000 of lifetime gross profit. A $10 lead that never books is worth nothing at any price.

Where people lose money.

- A 12-month agency contract that reports clicks and reach, never jobs.
- The working capital spent in a quarter, with no idea which job came from where.
- One bad week, and "ads don't work in my industry".
- Ads switched on before anyone answers the phone in five minutes.

Or you can keep doing what most do: rent growth from an agency and hope. Or do it with me, and build the acquisition numbers into the deal model, so you know your ceiling before you settle.

If you're someone who'd rather own the growth than rent it, this is for you. Thirty days from now you're either still in the same job reading about other people's businesses, or you've got a target business in view and a ceiling for the first ad dollar.

## EP 55 · 40 reviews vs 400: the asset nobody prices when they buy a business

Two businesses in the same suburb make the same profit. One has 40 reviews and one has 400.

They are not the same business. One buys customers cheaply because strangers already trust it. The other pays full price for every job.

There's a well-kept secret in buying small businesses. You buy years of happy customers, and almost nobody turns them into proof. I'll show you what that's worth, and the five habits that build it.

Accounts don't show reputation, so most buyers don't price it, and most owners never build it. That's the opportunity. If you're putting capital into a business, proof makes every ad, quote and call from this act convert better, and a buyer at the exit can see it in two minutes on Google. This isn't for anyone thinking of faking reviews. That's how you lose a reputation, and possibly end up in front of a regulator.

Proof is borrowed trust. A stranger who's never heard of you borrows it from the neighbours who left a review.

I know how borrowing works. At 25 I had no track record, so I borrowed credibility. I built a board of credible operators, and banks and sellers took me seriously. Proof does the same job for a business.

If a business has 40 reviews after 20 years, it has nothing to do with bad work or unhappy customers. It has everything to do with nobody asking at the right moment.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down, then grow them and make them worth more. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Ask at the win.**

The job's done, the customer's happy, the hot water's back on. That's when you ask. Not in a survey three months later. A text with a direct review link, before the van leaves the street. In a clinic, it's the moment a patient says "I can finally lift my grandkid".

Give the crew a wins chat. Every good job, a photo. Collecting proof afterwards is nearly impossible. Customers move on, change numbers, forget. Collect it at the win or not at all.

Then count. Reviews a month is the number. If the business does 200 jobs a month and gets three reviews, nobody's asking.

**Record the before.**

A result needs something to compare against. A before photo, same angle, every job. For a commercial cleaner, the audit score before you took the contract. For a clinic, the patient's own measures at the first visit, kept in their record, with consent. Privacy rules apply to health information.

**Package it in three seconds.**

Name and suburb, the problem, the fix, one result. "Sarah, Ringwood. Ducted system that kept tripping. Installed in a day. Power bill down." With her permission. The same result can land several ways: a dollar figure, a percentage, a time saved, or a before and after. "Saved 30% on power" moves one customer. "Done in a day" moves another. One two-minute video of a happy long-term customer, filmed once, can sit in quotes, ads and the website for years.

**Match proof to the objection.**

Worried about mess, show the clean-up photo. Worried about price, the power bill. Worried about trust, the review count. And never hand out customers' phone numbers as references. Tell them you won't give theirs out either.

**Guard it.**

The objections you never hear hurt most. People see one bad review and never ring. The owner answers bad reviews in public. Calm, specific, no arguing. A thoughtful reply often does more than another five stars, because the next customer reads how you handle a problem.

And stay inside the rules. Platforms like Google generally don't allow incentives for reviews, and consumer law goes after fake or cherry-picked ones. Reward the team for asking every customer, never pay a customer for a good review, never hide the bad ones. In Australia, health practitioners are generally restricted from using testimonials in advertising, so a physio can't run patient stories as ads the way a plumber can.

You don't need a track record to start, either. Jy Kingston, who I mentor, came from IT with no finance experience. He built his board in under 30 days through LinkedIn. He didn't wait for proof. He borrowed it. When you buy a business, it's easier still. You've bought years of finished jobs. Most of those customers were never asked.

[JOSH: one line on what proof the physio clinic had when you bought it, and what you did with it]

Same spend, same team, eight more jobs a month. And the next buyer looks you up before they look at the accounts.

Here's the test. Search the business name on your phone, the way a stranger would. Rating, count, date of the last review, and whether the owner replies. If you wouldn't ring it, neither will they.

Reviews are the cheapest marketing a business will ever have. Most owners just never ask.

Where people lose money.

- They rebrand in month one and lose the name 300 reviews were attached to.
- They copy a review-gating trick and break the platform's rules.
- They leave reputation off the due diligence list, so they can't price it or protect it through the handover.

Or you can keep buying on the accounts alone, and pay full price for every customer. Or do it with me: we list proof and reputation as assets in due diligence, so you protect them through the handover and build on them from day one.

On the call we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. My first deal was a physio clinic, 100% vendor finance, no deposit, at 25, after four failed deals.

If you're someone who wants your capital working in a real business, and would rather build trust than buy it, this is for you. Thirty days from now you're either still reading listings, or you've got a target business and a due diligence list that includes its reputation.

# Act 12 · Grow: team and operations

## EP 56 · Owner-run vs GM-run: what a $150K manager does to a $600K business

Owning a small business is great until about 20 staff. Then you go from owner to bottleneck.

Here's the bit most owners miss. A business making $600,000 with the owner running it is really a $450,000 business. Every bank and every buyer does that sum.

So the GM isn't a cost you add later. You're already carrying it. I'll cost owner-run against GM-run, give you a test, and tell you which one to buy.

This shows up in two places. Debt cover, because the seller and the lender are paid out of the business. And exit value, because the next buyer pays a lower multiple for a business that leaves with its owner. This is for owners who want a second business, or want to sell the first one properly. It's not for someone who wants to buy a job and do it forever. That's a fine life. It's not what we're building.

One line: a buyer pays for the profit left after someone is paid to run the business.

Two options. Option A, owner-run. Option B, GM-run. I'll cost both, then give you the test and the verdict.

You were told owner-operated is the cheap model. You were told you hire a manager when you're drowning. You were told a GM is overhead. None of that holds once you look at the business the way a bank does.

And if you're the person everyone waits on, it has nothing to do with being a control freak or bad at delegating. It has everything to do with the order the business was built in. One person did every job first, and nobody wrote down what that person does.

I help owners buy businesses with little to no money down and set them up to run without the owner. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A: owner-run.**

It looks cheapest. No manager on the payroll. Profit on the P&L is $600,000.

But your hours are a salary nobody's paying. A bank or buyer asks one question: who runs this when you're gone? If the answer is nobody, they cost in a manager. Then they discount again for the risk.

That's two hits. Profit drops by the cost of a manager. And the multiple drops, because every big customer and every hard decision sits with one person. That's key-person risk.

The rule: if your name is on every decision, your real profit is the P&L minus a GM.

**Option B: GM-run.**

The GM costs $150,000, illustrative. Profit is $450,000. On paper it looks worse.

But $450,000 is what the buyer was going to underwrite anyway. Now there's no key-person discount on top, so the multiple goes up.

It also changes the deal you're buying. In a 3C deal, free cash flow has to cover debt payments at least 1.5 times. Say the payments to the seller and the bank are $300,000 a year. Owner-run, $600,000 covers that twice. Add the GM and it's 1.5. Right on the floor.

So you have two choices. Structure the deal so the payments fit with the GM already in: a longer term, a payment holiday, a lower price. Or plan the GM for year two, once profit has grown. What doesn't work is pretending you'll run it yourself forever.

**Making the hire.**

Three steps, in order.

Scorecard first. One page. The job in one sentence: runs the day-to-day so the business hits its numbers without the owner. Then three to five numbers. For a trades business or a clinic: gross margin on jobs, jobs done on time, staff turnover, debtor days, one customer number. Write it before the job ad. A manager who writes their own scorecard writes one they can hit.

Search inside first. In the 3C process you meet the staff before you close, so you already know who people go to when something breaks. That person might be your GM now, or in a year. If not, hire from a bigger, more structured business. Give the finalists last month's numbers and ask what they'd fix first. One hour of that beats three interviews.

Hand over to the GM, not to you. The seller's transition period is written into the deal. Most buyers use it to learn the business themselves. That's backwards if you want a second one. Hand the top 20 customers, the key suppliers and the staff who only listen to the old owner to the GM. Make the list. Tick it off.

**The test and the verdict.**

The test: if you left for four weeks, would gross margin, on-time jobs and cash collection hold? If not, you own an owner-run business, whatever the org chart says.

The verdict: buy owner-run, run GM-run. Owner-run businesses are cheaper to buy because of the key-person risk. Your job is to remove it. That spread is the money.

Saul, one of our Academy members, is in early due diligence on exactly this shape. Electrical and aircon, 25 staff, about $3.8 million revenue, owner retiring in two years.

I won't say what Saul will do. But in that shape the GM question comes first. Whoever runs it when the owner goes has to be named, scored and in place before the owner walks out.

[JOSH: who runs day-to-day in your healthcare group, how you found them, and one thing you'd do differently]

Same profit, same GM cost in the end. The GM-run version is worth about $675,000 more, because the buyer isn't pricing in your absence.

Where people lose money.

- Leaving the GM out of the deal model. Cover reads 2.0 and is really 1.5. One bad quarter and you're under the floor.
- Letting the seller's relationships move to you. You've bought a job, and the multiple stays at the bottom of the range.
- Hiring the first confident person with no scorecard. A bad GM costs a year of salary and a year of drift.

You can do this alone. You close, step into the seller's shoes, and the GM never makes it into the numbers. Three years in you're still the only manager, and the bank and the next buyer both see it. Or you do it with someone who's already made these mistakes, and the GM goes into the structure before you sign.

The Academy is the community, the full course, every template and daily deal discussion. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you.

If you're someone who wants to own businesses, not be owned by one, and you'll write the scorecard before the job ad, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with a GM plan inside your deal.

## EP 57 · Every level from "in the owner's head" to sellable: 10 SOPs in 1 week (step-by-step)

There's a well-kept secret about small businesses. Most of them run on one person's memory.

The buyers who know it record the ten jobs that matter in one week, and turn four cents of every revenue dollar into profit. The rest spend three months on a binder nobody opens.

Here's every level from "it's in the owner's head" to sellable, and how to climb it in a week.

Three people pay for this. Your lender, who wants to know the business survives one person leaving. Your seller, who is leaving and carries half the business in their head. And your next buyer, who pays more for a business that can be handed over. If you're the only one who knows how things work, that feels like job security. To a buyer it looks like risk, and they price it.

One line: a process nobody can follow without you isn't a system, it's a liability.

There are four levels. Most businesses are stuck on Level 1.

People think systems mean a consultant, an operations manual and software that costs more than the person using it. That's not how it works. The person who does the job records it once, someone new tests it, and you track one number.

And if your business runs on your memory, it has nothing to do with being lazy or disorganised. It has everything to do with twenty years of being flat out doing the work, with nobody to write it down.

I help owners buy businesses with little to no money down and make them worth more once they own them. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1. In the owner's head.**

No documents. The owner prices the odd jobs, knows which supplier to ring when stock is short, knows which customers need a call instead of an email.

The trap: it walks out the door on the last day of the seller's transition. The one move that unlocks Level 2 is choosing the right ten jobs.

Sit with the team for an hour. Write every job that happens at least once a month. In a trades business: taking an enquiry, writing a quote, booking a job, ordering parts, invoicing, chasing a late payment, onboarding a new tech, a safety check. In a clinic: booking, intake, billing, recalls, rostering.

Score each one. How often, one to five. How bad when it goes wrong, one to five. Multiply. Your top ten is the week. The top of the list is usually money: quoting and invoicing.

**Level 2. Recorded.**

Don't write. Record. The person who actually does the job does it once while recording. Screen capture for computer work. A phone on a tripod for anything physical, with the person talking through what they're doing.

They know the shortcuts and the traps, and they'll own the result. Ask the best person first and give them the credit. The rest follow.

If you've just bought the business, put the seller first. Record the jobs only they do, before they go. How they price the odd jobs. The supplier they ring when stock is short. Which customers need a phone call, not an email. That's part of what you paid for.

**Level 3. Tested.**

Turn each recording into a one-page checklist. Numbered steps, a photo where it helps, the video link at the top. People watch the video once and use the checklist forever.

Then on Friday, give one checklist to someone who has never done that job. Watch. Don't help. Every time they stop, that's a missing step. Fix it, and it's done.

Add a second set of eyes on anything that leaves the building. In most small businesses that's quotes. Nothing over a set size goes out unchecked.

**Level 4. Measured.**

One number: labour cost per revenue dollar. As the business grows, it should fall. Not because anyone's pay is cut. Because new hires get productive faster and fewer jobs get redone.

If it's rising, you're adding people, not systems.

This is the level a buyer pays for. Level 1 says the business needs its owner. Level 4 proves it doesn't, with a number they can check every quarter.

Timing matters. My first deal, a physio clinic, came with a nine-month payment holiday. That's the window where this work gets done, before the first payment is due. Most businesses also have a slow month or two a year. That's your SOP month.

[JOSH: one process at the physio clinic or your healthcare group you documented early, and what changed]

Four cents on $2.4 million is $100,000 a year. In a business that sells on a multiple of profit, one week of recording is worth several times that at the exit.

Where people lose money.

- Writing a binder. Three months, nobody reads it, and the number never moves.
- Letting the owner write the SOPs. They skip the steps they do on instinct, which are the ones that matter.
- Buying software before you have ten processes. A shared folder and a video link is enough to start.
- Letting the seller's transition end before their jobs are recorded. That knowledge is part of what you paid for.

You can keep doing what most owners do. Keep promising yourself you'll document it next quarter. Keep being the person every question comes back to. Or you can plan the recording into your first 100 days, before you sign.

If you're someone who's tired of being the only one who knows how it works, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with ten jobs recorded and a business that doesn't need you in every room.

## EP 58 · $18,000 to keep your best person: 4 mistakes that lose key staff after you buy

How come buyers with capital, good advisers and clean numbers still lose their best people in the first six months?

They did the diligence. They matched the financials to the tax returns. Then the senior tech hands in his notice, and the debt payments don't move.

Here are the four mistakes that cause it, ranked, and the $18,000 fix.

When you buy a small business, you're buying a team. The cash flow that pays the seller and the bank is produced by a handful of people. Lose one of them in month two and your payments stay exactly where they were. This is for buyers close to a deal, or just closed. It's not for people who think staff come with the furniture. They can leave on a Friday.

One line: staff rarely leave because a business was sold. They leave because nobody told them what the sale means for them.

Most buyers think people leave over money. They think it's the new owner's personality. They think it's random, and you just replace them. That's not how it works. In a small business, people leave when the future goes quiet. The owner they knew is gone, nobody's said what happens next, and a competitor rings at the right moment.

So if you've lost good people after a change before, it had nothing to do with your offer or your personality. It had everything to do with the silence.

Four mistakes. The most expensive one is second.

I help buyers get into businesses with little to no money down and plan what happens to the people inside them. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Mistake 1. Not knowing who's key before you close.**

Small businesses usually have three kinds of key person. The one who holds the customers, because the big accounts ring them, not the owner. The one who holds the know-how, the senior tech or head clinician. And the one who holds the office together: rostering, invoicing, the supplier who only answers their calls.

In due diligence, ask the seller: "If these three left next month, what would happen?" Watch their face. Then meet those people yourself before you settle, and make employment agreements for them a condition of the deal.

When you buy a small business, you're buying that context. A new hire with a better CV takes a year to learn what these three already know. Don't let it walk out the week you take over.

**Mistake 2. Silence in week one.**

This is the expensive one. Settlement happens, the seller gives a two-minute speech, and nobody hears anything for a month.

Instead, sit with each key person one-on-one in the first week. Not an all-staff email. Four parts.

What's not changing: pay, role, who they report to. If you don't know yet, say when you will. What's in it for them: a bigger business, a path up, the bonus you're about to describe. How they'll hear things: a weekly catch-up, your mobile, whatever you'll actually keep doing. Then ask what worries them, and listen.

Anything that matters, say in person. In your first months every message gets read three times.

**Mistake 3. No retention bonus, or one paid up front.**

A key supervisor on $90,000. A retention bonus of 20%, $18,000. A third at 90 days, two thirds at 12 months.

It's weighted to the back for a reason. The first 90 days are when people decide whether they like the new owner. The 12-month payment carries them through the first full year, which is when your numbers get proven to the bank. Paid up front, it's just a gift.

Put it in writing in week one, in the same conversation as everything else. A bonus they hear about in month five doesn't stop the call from a competitor in month two.

The bonus can often be built into the deal structure rather than paid out of your pocket.

**Mistake 4. Getting equity wrong.**

For the one or two people who really carry the business, a small slice of equity or a profit share does what a bonus can't. It's the same idea as the board: in the 3C model, board members get founders' equity, usually 2 to 10% each, instead of cash. For staff it's smaller, and it vests over time.

The mistake is setting it up loosely. Minority holders get no veto and no voting control, and there's a drag-along from day one, so when you sell the group, everyone sells. If equity is too complex, a profit share on the operating company does most of the job.

And keep it small. Debt over dilution. I keep 73% of my company.

[JOSH: what you said to the staff in the first week at the physio clinic, whether anyone left, and what you'd change]

Do your own version. Even a rough one usually lands well above the bonus. And unlike the bonus, the loss lands in the same year your debt cover is being tested.

Where people lose money.

- Letting the seller tell the staff alone. Agree when and how, and be in the room.
- Promising "nothing will change" and then changing the roster in week two. You only get to break that once.
- Handing out equity without vesting or a drag-along. It's cheap now and expensive at the exit.

There are two paths. Alone, you focus on the bank and the lawyers, settlement happens, and by Christmas your best supervisor is working down the road. Or the staff plan, the bonuses and the agreements go into the deal before you sign.

If you're someone who knows the real asset in a small business walks in every morning, and you want it protected before you sign, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with your key people named, priced and planned for.

## EP 59 · A $3M, 20-person business on one page (copy this org chart)

There are only two types of small business. The ones with an org chart, and the ones with an owner and everyone pointing at them.

The first kind can be sold, bought or run from somewhere else. The second kind stops the day the owner gets sick, and a buyer prices it that way.

I'll put a $3 million, 20-person business on one page and read it the way a buyer does.

Every box with the owner's name in it is key-person risk, and key-person risk comes off the multiple. On $500,000 of profit, the difference between 3.5 and 4.5 times is half a million dollars. This is for owners and buyers who want the business to keep working when they're not in the room. If you enjoy being the answer to every question, keep it. You just can't buy a second business while you're doing it.

One line: every job in the business sits in one of three boxes, and each box needs one named owner who isn't you.

The three boxes from episode 29. Win the work. Do the work. Protect the business. A buyer reads the chart in four passes: the owner's name, the box owners, the numbers, the pay.

You were told a 20-person business is too small for an org chart. You were told the fix is one superhero manager. You were told the owner being across everything is a strength. To a buyer, it's the discount.

And if you're the one everyone points at, it has nothing to do with being bad at letting go. It has everything to do with how small businesses grow. The owner does every job first, then hires helpers, and nobody draws the boxes.

I help owners buy businesses with little to no money down and set them up to run without the owner. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Pass one: where is the owner's name?.**

Draw it. Win: the owner on the phone and in the ute quoting, an office person taking enquiries, one estimator. Do: a senior supervisor, two crew supervisors, around twelve techs and apprentices. Protect: a bookkeeper who also does payroll, an office admin, the external accountant and lawyer.

The owner sits at the top of Win, and probably at the top of Do, because every hard job ends up with them. That's normal for a retiring owner. It's also the first thing a buyer circles.

Saul, one of our Academy members, is in early due diligence on a business of this shape: electrical and aircon, 25 staff, about $3.8 million revenue, owner retiring in two years. These are round, made-up numbers, not his.

**Pass two: one owner per box.**

Each box gets one named person. Not a committee. Not "the owner and the supervisor."

The mistake here is hiring a superhero: one person who quotes, manages the crews, talks to the bank and fixes the van. You won't find them, and if you do, you've built the same risk with a new name.

Break the owner's job up instead. Billing and late payments go to Protect. Scheduling and parts go to a coordinator. The supervisor mostly supervises. The estimator quotes.

Add a role only when the work someone is covering becomes a full-time job. Until then, one name can sit in two small boxes, as long as it isn't yours.

**Pass three: three numbers per box.**

Win owns enquiries per week, quote-to-win rate and average job value. Do owns gross margin per job, jobs done on time and callbacks. Protect owns debtor days, payroll on time and accurate, and licences current.

Nine numbers, each with a name next to it. Same idea as the Monday numbers from episode 30.

**Pass four: pay by box.**

Win and Do are where profit is made, so pay them partly on results. A base plus a bonus on won work for the estimator. A base plus a margin or on-time bonus for supervisors. Protect is hard to measure fairly, so it's salaried. Pay on margin and on-time, not volume, or you'll get busy and broke.

And build in order. Never let Win outrun Do. A great estimator who doubles the work won will break the crews if delivery isn't ready.

**Where the board fits.**

In a 3C deal, the board and the external team cover a lot of Protect. But a board isn't a box owner. Every box needs a named person inside the operating company, with the board above it.

I built my board through LinkedIn and cold outreach, and I keep 73% of my company. That board means I'm never the only brain in the room. It doesn't replace someone running each box day to day.

[JOSH: how your healthcare group splits Win, Do and Protect today, and who owns each, by role]

Same profit. The only change is whose name is in the boxes, and it's worth $500,000.

Where people lose money.

- Putting your own name where the seller's was and calling it temporary. Three years later it's still there, and so is the discount.
- Paying the estimator on volume. You win the jobs that lose money.
- Counting the board as a box owner. The bank and the buyer can tell the difference.

You can keep doing what you're doing. Keep taking every call. Keep quoting every job. Keep being the reason the business can't be sold. Or you can draw the one-page chart before you settle and plan who takes each box.

If you're someone who's built a business that needs you, and you're ready to own one that doesn't, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with your name out of two boxes and a plan for the next business.

## EP 60 · The $90,000 mistake new owners make in week two

James buys a business and changes everything in week two. New software, new roster, a big speech about growth. By Christmas his two best people have gone.

Sam buys the same kind of business and changes almost nothing for 90 days. Fixes the one thing everyone hated. Keeps every key person.

Same business, same debt. James's version costs about $90,000 more in year one and drops below the bank's floor. Here's why the fastest new owner is usually the poorest.

In a deal funded by the seller and the bank, the debt is paid from what the staff do every day. Everything in this act, the GM, the SOPs, the key people, the org chart, depends on them staying. This is for anyone about to walk into a business someone else built. It's not for people who want to stride in on day one and fix everything. That's the most expensive way to lose the staff you just paid for.

One line: in a small business the culture is usually one person, the owner. When they leave, it leaves with them, unless you replace it with rules people can see.

If you've watched a workplace change hands and fall apart, it had nothing to do with difficult staff or an old-fashioned business. It had everything to do with the rules living in one person's head.

Think of moving into a share house. You don't rearrange the kitchen on day one. You learn where things go, fix the tap that's been dripping for a year, and then you earn a say.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down and take them over without breaking them. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Why people believe the opposite.**

New owners feel they have to prove something. They've paid for it, the bank is watching, and change feels like progress. In a corporate job, a new boss announces a plan in week one.

But a 20-person business isn't a division. Every change lands on someone by name. The tech whose van setup you moved. The office manager whose system you replaced. They don't see a plan. They see that the place isn't theirs any more.

**The first all-hands.**

Do it in the first week, with the seller in the room. The seller introduces you. That handshake does more than anything you'll say.

Then five minutes, not fifty.

Who you are, briefly, and why you bought this business. What's not changing: pay, jobs, who people report to, the name on the van. Be specific, because people fill silence with the worst story. What will change and when: "nothing big for 90 days while I learn how you do things" is a good answer. How they'll hear things, and then keep doing it. Take questions, and answer the hard ones straight.

**The test for every change.**

Before you change anything, ask: does a customer feel this, or does a staff member love it? If yes to either, leave it for 90 days. The Friday barbecue. The way the senior tech sets up the van. These cost you nothing, and they're how people know it's still their workplace.

What you fix early is what everyone already hates. The broken tool. The roster that never works. The invoice system that makes people chase their own pay. Fixing one in month one buys more trust than any speech.

Then replace the owner's head with standards people can see. Set each one from your best real performer. "Our best supervisor gets 95% of jobs done on time. That's the standard." People accept a standard someone in the room already hits.

And own everything that goes wrong in your first months, even when the seller caused it. The team is watching who takes the blame. If it's you, they'll start bringing you problems early, while they're still cheap.

**The proof.**

The usual objection is "I've never run a team, and half the staff are older than me." When I bought my first business, a physio clinic, I was 25 with zero experience. I pulled $150,000 out in year one. That only works if the team keeps doing the work while the new owner learns.

[JOSH: what your first week as the new owner of the physio clinic looked like, and one thing you deliberately didn't change]

The $90,000 gap is close to a fifth of profit. It's the difference between above the 1.5 floor and under it, in the year the bank is watching hardest.

Where people lose money.

- Changing software or the roster in the first month. You pay for it in resignations, not licence fees.
- Sending anything that matters by text or email. Say it in person.
- Losing your temper over a botched job in week three. You teach the whole team to hide problems.

That's Act 12. Before it, the team was what happened after you closed. Now you know it's part of the deal: a GM in the model, ten jobs recorded, key people kept, one page of who owns what, and a calm first all-hands.

Two paths. Alone, you walk in with a plan for everything and the people who carried the business decide it isn't theirs. Or you plan the first all-hands, the first 90 days and the staff conversations before you settle.

On the call we work out your strategy, then your first structure, then whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy is the community, the full course, every template and daily deal discussion. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you.

If you've spent years under the new boss who changed everything in week two, you already know which kind of owner people work harder for. If you're someone who'll listen first and change things second, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with a first all-hands written, a 90-day plan, and a deal you can walk into.

# Act 13 · Scale: build the group

## EP 61 · Every group structure, from one company to a $1M-profit holdco

One business is easy to own. You know every number and every person.

Until you buy the second one. Then a structure you never thought about decides two numbers: how much the bank lends you, and what a buyer pays for the lot.

In the example I'll run today, that's the gap between four million and eight million. Same businesses. Different paperwork.

This is for owners who plan to own more than one business. If you'll only ever own one, a single company may be fine, and your accountant can tell you. Everyone else: the structure is cheap to set up before deal two and expensive to fix after deal four. It's also the first thing a lender and a buyer's diligence team look at.

The whole idea in one line: one deal, one company, with a holding company on top.

There are four levels of structure, and most owners are stuck on level two without knowing it.

If your first business went into whatever company was handy, that has nothing to do with how careful you are. It has everything to do with timing. Nobody plans for deal five while they're scrambling to close deal one.

I help owners set this up, from a first deal with little to no money down to a group a buyer will pay a premium for. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1: the partnership.**

You and the business are the same thing legally. Every debt and every claim can reach you.

Rule: don't buy a business this way. Ever.

**Level 2: one company.**

You own a company, the company owns the business. Fine for one deal.

The trap is deal two. Buy the second business into the same company and both now share one set of debts, one set of books and one set of risks. The bank sees one pile and takes security over the whole pile. A bad year in business two can now reach business one.

Most people think structure is admin you sort out after the deal. That's not how it works. Lenders price your structure. Buyers price it. It's part of the deal.

**Level 3: holdco plus one company per business.**

This is where most groups should live.

A holding company sits on top. It's the permanent vehicle. Your board's founders' equity sits here. Every business you buy goes into its own new company underneath, with its own bank account, its own lender and its own seller note.

Why the lender cares: they want to see this business's profit paying this business's debt. Ring-fenced, they can lend to that company alone and take security over its assets, not over everything you own. Don't sign a guarantee where one company stands behind another's debt unless your lawyer has read it. That one clause turns five ring-fenced companies back into one pile.

Why the seller cares: in a vendor-finance deal, the seller's note sits in the company that owns their old business, limited to that company where you can get it, and behind the bank. Your lawyer will call that non-recourse and subordination. Ask them to show you both on your own structure.

Why the buyer cares: clean pieces. A buyer can take one business or all of them. Tangled, their diligence team spends months unpicking shared accounts, and every question they can't answer comes off the price.

**Level 4: the octopus.**

Under the holdco you run arms. One per region or one per industry, each with its own manager, its own businesses, sometimes its own lenders. Lose an arm, the animal lives.

You won't need it for a while. The rule is to build level three so it can grow into level four without being torn down.

**Day-one settings.**

Two things to set up before anyone else owns a share.

Issue plenty of shares. A million, not a hundred. It's far easier to hand a board member ten thousand out of a million than to reissue later.

Set minority rules from the start. Drag-along, so a minority can't block a sale of the group. No veto and no voting control for minorities. Board members, sellers rolling over equity and managers all sign up to the same agreement.

That's how I've done it. My board sits at the top. The founder of the Australian Institute of Fitness is a shareholder. I keep 73% of my company. **[JOSH: one line on how your healthcare group is structured, e.g. holdco and number of operating companies, or cut.]**

The multiple gap is the roll-up. Buy at around four times, combine and grow, sell the group at eight, ten, sometimes fifteen. It only shows up if a buyer can see clean, comparable numbers. The structure is how you keep them that way.

Private equity is the buyer at the top of that ladder. My friend Jason Nagy's first deal was a five and a half million dollar radiology business, zero down. Seven years later he sold to private equity for two hundred million. Buyers at that level don't untangle shared accounts for you. They price the mess, or they walk.

Where people lose money.

- Business two goes into the same company because it was quicker. Cost: security over everything, and a buyer discount at exit.
- Cross-guarantees signed at settlement without a lawyer reading them. Cost: one bad business can take down the rest.
- Shares handed to a board member with no shareholders' agreement. Cost: a minority who can block the sale of the whole group.
- Fees between companies with no real service behind them. Cost: a tax problem. Management fees need real services at a fair price; your accountant decides the detail.

You can work this out alone. Most people who do it alone find out at the exit, when the buyer's lawyers send back forty questions about shared accounts. Or you can set it up once, with someone who's already made these calls.

On a call with me we look at what you own today, what you want to own, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy gives you the community, the full course and every template. 1:1 is me working your structure and your lender presentations with you.

If you're someone who plans to own more than one business, and you'd rather set it up right once than fix it four times, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older either way. Either with the same tangled page, or with a structure your lawyer is building and a plan for deal two.

## EP 62 · Centralise vs keep local: the $50K back-office decision

Three businesses. Three bookkeepers, three payroll systems, three insurance brokers.

How come owners with three good businesses often feel busier than when they had one? Because they're paying for the same work three times, and they're the glue holding it together.

In a three-business group, fixing that is worth about fifty thousand a year. At exit, about three hundred thousand. Customers never notice.

This is for people who own two or more businesses, or are about to. The saving looks small at three. At five it's real money, because the same shared team absorbs each new business for a small extra cost. And at sale, a buyer sees one finance function and one set of books instead of five little kingdoms. If you're hoping a back office will fix a business that doesn't make money, it won't. Fix that first.

One line: centralise what customers never feel, keep local what they pay for.

So every function in your group is Option A, centralise, or Option B, keep local. I'll give you both, the test, and the order.

If your back office is a mess right now, that has nothing to do with discipline. It has everything to do with how you got here: one deal at a time, each business arriving with its own setup.

I help owners build groups like this, starting from a first business bought with little to no money down. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A: centralise.**

You were told every business needs its own bookkeeper, its own office manager doing payroll, its own broker. That was never a rule. It's how a standalone business has to run. Inside a group, it's a choice.

Centralise anything that's the same job in every business. Bookkeeping. Payroll. HR admin. Insurance. Compliance. Software. Buying.

One accounting system. One chart of accounts, so wages are wages and rent is rent in every business. One person or firm closing the month for all of them. That matters more than the saving. Until every business reports the same way, you can't compare their margins, and you can't see which one is quietly losing money. One payroll, with one set of contracts and policies, adjusted where the law or your industry's award needs it. One broker. Group buying on fuel, consumables, phones and software seats. Suppliers give better terms to a bigger customer, and now you are one.

**Option B: keep local.**

Keep anything a customer would notice changing. The person who answers the phone. The tech on the tools. The clinician. The local name on the van.

That's what you bought. Don't rename a forty-year-old local business after your holdco because it looks tidy on a slide. Centralise the engine behind marketing if you like, one person running ads and reviews for every business, but the customer still sees the local name.

**The test.**

Ask one question of every function: would a customer notice if this changed tomorrow?

No: centralise it. Yes: leave it local.

Then a second question for anything you centralise: will the person who uses the tool help pick it? Let the people who'll use a system test two options before you buy. Don't put the fanciest software on a twelve-person plumbing business because it looked good in a demo.

The usual pushback is "my businesses are too different to share anything." Every business pays wages, does its books and buys insurance. Start there. The other one is "my staff will hate it." Some will, if you do it in one go without telling them. Do it one function at a time and explain why.

**The order.**

Books first. You can't run a group whose numbers arrive in five different shapes. Then payroll and HR, where staff notice change, so tell them what's changing and what isn't before you do it. Then insurance and buying. Marketing last, and carefully.

Verdict: both, in that order, one function at a time. Not before you have two businesses running steadily. Never all four in the same month.

Fifty thousand back in profit, plus one set of numbers you can trust. And notice it isn't huge on day one. That's normal. The bigger win comes at business four and five. A fourth business on its own would need its own bookkeeper and payroll. Inside the group, the same team takes it on with a small top-up, not a new hire. Every dollar it doesn't spend on back office is a dollar of profit, and every dollar of profit is six at exit.

Groups often charge this through a management fee from the holdco. It has to cover real services at a fair price, and how it's taxed depends on where you are.

Where people lose money.

- Centralising everything in one month. Cost: every business stops working properly for a month, and staff leave.
- Buying software because a friend uses it. Cost: a migration, then a rollback, paid twice.
- Taking the one competent office manager out without a plan. Cost: the hundred small things she did that nobody wrote down.
- Stripping the local touch. Cost: the customers you paid for when you bought the business.

You can carry on as you are. Keep paying for the same work three times. Keep reconciling three sets of books on a Sunday. Keep a group that, at sale time, looks like five small businesses in a trench coat.

Or you can fix it one function at a time, in the right order. On a call with me we look at what you own, what the group should look like, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory is the better fit. The Academy has people who've already set up a shared back office you can ask. 1:1 is me working your structure with you.

If you're someone who owns more than one business and you're tired of being the glue, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either still paying for three of everything, or with the books already moving into one system.

## EP 63 · This $150K bolt-on adds $1.2M to the group (teardown)

A business making a hundred and fifty thousand a year, owner retiring, nobody else bidding.

There's a well-kept secret in buying businesses: a lot of a group's value is built with deals like this. Small, boring and ignored. On its own it's a job. Bolted onto a group, it can be worth one point two million.

I'll tear one down in the order a lender sees it.

This is for people who own at least one solid business, the platform, or have capital looking for cash flow. If you haven't bought business one yet, watch this for where it goes, then start at deal one. If you're collecting tiny businesses for the sake of a count, this isn't that. Done badly, bolt-ons drown your best manager and turn a group into a pile of problems.

One line: buy small at a low multiple, make it part of a bigger business, and the bigger business sells at a higher one.

A bolt-on is a small business you add to a platform. A platform is the business everything plugs into: a manager who isn't you, clean monthly numbers, and a back office with spare capacity.

If you've been chasing one big deal and getting outbid, that has nothing to do with your offer or your nerve. It has everything to do with where you've been looking. The big deals are where the crowd is.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down, then build them into groups like this. Book a call below. Now the teardown.

**The target.**

Your platform makes a million in profit. A business across town in the same trade makes a hundred and fifty thousand. The owner is sixty-eight. No successor.

What makes it a good bolt-on: same industry, so your staff understand the work and your back office understands the paperwork. Same region, so staff can move between sites. An owner who'll stay for a handover, because small-business customers often buy from the owner, not the brand.

How you find them: map every business in your trade within an hour's drive. Mark the owner-run ones with an owner over sixty. Call five this week and ask one question: "Have you ever thought about selling?" That's the profile. One of our Owners Academy members found an electrical and air-conditioning business that way, direct to the owner by cold call: twenty-five staff, about three point eight million in revenue, owner retiring in two years. He's in early due diligence now.

Same diligence as any deal. Three years of financials, matching tax returns, revenue by year, before you make an offer. Small books are usually messier. Expect to adjust.

**The price.**

Three times profit. Four hundred and fifty thousand.

You price it on the business as it stands today. Never on what it'll be worth inside your group. The seller gets paid for what they built, not for your back office.

Most people believe the value is in finding a bigger deal. The roll-up says otherwise. Buy businesses at around four times profit, or three for small ones. Combine and grow them. Sell the group at eight, ten, sometimes fifteen. Small businesses sell cheap because most buyers ignore them. Bigger, cleaner groups sell high because they're more stable. You're buying at one price and selling at another.

**The structure.**

Paid over time, with a payment holiday up front, the way you did deal one. Say about a hundred thousand a year to the seller.

Now the lender's first check. A hundred and fifty thousand of profit covers a hundred thousand of payments one and a half times. On its own numbers. If it only works because the platform's cash props it up, cross it off.

Two common patterns. Buy the assets, not the shares, so old liabilities stay behind. And if you're unsure how many customers stay once the owner leaves, tie part of the price to it: an earn-out on revenue from existing customers over the next year or two.

Where it sits, its own company under the holdco or folded into the platform, depends on the lender, the tax position and how you'll sell. Decide that with your accountant and lawyer before the LOI.

**Inside the group.**

It closes. It joins the shared back office. Its own bookkeeper, software and broker fall away. It picks up group buying. Say that's fifty thousand of costs it no longer needs. It now makes two hundred.

The group goes from one million to one point two million.

You agree four hundred and fifty, paid out of the business's own profit, for something worth one point two million at exit. None of it is guaranteed. The group multiple only shows up if the group is clean, growing and runs without you.

Where people lose money.

- Paying the seller for synergies. Cost: your margin of safety, handed over at signing.
- Buying faster than the platform can absorb. Cost: integration falls behind, the manager drowns, the platform's profit slips.
- Drifting into another industry because the price looked cheap. Cost: no shared back office, no shared staff, no group multiple.
- Leaning on platform cash to cover a bolt-on below 1.5×. Cost: one bad year in a small business hits the big one.

You can do this alone and learn each of those mistakes at full price. Or you can have someone check every bolt-on against the rules before you sign.

On a call with me we'll look at your platform, your bolt-on list and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 fits. In the Academy you can put a bolt-on in front of people who've done them. In 1:1, I work the structure and the lender presentation with you, deal by deal. The NBIO template you get when you join is the one you'll use on every bolt-on.

If you're someone with a platform, or capital looking for one, and you'd rather buy small and well than big and contested, this is for you.

Thirty days from now, you're either still waiting for the big deal, or you've got a scored bolt-on list and your first offer out.

## EP 64 · 4 mistakes when buying your competitor (the $1.05M deal)

Two owners. Same town, same trade.

James runs more ads, sends more quotes and works more hours every year, and still wins half his jobs against the business across the road on price. Lorenzo doesn't cut his price or work more hours. A few years ago he bought the business across the road.

In the deal I'll run today, that's one point oh five million, paid over time, for about three hundred and ninety thousand a year of extra profit. And four ways to wreck it.

Your competitor is the one business you already know almost everything about. That makes it the easiest deal to value. It also makes it the easiest to wreck, because they know you too. This is for owners with a running business who've thought, "I should just buy that lot down the road." If you want to buy them to punish them, stop. You'll overpay.

One line: approach carefully, price on their profit as it stands, and plan the people before you close.

Most owners think the way to beat a competitor is to outwork them. In a boring local industry, with owners retiring and no successors, that's not how it works. Buying them gets cheap when you pay over time.

If you've been fighting the same rival for years, that has nothing to do with your effort. It has everything to do with a market that has two of you in it.

Here are the four mistakes, ranked by what they cost.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down, including the one across the road. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Mistake 4: handing over your secrets.**

Competitors know each other. Same suppliers, same association, same gossip. They'll take your call. They'll also wonder what you're after.

Be direct: "We've both been at this a while. Have you ever thought about selling? If you have, I'd like to be the first call."

Then protect yourself. Mutual NDA before numbers move either way. Ask for their three years of financials, matching tax returns and revenue by year. Release yours in stages. Before an LOI they need to know you can fund and close. They don't need your customer list, your pricing or your staff names.

A competitor who stalls on the NDA, then wants to "just have a coffee and compare notes", may be fishing. In some industries and sizes, a competitor deal can raise competition-law questions. Ask your lawyer early.

**Mistake 3: losing the people.**

You'll have two of some roles. Two office managers. Two senior techs who both think they're the senior tech.

Decide before you close who stays and in which role. Meet their key people before settlement, with the seller's agreement. For the ones you can't lose, a retention bonus at around ninety days and again at a year is common. Small next to losing the person every customer asks for. Your staff are watching how you treat theirs. So is the town.

Then the culture. You're not just changing owners. You're merging two teams who spent years competing for the same jobs. Pick one way of doing things, explain why, and give it time. The staff are most of what you're buying.

**Mistake 2: assuming every customer stays.**

Some customers used both of you on purpose, so they always had a second option. They'll find a new one.

Ask for revenue by customer under the NDA and match it against yours at the right stage, with your accountant handling the comparison if the seller's nervous. Revenue from customers you already serve is worth less to you than it looks. And if one big customer is a large share of both businesses, the merged business has a bigger concentration problem than either did alone.

**Mistake 1: paying for your own savings.**

This is the expensive one.

Price the deal on their profit as it stands. Three to five times. Count only the savings you control: one premises instead of two, one back office, one set of software and insurance. Treat extra sales from cross-selling as a bonus. Never in the price, never in the lender's model.

Check cover on their numbers alone at one and a half times, before any savings. The savings are your margin of safety, not your plan. Paid over time, payment holiday if you can get it, its own company under your holdco.

And never say "vendor finance" to them. Say "getting paid over time". A retiring owner who doesn't want to wait sixteen weeks for a bank often prefers a buyer who knows the trade, will look after the staff, and can close in weeks. If they won't move on price, your best tactic is the one most owners won't use: walk away. Second best is a time limit on the offer.

Still a good deal after the customer loss. But if you'd paid the seller for your hundred and forty thousand of savings, it wouldn't be.

Where people lose money.

- Numbers handed over at a coffee, nothing back. Cost: your pricing in their hands, no deal.
- Savings in the price. Cost: you pay twice for value you created.
- Every customer assumed to stay. Cost: a price built on revenue that leaves.
- Staff hear the news from the rumour mill. Cost: the senior tech walks, and his customers follow.

You can keep quoting against them on every job for another ten years. Or you can plan the approach, the price and the people with someone who's done it, before you pick up the phone.

On a call with me we look at your market, the competitor and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. In the Academy you can sanity-check an approach before you make it. In 1:1, I work the structure and lender presentation with you, including how to approach them without giving the game away.

If you're someone who'd rather own the competition than outwork it, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you're either still quoting against them, or you've had the first conversation and an NDA is on the table.
