## EP 65 · Why the bank's best offer at deal three is the dangerous one

On deal one, nobody would lend to you. By deal three, the bank wants to lend to everything.

There are two types of owners who get there. The ones who use that offer to build the group, and the ones whose group gets tied in a knot by it.

In the example I'll run, one business losing a hundred thousand in a bad year puts the whole group in breach. The difference is one clause.

This is for owners with two or more businesses, or one solid one and a pipeline. The bank's group offer usually looks better on every line: bigger limit, lower rate, one facility. The cost sits in the security clause, and most owners don't read it until something goes wrong. If you're still on deal one, go back to the Capital episodes first.

One line: the rules don't change at deal three, but the bank's offer tries to change them.

Every deal still covers its own debt at one and a half times, on its own numbers. Vendor finance is still the first tool. What changes is that a lender will now look at the whole group, and that's a trade.

If it feels like you don't have the capital to buy more, that has nothing to do with your savings or your salary. It has everything to do with what your businesses can now prove.

I help owners buy businesses with little to no money down and then finance the group as it grows, without tying it in knots. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Why the bank says yes now.**

You were told the bank decides how big you get, and that each new deal needs more of your own money. That's not how a group gets funded. The businesses fund the businesses.

On deal one you borrowed credibility from a board. By deal three you've got two businesses of monthly numbers, a lender who's watched you hit them, and sellers who've been paid on time. That's why deal three usually moves faster than deal one.

My friend Cam did two deals in twenty-three days, both a hundred percent vendor finance, with five hundred thousand dollars of working capital from the bank. That's what a lender relationship can do.

**What the offer costs.**

The bank says: "We'll refinance A and B and fund part of C in one facility. Lower rate, bigger limit. We just want security over all three, and the loans cross-default."

Cross-collateral means security over all your businesses. Cross-default means if one business breaks its loan terms, every loan is treated as in default.

Separately, a bad year in C is C's problem. C's lender and C's seller talk to you about C. A and B keep running. In one group facility, C's bad year is everyone's problem.

**How to take the money without the knot.**

First, every new deal stands on its own numbers anyway. If it only works because the bank lends across the group, it doesn't work.

Second, push back on security. Common fallbacks: security only over the business being funded; security over named assets, not everything; or a higher cover covenant, say one point seven five, in exchange for less security.

Third, check the seller notes. They usually sit behind the bank. Make sure a group facility doesn't change that in a way the sellers' documents don't allow, and that a default in one place doesn't trigger defaults in your seller agreements.

Fourth, personal guarantees. On larger deals, over about five million, one often isn't needed, because the business's assets give the bank its security. Typically. Every lender and deal is different. Ask again at every deal.

**Make lenders compete.**

Lenders lend on trust, and trust is a forecast you hit. Every quarter, send each lender what you said each business would do, what it did, and why. A year of that and your next credit application half writes itself.

I did over thirty million dollars of deals in banking by twenty-one. **[JOSH: one line from your banking years on what made a lender say yes to a repeat borrower, or cut.]**

Keep two or three banks that know your group, plus your sellers. Keep updating the ones you didn't use. Competition is your best rate. And never fill a cash gap in one business with a credit card or a short-term loan. Keep a reserve, separate.

Two objections I hear. "Cross-collateral is standard, I can't avoid it." Sometimes you can't. You can usually limit it. Ask. And "my bank's been great, I trust them." Good. Trust them and read the documents anyway. Bank staff change. Clauses don't.

Separately, C falls below one times on its own, and that's one conversation with one lender. In a group facility, one business drags all three into breach.

Where people lose money.

- Signing the bigger, cheaper facility without reading the security. Cost: every business on the line for one.
- A deal that only works on group cash flow. Cost: a weak business propped up by the strong ones.
- A cash gap filled with a card. Cost: expensive, unsecured debt exactly when the numbers are weakest.
- Reporting that slips after the deal closes. Cost: the trust that funds deal four.

You can sign whatever's put in front of you, because the rate looks good and the bank's been great. Or you can walk into that bank with someone who's put these deals in front of lenders before, with your bear case already run.

On a call with me we go through your group's numbers, the facility on the table, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 suits. For a group facility, 1:1 is where I work your structure and lender presentation with you.

If you're someone planning deal three, and you'd rather read the security clause before you sign than after, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either signed up to whatever was offered, or knowing your bear case, your covenants and your next deal's numbers.

# Act 14 · Scale: you as the owner

## EP 66 · Every level from 1 business to 5 (where the owner's hours go)

Owning one good business is a great job. Until you buy the second one.

Then you go from fixing problems to being the problem. By business five you're the busiest person in the group, and the reason it's worth less.

Five businesses making four hundred thousand each, bought at four times, is eight million dollars of assets. Sold as one group at eight times, it's sixteen million. A buyer pays the second number for a group that runs without you. Here's every level from one business to five, and where your hours go at each.

That gap is the roll-up. Buy small at three to five times, combine, sell bigger. But it only shows up if the group runs on managers and systems. If every decision still goes through you, the buyer sees one tired person holding the whole thing up, and prices it in.

This is for owners who want the group and the exit. It's not for anyone who bought a business to work four hours a week. Five businesses won't give you that either.

The whole idea in one line: at every level, your hours move one step further from the site.

There are four levels. Most owners get stuck on level two.

And when an owner drowns after deal two, it has nothing to do with work ethic, or intelligence, or how much they care. It has everything to do with a week built for one business that nobody rebuilt.

I help owners build this, from the first deal with little to no money down to the group. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1: one business, you're the operator.**

At level one you are the business. You know every customer and every number, and you can fix almost anything by walking in the door.

That feels like control. It's a ceiling. The business can't grow past your calendar, and the bank looking at business two can see that.

The move to level two: build the Monday pack. One page, nine numbers or fewer, every week. Revenue against budget, margin, cash, debtor days, the one sales number that drives the business. Then write the job description of the person who could run it without you.

**Level 2: two businesses, the first GM.**

This is where people get stuck. Business two settles and they run both by hand. They drive between sites, override the manager on the spot, and the pipeline for business three dies because there's no time left in the week.

Here's the mechanism. Every time you override a GM, they learn to wait for you. Now you're the bottleneck in two places.

The rule: one GM per business, and you change things through them, never around them. You see each business through its GM and its numbers. Hire that GM before settlement, not six months after.

**Level 3: three to five, one industry, one playbook.**

Five unrelated businesses are five distractions. A clinic, a car wash and a café are three games with three rulebooks. You'll be bad at two of them.

Five clinics, or five electrical contractors, under one holding company are one business with five sites. Same staff types, same suppliers, same numbers. Finance, HR and marketing run once for the group.

Most people think owning more means working more. That's not how it works. At this level an hour spent fixing one site is one-to-one. An hour spent fixing the system all five use is one-to-five. A booking script, a price change, a hiring scorecard. Same hour, five times the effect.

So every hour gets one test: does this fix a site, or the system?

This is also where the lender's view changes. Five sites reporting the same numbers, the same way, every month, is a group a bank can lend to as one. Five sets of books in five formats is five separate conversations.

**Level 4: the group owner.**

At level four the week runs on a rhythm, not on you turning up.

Monday morning, five packs land by nine. You read them for red flags only. Two hours.

Tuesday is meeting day. Each GM gets forty-five minutes: last week's numbers, the one problem, the one decision they need from you.

Wednesday and Thursday are the owner's real job. Lenders. The pipeline for business six. Calls to owners who might sell. Nobody else in the group will do this for you.

Friday is people, and one site visit, rotated so each business sees you about once a month.

If that week is boring, the machine's working. An exciting week means a GM left, a lender's nervous or a site's losing money.

I run a healthcare group full time, so this isn't theory. **[JOSH: one line on what a normal week running your group looks like, and the one report you'd never drop.]** **[JOSH: one thing you changed in your own week after the group grew, and why.]**

The eight-million gap is the prize. The bottom row is what earns it: most of the week on capital, deals and people, almost none of it inside one site.

Where people lose money.

- Buying business two in a different industry. Two playbooks, no shared costs, and no group a buyer will pay a group multiple for.
- Overriding GMs. The good ones leave or stop deciding, and you're back at level one with more debt.
- No Wednesday. With no time for deals, the pipeline dies and the group stalls at two.
- Hiring the GM after settlement. You run the site yourself for six months while you search, and business three waits.

There are two paths from here. Work the rhythm out alone, after business two has already swallowed your week. Or set it up with someone who's built one, before the second settlement.

On a call with me we look at your goal, your hours, your assets and your industry, then your next structure and the level you're building toward. Then whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy gives you the community, the full course and every template. 1:1 is me working your strategy and structures with you.

If you're someone who wants a group, not five jobs, and you'd rather fix the system than the site, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Either still running everything by hand, or with a Monday pack, a meeting day and a plan for business two.

## EP 67 · The 2-hour board meeting for a 5-business group (copy it)

There's a well-kept secret among owners who run groups of businesses.

Their board meetings aren't for updates. They're for decisions. And one decision, made on time, can be worth a hundred and twenty thousand dollars a year in profit, and close to a million at exit.

Here's the whole meeting, in the order your board sees it. Copy it.

Your board is why sellers and banks took you seriously in the first place. After settlement, the same people should be making you money.

Board members on founders' equity, typically two to ten percent each, give you a few hours a month. Spend those hours reading them numbers they could have read at home and you've paid equity for nothing.

This is for owners building a group to sell. It's not for anyone who'd rather nobody looked at the numbers.

The whole idea in one line: a board meeting exists to make decisions. If it could have been an email, it wasn't a board meeting.

There are six parts, in this order. The pre-read pack. Last month's decisions. The numbers. The decision papers. Capital and deals. The decision list.

You were told a board is a formality for big companies. Compliance and minutes. You were told small businesses don't need one. For a group that borrows money and plans to sell, none of that is true. A working board is how several businesses become something a bigger buyer wants.

And if your meetings drift, it has nothing to do with your board's talent or your charisma. It has everything to do with a meeting that was never designed to decide anything.

I help owners build the board, buy the business and run the group. Book a call below. Now the teardown.

**The pre-read pack.**

The pack goes out at least two days before the meeting. One page per business, one page for the group. Same numbers, same order, every month, so the trends jump out.

Per business: revenue against budget, gross margin, profit, cash, debtor days, the one sales number that drives it, staff turnover, and the GM's one problem.

For the group: total profit, debt service cover against the 1.5 floor, cash against your reserve floor, the deal pipeline, and lender covenants.

Every number has a named owner who explains it. If nobody owns it, nobody fixes it. And send the pack to your GMs too. If they can see what the board sees, they fix things before the board asks.

**Last month's decisions.**

Ten minutes. The meeting opens with the list from last time. Done, not done, why.

Anything that's slipped past two meetings gets discussed on its own. That's what stops "let's look at it next month" becoming the culture.

**The numbers.**

Twenty minutes. No walk-through. The board has read the pack.

Questions only, and only on what's off budget or trending the wrong way. A site that's on plan gets no airtime. That's the reward. The question a good board asks more than any other: what do you expect to happen, and how do you know?

**The decision papers.**

Forty minutes, one or two decisions. Each comes with a one-page paper: the options, the numbers, your recommendation.

Here's one. Five clinics, each booking four hundred appointments a month at a hundred dollars. That's two hundred thousand a month for the group.

The paper proposes two moves. Price up five percent at all five: ten thousand a month. Fill two empty practitioner days at the two busiest sites: another ten thousand. Each move has an owner, a date, and the number that shows it's working.

"Grow twenty percent" isn't a decision. That is.

Agree who decides what before the meeting. The operator decides operations. The finance lead decides how the debt is structured. You break ties as the owner, and the minutes record who decided.

**Capital and deals.**

Thirty minutes. Pipeline, lenders, covenant headroom. Whether to sign the LOI on business six. This is where your finance lead earns their equity.

Then ten minutes on risk: what could hurt the group in the next ninety days.

The board also sets the scorecard for every senior hire before the search starts. Never let a new GM write their own. The person being measured shouldn't choose the ruler.

**The decision list.**

Ten minutes. Decision, owner, date. Read it out before anyone leaves. That list is item one next month.

I found my board through LinkedIn and cold outreach. The founder of the Australian Institute of Fitness is a shareholder in my company. When people like that give you two hours, the meeting has to be worth it. **[JOSH: how often your board meets, for how long, and one decision a meeting changed in your group.]**

The price rise falls almost straight to profit. The fill-rate move adds revenue but carries practitioner cost, so I haven't counted it. One decision, owned and dated, is worth close to a million at exit.

Where people lose money.

- Presenting the pack in the meeting. Two hours of expensive people reading, and nothing decided.
- Decisions with no owner or date. They come back next month, and every month late is a month of profit gone.
- Hearing about covenant headroom from the bank instead of the pack. By then your options cost more.
- Letting a senior hire set their own KPIs. You end up paying for activity, not results.

You can keep doing what most owners do. Run meetings that are updates. Carry every decision alone. Find out the headroom got thin when the bank rings. Or you can run the group with a board and a pack that already work. Standing still is the first option.

On a call with me we look at your board, your numbers and your next structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather leave a meeting with a decision than a feeling, and you want a group, not a ceiling, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll have had one more month of meetings either way. The only question is whether it ended with a decision list.

## EP 68 · 5 things I'd never sign to buy a business (1.5× DSCR first)

This one isn't for most people. If "no money down" sounds like "no risk" to you, it isn't for you.

No money down is a capital strategy, not a risk strategy. Deals that blow up rarely blow up on price. They blow up on something someone signed.

Here are five things I'd never sign, ranked from expensive to the one that costs you the exit.

When you buy with seller finance and bank debt, your equity is the thin layer on top. A twenty percent dip in profit doesn't cost you twenty percent. It can cost you the business, because the repayments don't dip with it.

This is for owners and capital deployers building something big with borrowed money. It's not for anyone who'll sign whatever's put in front of them to get a deal done.

The whole idea in one line: write your risk rules on a calm day, because on a bad day you'll talk yourself into anything.

There are five lines. When an owner loses a business this way, it has nothing to do with being reckless or greedy. It has everything to do with having no written rules before the pressure arrived.

Most people think nothing-down deals are fragile by design. Look at Cam, a good friend of mine. Two deals in twenty-three days, a hundred percent vendor finance, and five hundred thousand dollars of working capital from the bank. Nothing down, with a cushion built in from day one. Structured right, those two go together.

I help owners structure deals the business can actually carry. Book a call below. Now the list.

**Mistake 1: a deal below 1.5×.**

DSCR is free cash flow divided by debt repayments. The floor is one and a half.

A business throws off three hundred thousand of free cash flow. Divide by one and a half, and the most it should pay in debt service is two hundred thousand a year. The other hundred is your buffer.

Now the bear case. Profit drops twenty percent. Three hundred becomes two-forty. Against two hundred of repayments, that's 1.2. Tight, but you're paying. Close at 1.2 instead, and the same dip puts you under one. You're funding the bank from your own pocket.

Rule: no deal closes below 1.5 in the base case. Check each business on its own, and the group as a whole. A strong business can hide a weak one in the group total, and the bank will find it before you do.

**Mistake 2: settling with no cushion.**

Being undercapitalised at close is one of the most common ways a good deal goes bad. Wages, a slow debtor and a broken machine, all in month two.

Write a reserve floor. Months of fixed costs plus debt repayments, held in cash or an undrawn facility, that you never go below. The right number depends on your business and your lender. The point is that it's written, and it's on the board pack every month.

Then put a stop-loss on every new bet. A new site or service line gets a budget and a kill line before it starts. Fifty thousand. If it isn't paying back by month four, we stop. Illustrative, and written in the minutes.

**Mistake 3: a personal guarantee by default.**

On larger deals, often over about five million, an asset-rich business can give the bank its security, and a personal guarantee often isn't needed. Typically. Every lender and deal is different.

On smaller deals you may have to sign one. Then you negotiate it. Cap the amount. Put a time limit on it. Have it fall away when the debt drops to a set level. Never sign the default. The first draft is the bank's opening position, not its last.

**Mistake 4: unsecured personal debt.**

Credit cards, personal loans, anything that can follow you home. It's fast, which is the problem. It lets a business that's bleeding look like one that's growing, and it's the dearest money there is.

Never stack it on top of a seller note. You'd be paying the seller from the business and the bank from your house.

Your personal costs should be covered before you sign. Savings, a partner's income, or a salary the business can carry. If your rent depends on the business having a good month, your risk is personal, not commercial. That's when people sign everything on this list.

**Mistake 5: equity sold to plug a cash gap.**

This is the most expensive line, and it feels like the safest. Selling equity in a panic has no repayments, so it looks like the low-risk option.

It isn't. Debt gets paid off. Equity is forever. Sell ten percent to cover a bad quarter, and if the group later sells for sixteen million, that bad quarter cost you one point six million. Maybe the control over when and to whom you sell, too. A new investor who came in on a bad day has every reason to want a say on the good one.

Debt over dilution, inside the 1.5 rule. It's why I keep 73% of my company. **[JOSH: your own reserve floor or the one line you won't cross, and why; any document you've refused to sign, without names.]**

The top rows are why the floor is 1.5. The bottom row is why you cover a gap with debt you can carry, not equity you can never buy back.

Where people lose money.

- Running only the base case. The base case never arrives on time.
- Counting property or equipment as the reserve. It doesn't pay this Friday's wages.
- Hiring fixed overhead ahead of proven revenue. Payroll doesn't fall when sales do.

There are two paths. Learn these lines alone, usually right after you've crossed one. Or have someone check every structure before you sign it, in the exact moment you want to break a rule.

On a call with me we look at your deal or your next one, check it against these five lines, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who wants the upside of buying businesses, and refuses to bet everything to get it, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll either have a written reserve floor and a never-sign list, or you'll still be meaning to write them.

## EP 69 · Decide at 60%. Don't run at 100%.

James works seventy hours a week and answers email at nine at night. He hasn't had a week off in two years. Chad works hard too, but he stops at dinner and his holidays are booked a year out.

Chad's group is the one that's growing. Not because he works less. Because he decides better.

Running at a hundred percent isn't commitment. In a group, it's the most expensive habit an owner has.

At one business, hours are output. At five, the owner's output is a handful of decisions a quarter. A GM who isn't working. A covenant getting tight. Whether to sign the next LOI. Tired owners make those calls late, and a late call on one site can cost more than a year of your salary.

If you left a corporate job to own a business, the trap is rebuilding the same job with more debt attached. Same seventy hours. Now the risk is yours.

This is for people who'll put forty to a hundred hours a week into building this. It's not for the four-hour-work-week crowd. I mean that here most of all.

The whole idea in one line: decide at sixty percent, don't run at a hundred. They're different things.

There are three parts. Why people believe hours win. What actually moves the numbers once you own the businesses. And a test.

When an owner burns out, it has nothing to do with being soft, or not wanting it enough. It has everything to do with never deciding what they were willing to trade.

I help owners build a group without it eating them. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Why people believe hours win.**

Because early on, they do. Before my first deal I made about a thousand cold calls, and four deals fell over. That stage is volume. You make the calls, send the offers, do the reps.

The mistake is staying in sprint mode after the job has changed. Once you own the businesses, the volume belongs to your GMs. Your job is the few decisions only you can make.

You were told the owner who works the most hours wins. You were told rest is for later. You were told that if you're not exhausted, you're not serious. None of that holds once you own the business.

**What actually moves the numbers.**

Decide at sixty percent. There is never a perfect moment. Waiting for a hundred percent certainty isn't safe. It's passive risk. You lose the deal, or the year.

But the decision is the fast part. The pace is what you can hold.

A long-haul driver doesn't arrive sooner by skipping every stop. He arrives later, or not at all. It only takes one bad call at the wheel.

So pick one number-one priority for the year. For most people watching, that's the next deal. Then write a floor under everything else that matters. Sleep, movement, the people at home. Minimums, not dreams. In a bad week, you protect the floor.

[JOSH: your own floors for sleep, training, time off and family while running the healthcare group.]

Hand off early. The things only you can do in a 3C deal are few: the seller relationship, the decisions, the lender meetings. The spreadsheet clean-up and the data-room admin can go to someone else sooner than you think.

And plan the year in seasons. Two pushes, usually one deal each. Two maintenance quarters to fix systems, tidy the numbers and rest the team. Real time off, booked like a board meeting.

**The test.**

If you went offline for two weeks, which decisions would wait for you? Write them down. Then hand one of them to a GM this month, with the numbers they need to make it.

If that list is long, the group runs on your energy, and so does its price. A buyer looks at a business that needs one tired person to hold it up and calls that risk.

It's also why so many sellers sell. A generation of owners carried their business for decades, and they're done. You're building the opposite.

Where this doesn't apply: the first ninety days after settlement. You will run hot. New staff, a new bank, a seller to look after. That's fine. Choose it on purpose, with an end date, and keep the floors. The danger isn't working hard. It's working hard by default, with no floor and no plan for when it eases.

[JOSH: optional. A time you ran too hot and what it cost.]

Three months of a stretched owner putting off one call. Eighteen thousand in cash, and over half a million off the exit if nobody ever fixes it. Compare that with the cost of a week off. It isn't close.

Where people lose money.

- Waiting for a hundred percent on a deal. The seller signs with someone who didn't.
- Sprinting for two years with no floor, then stalling right when deal two needs you.
- Doing admin someone else could do while the lender meeting waits.
- No quiet season. The team burns out with you, and the best GM leaves.
- Treating the owner's energy as free. It's the one input in the group nobody else can top up.

Or you can keep doing what you're doing. Running at a hundred percent. Carrying every decision alone. Waiting to feel ready. Feeling ready is the hundred percent you'll never reach.

Or you can build at full intensity with floors, seasons and someone to ask. Where your energy goes decides whether this works.

On a call with me we look at your goal, your hours, your assets and your industry, and the pace your situation can actually hold. Then whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who wants the big outcome, will work hard for it, and wants to still be standing when it arrives, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days further in either way. Still running at a hundred with no plan, or running hard with floors, seasons and a plan for the next deal.

## EP 70 · 50/50 handshake vs 4 clauses: how partnerships break when the money arrives

There are only two types of business partnerships. The ones that wrote down what happens when the money arrives, and the ones that break when it does.

Partnerships rarely break when things are hard. They break when things start working.

On a sixteen-million-dollar exit, four clauses decide who gets what. Here's the handshake against the agreement, and a test to tell you which one you've actually got.

In the 3C model you'll have more partners than you think. Co-buyers. Board members on founders' equity. Sellers who roll equity into the new company. Each one owns a slice of the exit.

If the paperwork doesn't say what happens when someone stops, leaves or disagrees, the exit is when you find out. That's the worst possible day to negotiate.

This is for anyone buying with a co-buyer, building a board on equity, or taking a seller's rollover. It's not for anyone hoping a handshake will hold for ten years.

The whole idea in one line: contribution changes over time, so the terms have to be able to change too.

Two options. Option A is the handshake. Fifty-fifty, goodwill, sort it out later. Option B is four written clauses: roles and vesting, deadlock, buy-sell, and minority exit. Plus one trigger.

When a partnership breaks, it has nothing to do with bad people, or greed, or one partner getting lazy. It has everything to do with terms written for year one that nobody touched again.

I help owners structure this from the start, so the group survives the money arriving. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A. The handshake.**

People choose it because it's fast, it's friendly, and writing things down feels like you don't trust each other.

Here's how it plays out. Two co-buyers, fifty-fifty, buy a business making four hundred thousand. One runs it full time. The other finds the next deal. By year three the seller note's paid down, the business throws off cash, and the deal-finder has taken a job elsewhere.

Same fifty-fifty. Completely different contribution. The operator is doing all the work for half the profit, and resentment does the rest.

Now add a board member given five percent on a handshake, who stopped turning up after six months. And a seller who rolled in with a veto. The group grows to sixteen million, a buyer wants a hundred percent, and three people can each slow the deal down.

Most people think trust is enough. They think writing it down means you don't trust each other. That's not how it works. Writing it down is what lets you keep trusting each other in year five.

**Option B. The four clauses.**

Your lawyer drafts these. You decide what goes in.

One. Roles and vesting. Who does what, how many hours, and what happens if someone stops. Only partner where the jobs don't overlap. One person finds and finances deals, the other runs the businesses. Two people doing the same job is one too many. Equity vests against contribution. For board members, that's founders' equity, two to ten percent, vesting from the signed purchase agreement.

Two. Deadlock. Agree decision rights in advance. The deal person decides on structures. The operator decides on operations. Then a formal last resort, which your lawyer sets out.

Three. Buy-sell. If a partner wants out, dies, divorces or stops contributing, how is their share valued, who can buy it, and how is it paid for? Agree the formula now, while nobody knows which side of it they'll be on.

Four. Minority holders on exit. Drag-along, so a minority can't block a sale of the group. No veto and no voting control for small holders. Set up from the start.

Then the trigger. The terms reopen when the seller note is paid off, or when anyone's hours drop below an agreed level. Maybe the operator gets a salary first, then profit splits. Maybe the partner who stepped back sells some shares under the buy-sell formula. The conversation is already in the contract, not left to resentment.

**Sellers are partners too.**

Any seller who rolls equity into the new company is a partner who used to be the boss.

My friend Jason Nagy's first deal was a five-and-a-half-million-dollar radiology business, zero down, on a sixty-forty structure. Seven years later he sold to private equity for two hundred million.

At that end of the market, the buyer wants a clean shareholder register. So the same rules apply to the seller. Their role after settlement. What they can and can't vote on. And drag-along, so they come with you when the group sells.

It's also why I care about control. I keep 73% of my company. Equity can be shared. Control should sit where the decisions get made. **[JOSH: how your own shareholders' agreement handles deadlock or exit, or a partnership you've seen break, without names.]**

**The self-test.**

Two questions. If your partner stopped working tomorrow, could you say in one sentence what happens to their shares, at what price, and who pays? And if a buyer offered for a hundred percent of the group, could anyone block it?

If you can't answer the first, or the answer to the second is yes, you're on Option A, whatever you think you agreed.

Option B, every time you share equity. No exceptions for friends. Friends are who it protects most.

And if you can't find a partner whose job is different from yours, go solo and keep a hundred percent. You'll still have a board and maybe a seller rolling in, so clauses three and four still apply.

None of those is a fight on day one. Every one of them is a fight on exit day, with a buyer waiting.

Where people lose money.

- Equity with no vesting. Someone leaves in year one and owns a slice of year ten.
- Two partners in the same job. One of them is overpaid, and both know it.
- A seller rollover with a veto. The person you bought from can block the sale you built.
- No buy-sell formula. The valuation fight happens at the worst moment, with lawyers on the clock.

There are two paths. Sort it out over a beer and hope it holds. Or structure it from the start with someone who's set these clauses up before.

On a call with me we look at your goal, your hours, your assets and your industry, then your first or next structure, including who you're doing it with. Then whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. Bring your partner if you have one.

If you're someone who wants to grow past one business, and you'd rather have the awkward conversation now than the expensive one later, this is for you.

Thirty days from now you and your partner will either have the four clauses on paper, or you'll still be relying on a handshake.

And this is the end of Season 2, which makes it a good line to draw. Before it, you wanted to buy a business. Now you know how to own one, grow it and build the group around it. Your week, your board, your risk lines, your pace and now your partners. That's the owner a buyer wants to deal with. What you do with that is the only question left.

# Act 15 · The exit

## EP 71 · What private equity pays for: every level from 3× to 8×

There are two types of people who buy a business. The ones who buy themselves a job. And the ones who buy something a bigger buyer will pay for.

Same $1M profit. One sells for $3M. The other sells for $5M.

Nothing about the second one is clever. Here's every level between them, and how to build it in from the day you buy.

Season 3. The map in two lines: Credibility and Capital get you a business with little or none of your own money in it, and Closing gets it signed. Grow makes it bigger, and Exit is where you get paid for all of it.

A buyer pays a multiple of profit. One turn of multiple on $1M of profit is $1M. So the only question in this episode is what moves the multiple. If you want a lifestyle business you never sell, skip this one.

The multiple is the buyer's answer to one question: will this profit still be here after the owner leaves? The surer they are, the more they pay.

You were told value comes from hard work, years in the chair and revenue growth. If you believed that, it's not on you. It's what owners tell each other. Buyers don't price any of it. They price certainty.

There are four levels. Each one adds one thing a buyer can trust.

I help owners and buyers do exactly this: buy with little to no money down, then build the business up these levels. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1: A job you own (~3×).**

The owner wins the work and does the work. Customers come back every year, but on a handshake. The books get done once a year, for tax. One customer is a third of revenue.

A buyer sees profit that walks out the door with you. So they offer around 3×, and hold part of it back in an earn-out to keep you tied in. Episode 74 covers those.

The test for Level 1 is simple. Take four weeks off with your phone off. If revenue drops, you're here, and so is the price.

If you're buying, this matters. Most retiring owners sell you a Level 1 business. That's partly why you can buy at 3 to 5× profit. It's not a problem. It's the gap you get paid to close.

**Level 2: Revenue a buyer can count (~4×).**

Contracts, maintenance plans, memberships, retainers. In most traditional businesses the work already repeats. It just isn't on paper.

A contract is revenue a buyer can count. A handshake is revenue they have to guess at, and buyers discount guesses.

Turning handshakes into contracts is usually a letter and a phone call to customers who already come back every year. On $1M of profit, moving from 3× to 4× is $1M of exit value. That's the best-paid admin you'll ever do.

The rule: get more than half of revenue on contract or a fixed schedule. Then spread it. Once one customer is over 20 per cent, a buyer prices in the day that customer leaves.

**Level 3: Runs without you, numbers they can check (~5×).**

This is the biggest jump. A general manager runs the day to day. You don't win the work and you don't do it. And there's a stretch of months that proves it ran while you weren't there.

Next to that, numbers that check out. Monthly profit and loss reconciled to the bank. Revenue by customer and by source. Every add-back with a document behind it. Clean numbers shorten diligence, and a short diligence keeps the price you agreed. That's next episode.

The rule: hire the GM one to two years before you want to sell, not one month. A buyer wants to see the year after you stepped back, not your promise.

**Level 4: Big enough for private equity (8× and up).**

Size changes who's buying. One business making $1M attracts individuals and competitors. A group with a management team attracts private equity, and private equity pays more for size and systems.

That's the roll-up in one line. Buy ten businesses at around 4× profit, grow and combine them, sell the group at 8, 10, sometimes 15×. You don't need any one of them to be special. You need each one at Level 3 before it joins the group.

My friend Jason Nagy bought one radiology business for $5.5 million with zero down, using a lender plus the seller staying in. Seven years later, private equity bought the group for $200 million. The seven years are his story to tell. What it proves is that a zero-down first deal doesn't cap where you finish.

Same profit, $2M apart. And B is paid mostly at settlement, so the buyer's risk isn't parked in an earn-out that might never pay. First you buy A. Then you put in the contracts, the GM and the monthly books. Once you have those, you own B, and you never paid a B price.

Where people lose money.

- **Chasing revenue instead of certainty.** Another $2M of handshake revenue still sells at a Level 1 multiple.
- **Hiring the GM in the sale year.** Buyers see it. The price drops a turn or comes with an earn-out. On $1M of profit, a turn is $1M.
- **Leaving one customer at a third of revenue.** The buyer cuts the price or walks.
- **Buying without scoring the target.** You can't plan a fix you never measured. Score every target one to five on contracts, owner-dependence, concentration and monthly books.

Two paths. Buy whatever you can get, run it hard, and find out at the sale it's worth $2M less for things you could have fixed three years earlier. Or build the exit in from deal one.

On a call with me we score your business or your target on these four levels, sketch your first structure, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather own an asset than a job, and you want to know what it's worth before you buy it, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. The difference is whether you've also got a target list scored on the four levels and a structure on paper.

## EP 72 · The 7-folder data room that protects a $6M sale (copy it)

There's a quiet rule among people who buy businesses for a living. Most deals don't die on price. They die in diligence, waiting for documents nobody can find.

On a $6M sale, six slow months can cost you $500,000.

This is the folder that stops it, opened in the order a buyer's accountant opens it. Copy it.

A data room is one online folder holding every document a buyer will ask for, with access you control. It pays twice. On the way in, it's your checklist: you can find the gaps in a target in an afternoon. On the way out, it's what keeps the price you agreed. If you won't keep monthly books, nothing in this episode will help you.

Most people think diligence is the buyer's problem. That's not how it works. The price in your LOI is a draft. Diligence is where it gets tested, and every missing document is a delay, a price cut or a reason to walk.

If you've looked at a deal and couldn't tell a good one from a bad one, it has nothing to do with your intelligence or how much you've read. It has everything to do with never having seen a complete set.

Seven folders, in five stops.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down and run them so they're ready to sell. Book a call below. Now the folders.

**Folders 1 and 2: Financials and tax.**

Opened first, every time. Three years of annual accounts. Monthly profit and loss and balance sheet for at least the last two years, reconciled to the bank. Tax returns that match the accounts.

Monthly matters because buyers look at trend and seasonality, not just the year. An annual figure can hide a bad last quarter. A buyer will find it, and price it as if it's the new normal.

When you bought, you asked the seller for three years of financials, matching tax returns and revenue by year. That was their data room. Now keep yours.

[JOSH: one line on what you asked the physio seller for before your offer, and what was missing.]

Then the add-backs: owner costs you say won't carry over to the buyer. Each one needs the document that proves it. No document, no add-back. Every $50,000 you can't prove comes off profit, and at 5× that's $250,000 off the price.

**Folder 3: Customers and revenue.**

Revenue by customer, by service line and by source, every month. Your top twenty customers and their share. Who left, and why.

This is where the buyer prices growth. If you can't show where revenue comes from, they can't see what drives it, so they won't pay for it.

It's also where they check concentration. If your top customer is 30 per cent of revenue, expect a lower multiple or part of the price moved into an earn-out. Better to know that from your own folder than from their accountant.

**Folders 4 to 6: Contracts, people, licences.**

Every customer contract, supplier contract and lease, each one marked: assignable to a new owner, or needs consent. The trap is a key contract with a change-of-control clause found in week ten. The deal reopens.

People: an org chart, employment agreements for every key person, pay and entitlements. In licensed industries like healthcare, every practitioner's registration.

Licences: every permit the business needs to trade, any issue, and how it was fixed.

On the way in, these three folders are where a target hides its problems. A lease with eighteen months left. A key practitioner on no contract. Ask for all three before you finalise the price, not after.

**Folder 7: Corporate and legal.**

Company records, the shareholders' agreement, board minutes, any disputes. This is where your day-one structure pays off. Drag-along rights, and no veto for minority shareholders. If someone with 3 per cent can block the sale, the buyer finds it here.

Fix it early. Changing a shareholders' agreement before a sale is a lawyer's afternoon. During a sale, the minority holder knows they have leverage, and they'll price it.

**The KPI pack and the access rules.**

The KPI pack is the story the folders prove. Twelve months of revenue per customer, gross margin, churn and concentration. If you've been doing the Monday numbers, you already have it.

Access: signed NDA first, serious buyers only. Customer names and staff pay only after a signed LOI. It's the same rule we use as buyers: the NDA is the first test of who's serious.

A quality-of-earnings review is an accountant testing whether the profit is real and will repeat. You ran one on your seller. One day a buyer runs one on you.

Timing: build the folders the week you settle, add to them every month, and a year before you sell, get your own quality-of-earnings review so nothing in the buyer's surprises you.

Every request without the folder takes two weeks. Ninety days becomes six months, a slow season lands in month five, the buyer's model drops and they come back at $5.5M. You've waited too long to start again, so you take it. That's $500,000 lost to time.

Where people lose money.

- **Add-backs with no paper.** $50K unproven at 5× is $250K off the price.
- **Tax returns that don't match the accounts.** The buyer believes the lower number.
- **Customer names handed out before an LOI.** Some "buyers" are competitors fishing.
- **Waiting until a buyer calls.** You can hire someone to sort documents. You can't hire someone to create two years of monthly accounts that were never done.

Or you can keep doing what most owners do. Documents in three inboxes and an old laptop. Meaning to sort it out. Finding out, when a buyer turns up, that six months of digging just cost you the price you agreed.

On a call with me we run the seven folders against your business or your target, sketch your first structure, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who'd rather know than guess, and you want your capital in a business you've actually checked, you already know what to do.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either with seven folders filled and a way to check any deal, or still wondering what you missed.

## EP 73 · Sell 100% vs sell 60% (the $32M second bite)

Growing a group of businesses is a great position to be in. Cash flow, a team, control. Until the next step needs more money and more people than you have.

That's when most owners think there are two options: stay stuck, or sell the lot. There's a third. In the example I'll show you, the second cheque is bigger than the first.

It's called a recapitalisation, or recap. A buyer, usually private equity, buys a majority of your company, say 60 per cent. You're paid for that now and you keep the rest. When the group is sold again, your 40 per cent gets paid too. That's the second bite.

This is for owners who've hit a ceiling and think the next five years are worth more than the last five. If you want one clean cheque and never to think about the business again, sell 100 per cent. That's a fine answer.

Most people think selling means walking away. At the top end, that's not how it works. A recap trades certain money for a bigger, uncertain second cheque, and trades control for a partner with more capital than you.

If you've hit the ceiling, it has nothing to do with your work ethic or your industry. It has everything to do with arithmetic. One owner's money and one owner's hours only stretch so far.

Option A, option B, the rights that decide it, and a test.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down and structure them for an exit like this. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A: Sell 100%.**

Certain, simple, done. You get today's multiple on today's profit.

What it costs you is the next jump. The buyer is paying you a group multiple so they can buy more businesses at 3 to 5×, combine them, and sell at 8, 10 or more. Sell everything and that arbitrage is theirs, not yours.

In our example, that's $32M today, before debt, fees and tax. Nothing left at risk and nothing left to gain.

It's the right call if you're done, or if you don't believe the next five years beat the last five.

**Option B: Sell 60%, keep 40%.**

This is the mirror image of the 60/40 buying structure from episode 14. When you buy that way, a lender funds most of the price and the seller rolls part of their value into equity and stays in. In a recap, you're the seller who stays in.

In our example the buyer pays $19.2M for 60 per cent, and your 40 is worth $12.8M on paper. Paper, not cash. It only turns into cash at the next sale.

The buyer wants you to keep 40 per cent. It keeps you motivated and tells them you believe the numbers. If an owner wants every dollar out on day one, a buyer asks why.

Now the case against. Profit could fall. The buyer could overpay for what they add, or load the group with debt. And you stop being the boss. Every rule you set for your own minority shareholders, drag-along, no veto, no voting control, now applies to you.

**The rights that decide it.**

Agree these before you sign, while you still have something they want. Proper lawyers. Not a template.

- **Tag-along.** If they sell their 60, you can sell your 40 on the same terms.
- **Information.** Monthly accounts, the board pack, the debt position.
- **A board seat,** or at least the right to attend.
- **Consent on the big things:** much more debt, new shares that dilute you, selling a major part of the business. You won't get a veto on everything. Spend your leverage on debt and dilution, because those are what shrink your 40.
- **Leaver terms.** What happens to your 40 per cent if you leave or they push you out. Read it twice.

[JOSH: optional. One line on any recap or partial sale offered to your healthcare group and what you thought of it. Cut if none.]

**The test and the verdict.**

Write down your first-bite number: the amount that changes your life even if the second bite never comes.

If 60 per cent of today's value clears it, a recap is on the table. If it doesn't, sell 100 per cent or don't sell yet.

Then check what the second bite depends on: the buyer's plan, how much debt they'll put on the group, and whether you'll still be running it. Ask to see the plan before you sign. If they won't show you, that's your answer.

The verdict: recap when the first bite clears your number, you believe in the next five years, and you've got the rights in writing. Otherwise, take the whole cheque.

The good case pays $19.2M more than a full sale. The bad case pays $4.4M less. It's a bet, so size the first bite to be fine if it loses.

Where people lose money.

- **Signing the buyer's shareholders' agreement as drafted.** Their lawyers, their terms. You find out what's in it in year three.
- **A first bite too small to live on.** If the second never comes, you've sold 60 per cent and carried all the risk on the rest.
- **No tag-along.** They sell their 60 to someone you didn't pick, and your 40 is stuck with them.
- **Weak leaver terms.** Get pushed out and your 40 per cent can be bought back at a discount.
- **Ignoring the debt stack.** Lenders are paid before equity. Load the group with debt and your 40 per cent of the sale price shrinks after they're paid.

Two paths. On the first, the minority agreement is written by the other side's lawyers and you find out what it means three years later. On the second, your structure is set up from deal one, so at the end you've got options, not surprises.

On a call with me we map your exit doors, sketch your first structure, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who's outgrown doing it all yourself, and you want the exit to be your choice and not a fire sale, you've already made the decision.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either still at the same ceiling, or thirty days into buying past it with a structure that keeps your options open.

## EP 74 · 5 earn-out mistakes that turn a $1M sale into $700K

How come people with capital, good lawyers and a signed deal still end up in a fight two years after settlement? They had the money. They had the advisers. What they also had was an earn-out written like a handshake.

On a $1M deal, that's $300,000 riding on one paragraph. Here are the five mistakes that lose it, cheapest first.

Typical shape: a base of 70 to 80 per cent, and an earn-out of 20 to 30 per cent paid over one to three years if the business hits agreed targets. You'll meet it from both sides. As a buyer, it means you don't pay for "maybe" at settlement. As a seller, it's how buyers bridge the gap between your price and their doubts. This is for people doing real deals. If you're still deciding whether to start, go back to episode one.

You were told an earn-out is a bonus. Upside. A sweetener. It isn't. It's part of the price that might never arrive, and whether it does depends entirely on what's written down.

If you've seen one go badly, it had nothing to do with trusting the wrong person. It had everything to do with a target nobody defined properly.

Here's the example we'll use.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down and structure deals like this so they hold. Book a call below. Now the mistakes.

**Mistake 1: Too many metrics.**

Five targets is five arguments. Revenue, margin, headcount, new customers, retention, each with its own definition. Hit three, miss two, and now you're arguing over what a partial payment should be. Every one becomes a dispute about how it was counted.

Each argument costs legal fees, management time and goodwill with the person you need running the handover. On a $300K earn-out, one dispute can eat a third of it before anyone wins.

The rule: one or two metrics. Never five.

**Mistake 2: No cap, no reporting.**

As the buyer, an uncapped earn-out means one lucky year can cost you more than the business was worth. Say a big one-off contract lands in year one. Uncapped, you pay a share of revenue you'll never see again. Cap it at the agreed amount.

You also keep control of operations. A seller chasing a number will run the business for this year's target at the expense of the next five.

And both sides get monthly reporting. Nobody should find out at year end that the target was missed in March. If the seller sees the trend in month four, they can still act on it. If they see it in month thirteen, they call a lawyer.

**Mistake 3: Undefined terms.**

Most earn-outs are a paragraph that says "revenue" and stops. Four fights come up again and again. Each one has a clause that stops it.

- "You changed how revenue is counted." Define revenue line by line, the way it was counted in the accounts you diligenced.
- "You moved my customers into your other business." Say what happens to revenue that moves between businesses.
- "You fired the team that was going to hit it." Agree what the buyer can and can't change during the earn-out.
- "You sold before year three." Say what happens to the unpaid earn-out on a sale. Usually it's paid out, or a fair share of it.

**Mistake 4: A profit target instead of revenue.**

This one costs the whole earn-out. Profit depends on a hundred cost decisions, and after settlement the buyer makes all of them. They merge your team into theirs, move overheads around, and your profit target is missed through nothing you did.

Say the buyer moves $80,000 of group overheads onto your business. Revenue hits the target. Profit misses by $80,000. Earn-out: zero.

The rule: revenue wherever you can. It's harder to move and easier to check.

**Mistake 5: Accepting a base you wouldn't take alone.**

This is the most expensive one, and the test is simple. Would you accept the base alone? If not, you haven't sold the business. You've sold part of it and taken a bet on the rest.

Three million with two of it in earn-out is really one million and a lot of hope.

Here's what most people miss. The fix isn't in the contract. A buyer loads the earn-out when the business depends on you: they think the profit walks out with you. Episode 71. Make it run without you before you sell, and the base goes up.

As a buyer, offer one in two cases only. First, when you'd otherwise be arguing about price: the seller says this year will be the best yet, so you pay for it when it happens, out of the extra profit it brings. Second, when the seller holds the customer relationships and you need them through the handover. That's the six-month to three-year transition, with money attached.

Same business, same performance. One paragraph decides $300,000.

Where people lose money.

- **Agreeing the earn-out before the base.** Negotiate the base as a fair price on its own first, then talk about upside.
- **Planning your life on the total.** Plan on the base. Treat the earn-out as money you might not see.
- **"We'll sort the detail later."** The detail you didn't write is the fight in year two.
- **Stacking an earn-out on an all-vendor-finance deal.** Getting paid over time already shares the risk. An earn-out is for the gap that's left.

Or you can keep doing it the usual way. One paragraph, agreed over lunch between two people who like each other. Year two is when you find out what it meant, and what it cost.

On a call with me we go through your offer or your target, sketch the structure, earn-out or not, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who wants your capital protected by structure, not trust, and you'd rather write the clause now than argue about it later, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either with your next offer structured to hold, or still hoping the handshake means what you think it means.

## EP 75 · Sell to your own managers for $1.66M (vendor finance in reverse)

Two owners, same business, both ready to retire. James lists with a broker, opens his books to strangers and waits a year. Tom sells to his general manager, who pays him over ten years out of the business's own profit.

On $250,000 of profit, that can be about $1.66 million to Tom. He never met a stranger.

I know this structure from the other side. At 25 I was unemployed, with no savings and no experience. I bought my first business, a physio clinic, on 100 per cent vendor finance. Seven years at 4 per cent, a nine-month payment holiday, no deposit. I pulled $150,000 out in year one.

A management buyout is that deal with you as the seller. It's for owners stuck inside their business. If you need the highest cash price at settlement, private equity is usually your buyer, not this.

Your best buyer might already be on your payroll, and the money to pay you is already in the business.

If you feel trapped in your own business, it has nothing to do with being a bad manager or picking the wrong industry. It has everything to do with being told there's one way out: find a stranger with cash. Strangers with cash are rare, slow and picky. Your managers are already there.

Before you knew this, the speed run was buyer's maths. After this episode, it's your exit plan. Five parts.

I help people buy businesses with little to no money down and plan the exit from day one. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Why my seller said yes.**

A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire, and they carry the burden of ownership. They don't want to wait sixteen weeks for a bank or haggle with a broker. They want out, and they want the business and the people looked after.

[JOSH: one line on why your physio seller agreed to be paid over time.]

And getting paid over time can be worth more than cash. Sellers on terms can get 6 to 7× where cash offers sit at 3 to 5×, and close in two to four weeks instead of six to sixteen. One day that seller is you.

**Why your managers can be the better buyer.**

Four reasons. Speed: they know the business, so diligence is shorter. The team stays, and customers never see a stranger walk in. Your numbers don't go to competitors who might be fishing. And you're paid on terms, at a better price.

Your managers usually don't have the cash, and they don't need it. Neither did I. The money comes from the business. That's the whole model.

The case against: a bigger buyer may pay more at settlement, and you carry the risk. If your managers run it into the ground, your money goes with it.

**Leave the 1.5 headroom.**

Here's the speed run from the seller's side. Profit divided by 1.5, the cover rule, is what the business can safely pay in debt each year.

Say a bad year cuts profit by 30 per cent, to $175,000. At $166,000 of payments, the business still pays you. At $250,000 of payments, it's $75,000 short, and the business is in default in year one.

As a buyer, the 1.5 protected you. Now it protects you as the seller. Squeeze the payments up to every dollar of profit and the first bad year breaks the business, and your money with it.

**The terms you'll want as a seller.**

As a buyer I got no deposit and a nine-month holiday. Your managers will ask for the same. Decide in advance what you'll trade it for.

- **Security over the business,** so you can step back in if payments stop.
- **Quarterly reporting,** at least.
- **A default clause:** what happens if a payment is missed, and how long they have to fix it.
- **Covenants:** a minimum level of cover, and limits on new debt, so they can't borrow ahead of you.

If a bank lends part of it, the bank gets paid first and your note sits behind it. That's the subordination clause you accepted as a buyer.

**Build your buyer years out.**

First, name the person. Ideally a GM hired one to two years before you want to go, who already owns a whole part of the business, so owning all of it is a smaller step.

Then give them a small stake early, vesting over time, so they think like an owner. Once they're ready, the bank will want what it wanted from you: financials, a model with a bad-year case, the cover rule. A data room from episode 72 does most of that. And help them build their own credibility: an accountant, a lawyer, a small board. It's the 3C model, pointed the other way.

[JOSH: optional. One line on a management buyout plan in your group, or one done well in healthcare. Cut if none.]

That's well above what many cash buyers would pay for a business this size. The $84,000 of headroom is what keeps it paying in a bad year.

Where people lose money.

- **Squeezing payments to 100 per cent of profit.** First bad year, they default and you're owed money by a broken business.
- **An unsecured note with no default clause.** If they stop paying, you're a creditor, not an owner.
- **Picking the buyer in the last year.** No track record of running it without you, so the bank won't lend.
- **Selling on a handshake to a loyal manager.** Loyalty isn't a covenant.

Two paths. Keep working in it, keep waiting for a stranger, and hope a loose note with a loyal manager holds up in year three. Or set it up properly, so it works for both of you.

On a call with me we look at your business or your target, sketch your first structure with the way out built in, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who wants out of working in the business without abandoning the people in it, and you want to choose the exit, you've already made the decision.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either still the only person who can run the place, or thirty days into building the buyer who'll take it off your hands.

# Act 16 · Industry playbooks I

## EP 76 · The $800K mistake in a clinic sale (teardown)

A clinic reporting $500,000 profit can be worth $800,000 less than the asking price. The numbers aren't wrong. A third of them just walk out the door with the owner.

My first deal was a physio clinic. No deposit. Seven years of vendor finance at 4%. Here's the teardown I'd run on any clinic, in the order a bank reads it.

Clinics sell at roughly 3 to 5 times profit. So every $100,000 of "profit" that's really the owner's wage adds $300,000 to $500,000 to the price. You pay that once, and you service the debt on it for seven years.

The sellers are there. Healthcare is full of practitioner-owners who treated patients all day for twenty years and did the payroll at night. They don't want to wait 16 weeks for a bank. They want out, and they want their patients and staff looked after. That's why getting paid over time lands well with them.

This is for owners whose one clinic has hit the ceiling of their own diary, and for buyers who want a group, not a job with a lease. Not for anyone after a clinic they visit on Fridays.

A clinic's value is the profit that stays after the owner leaves. That's the whole idea.

There are five lines to read, in the order a lender reads them: the headline profit, billings by practitioner, the owner's hands, utilisation, and the debt test.

And if your own clinic has stalled, it has nothing to do with how good a clinician you are or how many hours you put in. It has everything to do with a business built around one person. That's a design problem. The next owner can fix it.

I help owners and buyers do this with little to no money down. Book a call below. Now the teardown.

**The headline profit.**

Start where the seller starts. Three years of financials that match the tax returns.

Here, illustrative: $2 million revenue, $500,000 profit. At 4 times, the seller's number is $2 million. Write it down. Then go looking for what's propping it up.

Lenders often like healthcare, because demand tends to hold when the economy doesn't. What they check is predictable: revenue that doesn't sit with one person, practitioners staying, a lease that can be assigned with enough term left, registrations and billing in order. And they look at who's behind you. A board with a finance lead and someone who's run clinics carries real weight.

**Billings by practitioner.**

The name is on the door. The revenue sits with people. Ask for billings by practitioner, three years.

Here, the owner bills $600,000. That's 30% of the clinic. Treat any one person above about 30% like customer concentration, because that's what it is. Those patients may follow that person out.

**The owner's hands.**

A lot of owner-practitioners pay themselves through the profit, not a salary. So the profit looks bigger than it is.

Replace the owner's clinical work with a practitioner on a fair rate. Say $200,000 a year. True profit: $300,000. At 4 times, that's $1.2 million. The $800,000 gap is the price of the owner's hands. Don't pay it.

Most buyers think a clinic's value is its profit, its name, its location. That's not how it works. It's the profit after the owner goes. Price that, and the rest is negotiation.

**Utilisation.**

Booked appointments against available appointments, per practitioner. This is your growth line.

A clinic at 60% has room to grow without hiring. At 95% it's capped until you recruit. Same profit, very different upside, and the seller rarely prices it.

Lift five practitioners from 60% to 75% and you've added a quarter more appointments on the same rent, the same front desk, the same software. That's the cheapest profit you'll ever buy. You don't pay for it. You find it.

**The debt test.**

Now the bank's question. Can the clinic pay for itself? Free cash flow should cover debt repayments at least 1.5 times.

$300,000 divided by 1.5 is $200,000 a year to the seller. Over seven years, $1.4 million. Your $1.2 million offer fits with headroom.

Then the regulation. Healthcare is licensed. In some places the rules decide who can own a clinic and how it bills. Check before you spend a week on a target.

[JOSH: one thing you checked in the physio clinic's numbers or staff before settlement that every clinic buyer should check]

The $800,000 between the two prices is the owner's job. Protect the 30% you do pay for with a handover, and part of the seller's payments tied to those billings holding.

Where people lose money.

- The owner is the top biller and leaves early. The handover was a handshake. The billings go with them.
- A practitioner walks across the road with their patients. Whether a restraint holds depends on where you are and how it's written. Ask your lawyer.
- Billing problems with a government scheme or insurer come back after you own it. Get someone who knows healthcare billing to look.
- The team hears about the sale in the car park. Talk to the key people before close, with the seller's agreement.

After you own it, the order is simple. Fill the empty appointments before you hire, starting with answering every enquiry fast. Episode 51 covers speed to lead. Then recruit, because every practitioner with a full book makes the clinic less dependent on any one person. Then a second clinic nearby, sharing the practice manager, the marketing and the bookkeeping. Buy at small-business multiples, combine, and a group can sell at a bigger buyer's multiple.

[JOSH: one change you made after taking over the physio clinic that lifted revenue or profit]

Two paths from here. Alone, you offer on the headline and find out in diligence, or after settlement, that the owner was the clinic. The numbers were real. They just weren't the clinic's. Or you read the billings with someone who's bought a clinic, before you offer, and the $800,000 mistake never happens.

That's what the call is for. Owners Academy, or 1:1 advisory where I work the structure and the lender pack with you. We'll work out your strategy and first structure, and whether either fits. If neither does, I'll tell you.

If you're someone who wants a group, not a job… who'd rather read the billings than guess… then thirty days from now you can be thirty days older, or thirty days older with three clinics on a list and their billings requested.

Book a call with me below. Not a setter. Me.

## EP 77 · $3.8M revenue, 25 staff: 4 mistakes that kill a trade business deal

There are only two types of people who leave a job to buy a business. The ones who chase the exciting one. And the ones who quietly buy the aircon company with twenty-five staff and a yard full of vans.

Saul, a member of the Owners Academy, cold-called the owner of an electrical and aircon business. 25 staff. About $3.8 million revenue. Owner retiring in two years. NDA signed, early diligence. Here are the four mistakes that kill deals like his.

Trade businesses tend to sell at sensible multiples, because nobody makes a documentary about an aircon company. They often carry vans, tools and stock a lender can look at. And the owners are retiring in numbers.

A lot of trade owners started on the tools. Thirty years later they're running twenty people, quoting at night, and their knees have had enough. The kids often don't want it. Saul's owner is retiring in two years, which is useful: time for a proper handover, and an owner who cares who takes over his team.

The money is real. So are the traps. Each mistake below costs either the deal or six figures of cash. This is for people who'll put real hours in and want a group. Not for anyone who wants a business they never visit.

A trade business is worth its licence, its recurring work and its cash cycle. Not its busiest year.

You were told the good businesses are the new ones. You were told you need to know the trade to own a trade business. Neither was ever true. And if one of these ever looked out of reach, it had nothing to do with your trade skills or your savings. Nobody showed you where the money in a trade business sits.

Four mistakes, ranked by how fast they kill a deal.

I help people buy businesses like this with little to no money down. Saul found his by cold call, no broker. Book a call below. Now the four.

**Not checking who holds the licence.**

In a lot of places, an electrical or refrigeration business must have a licensed person nominated on its licence. If that's the retiring owner, the licence can walk out with them.

Find out who the nominee is in week one. Then plan the replacement: a licensed manager in place before the owner leaves, written into the deal.

This one is first because it doesn't cost you money. It costs you the deal. A business that can't legally trade on settlement day has no profit to multiply. And it's the check people leave to the week before settlement.

**Paying for installs like they repeat.**

Split revenue into recurring and one-off. Maintenance contracts and service agreements come back every year. A big install year doesn't.

Banks like contracted revenue. The next buyer pays more for it. So price the recurring share at a full multiple, and treat a bumper install year as a good year, not a new baseline.

Illustrative: an install boom adds $100,000 of profit for one year. At 4 times, paying for it as if it repeats adds $400,000 to the price for profit that may not come back. Ask for three years, split by job type, and check gross margin on each. Owners often know the business makes money. They don't always know which work makes it.

**Leaving the business in the owner's head.**

Two things live there. The quoting: thirty years of instinct nobody else can do. And the senior techs' loyalty.

Get the quoting out while the owner is still there: price lists, a simple estimator, recorded walk-throughs. Talk to the key techs before close, with the seller's agreement. Licensed staff are hard to replace.

[JOSH: anything from Saul's diligence he's happy to share, in one line, or cut]

**Ignoring debtor days.**

The counterintuitive one. Trade businesses can be profitable and short of cash at the same time. Customers pay late. Materials get paid up front.

Debtor days is how long customers take to pay, on average. It's where the cash hides. And at settlement there's usually a working capital line to agree. Get it wrong and you fund the gap yourself.

Deposits, progress billing and someone chasing invoices every week. Same customers, same work, a quarter of a million in cash. Run the other way, you need that much again just to stand still.

Where people lose money.

- Warranty callbacks on last year's jobs. Nobody agreed who pays. Put it in the contract.
- The working capital target is set off a good month, and you top it up at settlement.
- All the work comes by word of mouth. No volume knob, so growth is a guess. Test whether outbound or ads can book jobs before you plan a group on it.

The good news on lending: trade businesses often have something clinics don't. Vans, tools, stock, sometimes a yard. That can give a lender security, typically. Ring a commercial lender and ask how they treat vehicles and equipment before you write an offer. After you own it, turn every install into a maintenance plan. Episode 48 covers that.

You can do this alone. Most people who do take years and lose deals. Or you can keep doing what you're doing: keep the job, keep scrolling listings for something exciting. Keep finding out in week six of diligence that the owner is the only licence holder and the debtor book is full of 120-day invoices. All findable in week one, if you know to ask.

The call is how you ask in week one. Owners Academy, the community Saul is in, or 1:1 advisory where I work your structures and lender presentations with you. We'll work out your first structure and whether either fits.

Electrical, aircon, refrigeration, solar. Buy a few at around 4 times profit, combine them, and a group can sell at a bigger buyer's multiple.

If you're someone who'd rather own the boring business than talk about the exciting one… who'll make the cold call… then thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Older, or older with ten owners called and one NDA in play.
