## EP 78 · Cash vs paid over time: buying a $1.2M accounting firm

There's a well-kept secret in accounting firm sales. The buyers who do well don't pay for the client list. They pay for the clients who stay.

Buy a 400-client practice for $1.2 million in cash. The partner retires, 60 clients follow them out, and you've paid about $180,000 for people who left. Here's the other way to pay.

An accounting firm looks like the safest business there is. Same clients, same work, every year. That's why people with capital like it.

And the sellers are there. A lot of practices are owned by partners in their sixties with forty tax seasons behind them, no obvious successor, and young accountants who don't want to buy in. They want out, their clients looked after, and their staff kept on.

But it's a people business. The asset is relationships, and relationships can leave. This is for people with capital who want steady cash flow and a structure that protects it. Not for anyone after a quiet practice to coast in.

In a people business, the price should follow the people who stay.

Two options. Option A: pay a fixed price in cash on day one. Option B: pay over time, out of fees, with the price tied to retention. One self-test. Then the verdict.

And if a deal like this has burned someone you know, it had nothing to do with their intelligence, their capital or how carefully they read the numbers. It had everything to do with a structure that paid for clients before anyone knew who'd stay.

I help people structure deals like this with little to no money down. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**What you're buying.**

Recurring fees. Annual compliance, tax returns, bookkeeping, payroll. Work that comes back without a sales call. Don't pay for one-off projects like they repeat.

Three numbers. Recurring share: what percentage of fees repeat. In a lot of practices it's high. Check it, don't assume it. Three-year retention: of the fees clients paid three years ago, how much is still there. That tells you how sticky the firm is before you arrive. Lock-up: unbilled work plus unpaid invoices. Some firms do a year's work and bill it in one hit, and you fund that gap.

Lenders will want the same three years of financials, fees by client and by year, and proof the recurring fees really recur. Some have specific professional-practice lending. Ask.

And it's a regulated profession. Who can own a practice, sign returns or hold a practising certificate depends on where you are.

**Option A: cash on day one.**

Most people with money think the safe move is to pay cash, take a discount, own it outright. That's not how it works in a people business.

Paying it all on day one moves all the retention risk onto you. The partner has your money. The clients have a choice. If 15% of fees leave, you've paid full price for a smaller firm, and there's no one to send the bill to.

**Option B: paid over time, tied to retention.**

Lenders tend to see an accounting firm as asset-light. Little equipment, often no property. So the seller often carries more of the price anyway.

Use that. The seller is paid over six years, out of fees. After year one you measure recurring fees again. Above an agreed line, say 90%, the price stands. Below it, the price comes down in proportion. Arrangements like that are common in professional-practice sales. The wording is your lawyer's job.

Now the seller wants the handover to work as much as you do. They stay a year, introduce the top clients in person, and get paid when those clients stay.

**The self-test.**

One question. What share of fees does the retiring partner personally look after?

If it's small, and three-year retention is high, Option A is a defensible bet. If it's most of the fees, Option A is a guess with your money on it. Fees by partner tells you which one you're looking at, in one line.

The verdict: Option B by default. Pay over time, out of fees, tied to retention. Move toward cash only when the firm passes the test, and even then keep a slice deferred until year one is measured. Your capital still has a job. It sits behind the deal as comfort for the lender and the seller, not in the seller's account on day one.

And if the partner won't accept any link to retention, that tells you what they think of their own client list. Walking away is the best negotiating tactic you have.

Same firm, same client loss. Under A you've paid about $180,000 for clients who left. Under B, the seller absorbs it, because they held the relationships.

Where people lose money.

- The handover is one email. The top clients follow the partner to a mate's firm.
- Settlement lands in tax season. The team is flat out and the new owner is asking questions. Time it with the calendar.
- Lots of small, low-fee clients that take as long as the big ones. Revenue looks fine. Profit per hour doesn't.

After you own it, move clients to fixed monthly fees in advance to cut lock-up. Add advisory work for the clients who already trust you. Then roll up: there are partners retiring in every town.

Two ways to put money into a practice. Alone, you pay a fixed price, a chunk of the fees leave with the partner, and you're paying full price for a smaller firm. Or you ask for so much protection the partner walks. Or you structure it with someone who's done it, so the price follows the fees.

That's the call. Owners Academy, or 1:1 advisory where I work your structures and lender presentations with you. The NBIO template you get on joining is where a retention clause like this first gets written down.

If you're someone with capital who wants it in a real business, and wants the risk in the contract, not in your gut… thirty days from now your money is either still sitting where it is, or you've got ten practices on a list and a structure on paper.

## EP 79 · Every number from $520K profit to a $1.6M offer: buying a vet practice

Picture two people in office jobs who want to buy a vet practice. James saves for years, studies the industry, waits until he feels ready. He's still waiting.

The other is my student Jy Kingston. IT background, no finance experience. Board built in under 30 days through LinkedIn, 12 deals active. The difference wasn't money. It was the order.

A vet practice reporting $520,000 profit looks like a $2.1 million business at 4 times. What it can actually pay for is closer to $1.6 million. Pay the headline and the gap comes out of your repayments, usually the year the X-ray machine dies.

The sellers are there. A lot of practice owners are vets first: long clinical hours, after-hours calls, then the payroll. Burnout is a real issue in the profession in many places, and there's often no younger vet ready to buy in. Banks often like vet practices for the same reason they like healthcare. People keep looking after their animals, typically.

This is for people in a job who want to own something real and can give it serious hours. Not for anyone after passive income with puppies in it.

A vet practice is worth what's left after the vets and the equipment are paid for. Not what's on the P&L.

Five levels, from the reported profit down to the price. Each one takes something off. Most buyers stop at level 1.

I help people buy businesses like this with little to no money down, and Jy is one of them. Book a call below. Now the levels.

**Level 1. Reported profit.**

Three years of financials matching the tax returns. Here, illustrative: $520,000 profit. At 4 times, $2.08 million. That's the seller's number, and the one most buyers anchor on.

Before you go down a level, check the trend the profit line hides. Active clients: animals seen in, say, the last 12 to 18 months. Growing, flat or shrinking? A practice can hold its profit for a year on price rises while the client base quietly shrinks.

**Level 2. Take out the owner-vet.**

Revenue in a vet practice follows the vets. Ask for revenue by vet. If the owner is the main vet and isn't taking a market wage, the profit is overstated.

Replace their clinical work with a vet at a fair rate. Say $120,000. Profit is now $400,000. And in many markets vets are hard to hire, so check who's staying before you count on anyone.

**Level 3. Take out the equipment.**

Imaging, anaesthesia, dental, lab. Expensive, and it wears out. Ask for the equipment list with ages. If replacements are due in the next three years, they're a cost hiding in the profit.

Financed on their own, say the repayments are $60,000 a year. Profit you can lend against: $340,000.

**Level 4. The debt test.**

Free cash flow should cover repayments at least 1.5 times. $340,000 divided by 1.5 is about $227,000 a year to the seller.

Skip level 3 and you'd think it could pay $267,000. Then the equipment fails in year two and you're short.

The 1.5 isn't the bank being fussy. It's your buffer for a slow winter, a vet on leave and a vaccine fridge that dies. Below it, one bad quarter and you're funding the practice from your own pocket.

**Level 5. The price, and how to split it.**

$227,000 a year over seven years is about $1.6 million. That's the price that fits.

Split the lending. Equipment can often be financed on its own, by an equipment lender, against the equipment. The goodwill gets paid to the seller over time. Two pots, each structured for what it is. Ring both desks before you offer, and you know your real numbers before the seller does.

If the seller owns the building, that's a third conversation: a lease to you, the rent, maybe a deferral. Episode 88 covers it.

And it's regulated: vet registration, sometimes premises registration, and medicines licences, including controlled drugs.

Two lines take about half a million off the headline. Neither is a negotiating trick. They're costs the next owner pays either way.

Where people lose money.

- The owner-vet leaves early and can't be replaced for months.
- Equipment that looked fine fails, and the replacement wasn't in the model.
- The medicines licence or premises registration doesn't transfer the way anyone assumed. Have your lawyer check.
- A bigger group outbids a slow offer. In a lot of places groups have been buying practices. What you can offer is speed, a personal handover and the practice keeping its name.

After you own it: wellness plans, with vaccinations, checks and preventatives paid monthly, turn one-off visits into recurring revenue. Episode 48. Then keep your vets. Rosters, after-hours load, the equipment they work with. In a profession where people are hard to find, retention is a growth strategy. Then nearby practices sharing a manager, purchasing and marketing, bought at small-business multiples and sold as a group.

Jy's company is KingVets. **[JOSH: confirm KingVets targets vet practices, and one line on what Jy is looking for]** **[JOSH: update Jy's status if his first deal has closed by filming]**

He didn't have money you don't, or a vet degree. He built his board first, accountants and lawyers in about two weeks, then went after deals. If it's taken you years to start, it has nothing to do with your background or your savings or your contacts. It has everything to do with the order.

A board isn't a favour. It's credible operators taking founders' equity, often 2 to 10% each, instead of cash. A lender looks at who's behind you before the spreadsheet. A seller does too. That's how you borrow ten years of experience in thirty days.

So two paths. James's: save, study, wait, then offer on level 1 and miss the equipment. Or lose it to a group because the offer took eight weeks. Jy's: board first, deal second, with people who've done it beside him.

Jy's path is the one the call puts you on. Owners Academy, or 1:1 advisory on your structures and lender presentations. Join, and one of your bonuses is a 1:1 session with Jy himself.

If you're someone in a job who's always wanted to own something, and would rather borrow experience than wait ten years for it… thirty days is about how long Jy took to build his board. Thirty days from now you're older either way. Older, or older with a board forming.

## EP 80 · $600K profit, one customer at 30%: how to buy a B2B service firm

How come service firms with good contracts, good staff and good margins still sell for less than their owners expect?

Because in a lot of them the real asset isn't the contracts. It's the owner's phone. And a firm with $600,000 profit and one customer at 30% can fail its debt the month that customer leaves.

Commercial cleaning, facilities maintenance, IT support, security. Asset-light, often contracted, often owned by someone who sold every contract personally and is now tired of being the firm.

The light's on Exit, because what you check before you buy is exactly what the next buyer pays for when you sell. Buy around 4 times while the firm runs on its owner. Fix that, combine a few, and a group can sell at a bigger buyer's multiple. For owners who want a portfolio, not a phone that never stops.

A service firm is worth what it does without its owner. Concentration isn't a reason to walk. It's a reason to structure.

Four parts: the claim, why owners believe the opposite, the test, and the structure.

I help owners buy and structure firms like this with little to no money down. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The claim.**

Most owners think a firm is worth more the harder they work in it. That's not how it works. It's worth more the less it needs them.

A buyer pays for revenue that stays after settlement. If the top clients ring the owner at 9pm, they're loyal to the owner, not the firm. That's key-person risk, and the buyer prices it in.

Think of a band where the singer writes every song. Fans buy tickets for the singer. Sell the band without them and you've sold a name. A service firm where the owner wins and keeps every client is the same deal. The buyer is paying for a name and hoping.

**Why owners believe the opposite.**

Because it's how the firm was built. One person won every contract and kept every relationship. The firm grew around their phone number.

And if that's your firm, it has nothing to do with how hard you've worked or how good you are at sales. It has everything to do with a business that grew around one person. Nobody designed it that way. Which means it can be redesigned.

That's also why the seller is often open to staying on for a proper handover, paid over time. Their fatigue and your risk point at the same answer.

**The test.**

Three numbers on the first call.

Top-two customer share. Above about 30%, it's a risk you deal with in the structure.

Contracted revenue and term left. What share is under signed contracts, how many months remain, and can they be assigned? Some let the customer walk on a change of control. Your lawyer reads every major one.

Owner-managed revenue. What share comes from customers whose main contact is the owner. That's how much can walk when the owner does.

The decision rule: if all three are high, you're not walking away. You're changing the structure. Concentration with long, assignable contracts is a pricing question. Concentration on month-to-month terms, managed by the owner, is a structuring question. Both can be fair deals. Neither should be paid for in fixed debt.

And if the service is licensed, security or electrical work for example, check the licence too.

**The structure.**

Lenders look hard at concentration in an asset-light firm. So the seller often carries more of the price here. Good. Make part of it depend on the thing that worries everyone.

Size the fixed debt off the profit without the top customer. Make part of the seller's payments depend on that customer renewing. If they stay, the seller gets paid more. If they go, you're not broke. Episode 74 covers payments tied to performance.

Then fix the firm, because that's where the exit value is. Put a manager on every big relationship and introduce them while the owner is still around. Renew contracts on longer terms, offering something the customer values in return. Win mid-sized customers so the top one becomes a smaller share. And track where new work comes from. If outbound can win contracts, you've got a volume knob, not a referral habit. Episode 59 covers building the team that runs it without you.

Each of those moves the firm from "runs on the owner" to "runs on a team". That's the difference between the multiple you buy at and the multiple a bigger buyer pays for a group.

Same firm, two structures. One is fine until month four. The other survives the bad case and pays the seller well in the good one.

Where people lose money.

- The top customer has a change-of-control clause and uses the sale to retender.
- The handover is one lunch per client. Put a manager on every big relationship and introduce them while the owner is still there.
- Most contracts are month-to-month and nobody checked.
- The account manager the clients actually like resigns, because nobody told them about the sale.

You can do this alone. Most people who do take years and lose deals. Or you can keep doing what you're doing. Keep running the one firm that runs on you. Keep taking the 9pm calls. Or buy a second firm alone, where the debt cover looks fine, and the big customer retenders in month four. The protection had to be in the structure.

The call is where we build it. Owners Academy, or 1:1 advisory where I work your structures and lender presentations with you. A concentrated deal is exactly the kind we'd work through together.

If you're someone who's tired of being the phone, and wants a second firm, then a third… thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Still taking every call, or with three firms screened and a structure on paper.

# Act 17 · Industry playbooks II

## EP 81 · Every Way to Fund a $2.4M Manufacturer, From Debtors to the Seller

A $2.4 million manufacturer can carry about half its own price.

The invoices, the stock and the machines are security a lender can see. Most buyers never ask for it, so they put their own cash at the front of the deal.

Here's every layer a factory gets funded with, from the debtors to the seller.

This is for people with capital to deploy, or a business behind them, who'd rather own cash flow with hard assets under it than sit in low-yield paper. Done right, the business funds its own purchase and your capital stays free for the next one. Done wrong, you've bought a shed full of problems and borrowed your own buffer to do it. If you want a four-hour week, a factory is the worst place you could look.

The whole idea in one line. In a manufacturer the lender isn't only asking "does profit cover the repayments?" It's asking "if this goes wrong, what can we sell?"

Answer both questions and most of the deal funds itself. There are four levels to the stack. Most buyers stop at level one.

If a bank has knocked you back on a deal like this, it had nothing to do with your CV or your savings. It had everything to do with asking a cash-flow lender a question only an asset lender answers.

I help owners and investors structure deals like this with little to no money down. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1: The cash-flow lender.**

A cash-flow lender lends against profit. One rule decides how much: free cash flow has to cover all debt service at least 1.5 times. That's the 1.5 rule, and it covers every dollar of debt in the deal, bank and seller combined.

Most people think that's the whole conversation. Profit, a multiple, then a yes or a no. That's not how it works in an asset-heavy business.

Level one sets the ceiling on your repayments. It doesn't decide where the money comes from.

**Level 2: Debtors and stock.**

Asset-based lending. ABL for short. The lender advances a percentage against what's already on the balance sheet.

Debtors first. The invoices customers owe the business. Usually only the good ones. Old or disputed invoices are often left out.

Stock second. Often advanced at a lower percentage, because it's harder to sell. Finished goods and raw materials can be treated differently to half-made product.

No percentages from me. Advance rates vary a lot from lender to lender. Ring two and ask for theirs.

**Level 3: Plant and equipment.**

The machines. What they lend against depends on the valuation, the age, and how easy the machine is to resell. A common machine other plants want lends better than a custom line built for one product.

This is also where personal guarantees change. On bigger deals, roughly over $5 million, banks often don't need one, because an asset-rich business gives them their security. Typically. And if a lender wants security over everything, you have fallbacks. Security on specific assets only. Or partial security, say on the equipment but not the debtors and stock.

**Level 4: The seller.**

Whatever the assets don't cover, the seller funds. Getting paid over time. Never say "vendor finance" to a seller. Say "getting paid over time."

Plenty of these sellers will listen. A founder started making one thing thirty years ago. Now it's 30 staff, a leased factory and a customer list built on handshakes, and nobody lined up to take over. Too small for most private equity, and a lot of individual buyers get scared off by the machinery. A thin buyer pool and a tired owner is exactly when "paid over time" sounds good.

One more thing the lender wants: credibility. Put someone who's run a plant on your board. Founders' equity, typically 2 to 10%, not a salary.

Exactly on the line. No headroom. So the maths tells you what to negotiate: a payment holiday on the seller's piece, a longer term, or a lower price. A payment holiday means payments start later and the full price is still paid.

And then the value. It's common to find small manufacturers that haven't repriced properly in years. Every dollar of price rise on the same volume is pure margin. Fix price first, then add the salesperson these businesses never had, then buy the competitor down the road. Buy at around 4 times, combine, and a group can sell at a higher multiple than any one plant.

Where people lose money.

- **Borrowing your own buffer.** Debtors and stock are your working capital. Use them to fund the price and that facility can't also carry a slow month. Line up a separate working capital line, and make sure it's there on the day of settlement.
- **One customer over about 30% of revenue.** Common in manufacturing. Check whether their contract survives a change of owner. Some don't.
- **Margin that's quietly slipping.** Material costs move. Ask for gross margin by product line for three years, not one blended number.
- **Deferred maintenance.** A seller planning to exit can stop spending on the machines. Profit looks better, then a machine dies in your third month. Compare three years of equipment spend with depreciation, and pay an engineer to walk the floor before you sign.

Two paths from here. The hard one: you ask your usual bank, get a no, and decide factories can't be funded. Or you find an asset lender, spend all of it on the price, and run dry in month four. The other path: someone who's done the structures sits next to you, so you know which lender to ring, what to ask, and how to keep the buffer.

[JOSH: optional. One line on a manufacturer you or a member has looked at and what stood out; cut if none.]

On the call we work out your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy is the community, the full course, every template and daily deal discussion. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you. If neither fits, I'll tell you.

If you're someone who'd rather your capital sat behind real assets, and you'll sit in the lender meetings yourself, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. The difference is whether you've spoken to two asset lenders and have a list of 20 factories, or you're still driving past them.

## EP 82 · 5 Mistakes That Overpay for a $4M Transport Business

How come owners who already run a business overpay for their second one?

They can read a P&L. They've survived the bad years. Then they look at a transport business doing $4 million and price it on the revenue.

Here are five mistakes that turn a $1.6 million offer into a deal the cash flow can't carry.

Transport is thin-margin, asset-heavy and full of tired owners. That's a buyer's market for anyone who reads the numbers properly, and a trap for anyone who doesn't. On today's example, getting it right is the difference between a $1.6 million offer and the roughly $1.16 million the business can actually pay a seller. This is for owners who want a second business that doesn't need them at the depot at four in the morning. If you won't read boring spreadsheets, skip this industry.

One line. In a thin-margin business, small moves in cost are big moves in profit, so you price on profit after every debt, never on revenue.

If you've ever overvalued a business, it had nothing to do with being bad at maths. It had everything to do with how businesses get talked about. You were told a bigger top line means a bigger business. You were told the trucks in the yard are assets. You were told a twenty-year customer is a safe customer. None of that is true until you check it.

Five mistakes. Roughly in order of what they cost.

I help owners buy their second business with little to no money down, and check the numbers hold before they sign. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Mistake 1: Pricing on revenue.**

$4 million of revenue. $400,000 of profit. That's 10%. Thin margins are common here.

Ask for margin after the big three costs, fuel, drivers and repairs, by customer or by run. You'll often find one or two customers the business is quietly losing money on.

Rule: the offer is built on profit. Revenue is context.

**Mistake 2: Forgetting the truck finance.**

This is the expensive one. Trucks are usually on equipment finance. When you buy the business, that finance either comes with it or has to be paid out. Either way it's debt service, and it eats the same profit you're using to pay the seller.

Run the 1.5 rule. Free cash flow has to cover all debt service 1.5 times. $400,000 divided by 1.5 is about $266,000 a year for all debt. Truck finance is $150,000 of that. About $116,000 a year is left for the seller. Over ten years, about $1.16 million.

That's your ceiling, not your opening offer. The truck loans are part of the price.

Get every finance agreement and payout figure before you write an offer. And check fleet age. Old trucks mean repairs now and replacements soon.

**Mistake 3: Modelling last year's fuel.**

Say fuel is $1.2 million a year. It goes up 5% and the contracts don't let you pass it on. That's $60,000. Profit drops from $400,000 to $340,000. A 5% move in one cost took 15% of profit.

So check how much revenue sits under written contracts, how long they run, and whether they pass on fuel increases. That matters more than the names on the customer list.

It's also your first move after you buy. Reprice the runs that lose money, or let them go. Put fuel clauses in every new contract. Once one depot runs without you, add bolt-ons: small fleets with retiring owners, one depot, one dispatch team.

**Mistake 4: Buying the owner's handshakes.**

The owner started as an owner-driver and built it truck by truck. Plenty of the work sits on a handshake with a customer who's used him for twenty years. A handshake isn't nothing. But a bank can't lend against it, and the loyalty may leave with him.

If one or two customers are over about 30% of revenue, that's a red flag. Meet the big customers with the seller before close. Keep the seller in for the transition. And talk to the senior drivers yourself. Drivers can be hard to find and keep. Losing three in month one costs more than any price you chipped off.

Check the compliance record too. Safety, fatigue and maintenance logs. In regulated freight a bad record is a risk to the whole business, not just a fine.

**Mistake 5: Starting with an empty tank.**

Customers often pay on 30 days or more. Fuel and wages go out every week. So you want a working capital line in place at settlement.

And negotiate a payment holiday on the seller's piece. Payments start later, full price still paid. The first months are when you're learning the business, and a slow patch then shouldn't break you. Sellers tend to agree once the headline price is settled.

My good friend Cam got a $500,000 working capital line from the bank on his deals. Different industry, same principle.

The headline offer is about $440,000 more than the cash flow can carry once the trucks are paid. And a 5% fuel move takes another $60,000 a year off the profit that whole stack rests on.

Where people lose money.

- Finding the fleet debt late in due diligence, after the price is agreed. Ask for it before the offer.
- Assuming the twenty-year customer stays. Their loyalty was to the owner.
- Losing key drivers in the first month because nobody spoke to them before close.

You can keep doing it the way most people do. You can keep pricing businesses on revenue. You can keep finding the truck finance after settlement. You can keep modelling deals on last year's fuel price. Alone, that usually takes years and costs deals.

Or you can have someone go through the numbers with you before you sign anything.

The Academy is the community, the full course, every template and daily deal discussion. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you. If neither fits, you'll hear that from me.

If you're someone who already runs a business and knows it can't be the only one, and you'd rather check the finance agreements than trust a big revenue number, you already know the next step.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older whatever you do. The difference is whether you've got a list of 20 fleets and a question sheet in your hand, or you're still wondering if a second business is possible.

## EP 83 · Plumbing Job vs Plumbing Business: The $480K Question

A plumbing business is a good business. Local work, customers who come back, and nobody can fix a burst pipe from overseas.

Until it gets to the size where the owner can't quote every job. Then it goes from a full diary to a phone nobody answers.

On one illustrative deal, telling those two apart is worth $480,000 of price.

Two trades businesses can show the same $400,000 profit. One is a business. The other is a job with a crew attached. Pay a multiple for the second and you've bought profit that walks out the door with the seller. This is for owners at the ceiling of one business who want the next one to grow without them. If you want a van and a quiet life, this isn't that.

One line. You only pay a multiple for profit that stays when the owner leaves.

Option A is the trades job. Option B is the trades business. Then what the lender sees, a three-question test, and a verdict.

If every trades business you've looked at seemed too tied to the owner, it had nothing to do with your judgement or not having a trade. It had everything to do with what's out there. A lot of them really are jobs.

I help owners buy trades businesses with little to no money down and put a growth plan behind them. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A: The trades job.**

The owner quotes every job, wins the work and does the hard ones. Ninety per cent of new work is word of mouth. Reported profit looks strong, because the owner takes almost no wage.

The seller is real. Thirty years on the tools, back gone, knees gone, kids in other careers. But part of that profit is his labour. Replace him with an ops manager and estimator at $120,000, and $400,000 of profit is really $280,000.

And a word-of-mouth business can't choose to grow in a given month. The phone rings or it doesn't.

**Option B: The trades business.**

Someone other than the owner quotes. Much of the work repeats: maintenance plans, commercial and strata accounts, builder and insurance work that comes back every month. And some new jobs can be bought, through local search ads, emergency-call ads or outbound to property managers.

That last part is the growth lever. If you know what a booked job costs you and what that customer is worth, you can turn volume up or down. That's what makes buying the next one, and the one after, worth doing.

The usual belief is that owners like this only sell to someone they know. A nephew, a long-time employee, a mate from the trade. Saul, an Owners Academy member, has an NDA signed and is in early due diligence on an electrical and aircon business. 25 staff, around $3.8 million in revenue, owner retiring in two years. He found it with a cold call, straight to the owner. Not plumbing, but the same kind of business and the same kind of seller.

**What the lender sees.**

Trades are light on assets. Vans, tools, maybe some stock. So a lender leans on cash flow and on how stable the work is. Repeat and contracted work helps. One-off jobs from people who know the owner hurt.

Two more checks. Licences: in many places this work has to be done or supervised by a licensed person. If the owner holds the only licence, that's solved before close, not after. And seasonality: roofing can drop away in wet months, emergency plumbing can spike in cold snaps. Ask for revenue by month for three years. A business that makes its profit in six months needs a bigger buffer and a repayment schedule that survives the quiet months.

**The test and the verdict.**

Three questions to the owner. Who quoted the last 20 jobs? Where did the last 20 jobs come from? How many callbacks last year, crews going back to fix work for free?

If the answers are "me," "they all know me" and "we don't track it," you're looking at Option A.

The verdict. Buy B at a fair multiple. Buy A only if you price it as A, on profit after replacing the owner, and you have a plan to turn it into B in year one: an estimator, a booking process that answers fast, maintenance plans. Never pay a B price for an A business.

One question took $480,000 off the price. And the 1.5 rule runs off the real number, so about $186,000 a year is all you can pay the seller and the bank together.

Where people lose money.

- **The owner leaves and the phone stops.** The customers were calling him. Keep the seller in for a proper transition, at least six months, often longer.
- **The best crew leader leaves.** He wanted to buy it himself. Meet crew leaders before close and think about retention bonuses.
- **A warranty claim lands in year two.** In roofing especially, claims can turn up years later. Write down who carries pre-sale work in the contract.
- **Pricing on the growth plan.** Ads may not work in your market. Test before you rely on it, and price the deal on today's profit.

Two paths. Alone, the mistake in trades is buying the owner. You see a strong profit number, never ask who quotes the jobs, and pay a multiple on profit that leaves with the seller. Or someone who's looked at these businesses sits next to you and asks the uncomfortable questions before you sign.

If neither fits, I'll say so.

If you're someone who's hit the ceiling of one business, and you'll ask an owner where the last 20 jobs came from, you've made the decision already.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with five owner conversations behind you, knowing which business is a business and which is a job.

## EP 84 · Cutting Staff Is the Wrong Way to Profit From Childcare

There are two types of childcare buyer. The ones who plan to cut staff to make it profitable, and the ones who plan to fill the empty places.

The first type runs into the law. The second finds about $300,000 of revenue already sitting in the building.

Childcare and education pull in a lot of corporate people who want to own something that matters. Good. It's also regulated, people-heavy and tied to one site. So the money is in occupancy, and the risk is in approval and the lease. Get those right and the growth plan is already in the rooms. Get them wrong and you sign an offer you can't get approved. If you want something you never have to think about, look elsewhere.

The claim. In childcare, profit doesn't come from cutting wages. It comes from filling places in rooms you're already staffing.

Four parts. Why people believe the opposite. The empty-seat maths. The regulator. And the test.

If you've been told you can't buy a regulated business without having worked in it, that had nothing to do with your background. It had everything to do with the team around you.

I help people buy businesses like this with little to no money down, and build the team that gets them approved. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**Why people believe the opposite.**

Wages are usually the biggest cost by a distance. So cutting them looks like the lever.

That's not how it works here. Ratios set a minimum number of educators per room. You don't go below the floor. Ever. Push against it and you lose staff, then families, then possibly your approval.

**The empty seat.**

A flight with empty seats still takes off. The pilots, the crew and the fuel are paid either way, so every extra passenger is mostly margin. A room staffed to ratio works the same, right up to the child that needs one more educator.

A centre is licensed for 100 places. Say each full-time place brings in about $30,000 a year. At 70% occupancy that's 70 places and $2.1 million. Lift it to 80% and that's ten more places and about $300,000 more revenue. If those places land in rooms that already have enough educators, a large part drops to profit. If they need another educator, less does.

So ask for occupancy by room and age group for three years, not one average. A centre can be full for older kids and half-empty in the baby room, and those rooms staff differently.

Then fees. Many independent centres set fees once a year without looking hard at what's nearby. Families mostly choose on trust, the educators and the location, so a well-run centre doesn't have to be the cheapest in the suburb.

Now flip it. If occupancy falls 10 points, the same maths works against you. So you price the deal on the occupancy you have, not the occupancy you hope for.

**The regulator is your other buyer.**

In many places the approval to operate sits with the operator, and a change of owner can need regulator sign-off. So credibility here isn't only for the bank. The regulator wants to know who the approved operator is, who's responsible day to day, and whether they have the experience and qualifications.

So the order matters. First, the right person on your board: a former centre director or area manager, on founders' equity, typically 2 to 10%, not a salary. Then make regulatory approval and lease assignment conditions of your offer. No approval, no deal. Then it closes.

[JOSH: optional. One line on how regulator or licence approvals worked when you bought in healthcare; cut if not.]

The lender wants occupancy history, the lease and the compliance record. A full centre on a long lease with a clean record is a very different loan to a half-full one with two years left.

**The test.**

Three numbers. Occupancy by room against licensed places. Wages as a share of revenue, and whether they're rising faster than fees. And the lease: years left, renewal options, rent as a share of revenue, and whether it can be assigned to you. If it can't, or it runs out in two years, the business may not be what it looks like.

For education outside childcare, tutoring, training colleges, music schools, swap occupancy for enrolments and how many students come back each term. Then ask how much revenue rides on one accreditation or one funding body. If that changes, how much is left?

Ten points either way is $300,000 of revenue. Up is your growth plan. Down is why you price on today's occupancy and run the 1.5 rule on it.

Where people lose money.

- **Approval takes longer than planned** and the seller's patience runs out. Build the team before the offer.
- **The director families trust leaves after the sale.** Interview the director and senior educators before close, think about retention bonuses, and keep the seller involved for six months at least.
- **A lease clause or compliance issue nobody mentioned.** Get a lawyer who knows the sector to read both.
- **Subsidy policy changes.** In many places government subsidies are a big part of fees. Treat that like one big customer and price the risk in.

You can keep going the way most first-time buyers do here. Sign an offer, start due diligence, then find out you can't be the operator without someone qualified on the team. Watch the seller's patience run out while the paperwork catches up. Or you can build the team first, with someone who knows the order it has to happen in.

The Academy is the community, the full course, every template and daily deal discussion. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you. In this industry the board comes first, and Jy Kingston, who I've mentored, built his in under 30 days. If neither fits, you'll hear it from me.

If you're someone who wants to own something families in your area rely on, and you'll build the team before you make the offer, the decision's already made.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. The difference is whether you've read your regulator's rules, messaged a centre director about a board seat and listed 20 centres, or you're still in the same job thinking about it.

## EP 85 · The 40-Point Sheet I'd Score Every Industry On (copy it)

Two buyers. Both in corporate jobs. Both want out.

James picks his industry like a restaurant, whatever looks good that night. Six months later he's still calling owners in an industry no bank will fund.

Chad doesn't work longer hours or know more people. He spends twenty minutes on one page before his first call. Eight lines, 40 points. Here it is. Copy it.

The industry you pick sets your ceiling before you make a call. Whether a bank funds it. What multiple you buy at. Whether you can buy the next one and the one after, and sell the group for more than the parts. Six months in the wrong industry is six months with no deal flow. This is for people who'll act on the answer. If you'll score ten industries and call nobody, keep the sheet and skip the call.

One line. Score an industry on two things, can it be bought and can it grow, and kill anything that fails either.

If you've spent months unable to choose, it had nothing to do with not being smart enough. It had everything to do with having no way to compare one industry with another. You were told to buy what you love. You were told to wait for the perfect industry. You were told to read everything first. None of that tells you whether a bank will fund it.

Eight lines, each scored 1 to 5. Five for buyable, three for growable. Then the kill rules.

I help people pick the industry, build the board for it and get to a first offer. Book a call below and bring the sheet. Now the detail.

**Buyable: lines 1 to 5.**

Line 1, bankable. Will a lender fund a business like this? Assets give the bank security. Steady cash flow gives it comfort. Don't guess. Ring three lenders and ask their appetite for the sector. Twenty minutes, and it's the most important line on the sheet.

Line 2, boring. Traditional, stable, still needed in ten years. Score down anything technology is about to replace, and anything that's a fad.

Line 3, fragmented. Lots of small owners, nobody dominant. That's what makes a roll-up possible: buy at small-business multiples, around 4 times, combine, and sell the group at a higher one. An industry owned by three giants scores a 1.

Line 4, retiring owners. Plenty of owners in their fifties and sixties with no successor. That's the burden of ownership, and it's why "getting paid over time" lands.

Line 5, margins. Is there enough profit left to pass the 1.5 rule after the debt? Thin isn't automatically a no. Transport often runs thin. It just leaves less room.

**Growable: lines 6 to 8.**

Three caps stop a business growing. Score each one.

Line 6, the location cap. Can this business only ever serve one suburb, one building, one town? A single childcare centre scores low on its own. A group of them doesn't, because you get past the cap by buying more sites.

Line 7, the key-person cap. Does revenue usually depend on one person? The owner who quotes every job. The practitioner patients ask for by name. Score it low, or write down how you'll fix it in year one.

Line 8, the platform cap. In the businesses we buy, the platform is one big customer, one funder, one supplier or one referral source. A manufacturer living off one contract. A training college living off one funding stream. Score it low.

And to break a tie: can revenue be bought, with ads or outbound? If yes, you can grow on purpose.

**The kill rules.**

If bankable scores a 1, stop. No lender, no deal. If any cap scores a 1 and you can't see the fix, stop. And set a line for the total. Around 28 out of 40 is a sensible start. That's a rule of thumb, not science. Set yours and stick to it.

**Where the scores come from.**

Lenders, for bankable. They'll tell you which sectors they like and which they won't touch.

Owners, for everything else. Ten calls into an industry and you'll hear how old the owners are, where their customers come from, and whether one big customer runs their life.

Industry associations and public lists, for how many businesses there are and how big.

So your first sheet is a guess. Then you pick one and make the calls. After fifty, you score it again. Your second sheet is data.

Plumbing clears the line. Its weak line is key person, and the fix is an estimator and a sales process. Tutoring is under the line, so unless you plan to buy several, keep looking. The scores will differ in your city. The point is the sheet made you name the weak line and the fix.

Where people lose money.

- **Picking the interesting industry, not the fundable one.** Bankable is line one for a reason.
- **Scoring a 24 and going ahead anyway** because you like the industry. Months later, no lender.
- **Drift.** Five industries, a month each, never a hundred calls in any of them.
- **Staying "open to anything."** Sellers hear it. An owner takes a call more seriously from someone building a group in their industry.

Two paths. Alone, the failure at this step is drift, or picking what you find interesting instead of what a bank will fund. The other path is someone outside your own head checking the sheet with you and holding you to what it says. There's never a perfect industry. Decide at 60%.

The Academy is the community, the full course, every template and daily deal discussion, where people share how industries score in their own cities. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you, starting with which industry. If neither fits, I'll say so.

If you're someone who'd rather decide at 60% than research forever, and you'll make ten calls the week you pick, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. The difference is whether you've picked one industry and made your first ten calls, or you're still choosing.

# Act 18 · Advanced structures

## EP 86 · $1.2M once vs $200K a year for life: the 99-year offer

There's a well-kept secret in buying businesses. Some sellers don't want a price. They want an income.

Offer a 65-year-old owner $1.2 million once, or $200,000 a year for life, and a lot of them take the income. You put in no deposit. You don't need a bank. And every dollar of growth above that payment is yours.

A friend of mine in the US, Elliot, sent about 40 offers built on that idea. Three sellers signed. 100% seller finance, on 99-year terms.

Here's the cash offer against the lifetime offer, and how to tell which one wins.

A cash offer needs a bank, a deposit and a wait. A lifetime offer needs none of them. The seller is paid out of profit, like they've been paid for thirty years. The catch is real: over a long life you can pay out far more in total than the cash price. So this is for operators who will grow the business. It isn't for anyone who plans to hold it flat, and it isn't for a seller who needs cash now.

A lifetime offer is 100% seller finance, paid out of profit, for the seller's life. Two options, one test, one verdict.

When a good offer gets ignored, it has nothing to do with your age, your bank balance or your track record. It has everything to do with the shape of the offer. Most buyers still send a price, a deposit and a wait for the bank.

Meanwhile the market has moved. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. Their problem usually isn't price. It's the burden of owning the thing. They want out, and they want to be looked after. Most of them have never managed a lump sum. They've managed a business that paid them.

I work through structures like this with buyers one deal at a time. If you've got a seller who's warm but won't take a normal offer, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Option A: the cash offer.**

Illustrative. A business makes $300,000 a year in profit after paying a manager to run it. The seller is 65.

Four times profit is $1.2 million. You need a bank, a deposit and six to sixteen weeks. Then the seller holds a pile of money they have to invest, protect and make last. That's a new job, at 65, and most of them don't want it.

**Option B: the lifetime offer.**

Take profit and divide by 1.5. That's $200,000 a year, for life.

If the seller lives another twenty years, that's $4 million paid over time. More than three times the cash offer. And it arrives the way they've always been paid.

For you: $100,000 of headroom from day one. No deposit. No bank. And the real prize: if the payment is fixed and you grow profit from $300,000 to $600,000, the seller still gets $200,000. You keep $400,000.

Language matters. Never say "vendor finance" to a seller. Say "getting paid over time", or a pension from your business. That's a nickname, not a regulated pension product. Never present it as one.

**Elliot's numbers, honestly.**

Elliot sent around 40 offers. Three sellers signed LOIs at 100% seller finance on these terms. Forty offers, three signatures. That's the job.

His example: $500,000 of net operating income, $350,000 a year to the seller for life.

Now be straight about it. $500,000 divided by $350,000 is about 1.43 times coverage. My rule is 1.5. On headline numbers, it sits under my line. **[JOSH: why this structure still worked at ~1.43× coverage, below the 1.5 rule (flexing payments, step-down, holiday, or NOI before planned cuts); if it didn't fully clear, say so on camera]**

And the other two questions. **[JOSH: what happens to Elliot's payments when the seller dies, and whether he can pay the obligation out early]**

I'm showing you the gap on purpose. A clever structure that breaks the rule isn't clever.

**The three clauses.**

Every lifetime offer answers three questions before the LOI goes out.

Profit drops. A fixed payment hurts in a bad year. Your choices are a fixed payment, a share of profit, or a floor and a cap. Fixed is better for you if you grow. A share is safer if you don't. Pick on purpose.

The seller dies. Payments stop, pass to a spouse for a set period, or go to the estate. That one clause moves the deal's value more than any other.

You sell. Whoever buys your group won't want a lifetime obligation attached. So negotiate the right to pay it out for an agreed lump sum after an agreed year. Agree it now, while everyone's friendly.

Security and control sit with a top-tier lawyer, not a template.

**The test and the verdict.**

Run this test on the seller. Lifetime wins when three things are true. They're over 60 and want income more than a cheque. Profit has been steady for three years. And you have a real plan to grow it.

Cash wins when the seller needs money now, or profit swings year to year.

The verdict: don't choose for them. Put both in the LOI, side by side. Paid over a set number of years, or paid for life. Let the seller pick. Either way, you're inside 1.5. And never lead with "99 years". Lead with the income.

The seller gets more in total. You get no deposit and the upside. That trade is fair only if you actually grow it.

Where people lose money.

- Leading with "99 years". It sounds like a gimmick and the seller's accountant kills it. Lead with the income.
- No death clause. The family finds it in diligence and the deal dies.
- No buyout right. At exit, a lifetime payment hanging off the company cuts what a buyer will pay for it.
- A fixed payment under 1.5×. One bad year and you're funding the seller from your own pocket.

From here there are two paths. You can build this alone. You'll pitch it like a gimmick, forget the death clause, or sign without a buyout right and find out at exit. Or you can have someone who's done this check the structure before a seller ever sees it.

That's what the call is. We look at your seller and your numbers, and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy gives you the community, the full course and every template. 1:1 means I work your structures and lender presentations with you. If neither fits, I'll tell you.

If you're someone who'd rather own than earn a salary forever, and you'd rather send a better-shaped offer than wait years for a bigger deposit, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older either way. The only question is whether you're also holding a lifetime offer, checked by a lawyer, in front of a real seller.

Book a call with me below. Not a setter. Me.

Next: my friend Cam's structure. Ten years interest-only, with balloons in years three and ten.

## EP 87 · Ten years interest-only, balloons in years 3 and 10 (step-by-step)

Owning one good business is a phenomenal position. Until you want the second one.

Then every spare dollar goes back to the seller of the first. You go from building to treading water.

My good friend Cam closed two deals in 23 days. 100% vendor finance. Interest-only for ten years, with balloons in years three and ten.

Here's that shape, year by year, in the order the money moves.

Same business, same price. On a normal schedule, the debt eats most of the spare cash. Interest-only, you keep about $159,000 a year more in the first three years, in the example I'll show you. That's the money that buys the second business. The cost is two lump sums you must plan for on day one. This is for operators putting in real hours. A balloon punishes owners who aren't paying attention.

Interest-only means you pay the seller only the interest each year. A balloon is a lump sum of the price due on a set date. Put them together and you change when you pay, not what you owe.

You were told debt has to come down from day one. That principal and interest from settlement is the responsible thing. For a home loan, maybe. For a business you plan to grow, it's one option. When a deal feels too tight to grow, it usually has nothing to do with how hard you work. It has everything to do with the schedule you signed.

Five stops on the timeline.

I help buyers build structures like this and the plan to pay them out. If the normal repayments on your deal are too heavy, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Settlement: the coverage test.**

Illustrative, not Cam's deal. A business makes $400,000 a year of free cash flow. The price is $2 million, carried by the seller at 5%.

The rule for the whole series: free cash flow covers yearly debt payments at least 1.5 times.

Principal and interest over ten years is about $259,000 a year. Coverage: 1.54. You pass by a hair. One bad year and you're under.

Interest-only is $100,000 a year. Coverage: four times.

**Years one to three: the breathing room.**

You've got about $300,000 a year left over, against $141,000 on principal and interest. That gap is the point of the structure.

Use it two ways. Grow profit. And fill the balloon fund: about $170,000 a year into a separate account you don't touch gives you $500,000 by year three.

Cam also got $500,000 of working capital from the bank on top of the vendor finance. That's runway for the business. It isn't balloon money.

And 23 days wasn't luck. What made it possible was a board, a lender relationship and a structure ready before the seller said yes. Episode 92 goes through what had to be true.

**Year three: the first balloon.**

Say $500,000 is due at the end of year three. Illustrative. **[JOSH: the rough size of Cam's year-3 and year-10 balloons, as dollars or a share of the price, if he's happy to share]**

Three ways to pay it. Save for it. Refinance it: by year three you have three years of your own statements and tax returns, and a bank may lend you the balloon over a normal term. May. Or sell before it lands. The best plans use two of the three.

Start the bank conversation twelve months before the balloon, not two. If your only plan is "I'll refinance", you've made the bank's mood your plan.

**Years four to ten: the long tail.**

The balance drops to $1.5 million. Interest falls to $75,000 a year. Coverage on today's profit is over five times, and more if you've grown it.

This is the window to buy the next business. And the reason the seller agreed to any of this is simple. They get interest every year, a real chunk in year three, the full price by year ten, and a buyer who closed fast. Speed and certainty matter to a retiring owner more than most buyers think.

**Year ten: the final balloon.**

$1.5 million is due. If you're building a roll-up, this one is often paid out of the exit. Buy at three to five times, combine and grow, sell the group at a higher multiple, settle the note on the way out. Or refinance it against seven more years of your own numbers.

Interest-only costs you about $235,000 more interest over ten years. It buys you about $477,000 more cash in the three years that matter most. It's a cash-flow tool, not a discount. You owe every dollar.

The decision rule: take interest-only when you have a written plan to turn the spare cash into more profit, and a named source for every balloon. If you'd just spend it, take principal and interest and buy a business with more profit instead.

Where people lose money.

- Spending the breathing room on yourself. Then year three arrives and you've only delayed the problem.
- No default clause read. A balloon you can't pay is a default, and depending on what you signed, the seller may be able to take the business back.
- No extension right. Negotiate the right to extend a balloon for a fee or a higher rate, in case the bank says not yet.
- No stress test. Run year three with profit 20% lower. If the plan breaks, fix it before you sign.

You can keep doing what most buyers do. Sign repayment schedules that leave nothing to grow with. Treat the balloon as future you's problem. Find out twelve weeks out that the bank wants six months of paperwork. Or you can build the balloon plan on day one with someone who's seen how these go.

On the call we model your deal both ways and decide whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy is the community, the course and every template. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you, including the one in year three.

If you're someone who's already proved you can run one business, and you want the second without starving the first, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. The question is whether you'll also have your next deal modelled both ways and a balloon plan on one page.

Next: when the seller owns the building, and the lease becomes part of your finance.

## EP 88 · 4 mistakes that cost $240,000 when the seller owns the building

How come buyers with real capital still overpay for businesses?

They've got money, advisers and patience. But when the seller also owns the building, a lot of them miss one line in the accounts. In the example I'll show you, that line is worth $240,000.

Here are the four mistakes, cheapest to most expensive.

In owner-run businesses, the owner often owns the property too. That changes the profit you're paying a multiple on, the cash you need at settlement, and how keen the seller is to carry finance. Get it right and the building helps fund your deal. Get it wrong and you pay for profit that doesn't exist. This is for buyers who want to operate businesses. If you'd rather be a landlord, that's a different game.

When the seller owns the building, the lease is part of the price. Four mistakes, ranked.

When a buyer overpays here, it has nothing to do with being careless or bad with numbers. It has everything to do with accounts prepared by an owner who is also the landlord. They were never built for a buyer.

I help buyers structure deals where the property is part of the picture. If your target's owner also owns the building, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Mistake 1. Missing the above-market rent.**

The cheapest mistake is the one you miss in your favour. Some owners charge their own business above-market rent, because it's a tax-effective way to pay themselves. Profit looks worse than it is. Normalise it and you can be the buyer who sees value the others don't. That's money left on the table, not lost. It's still a mistake.

**Mistake 2. Treating the lease as paperwork.**

When the seller is your landlord, the lease is negotiated in the same conversation as the price. That's leverage.

Rent in arrears: you pay after the month, not before. On my first deal, a physio clinic, the rent was in arrears. No deposit, a nine-month payment holiday on the vendor finance, and rent paid after the month.

No bond: cash at settlement you don't have to find. A deferral: six months' rent deferred and paid back over two years. At $60,000 a year, that's $30,000 that stays in the business while you settle in. And a long lease with options, because lenders won't lend comfortably against a short one.

How to ask: agree the headline price first. Then raise the lease. "Given the transition, I'd like rent in arrears, no bond, and the first six months deferred. You still get every dollar."

**Mistake 3. Pricing on profit with no rent in it.**

Illustrative. A business shows $300,000 of profit. The owner owns the building and charges the business nothing. Market rent is $60,000 a year.

Once you own it, you pay rent. So real profit is $240,000. At four times, that's $960,000, not $1.2 million. A $240,000 swing from one line.

Before any offer you want three years of financials, matching tax returns and a revenue breakdown. Add one question: who owns the building, and what does the business pay for it? Then get a local agent's market-rent opinion. Your coverage maths has to include the rent too. If it only works with no rent, it doesn't work.

**Mistake 4. Buying the building.**

Buyers with capital think the building is the safe part. Property is the real asset, the business is the risky bit, so owning both is conservative.

That's not how it works. The business is the asset. Cash flow pays everyone, and cash keeps a newly bought business alive. Tie that cash up in bricks early and you've slowed the part that builds value: cash flow now, and multiple arbitrage when you sell a group.

And the seller keeping the building works in your favour. Sell the business, keep the property, and they have two incomes: your payments and your rent. The rent only keeps coming if the business keeps going. So their retirement depends on you doing well. That's alignment you can't buy.

It also shrinks what you fund. If you're not buying the property, you're not borrowing against bricks. The price is just the business. That's a smaller number for the seller to carry and a smaller number for a bank to look at.

Default: buy the business, lease the building, revisit the property in a few years. You'll also hear "sale-and-leaseback": sell a property to an investor and lease it straight back. Cash now, rent for years. It only makes sense if the rent still fits inside 1.5 coverage.

Priced on real profit, the deal clears 1.5 comfortably and the seller still gets about $184,000 a year from you. Priced on the flattered number, you've paid $240,000 for rent you'll be paying anyway.

Where people lose money.

- Closing without the lease signed. The seller can raise the rent once you own it. Set rent reviews and options in writing first.
- A short lease with no options. The bank won't lend against it, and the deal stalls at finance.
- Forgetting the rent in your coverage. Seller payments plus rent, against what the business really makes. If it only clears 1.5 with free rent, it doesn't clear.
- A third-party landlord ignored. The lease still has to be assignable and long enough for a lender. You just have less to negotiate.

Two paths. Alone, the mistakes at this step are expensive: a multiple on profit that only existed because rent was zero, or the business's cash spent on a building right when the business needed it. Or you have someone who's been through these leases look at yours before you sign.

On the call we look at your target and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy is the community, the course and every template. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you, leases included.

If you're someone with capital who wants it in real cash flow, priced on what a business actually earns, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you're thirty days older. Either way. The difference is whether market rent is in every model in your pipeline and your lease asks are written into the LOI.

Next: borrowing against the business's own receivables and stock.

## EP 89 · $1M of unpaid invoices is an $800K loan

Every day, someone online is telling business buyers how to fund the gap. Raise from investors. Take a personal loan. Save for two more years.

How about something different. A business with a million dollars of eligible unpaid invoices can support around $800,000 of borrowing. It's a loan the business hasn't asked for yet.

A newly bought business usually runs out of working capital before it runs out of profit. Wages, suppliers, the gap between doing the work and getting paid. Fund that from your pocket and you've put your own money in after all. Fund it from investors and they get a say in your company. Asset-based lending funds it from the business's own balance sheet. It suits businesses that invoice other businesses on terms. It doesn't suit a café.

Asset-based lending, ABL, is a line of credit secured on the business's receivables and stock. The lender advances a percentage of what's eligible. That total is the borrowing base.

When a newly bought business runs dry in month four, it has nothing to do with the new owner's effort. It has everything to do with a structure that funded the price and forgot the working capital.

Three parts: why the receivables are the security, how the base is worked out, and the test.

I help buyers put the full stack together: seller, bank, working capital. If your target carries big receivables or real stock, book a call below. Now the detail.

**The lender isn't lending against you.**

Before you know how a lender reads a business, receivables look like admin. Money that turns up eventually. After you know, they're a facility. Every invoice under 90 days is borrowing power. Every invoice that ages past it is borrowing power you just lost.

That's why this works for a buyer with no track record. The lender is looking at the invoices, the stock and your board. It's still credibility first, but the asset carries most of the weight.

It suits manufacturers, distributors, logistics, trades doing commercial work on 30 or 60 days. On bigger deals, roughly over $5 million, banks often don't need a personal guarantee, because an asset-rich business gives them their security. Typically. Every lender and deal is different.

**How the borrowing base works.**

Illustrative. A distributor turns over $8 million and makes $800,000 of free cash flow.

Receivables are $1.2 million. Lenders usually exclude very old invoices, say over 90 days, money owed by related parties, and anything above a limit for one big customer. Say $1 million is eligible. Advance rates on receivables are often around 70 to 85%. Use 80%: $800,000.

Stock is $1 million. It's harder to sell if things go wrong, so the rate is lower. Use 40%: $400,000.

Borrowing base: about $1.2 million. It's a ceiling, recalculated every month as receivables and stock move.

Use it for working capital first. A working capital line at settlement is often around 10% of revenue. Here, about $800,000. My good friend Cam got $500,000 of working capital from the bank on top of 100% vendor finance. That's the job this line does.

Then, maybe, part of the price. Some buyers draw on the line to pay part of the price so the seller carries less. It can work, but only with real headroom. If you spend the line on the price, there's nothing left for the slow month.

**The test.**

Before you count on a line, answer three questions about the target.

What are the debtor days? If customers pay in 75 days and the cut-off is 90, one slow quarter pushes invoices out of the base.

What share of receivables is the biggest customer? One customer at a third of the ledger gets capped, and if they stop paying, the line wobbles.

Who owns collections after settlement? With ABL, your receivables ledger is the loan. Collections stops being admin and becomes a finance job. Somebody owns the aged-debtors report every week.

If you can't answer all three, you don't know the size of your line yet. And if the target has almost no receivables or stock, ABL isn't your tool. Vendor finance and a normal working capital line are.

The line moves with the business. When sales grow, it grows. When sales drop, it shrinks, right when you'd want it most. So it never replaces the 1.5 rule. Seller payments, bank payments and ABL interest, against free cash flow. 1.5 minimum.

Where people lose money.

- Spending the line on the price. Then there's nothing for the slow month, and a bad quarter puts you over your limit.
- Springing subordination on the seller in week ten. An ABL lender wants first security over receivables and stock, so the seller's note ranks behind it. Explain it at the LOI: "The bank gets paid first on the invoices and stock. You still get every payment we agreed."
- Ignoring the costs and covenants. Interest on what you draw, set-up fees, sometimes an unused-limit fee. Break a covenant and the lender can freeze the line.

You can keep doing what most buyers do. Count every receivable when half are ineligible. Spend the line on the price. Let collections slide after settlement, so the line shrinks just as payroll is due. Or you can build the stack properly the first time, with someone who's done it.

On the call we look at your target's balance sheet and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy is the community, the course and every template. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you, including how the seller, the bank and the ABL lender fit together.

If you're someone who's run a business and knows the gap between doing the work and getting paid, and you want the next one without handing over equity, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. Either with a borrowing base worked out on your target and two lenders' answers on your sheet, or without.

## EP 90 · Every layer of a $10M deal, from bank debt to equity, explained

There are only two types of buyers on bigger deals. The ones who fill the gap with debt and keep the company. And the ones who fill it by giving the company away.

On an illustrative $10 million deal with a $2 million gap, that choice is about $1.4 million of interest, or about $8 million of your exit.

Here's every layer of the stack, from first paid to last.

Bigger deals are where the real money is. The roll-up is buying businesses at three to five times profit, combining and growing them, and selling the group at eight, ten, fifteen times. But on bigger deals the seller and the bank rarely reach the full price. How you fill the gap decides how much of that exit you keep. This isn't for a first-time buyer looking at a $500,000 business. Park it. It'll matter later.

A deal is a stack of layers. Each one ranks in order of who gets paid first, and the further down you go, the more it costs. Five levels.

When owners stall at one business, it has nothing to do with ambition or work ethic. It has everything to do with only being shown two options: save more, or sell equity.

I help buyers structure bigger deals and present them to lenders. If the usual layers don't reach your price, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Level 1. Senior bank debt.**

Top of the stack. First in line, cheapest money. On our illustrative deal, a business makes $2 million of free cash flow and the price is $10 million, five times. The bank lends 50%: $5 million at 7% over ten years. About $712,000 a year.

On bigger deals, roughly over $5 million, banks often don't need a personal guarantee, because an asset-rich business gives them their security. Typically. Every lender and deal is different.

**Level 2. The seller note.**

The seller carries 30%: $3 million, interest-only at 5% for the early years. $150,000 a year. Often the cheapest layer of all, and the one most buyers under-use.

Before you reach for anything more exotic, push this one. A lot of people assume bigger deals need a big cheque. My friend Jason Nagy's first deal was a $5.5 million radiology business with zero down, on a 60/40 structure. Seven years later he sold to private equity for $200 million. That first deal didn't need a big cheque. It needed a structure.

**Level 3. Working capital.**

The line that keeps the business running, often around 10% of revenue, sometimes against receivables and stock, like last episode. It's runway, not price. Don't spend it closing the gap.

On a $10 million deal that's real money, and it's also where lenders look hardest at you. Your lender presentation has to show the line is sized to the business, not to the gap.

Bank plus seller is 80%. There's still a $2 million hole.

**Level 4. Mezzanine.**

Mezzanine sits below the bank and above your equity. It's usually subordinated, ranking behind the bank. Often interest-only, principal due at the end. Sometimes part of the interest is added to the balance instead of paid, which helps cash today and costs you more later. Sometimes the lender wants a small slice of equity on top, called a kicker. Pricing varies widely. It's typically well above bank debt, often in the double digits.

Illustrative: $2 million at 14%, interest-only. $280,000 a year.

[JOSH: whether Arthur's $23M deal, or another deal you know first-hand, used mezzanine; one line on why, and confirm whether Arthur is a friend or student]

**Level 5. Equity.**

Bottom of the stack. Last paid, all the upside. Fill the $2 million gap by selling equity and an investor might want, say, 40% of the company. Illustrative. Now they get a say in when and how you sell.

Run it to the exit. Say you grow the group and the equity is worth $20 million in five years. 40% of that is $8 million handed over. Five years of mezzanine interest is about $1.4 million.

Debt over dilution. Mezzanine is the most expensive debt you'll put in a deal, and it can still be the cheapest option on the table. Use it when three things are true: the cheaper layers are maxed out, selling equity would cost you control or most of the exit, and the business still clears 1.5 with the mezzanine in.

It passes at 1.75. Then you stress it, and at 1.40 it's under the line. So it only works if you're confident in that profit, or you fix it: a longer bank term, a holiday on the seller note, or part of the mezzanine interest rolled up for two years.

Where people lose money.

- Using mezzanine to rescue a weak deal. It's a growth tool. If you need it to make the numbers work, the deal is wrong.
- Signing a kicker and rolled-up interest without working out what they cost at exit.
- The seller finding out in week twelve they rank third. If they're carrying $3 million behind two lenders, the intercreditor agreement gets explained to them in plain English before they sign, and they get their own lawyer.

Two paths. Alone, bigger deals get dangerous. More lenders, more lawyers, more documents, and it's easy to stack layers until the price is covered and forget to test a bad year. Or you build the stack with someone who's seen where these break.

On the call we draw your stack and work out whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits. The Academy is the community, the course and every template. 1:1 is me working your structures and lender presentations with you. On bigger deals, that lender presentation is most of the work.

If you're someone who's hit the ceiling of one business, and you'd rather fill the gap with a structure than give the company away, you've already decided.

Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older. The only question is whether your biggest target's stack is drawn, stress-tested and in front of a lender.
