# Act 19 · Deal breakdowns

## EP 91 · No deposit, 7 years at 4%: my first deal, line by line

The first business I bought had no deposit. And the seller still got paid every dollar.

There are two types of first-time buyers. The ones who argue about the price, and the ones who write the terms. I was the second type. A physio clinic. In year one I pulled $150,000 out of it and built mid six figures in equity.

Five lines carried that deal. One mattered more than the rest. I'll walk them in the order the seller saw them.

Price decides what you pay. Terms decide who pays it. Get the terms right and the business funds its own purchase. Get them wrong and you've bought a job with a loan attached. This is for people who'll read a term sheet line by line and do the maths themselves. If you want someone to hand you a business, skip this one.

One line: every term in a deal does a job that cash would normally do.

I was 25. Unemployed, no savings, zero experience running a business. So every line had to replace money I didn't have. There are five: the ask, the term and rate, the holiday, the rent, and the result.

If you earn well and still own nothing, it has nothing to do with your savings rate or how hard you work. It has everything to do with the path you were handed. Save a deposit, ask a bank, wait. That takes years, and at the end the bank can still say no, or private equity outbids you.

I help people build their first deal like this, term by term. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The ask: "getting paid over time".**

The seller didn't see a price first. They saw a plan for how they'd get paid.

100% vendor finance means the seller funds the whole purchase and is paid out of the business over time, instead of a lump sum from me. I never once said "vendor finance" to them. I said "getting paid over time." One sounds like a trick. The other sounds like a pension.

[JOSH: how you found the clinic, how long from first contact to settlement, and what the seller wanted out of it (retirement, the staff looked after, staying on).]

The rule: find out what the seller wants first. Then every term becomes an answer to something they told you.

"You said you want to step back but keep an eye on things. If you're paid over seven years, you've got a reason to stay close and help it go well." Same term. Framed as their benefit, not your lack of money. Then you put it in a written offer, so nobody remembers it differently a month later.

**Seven years at 4%, principal and interest.**

From the seller's side, seven years is a reasonable window, with interest on top of the price. From mine, 4% means the interest doesn't eat the cash flow. And principal and interest means the debt actually falls every year, instead of sitting there waiting to bite later.

[JOSH: the purchase price and the clinic's annual profit, or a range you're happy to say on camera. If not, the illustrative numbers below carry it.]

The test is one sum. Free cash flow divided by the yearly repayment. My minimum is 1.5. Under that, you change the term, the rate or the price until it passes.

**The nine-month payment holiday.**

This is the line I promised you. Nine months, no repayments.

The first months after you buy are the riskiest. Staff are nervous, patients are watching, and you're learning the systems. A holiday means the cash the business makes in that window stays in the business. You build a buffer before the first repayment ever goes out.

To the seller, you frame it around the team. "You said the first year matters because of your staff. A few months before the first payment gives the business a cushion while everyone settles in."

Read how it's written. Sometimes interest still builds up during the holiday. Get your lawyer to spell it out.

**Rent in arrears, no deposit.**

Rent in arrears means paying at the end of the period, not the start. A small line. But when you have no money, every dollar you don't have to find on day one counts. No deposit means exactly that. Nothing down from me.

[JOSH: who owned the premises (the seller or a third-party landlord), and what the $150K in year one was made of (salary, distributions or both).]

**The result, and the four that didn't close.**

Year one, $150,000 out and mid six figures in equity. At 25 that made me the youngest person in Australian history to close a no-money-down healthcare deal, JC Health Group. I run a healthcare group full time today.

Before the clinic, four deals fell over. Around a thousand cold calls. Before I understood structure, I kept getting the same result. **[JOSH: one line on what you were focused on in those four deals, and the one lesson from them that shows up in the clinic's terms.]**

Once I understood that the terms carry the deal, I stopped treating price as the whole conversation. I built every offer around what the seller wanted. Then the clinic closed. If I'd stopped at deal three, there's no clinic, no group and no episode.

Put it together. The seller got paid in full, over time, at a fair rate. The holiday protected the first year. After that, repayments came out of cash the business had already made. Every line solved a problem for someone. That's how you read any term sheet: what does this line solve, and for whom?

At $300,000 of free cash flow the deal passes with room. At $200,000 it's 1.2, too tight, so you'd change a line. And the seller walks away with about $166,000 more than a lump sum, which is why "paid over time" lands.

Where people lose money.

- Fighting over price and giving away the terms. A few percent off the price is worth less than nine months of kept cash.
- Not asking for a holiday at all. On these numbers that's about $225,000 of buffer gone.
- Letting interest build during the holiday without knowing it. On $1M at 4%, that's about $30,000 added to the debt.
- Panicking when a seller says no to one line. Trade instead. A shorter holiday for a better rate. A longer term for a higher price. The sum tells you what you can give.

You can do all of this alone. Most people who do take years, lose deals, and get the holiday written wrong. Or you can do it with someone who's already made those mistakes.

On the call we work out your strategy and your first structure, and whether that's the Owners Academy, with every template and daily deal discussion, or 1:1 advisory, where I work your structures and lender presentations with you.

If you're someone who'd rather read a term sheet line by line than wait five more years for a deposit, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with your five lines written and your first offers out.

It's not for four-hour-work-week people, or anyone who'll watch for another year without sending an offer.

## EP 92 · 2 deals in 23 days at 100% vendor finance: what the seller saw

There's a well-kept secret among people who buy businesses fast. They don't rush.

My good friend Cam closed two deals in 23 days. Both 100% vendor finance. Interest-only for ten years, with balloon payments in years three and ten. And $500,000 of working capital from the bank.

Speed like that is built before day one. Here's the deal in the order the seller saw it.

Time kills deals. A bank-funded deal often takes 6 to 16 weeks. Vendor finance can close in 2 to 4. Every extra week is a week for the seller to cool off, staff to hear rumours, or another buyer to turn up. If you already run one business, you don't have months of spare evenings to drag a deal out. This is for people who'll do the boring preparation first.

One line: speed is what preparation looks like from the outside.

Most people think fast deals are risky. They think speed means skipping steps, or that you need years of experience to move quickly. That's not how it works. A deal moves at the speed of the seller's decision when everything on your side is already done.

If a deal you looked at dragged on and died, it has nothing to do with your intelligence or how badly you wanted it. It has everything to do with the order you did things in.

The seller sees five things: the buyer, the document request, the offer, the money behind it, and the settlement.

I help people get that preparation done so they can move when a seller says yes. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**The buyer.**

Before any number, the seller asks one question. Who is this person?

You answer it before you call. A board of credible people. A one-page executive summary. A proper offer on letterhead. When that question is already answered, a seller can decide in days, because trust no longer has to be earned over months.

[JOSH: what Cam's two businesses were (industry, rough size, related or separate), and what he already had in place before day one: board, lawyers, lenders, templates.]

**The document request.**

Next the seller sees what you ask for. Three years of financials, the matching tax returns, and revenue broken down by financial year. All of it, on the first serious call.

A buyer who asks for the right documents straight away looks like someone who has done this before. And diligence starts that week, not a month later.

Compare the usual buyer. No lawyer yet, so two weeks finding one. Wrong documents requested, so diligence starts late. No bank conversation, so working capital is a question mark at the end. Each gap adds weeks. Together they add months, and months are where deals die.

**The offer: interest-only with balloons.**

Here's the structure from the seller's side. They get interest every year, plus two lump sums. A partial payout in year three, the rest in year ten. For an owner who wants to be looked after, that reads as income plus certainty.

From the buyer's side, low repayments early leave cash in the business while you grow it. The catch is the balloons. They're real money, and you plan from day one how you'll pay them: out of profit you've grown, by refinancing with a bank, or both. No plan, and interest-only is just a later problem.

**The money behind it: working capital.**

Then the seller sees that the buyer can actually run the place. Cam got $500,000 of working capital from the bank. That pays wages in a slow month, covers fees at settlement, and funds the growth that pays the balloons. It's the line first-time buyers forget. Ask a lender early: do you do working capital lines at settlement, and what do you need to see?

[JOSH: how Cam got the bank comfortable with the $500K line. No bank name unless Cam is happy to give it.]

**Settlement.**

Day 23 isn't luck. It's what happens when the lawyer, the accountant and the lender were lined up before the seller said yes.

Here's the kit I'd want on the shelf before calling a single seller. A one-page executive summary with your board on it. An NDA and a document request ready to send. An offer template with your structure options already written in. A lawyer who has agreed to act. An accountant who turns diligence around fast. And two or three lenders who already know your plan and your working capital ask.

None of that needs a deal to exist. All of it can be done this month. Then the clock starts on the seller's decision, not on the day you start getting organised.

Interest-only frees about $80,000 a year while you grow. The seller still collects close to $400,000 in interest. Both sides win, as long as the year-three $300,000 has a plan behind it. Three years of $80,000 kept is $240,000, most of that first balloon, if you actually keep it. If you can't see how you'd pay a balloon, don't agree to it.

Where people lose money.

- Finding a motivated seller before they're ready, then spending six weeks building the team while the seller walks.
- Signing interest-only with no plan for the balloon. That's a $300,000 bill in year three with nothing set aside.
- Forgetting working capital and running out of cash in the first slow quarter.
- Confusing fast with rushed. Rushed skips diligence. Fast has diligence starting on day one.

If you're going to buy a business, you have no choice but to get prepared first. The only choice is how long it takes. Most people do it in the wrong order: seller first, scramble second, deal gone cold.

If you're someone who'd rather do the boring work now so you can move fast later, this is for you. Thirty days from now your kit is either still a list, or it's on the shelf, ready for the next seller who says yes.

## EP 93 · A bank funded all of a $2.4M first deal. Here's what it checked

How come people with a salary, a degree and a clean record find it so hard to get a bank behind a business purchase?

Christian Dawes was unemployed. No higher education. A criminal record. His first deal was $2.4 million, and the bank funded all of it. More than all of it.

Banks don't lend to your past. Here's what this one checked, in the order it checked it.

Bank debt costs you interest. Investors cost you equity, control and the exit. A bank is set up to lend, and investors take months per cheque. So if you can get a bank to fund the deal, you keep the whole upside. This is for people who'll put their numbers in front of a lender and let the numbers talk. If you're waiting for your CV to be perfect, it isn't for you.

One line: a bank lends to a business's cash flow, not to your history.

Christian is an example I use, not a student of mine. **[JOSH: one line on how you know Christian's story, plus his industry and what the business earned if that's public. No bank name.]**

You were told you need a big deposit, a spotless record and years in the industry. Christian had none of those. A bank funded his deal anyway, and then some.

If a bank has knocked you back, it has nothing to do with your degree or your past. It has everything to do with what you put in front of them.

A credit file reads in five parts: the ask, serviceability, security, management, and the extra.

I help people build the lender pack that gets a bank to yes. Book a call below. Now the file.

**The ask.**

The first page is the ask. How much, over how long, and what for. A vague ask reads as a vague buyer.

Walk in as an interviewer, not a beggar. "I'm building relationships across the banking sector. Here's my team, here's what we're looking at. What are your parameters?" Minimum debt cover, industries they like, deal sizes, personal guarantee policy, and whether decisions are local or central. You're shopping, not pleading. Talk to lots of them. One bank's policy can rule out an industry another bank loves.

**Serviceability.**

Then the question that matters most. Can the business repay the debt out of its own cash, with room to spare?

My rule is free cash flow at least 1.5 times the yearly repayments. That one ratio does more for your application than anything on your résumé. Bring a simple model with a base case and a bad case, and show the cover in both.

Bring a pack, not a pitch. The one-page executive summary. Short bios of your board and external team. Three years of financials and matching tax returns. The model. A bank sees plenty of people with a dream and a spreadsheet. A tidy pack tells them you'll be easy to deal with after settlement too.

**Security.**

Next: if it all goes wrong, what can the bank sell? Equipment, property, receivables, stock. That's why you pick an industry that's asset-rich enough to lend against.

A service business with nothing on the balance sheet has to carry the whole loan on cash flow alone. An asset-rich one gives the bank a second way out, and that's what turns a maybe into a yes.

On bigger deals, roughly over $5 million, a personal guarantee often isn't needed because the assets give the bank its security. Typically. Every lender and every deal is different, and this one was $2.4 million, so ask.

**Management.**

Then: who's running it? Notice what's not in the file. Your degree. Your old job. This is where a record or no degree would usually hurt, and where borrowed credibility does its job. A credible board, an experienced operator, a proper M&A lawyer and a decent accounting firm. Then the answer isn't one person with a past. It's a team with a plan.

[JOSH: what Christian had around him (board, operator, advisers) and whether he gave a personal guarantee, only if known and public. Otherwise cut and keep the general point.]

**The extra: over-financed.**

Last page. Over-financed means the bank lent more than the price. On a first deal that extra is usually working capital and costs: wages in a slow month, the legal and accounting bills, a first-year buffer. It's the difference between owning a business and owning one you can afford to run.

[JOSH: roughly how much above $2.4M the bank lent, and what it covered, if you know.]

The over-finance isn't free. Every dollar of working capital raises the cash flow the business has to show. Know that number before you walk in.

Now compare the alternative. Raise that $2.4 million from investors instead, and you might give away a large slice of the company for good. The bank wants $300,000 a year and then it's done. The investor wants a share of every dollar, including the exit. As long as the deal passes 1.5, debt is usually the better trade.

Where people lose money.

- Walking in with a story and no numbers, or numbers and no team.
- Picking an industry with nothing to lend against, then wondering why the bank wants a personal guarantee.
- Talking to one bank, getting one no, and deciding banks don't lend to people like them. A no tells you about that bank's policy. Write down why, fix it, walk into the next one.
- Raising equity because the bank felt hard. On a deal that passes 1.5×, that's giving away part of the exit to avoid a few meetings.

There are two paths into a bank. Alone, you learn what the credit team wants one rejection at a time. Or you walk in with a pack that's already been pulled apart by people who've done it.

If you're someone who'd rather let the numbers talk than wait for a perfect CV, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older either way. Or thirty days older with a lender pack built and your first bank meetings done.

## EP 94 · One cold call to a $3.8M NDA (copy it)

Every day, the same listed businesses get shown to the same buyers. If it's on a website, you're one offer in a pile.

Saul skipped the pile. One cold call, straight to the owner, no broker. It turned into a signed NDA on an electrical and air-con business: 25 staff, about $3.8 million in revenue, owner retiring in two years.

Here's that deal in the order the owner saw it. Copy it.

If you've got capital sitting in low-yield assets, the money isn't the hard part. Finding the deal is. Listed businesses get bid up. An owner you call first has no auction to run. And this deal hasn't closed. It's at NDA and early diligence, which is the point. This is what the start looks like, and most people never get here.

One line: the best deals are bought, not found on a website.

If you've looked and found nothing worth buying, it has nothing to do with your judgement or your timing. It has everything to do with where you were looking. Plenty of good businesses never get listed, and some of their owners would talk to the right buyer if anyone asked.

Saul didn't wait for a listing or an inside line. He made the call. That's the whole difference.

The owner sees five things: the call, the NDA, the document request, the conversation about what they want, and the offer.

I help people go from cold calls to signed NDAs to real offers. Book a call below. Now the call.

**The call.**

Short and direct. Skip "how are you going today." Who you are, why you're calling, one question.

"Hi, it's [name]. I'm building a group that buys businesses like yours. Have you ever thought about selling?"

Most say no. Some say not yet. A few say maybe. If they ask whether you're a broker, be straight: you found the business, you like it, and you're buying, not selling. Owners hear from brokers all the time. A real buyer calling directly is rarer, and they can tell.

[JOSH: how many calls Saul had made before this one, and what the owner said when he asked about selling. Two lines, only what Saul's happy to share.]

**The NDA.**

When an owner's interested, you send an NDA. It protects their information and shows you're serious. It's also a test. An owner who won't sign a simple NDA probably isn't ready to sell.

Send it the same day they say maybe. Not next week. Have it sitting ready before you make your first call, so the owner's interest never has time to cool.

**The document request.**

Then you ask for three things. Three years of financial statements, the matching tax returns, and revenue broken down by financial year. An owner who sends them is motivated. If the financials and the tax returns don't match, that tells you something too.

In a trades business, look at the mix of one-off jobs and maintenance contracts. The contracts repeat, so they're usually worth the most. Check who holds the licences, often the owner. And with 25 staff, find out which ones the customers actually know.

[JOSH: what Saul found in early diligence that's worth sharing: contract mix, owner dependence, key staff.]

**What a retiring owner wants.**

A two-year retirement window isn't a problem. It's the deal. Owners in traditional industries want out, and they want to be looked after. They want certainty, a fair result and their staff kept on. They don't want to wait months for a bank.

So you never say vendor finance. You say getting paid over time. For an owner who's leaving anyway, being paid over ten years out of the business they built can mean more money than a lump sum. Vendor finance can pay above market, six to seven times instead of the usual three to five, and close in two to four weeks instead of six to sixteen. The numbers show how.

And if you have capital, this is where it earns more. You don't need to spend it as a deposit. It can sit behind the deal as the buffer and the security, while the business pays the seller.

**The offer.**

Once your board has been through the documents, the offer goes out as an NBIO or LOI. The usual range is 3 to 5 times profit. And revenue is not profit. A $3.8 million business could make a lot or a little. That's what diligence is for.

Then the cover check: yearly repayments no more than two thirds of free cash flow, which is the 1.5 rule. Keep the NBIO template ready, so the offer goes out the same week the documents are reviewed.

You don't need to know electrical businesses. Someone on your board should. That's what the board is for. Your job is the call, the documents and the structure.

Paid over time, the seller can receive far more than a lump-sum offer, and the business still covers the debt one and a half times. That's why vendor finance can pay above market and still work for the buyer.

Where people lose money.

- Making 30 calls and stopping. I made around a thousand before my first deal closed.
- Getting a "maybe" with no NDA ready, so it goes cold.
- Pricing off revenue instead of profit. On $3.8 million of revenue, that mistake can be worth millions.
- Losing the "not yet" owners. Keep a sheet: name, business, what they said, when to call back. Call, then email, then LinkedIn, months apart.
- Stopping calls once one NDA is signed. Walking away is your best negotiating tactic, and you can only walk away with other deals in the pipeline.

If you're going to buy off-market, you have no choice but to make the calls. The only choice is whether you make them with a script and a process, or without one. Or you keep scrolling listings and bidding against everyone else.

If you're someone who'd rather pick up the phone than wait for a broker's email, this is for you. Thirty days from now you've either still made zero calls, or a few hundred, with a short list of owners who said maybe.

## EP 95 · $5.5M zero down to a $200M exit: every stage, bank to private equity

Owning one good business is a phenomenal thing. Until you hit the ceiling of what one business can be. Then you go from growing to grinding.

My friend Jason Nagy's first deal was a $5.5 million radiology business. Zero down, a 60/40 structure. Seven years later he sold to private equity for $200 million.

Here's that deal in the order each party saw it. The bank, the seller, then the buyer who paid the big number.

Most of the money in a business is made at the exit, and most of the exit is the multiple. A small business usually sells for 3 to 5 times profit. A group private equity wants can sell for 8, 10, even 15 times. If you own one business at its ceiling, that gap is the biggest number you're not using. This is for people thinking in years. If you want a quick flip next quarter, skip it.

One line: the first deal isn't the payday. It's the platform.

If your business has plateaued, it has nothing to do with your work ethic or your market. It has everything to do with the multiple one small business sells for.

And the market has moved. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire, and many want to be paid over time. Private equity pays more for groups than for single businesses. The buyers who understand both are buying small and selling big.

Jason is a friend of mine, not a student. His deal shows the whole machine.

I help people choose and structure a first deal as a platform. Book a call below. Now the detail.

**What the bank saw: the 60%.**

A 60/40 structure commonly means about 60% of the price comes from a bank as commercial debt, and about 40% from the seller. The bank sees a business that services its share at 1.5 times cover, and it sees the seller staying in. That's a seller who still wants it to work.

On deals over about $5 million, banks often don't need a personal guarantee, because the assets give them their security. Typically. Every lender and deal is different. Ask.

[JOSH: what Jason's 60/40 actually was (bank and seller, or something else), and whether he gave a personal guarantee, only if he's happy to share.]

**What the seller saw: the 40%.**

The seller's 40% can be rolled into equity in the new business, or left in as a loan paid out over time. Either way, the seller keeps skin in the game, stays helpful during the handover, and is paid out of a business they know works. And the bank's 60% tells the seller a lender has already checked the numbers.

For you, zero down means every dollar of value built over the next seven years sits on equity you didn't pay cash for. That's the leverage. It's also why the cover rule is non-negotiable. Two lenders being paid from one business leaves no room for a structure that only works in a good year.

**The seven years between.**

Seven years is long. It's also normal. A roll-up usually takes three to ten years from first deal to exit.

What makes a first deal a platform? A fragmented industry with owners getting ready to retire, so there's always a second and a third to buy. Systems someone else could run, so you work on the next deal, not the front desk. A bank that's watched you make your repayments on time. And a board that's done it once with you. Without those, you've bought yourself a job.

In the first year or two not much looks like it's happening. You're learning the business, paying down debt, fixing the basics. Most people quit right there, just before it compounds.

The order is simple. First the platform. Then the second deal. Once you have three or four, shared management and shared systems. And once the group is big enough, private equity starts calling.

And decide the goal before you start. A big exit, a legacy business and a flip are built differently. Jason was building toward an exit, and you can see it in the result.

[JOSH: what Jason did in those seven years (more acquisitions, new sites, new services, or growth in the original business). One or two lines, only what he's happy to have said.]

**The shareholder terms.**

If you'll ever have minority holders, a board with founders' equity or a seller with rollover, set the terms on day one. Drag-along rights. No veto or voting control for minorities. So when the exit comes, one person can't hold it up.

**What private equity saw: the multiple.**

Private equity pays more for size, clean numbers, revenue that repeats, and a business that runs without the founder in every room.

[JOSH: what Jason's buyer valued most. No firm name unless it's public and he's happy to have it said.]

Look at it from their side. A single clinic is a job with a building. A group with ten sites, shared systems and a management team is an asset they can grow and sell again. They pay for the lower risk and the bigger runway. That's why a dollar of profit inside a group is worth more than the same dollar inside one small business.

That's multiple arbitrage. Same profit, different multiple.

On the first deal, the cover rule caps what the bank and seller can take out each year. On the group, $60 million of value comes from the multiple alone, before any profit growth.

Where people lose money.

- Buying a first business that doesn't fit a group, so there's no second deal to bolt on.
- Giving away equity early to avoid debt, and losing control of the exit.
- Signing shareholder terms that let one minority holder block a sale.
- Never deciding the goal. A big exit, a legacy business and a flip are built differently.

There are two paths up from one business. Alone, you learn every jump the hard way, one mistake at a time. Or you get the outside view before each jump, not after it.

On the call we work out your goal, your strategy and your first structure, and whether that's the Owners Academy, with the full course and daily deal discussion, or 1:1 advisory, where I work your structures and lender presentations with you. Equity or advisory roles are considered case by case.

If you're someone who'd rather build a group than grind at the ceiling of one business, this is for you. Thirty days from now you'll be thirty days older at the same ceiling. Or thirty days older with your goal written, your industry picked and your first platform deal in sight.

Book a call with me below. Not a setter. Me.

Results like Jason's aren't typical. The method that got there still is the method: buy well, grow, sell at a bigger multiple.

# Act 20 · The long game

## EP 96 · Time, money, energy: why money ranks last on a $0-deposit deal

How come people with a good salary, good credit and real savings so often never buy a business?

My first deal had no deposit. A physio clinic, 100% vendor finance. What it did take was about a thousand cold calls, and four deals that fell over first.

So money ranks last. Time and energy decide it. Here's how to score all three, and what each one is worth in a deal.

The structure carries the price. A retiring owner gets paid over time out of the business's profit, so the purchase price doesn't come out of your account. What nobody can finance is the work that finds the deal. Capital with no hours means you're still reading about this next year. Hours with no capital means slower, not shut out. This is for people who'll give it 40 hours a week. Not for anyone looking for a way to do it in four.

One line: you can borrow the money. You can't borrow the hours.

Three inputs, ranked from least to most important. Money. Time. Energy.

If you've been "working on this" for a year with no offer out, it has nothing to do with your intelligence, your background or your bank balance. It has everything to do with nobody showing you which input actually moves a deal.

I help people turn those three into a first deal, bought with little to no money down. If you've got the hours and want to know what your capital changes, book a call below. Now the detail.

**Money: it buys speed, not permission.**

You were told you need a deposit. You were told you need a track record. You were told the bank decides.

None of that was true for my first deal. Seven years at 4%. A nine-month payment holiday. Rent in arrears. No deposit.

So the structure buys the business. Money buys three other things. Speed: with cash, an existing business or property behind you, a first company in the next 30 days becomes possible. Room for mistakes, because deal one will have some. And runway, so you can live while the business pays its debt first.

The rule: the money question isn't "do I have a deposit?" It's "how many months can I live without drawing much from the first business?" Write that number down.

**Time: income hours, not busy hours.**

Time means hours you can name on a calendar from Monday. 40 at the very least. 60 is better. 100 is ideal while you're building.

Most people who say they're giving this 20 hours are giving it 20 hours of reading. Podcasts. Spreadsheets. Another video. Like this one.

Income hours put you in front of a seller, a board member or a lender. Calls, offers, lender interviews. Everything else is support.

An illustrative 40-hour week: 15 hours on calls, 10 on board outreach and lenders, 10 on financials, NDAs and offers, 5 on everything else.

My thousand calls was time. There's no shortcut through it. You can only get through it faster. If the hours aren't on the calendar, they aren't committed.

**Energy: the input that kills more deals than money.**

Energy is where your attention actually goes. Nobody measures it.

I've had people pay for advisory and still not take action. Paid, signed up, then nothing.

Low energy looks like three side projects. A plan to start "after this quarter." A spouse who hasn't been asked. Another book before the first call. Every hour on a side project is an hour not on the phone.

The rule: one thing. If this isn't the thing, the hours on the calendar won't survive the first hard week.

**The audit: score yourself 1 to 3.**

Time. One is "when I can." Two is ten to twenty named hours. Three is 40-plus, on the calendar, from Monday.

Money. One is nothing. Two is some savings. Three is cash, a business or property you could put to work.

Energy. One is "still deciding." Two is "doing it, alongside three other things." Three is "this is the thing."

A one on money is fine. A one on time or energy isn't. I'd rather work with a 3-1-3 than a 3-3-1. The second person has the money and will still be watching videos next year.

Fix them in order. Time first, because hours are the only thing you control this week. Energy follows, because the calls start giving you real conversations. Then the money question answers itself, because you're looking at one business's numbers instead of a fear.

Same thousand calls, same structure, same zero deposit. The ten-hour buyer hears the same "yes" about three months later, and a retiring owner who's ready now sells to someone else.

Where people lose money.

- Saving for a deposit the structure didn't need. Years of not owning, and the bank can still say no.
- Paying yourself from day one. It eats the payment holiday, and the first slow quarter puts you behind on the seller.
- Counting reading as hours. A year of "research" with no offer out is a year of a seller's profit going to someone else.
- Paying for help and not acting. The cost of advice with zero offers out is all cost.

There are two paths from here. Alone, the audit's free and the calls are free. And alone, your hours drift back to reading because nobody asks how many calls you made, and your energy leaks into whatever's newest. Most people on that path take years and lose deals. The other path is with someone who's already made the thousand calls and lost the four deals.

On the call, after a short application, we do today's audit on your real numbers. Your hours from Monday. Your assets. Your goal. What would stop you starting this week. You leave with your strategy, your first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who scored a three on time and a three on energy, and you're done treating money as the reason, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you'll be 30 days older either way. Or 30 days older with a few hundred calls made and a first structure on paper.

## EP 97 · 3 negotiation mistakes that turn a $2M deal into $2.5M

Two buyers look at the same business, making $300,000 profit.

James has the cash and one deal he's fallen for. The other buyer has five conversations, a number written down, and a board who'll tell him no. The seller asks both for $2.5 million. James pays it. The other buyer says "No worries. Good luck." And ends up with the better deal.

Here are the three mistakes that cost James half a million.

On this business, $500,000 of price is the difference between the business covering its repayments 1.5 times and 1.2 times. At 1.5 a slow year is uncomfortable. At 1.2 you're borrowing to pay the seller. This is for people sending offers, or about to, especially with capital and a fear of overpaying. It isn't for anyone who thinks negotiating means being nice until the seller gives in.

Walk-away power is the ability to say "no worries, good luck" and mean it. It isn't a tactic on the day. It's a position you build before the meeting.

Three mistakes destroy it, ranked. The last one is the one most people get backwards.

I help people find, structure and negotiate a first business, bought with little to no money down. If you want someone beside you when a seller pushes, book a call below. Now the mistakes.

**MISTAKE 1. One deal in the pipeline.**

If you've ever overpaid, or nearly did, it has nothing to do with being soft or bad with numbers. It has everything to do with having one deal.

You've spent three months on it. You've told your partner. You've pictured the first day. The seller asks for more, and you hear yourself say "I'll see what I can do."

A buyer with five live deals can leave, and sellers can tell. That's why you run parallel pipelines and never single-thread a deal. My friend Elliot in the US sent about 40 offers to get three signed LOIs, all at 100% seller finance.

[JOSH: one deal you walked away from: what the seller asked, what you said, what happened next.]

The fix: never fewer than three live conversations. If you have one, make 50 calls this week.

Walk-away power comes in an order, and none of it happens on the day. First a pipeline. Then, once you have deals, a number. Then a team, a board and advisers who'll say "this one's not it" when you're too close. And once you have all that, a runway. If you need this deal to pay next month's rent, you can't walk.

**MISTAKE 2. Walking in without a number.**

Most people think negotiating is a personality. Hard men win, nice people get taken.

That's not how it works. The buyer who wins did the maths before the meeting.

The business makes $300,000. Free cash flow has to cover repayments at least 1.5 times. $300,000 divided by 1.5 is $200,000 a year to the seller. Over ten years, about $2 million. Interest left out to keep it simple.

The seller says $2.5 million. Same ten years, that's $250,000 a year, and your cover drops to 1.2.

So $2 million over ten years is your walk line. You don't think in the room. You check the number.

When you hear "I've got other buyers interested," the answer is the same whether it's true or a bluff: "That's great, you should talk to them. My offer's here if it doesn't work out." If they have a better buyer, you were never getting it. If they don't, they call back. And to stop a deal drifting for months: "This offer's open for 24 hours."

**MISTAKE 3. Moving your line instead of the terms.**

This is the counterintuitive one. You can say yes to $2.5 million.

When a seller wants more, change the shape, not your line. A longer term. A later start to repayments. Part of the price tied to performance.

$2.5 million over 12 and a half years is $200,000 a year. Your cover is back to 1.5. The seller gets the headline number. You keep a business that carries its debt.

Remember what the seller is really comparing you to. Not another buyer. Their own Monday morning. Another three years running the place, or a bank that takes 16 weeks. Never say "vendor finance" to them. Say "getting paid over time."

And if no shape works, you walk. That's what a line is for.

"But if I walk, I lose the deal." Sometimes. A deal you lose by holding 1.5 times is usually a deal that would have hurt you. "The seller will be offended." Not if you're calm. A seller handing over their life's work wants a buyer who knows their number.

Column one is how capital gets lost. Columns two and three are the same deal for you, and only one of them gets the seller to yes.

Where people lose money.

- Moving the line $100,000 at a time because you're three months in and tired. Two moves and you're at 1.2×.
- Chasing after "other buyers." It tells the seller you have nowhere else to go.
- Letting an offer sit open for months. The seller shops it, and you've stopped calling.
- Needing the deal to pay next month's rent. No runway, no walk-away power.

You can keep doing it the way most buyers do. One deal at a time, falling for it, the only person in the room who's emotional. Or you can have someone outside the deal who's seen the shape before and says "walk" when you can't.

On the call, a short application, then me. Your capital, your hours, your industry, any live deal. We set your walk line and your first structure, and work out whether the Owners Academy, where you can post a seller's counter and get eyes on it the same day, or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone with capital who refuses to overpay, and you'd rather lose a deal than sign a bad one, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you'll be 30 days older either way. Or 30 days older with a written walk line and three live conversations.

## EP 98 · Every stage from 1 business to a 5-business group (year 0 to year 10)

Owning one business is a phenomenal thing. You set the hours. You keep the profit.

Until it can't grow past you. Then it goes from an asset to a very well-paid job.

A business making $500,000 is worth about $2 million at four times profit. Five of them, run as one clean group, can be worth $20 million to a bigger buyer. Here's every stage between the two.

The gap between four times and eight times is where the money in this whole model is made. It's called multiple arbitrage: small businesses sell cheap, groups sell dear. But you only get the higher multiple if the group can run, borrow and be sold without you. This is for owners and buyers who want a group: a roll-up, a legacy, something a bigger buyer wants. If you want one small business forever, this is more plan than you need.

One line: the plan runs in stages, not years, and each stage has one constraint.

Five stages. Get ready. Buy one. Buy more. Build the group. Sell or hold. Your job changes at every one. Most people get stuck because they keep doing the last stage's job.

I help people build this, starting with a first business bought with little to no money down and structured so it can be the base of a group. Book a call below. Now the stages.

**STAGE 0. Get ready (about six months).**

Build a board of credible operators, paid in founders' equity, 2 to 10% each. Line up lawyers and accountants paid at closing. Interview lenders before you need one. Start the calls.

Your job: caller. The constraint: volume. Every hour is calls, board outreach or lenders.

**STAGE 1. Buy one (year 1).**

The first deal is usually the hardest. Mine took about a thousand calls and four deals that fell over. Pick a business that covers its debt 1.5 times and that you could build on. Asset-rich, boring, bankable.

Your job switches to owner overnight. Keep the staff, keep the customers, keep the seller close for the handover. Then fix price and margin, then a way to bring in customers on purpose.

The constraint is you. Until there's a general manager, you own a job.

**STAGE 2. Buy more (years 2 to 3).**

If you own one business and you're stuck, it has nothing to do with your work ethic, your industry or your age. It has everything to do with nobody showing you that business two is bought differently from business one.

Business two is easier. You have a track record. You have a lender who's watched you make repayments. You have cash flow.

My friend Cam did two deals in 23 days. Both 100% vendor finance, interest-only for ten years with balloons in years three and ten, and $500,000 of working capital from the bank. Not because Cam had advantages you don't. Because he knew the structure.

Your job: buyer and manager. Hire a GM for business one so you can source business two. Set up a holding company so each business sits in its own entity. Give minority holders no veto and put a drag-along in from the start, so the group can be sold cleanly.

The constraint is management. Not money. People who can run a site without you.

**STAGE 3. Build the group (years 4 to 6).**

Finance, HR and marketing get done once for every business instead of five times. You add smaller bolt-ons that slot in. Maybe a competitor.

Your job: manager of managers. Monday numbers. Board meetings. Few decisions, made well.

The constraint is cleanliness. A year spent on clean books and businesses that run without their old owners can add more value than another acquisition, because that's exactly what a buyer checks.

**STAGE 4. Sell or hold (years 7 to 10).**

Sell, and you collect the multiple gap. My friend Jason Nagy did the real version: a $5.5 million radiology business with nothing down, sold to private equity for $200 million seven years later.

Or hold. Let the debt get paid down and take the cash flow. Both are fine. Next episode is about choosing on purpose.

The rule behind the whole ladder: one constraint per stage. Volume, then you, then management, then cleanliness, then the decision. Fix the one in front of you and leave the rest. And the test for where you are: if you stopped working for a month, what would happen to the profit? If it falls, you're still on stage 1, however many businesses you own. If it holds, you're ready for the next one.

"Ten years is too long." Think in stages. Getting ready takes months, and nothing says you have to sell at ten. "I can't picture running five businesses." You won't. You'll run the people who run them.

The profit didn't change between row three and row five. The multiple did, because the group can run without you. That's what stages 2 and 3 are buying.

Where people lose money.

- Buying business two before business one runs without you. Both go backwards, and both lenders notice.
- Structuring deal one in a way the group can't use. Minority holders with a veto can block the sale that pays for ten years of work.
- Staying in the caller's job after you own. The business owns you, and the multiple stays at four.
- Messy books at sale. A buyer discounts what they can't verify.

Two paths to year ten. Alone, every stage has a playlist in this series. And alone, people lose years: they buy too early, structure deal one for one deal, and stay in the wrong job. The other path is with someone thinking about year ten while you're on year one.

On the call, a short application, then me. Where you are on the ladder, your goal, your assets, your industry, and your next structure built to fit the group you want. Then whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who owns one business, knows it's capped, and would rather own the group than be the job, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you'll be 30 days older either way. Or 30 days older with a GM shortlist and a second target on paper.

## EP 99 · Jackpot vs legacy vs flip: the same $2M business bought three ways

There are only two types of business owners. The ones who decided what the business was for before they bought it, and the ones who find out when they try to leave.

Same business, $500,000 profit, $2 million. One buyer flips it for up to $3.5 million in three years. One holds it and keeps the $500,000 a year. One makes it part of a group sold at eight times. Three goals. Three different ways to buy it.

The goal sets what you buy, how you finance it, who you hire and when you sell. Get it wrong and the structure fights you: a flip with debt nobody can take over, a legacy business with repayments that eat the income you planned to live on. This is for owners and buyers after a big outcome. It isn't for the lifestyle business: one place, a few hours a week. Nothing wrong with it. It's just not what we do.

One line: pick the exit first, then build the deal backwards from it.

Three options. Jackpot, legacy, flip. Then a three-question test, and the verdict.

If you own a business and feel trapped in it, it has nothing to do with how hard you've worked or how good the business is. It has everything to do with nobody asking you what it was for.

On a call with me, your goal is the first question, because the structure follows it. Book below. Now the three options.

**OPTION A. Jackpot: buy many, sell the group.**

Here's what's changed. A generation of owners in traditional industries is ready to retire. They don't want to wait 16 weeks for a bank. They want out and they want to be looked after. Getting paid over time can pay them above market, six to seven times against the usual three to five, and close in two to four weeks.

That's why one buyer can now line up several businesses in one industry. Buy at small-business multiples, combine, clean up, sell the group at the multiple a bigger buyer pays. My friend Jason Nagy's first deal was a $5.5 million radiology business, zero down, on a 60/40 structure. Private equity exit at $200 million seven years later.

What it changes: a fragmented industry, bank relationships for many deals, and clean books and a management team from deal one.

**OPTION B. Legacy: buy to keep.**

Buy something that will still be needed in 30 years. Healthcare. Trades. Services. Structure the debt so the business carries it comfortably. Once it's paid down, the profit is yours.

Debt over dilution matters most here, because the whole point is keeping it. I still own 73% of my company.

[JOSH: one line on which of the three you're running with your healthcare group, and why.]

What it changes: you can be more patient on price because you're not selling. You care about people and culture more than the multiple. And you set up ownership, often a holding company, so it passes on cleanly. Get advice for your situation.

**OPTION C. Flip: buy one, fix, sell.**

Buy the $500,000 business at four times, $2 million, paid over time. Over three years, raise prices, cut costs nobody notices, and put in a manager so it runs without the owner. Profit goes to $700,000. Sold at four to five times, that's $2.8 to $3.5 million before paying off what's left of the debt.

What it changes: you hunt for obvious fixes, not perfect businesses. And the debt has to match the exit date. If you sell in year three, know what's owed then, and whether a buyer can take it over or pay it out. A year-three balloon is a gift or a trap, depending on your plan.

**THE TEST.**

Three questions, in order.

How long will you do this hard? Ten years of intensity is jackpot. Three is flip. A lifetime at a steady pace is legacy.

What do you want at the end? A cheque. A business your family owns. Or capital and a track record for the next one.

What do you have? Cash, a business or property lets you move faster on any path.

Verdict: pick one. You can change later. You can't run two at once. A jackpot buyer reinvests every dollar into the next deal. A legacy buyer takes the cash out. Try both and you build a group you can't sell while taking cash it needs.

And let the goal rise. Plenty of people start with a flip and finish with a group, because the first deal showed them what was possible. Changing goals between deals is fine. Running two inside one deal isn't.

"Jackpot is just for people with money." Jason's first deal was zero down. A roll-up is a structure problem, not a savings problem. "I don't know my goal yet." Pick the one that fits today. A flip is a fine first goal. You'll know more after deal one.

Same purchase. The last row is where owners lose money: the structure that suits one goal breaks the other two.

Where people lose money.

- A flip carrying a ten-year seller note nobody can take over. The sale stalls in year three.
- A jackpot built on a first deal that was never set up to combine. Re-papering costs time and the buyer's confidence.
- A legacy business with debt that eats the cash you planned to live on.
- No goal at all. Twenty years in, a business you can't sell, can't pass on and can't leave.

Or you can keep doing what most owners do. No goal. Structured for one, living another. Or you set the goal first, with me, and let it shape the deal from the first offer.

On the call, a short application, then me. Goal first. Then your hours, your assets, your industry, and what would stop you starting this week. You leave with your strategy and first structure, and whether the Owners Academy or 1:1 advisory fits.

If you're someone who owns a business and wants to know what it's for, or someone about to buy who wants the goal right first, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you'll be 30 days older either way. Or 30 days older with one word written down and a deal shaped around it.

## EP 100 · 100 episodes in one deal: $250K profit, $0 down, all five stages

This video isn't for most people who'll click on it. Not for anyone after a four-hour work week. Not for anyone waiting for permission. Not for anyone planning to watch for another year.

For everyone else: a business making $250,000 profit. No deposit. The seller gets about $1.66 million over ten years, paid out of the business's own profit. That one deal, run ten times, is the whole series.

If you're in a job you can't own, running a business that runs you, stuck at one business's ceiling, or sitting on capital earning less than it should, it's the same map. It's for people who can give this 40-plus hours a week, want a big outcome, and will act before they feel ready. Episode 100. Every stage, one deal, and the most direct pitch I'll make in the series.

One line: borrow credibility, let the seller be your bank, close off-market, grow the profit, sell the group.

Five stages. Credibility, Capital, Closing, Grow, Exit. I'll run one deal through them in the order the seller and the bank see it.

If you've watched a lot of this series and haven't sent an offer, it has nothing to do with your intelligence, your CV or your bank account. It has everything to do with waiting for a feeling that never comes. There is never a perfect moment. Decide at 60%.

I help people run that whole line. Book a call below: a short application, then me. Now the deal.

**Credibility: what the seller sees first.**

You have no track record, so you borrow one. A board of credible operators, paid in founders' equity, 2 to 10% each. Lawyers and accountants paid at closing. A one-page executive summary. Now the seller and the bank are dealing with a team, not a person.

I found my board through LinkedIn and cold outreach at 25, with no network. The founder of the Australian Institute of Fitness is a shareholder in my company today, and I still own 73% of it. My student Jy Kingston came from IT with no finance background and built his board in under 30 days.

**Capital: what the bank sees.**

Before I knew how this worked, I made about a thousand calls and watched four deals fall over. After, my first deal: a physio clinic, 100% vendor finance, seven years at 4%, a nine-month payment holiday, no deposit. I took $150,000 out in year one.

Not luck. The structure worked on the business's numbers, not mine.

The speed run. $250,000 profit, divided by 1.5, is about $166,000 a year to the seller. Over ten years, about $1.66 million. That's above most cash offers, and the $84,000 left each year is what keeps you alive in a slow one.

Two rules, every deal. Free cash flow covers repayments at least 1.5 times. And debt over dilution, so you keep control and the exit. "No money down" is the hook. The 1.5 rule is the safety rail.

**Closing: what gets it signed.**

Find it off-market. Cold calls and one question: "Have you ever thought about selling?" Before any offer: three years of financials, matching tax returns, revenue by year. Offer three to five times profit in an LOI. Due diligence on the quality of the earnings. Top-tier lawyers.

The best negotiation tactic is to walk away. The second best is a time limit. You earn both with volume. My friend Elliot sent about 40 offers to get three LOIs at 100% seller finance.

**Grow: what makes it worth more.**

Settlement isn't the finish line. Price and margin first, because a price rise the customers barely notice goes straight to profit. Then a way to buy customers instead of waiting for them. Then a team, so it runs without you. Each one raises the profit, and the last one raises the multiple. A business that needs its owner sells at the low end of three to five. One that doesn't is the one a group buyer wants.

**Exit: where the money is made.**

Do it ten times. Ten businesses at $250,000, bought at about four times. $2.5 million of combined profit. A bigger buyer might pay eight times for a clean group: about $20 million.

My friend Jason Nagy did the real version. A $5.5 million radiology deal with nothing down. A $200 million exit to private equity seven years later.

Or hold it for your family. Or fix one and sell it. Pick one.

Under all five sit three inputs. Time, hours you can name on a calendar. Money, which buys speed, not permission. Energy, where your attention actually goes.

That's the series at three depths. Season one, episodes 1 to 40, was the model start to finish. Season two was owning it: dead deals, profit, sales, team, a group. Season three was the payoff: what private equity pays for, industry playbooks, advanced structures, real deal breakdowns, and this act. Everything else in a hundred episodes is detail on five words.

The left column is how you start. The right column is why you start.

Where people lose money.

- Watching instead of offering. A year of episodes and zero offers is a year of someone else buying the business.
- Giving away equity to investors when debt was available. You lose control and the exit.
- Signing below 1.5×. The structure breaks in the first slow quarter.
- Moving the walk line because you're tired. That's the most expensive $100,000 in the deal.
- Building for one goal and living another. You end up with a business you can't sell or keep.

Two paths, honestly. Alone: every stage is in this series, free. I mean that. If you send offers every week, build your own board and have someone experienced to ring when a seller pushes back, you may well be fine. What usually happens is a year of watching, then another. A structure that breaks in a slow quarter. A walk line moved by a hundred grand. Not because people aren't smart. Because nobody was next to them when it mattered.

You've been watching. Maybe all hundred. How many offers have you sent?

The other path is with me. Speed, and someone to ask.

The doubts, one line each. "I don't have any money." It's a structure problem, not a savings problem. "Nobody will take me seriously." Borrow credibility. "I can learn this for free." Yes, you can. How many offers have you sent? "The deal might die." Some will. Mine did, four times. "It sounds too good." Judge the mechanism: seller finance, the 1.5 rule, multiple arbitrage. Ask any lender or accountant. None of it needs you to trust me.

So here's the direct version. If you can give this 40 hours a week from Monday, want a big outcome and will act before you feel ready, book the call. Not after one more video.

Two ways in. The Owners Academy: the private community, the full course, every template and script, and deal discussion every day. Or 1:1 advisory through G&L M&A Advisory: I work your strategy, your structures and your lender presentations with you. Join, and you get the NBIO template, which a client was quoted about $7,000 for, and a 1:1 session with Jy.

The call: a short application, then me. Your goal, your hours, your assets, your industry, what would stop you starting this week. You leave with your strategy and your first structure, and whether either fits. If neither does, I'll tell you.

If you're someone who can give this the hours, wants the big outcome and will decide at 60%, you've already decided. Thirty days from now you'll be 30 days older. Or 30 days older with a board conversation started, a few hundred calls made and a first offer drafted. You know the whole map now. You can't unknow it. Every seller you meet from here, you're either the person who knows how this works and acts, or the person who knows and waits.
